Fundamentals / Physics / Electrodynamics

Electrodynamics

Calculate fields from sources and boundaries, then use Maxwell’s equations to explain radiation, field forces and the propagation of pulses in a guide or cable.

Chapters, problem sheets and the applied study in reading order. For a paper copy, use your browser’s print command. Open the worked solutions you want to include before printing.

Download course PDF with solutions

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    Charges and currents specify sources. Use symmetry and boundary conditions to turn their integral laws into field predictions, then ask how changing flux produces a voltage around a loop.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Combine the electric and magnetic energy balances to follow energy through space. A time-dependent source can radiate. Far-field expansions and interface conditions determine which parts of a source remain observable at a distance.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Conductivity, walls and material interfaces change how a field propagates. Follow a signal through a guide and along a transmission line, then integrate the field stress to find the force on matter.

Study electrodynamics by question

Each route connects an introductory calculation, a field method and an application. Follow one question across the book or use the numbered contents to work through the full course.

Chapter 1 · Charge, potential and field energy

Reconstruct both the local electric field and the total energy of a charged body from the same continuous charge distribution.

From first principles

Count the charge within a spherical surface

A potential probe gives a number at a point. To predict that reading from charge density, begin with two physical inputs in vacuum. Gauss’s law relates the electric flux through a closed surface to its enclosed charge. In a static field the work per unit charge between two points is independent of path. For a spherically symmetric density \(\rho(r)\), contributions in tangential directions cancel. The radial field has the same magnitude everywhere on a sphere of radius \(r\). A shell of radius \(s\) and thickness \(ds\) contains charge \(4\pi s^2\rho(s)\,ds\). Hence

\[Q(r)=4\pi\int_0^r \rho(s)s^2\,ds,\]
\[4\pi r^2 E_r(r)=Q(r)/\epsilon_0 .\]

For a sphere of radius \(R\) carrying uniform density, the enclosed charge grows as \(r^3\) inside and stops growing outside. The surface through which the flux passes continues to grow as \(r^2\). Dividing gives two different field laws:

\[E_r(r)=\frac{\rho r}{3\epsilon_0}\ (r\le R),\]
\[E_r(r)=\frac{\rho R^3}{3\epsilon_0 r^2}\ (r\ge R).\]

Choose zero potential at infinity and integrate the work inward. For an interior probe split the path at the surface so that the appropriate field is used on each segment:

\[V(r)=\int_R^\infty E_{\rm out}(s)\,ds+\int_r^R E_{\rm in}(s)\,ds=\frac{\rho}{6\epsilon_0}(3R^2-r^2).\]

The centre has a finite voltage even though its field vanishes. The probe measures the accumulated work along a path, while the field is the local slope of that voltage.

Derivation and applications

How an interior voltage reveals a radial charge gradient

An outside probe cannot distinguish spherically symmetric charge distributions with the same total charge. To see what an inside probe adds, suppose an insulating sphere of radius \(R\) holds an immobile charge density \(\rho(r)=\rho_0(1-r/R)\) in vacuum. Ignore polarisation of the material and set potential zero at infinity. A spherical shell of thickness \(dr\) contributes its density times \(4\pi r^2dr\). The resulting enclosed charge is

\[q(r)=4\pi\rho_0\left(\frac{r^3}{3}-\frac{r^4}{4R}\right),\]
\[Q=q(R)=\frac{\pi\rho_0R^3}{3}.\]

Rotational symmetry fixes the direction of the field and makes its magnitude constant over the Gaussian sphere. Substituting the enclosed charge, then eliminating the density amplitude using the measured total charge, gives

\[E_r(r)=\frac{q(r)}{4\pi\epsilon_0r^2}=\frac{kQ}{R^2}(4x-3x^2),\]
\[x=\frac rR,\]
\[k=\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_0}.\]

At the surface this expression joins the exterior Coulomb field. Positive charge concentrated towards the centre makes the field peak before reaching the surface. Potential instead accumulates the contributions of the field along the whole radial path. Starting from the known surface potential fixes the integration constant even when the voltmeter only accesses the interior.

\[V(r)=\frac{kQ}{R}+\int_r^R E_r(s)\,ds=\frac{kQ}{R}(2-2x^2+x^3).\]

Worked example

Identify an unknown charge profile

A specimen of radius 60.0 mm has a surface voltage of 30.0 V relative to a distant reference. The inferred charge is \(Q=VR/k=0.200\) nC. The gradient model predicts a centre voltage of \(2(30.0)=60.0\) V, so a differential probe between centre and surface should read 30.0 V. A uniformly charged sphere with that same outside voltage gives 45.0 V at the centre. A sufficiently small probe could distinguish these source profiles with a single additional reading.

Second application

Find where a field probe reads its maximum

For another sphere, \(R=90.0\) mm and \(Q=0.900\) nC. Differentiate \(4x-3x^2\) with respect to \(x\). Its derivative vanishes at \(x=2/3\), with negative second derivative. The peak is therefore 60.0 mm from the centre and has magnitude \((4/3)kQ/R^2=1.33\) kV/m. The surface value is only 0.999 kV/m. The enclosed charge still grows beyond the peak, but increasingly dilute outer shells provide too little additional flux to keep pace with the increasing area.

Start from this chapter. For concentric conductors, use a cylindrical Gaussian surface and integrate the radial field to get a voltage. Compare the capacitance per length with the numerical charge on the centre conductor.

OneSimulation laboratory · Recover cable impedance from a cross-section

Can an exterior field tell you where the charge sits?

A spherical cloud and a thin shell each carry charge \(Q\) and have the same outer radius \(R\). Before moving either slider, sketch the two fields along a radius. Which part of the sketch could distinguish the sources in a measurement?

A Gaussian sphere of radius \(r\) encloses \(Q(r/R)^3\) in the solid ball for \(r<R\). Dividing by its area gives \(E=Qr/(4\pi\epsilon_0R^3)\). The shell encloses zero until its surface. Outside, both enclose the same \(Q\). The shell curve shows the exterior value at the discontinuity.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Keep \(Q\) fixed and double \(R\). The maximum field falls by a factor of four, while the interior slope falls by a factor of eight. Explain why those are distinct measurements.

Why a charge in an ionic solution has a finite electrostatic range

Put a weakly charged small bead in a large bath of two mobile ion species carrying charges \(+e\) and \(-e\). Each species has equilibrium number density \(n_0\) far away. The solvent is a continuum with uniform permittivity \(\epsilon\), and a heat bath fixes temperature \(T\). In a vacuum the measured potential of a spherical charge falls as \(1/r\), even very far from it. In this liquid the electric field redistributes material, and the material changes the field. We can determine the decay law and the cloud that produces it using the same Gauss law. Fix the potential reference at infinity, where the liquid is electrically neutral.

Let thermal motion compete with electrical energy

An ion's local electrostatic energy is \(+eV\) or \(-eV\). In dilute thermal equilibrium its local density acquires a Boltzmann factor, \(n_\pm=n_0\exp[\mp eV/(k_BT)]\), because the far-away solution acts as a reservoir of particles. A positive bead repels positive ions and attracts negative ions. The mobile charge density is therefore \(\rho_m=e(n_+-n_-)=-2en_0\sinh[eV/(k_BT)]\). This is a source in Poisson's equation, not a new constitutive law for the solvent. We have separately included the solvent's polarisation in \(\epsilon\).

If \(|eV|\ll k_BT\), expand to first order. The symmetric mixture cancels even powers. This gives \(\rho_m=-(2e^2n_0/k_BT)V\), and an externally prescribed bead charge density \(\rho_b\) now satisfies

\[\nabla^2 V-\kappa^2 V=-\rho_b/\epsilon,\]
\[\kappa^2=\frac{2e^2n_0}{\epsilon k_BT}.\]

The inverse length \(\kappa\) came from comparing the field-curvature coefficient with the charge response. Increasing either mobile species' density shortens the distance over which a disturbance survives. Increasing temperature weakens the concentration change caused by a given voltage. Within these assumptions that length grows as \(\sqrt T\) at fixed permittivity and density.

Solve the exterior and test its near-source assumption

In the liquid outside a spherical bead of radius \(a\), put \(v=rV\). The radial Laplacian becomes \(v''/r\), so \(v''-\kappa^2v=0\). Discard the exponentially increasing solution at infinity. For a bead with fixed free charge \(q\) and no mobile ions inside, take a spherical Gaussian surface just outside the surface. Spherical symmetry and the boundary condition give \(-4\pi a^2\epsilon V'(a^+)=q\). Solving for the remaining coefficient yields

\[V(r)=\frac{q}{4\pi\epsilon(1+\kappa a)}\frac{e^{-\kappa(r-a)}}r,\]
\[E_r(r)=\frac{q}{4\pi\epsilon(1+\kappa a)}e^{-\kappa(r-a)}\left(\frac1{r^2}+\frac{\kappa}r\right),\]
\[r\ge a .\]

The maximum potential magnitude in the liquid occurs at the bead surface. Its value \(q/[4\pi\epsilon a(1+\kappa a)]\) therefore tests the linearisation everywhere outside. Shrinking a bead at fixed \(q\) ultimately violates this local condition even if the distant tail remains small. An ideal point-source expression can guide a far-field fit without faithfully describing the molecular neighbourhood of the source.

Account for the apparent disappearance of charge

A collector of radius \(r\) measures free enclosed charge \(4\pi\epsilon r^2E_r\), counting the bead and the mobile ions. It therefore encloses the fraction

\[\frac{Q_{\rm enc}(r)}q=\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa a}e^{-\kappa(r-a)} .\]

At the bead it reads one. At infinity it tends to zero. The missing charge is precisely the countercharge in the surrounding liquid. It can be verified independently without differentiating the field: integrate \(4\pi r^2\rho_m=-4\pi\epsilon\kappa^2r^2V\) from \(a\) to infinity. The radial integral of \(r e^{-\kappa(r-a)}\) is \(a/\kappa+1/\kappa^2\), cancelling the factor \(1+\kappa a\). The resulting total mobile charge is \(-q\). A screening length describes a decay scale; charge is not abruptly cancelled on one particular shell.

For \(\kappa a=1\), at \(r=a+\kappa^{-1}=2a\) the voltage has fallen to \(e^{-1}/2=18.4\%\) of its surface value. Yet the collector still encloses \(3/(2e)=55.2\%\) of the bead charge. Voltage and enclosed charge are different operations on the solution. A simulation or probe returning the voltage ratio cannot be read directly as the fraction of ions that have neutralised the bead.

Predict what changes when the ion valence changes

For several ionic species with charge \(z_i e\), the Boltzmann expansion gives \(\rho_m=\sum_i z_i e n_{i0}-(e^2/k_BT)\sum_i z_i^2 n_{i0}V+\cdots\). Bulk electroneutrality cancels the first sum. The screening coefficient instead contains squared valences, \(\kappa^2=e^2\sum_i z_i^2n_{i0}/(\epsilon k_BT)\). The sign of a species determines which way it moves in a given field, while its contribution to linear screening is positive for either sign. A divalent ion both feels double the electrical energy change and carries double the charge when it rearranges.

Compare two salts at the same formula-unit number density \(n\). A fully dissociated 1:1 salt contributes \(2n\) to the squared-valence sum. A 2:1 salt with one divalent cation and two singly charged anions contributes \(4n+2n=6n\). The predicted length is smaller by \(1/\sqrt3\), even though both baths are electrically neutral. The comparison assumes dilute ideal ions with the same solvent response. At higher concentrations, ion-size and correlation effects can remove this simple dependence.

Recover a screening length from a pair of readings

A bead has radius 8.0 nm. Relative to infinity two probes read 3.0 mV at 12 nm and 0.90 mV at 20 nm. Infer the screening length and surface voltage within the model. How could a third probe falsify the interpretation?

Derive the result and check it independently

The ratio of \(rV\), not \(V\) alone, isolates the exponential. Here \(r_2V_2/(r_1V_1)=18/36=1/2\), so \(\kappa^{-1}=8.0/\ln2=11.54\) nm. Extrapolating to \(a\) gives \(V(a)=V(12)(12/8)e^{4/11.54}=6.36\) mV. At room temperature \(k_BT/e\) is approximately 25.7 mV. The largest ratio is 0.247 and the cubic correction in \(\sinh x/x\) there is about 1%, making linearisation a plausible first approximation.

Plot \(\ln[rV(r)]\) against \(r\) for a third and further radii. A single straight line with slope \(-\kappa\) must describe all of them, without any external calibration of \(q\). Curvature or dependence on the chosen pair of radii would challenge the isolated spherical linear model. Before interpreting a fitted slope as an ionic strength, independently measure or specify \(\epsilon,T\), species charges and background electrostatic conditions.

Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 6 · screening and the Debye–Hückel model

Enclose the source before integrating the field

Imagine an insulating sphere charged uniformly throughout its volume. A probe outside it measures the field of a point charge, yet that representation fails at its centre. Our first objective is to see how a local charge distribution determines which measurements can distinguish the two sources. Use electrostatics in vacuum, with a sphere of radius \(R\) and total free charge \(Q\). A conductor in electrostatic equilibrium would distribute its excess charge differently.

Gauss’s law states that the electric flux through any closed surface is the enclosed charge divided by \(\epsilon_0\). Flux is the integral of the component normal to the surface. Pulling the field magnitude outside that integral requires symmetry in addition to the law itself. For this sphere rotational symmetry forces the field to be radial and of equal magnitude everywhere on a concentric spherical surface. The test surface can pass through the charged material, since it is a geometrical construction and does not insert a conductor.

Match the interior and exterior solutions

The uniform density is \(\rho=3Q/(4\pi R^3)\). At a radius \(r<R\) the enclosed charge is \(Q(r/R)^3\). At \(r>R\) the surface encloses all of \(Q\). Substitution in \(4\pi r^2 E_r=Q_{\rm encl}/\epsilon_0\) gives an interior field \(E_r=Qr/(4\pi\epsilon_0 R^3)\) and an exterior field \(E_r=Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2)\). The field vanishes at the centre and is continuous at the surface. A finite change in volume density changes the slope there. A true surface sheet of charge could instead create a finite jump in the normal field.

Since a static field has zero curl, a scalar potential can be chosen such that \(\mathbf E=-\nabla V\). Take \(V(\infty)=0\) and integrate the radial field inward. The exterior potential equals \(Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0r)\). To reach an interior point, continue the integral from the surface with the interior field. This gives

\[V(r)=\frac{Q}{8\pi\epsilon_0 R}\left(3-\frac{r^2}{R^2}\right),\qquad 0\le r\le R.\]

Matching at the surface supplies the additive constant which an interior integration alone cannot fix. A zero electric field at the centre means that the slope of the potential vanishes there. Its value need not vanish.

Assemble the sphere and account for the work

Build the charge by adding successive spherical shells while holding the final density fixed. When the assembly reaches radius \(r\), its charge is \(q(r)=Q(r/R)^3\). Bringing a small additional shell \(dq=3Qr^2dr/R^3\) from infinity costs \(dW=q(r)dq/(4\pi\epsilon_0 r)\). Integration gives \(W=3Q^2/(20\pi\epsilon_0 R)\). This positive work is stored in the electrostatic configuration. The shell’s interaction with itself is higher order in the infinitesimal added charge.

A complementary description uses the energy density \(\epsilon_0 E^2/2\). Integration over the exterior alone gives \(Q^2/(8\pi\epsilon_0R)\), which is five sixths of the total. The remainder resides inside the charge distribution. A thin spherical conducting shell of equal radius and charge produces the same outside field, yet it has no interior field in its empty cavity. Its total energy is therefore lower.

Interpret three probe readings

For \(R=0.10\) m and \(Q=2.0\) nC, the surface field is \(1.80\times10^3\) V/m. At half the radius the field halves to about 899 V/m. At twice the radius it is roughly 449 V/m. The surface potential is 180 V, while the centre is near 270 V. Their difference predicts the work per unit charge required to carry a small positive test charge from surface to centre. A sufficiently large test charge would disturb the source distribution and violate the assumed measurement model.

The assembly energy is only \(2.16\times10^{-7}\) J. Doubling \(Q\) multiplies the field and potential by two but multiplies the stored energy by four. A comparison of fields outside two spherical samples probes their total charge. A measurement of assembly work at fixed radius can reveal how that charge was distributed inside them.

If the charge is reduced gradually while its spatial shape is preserved, the surface voltage is proportional to total charge. The incremental work needed to assemble the next element therefore grows with charge already present. Integrating that growth explains the familiar factor of one half in electrostatic self-energy expressions.

Insert a dielectric into a spherical capacitor

An enclosed-charge construction also works when different radial regions store different electric energies. Put a conducting sphere of radius \(a\) inside a concentric thin conducting shell of radius \(b\). Give the inner sphere free charge \(+Q\) and the outer shell \(-Q\). Fill \(a<r<c\) with a linear dielectric of permittivity \(\epsilon_1\) and \(c<r<b\) with \(\epsilon_2\). There is no free charge at the interface. Assume the shells and both materials share a common centre so that a spherical Gaussian surface has uniform radial displacement.

Gauss's law for free charge gives \(D_r=Q/(4\pi r^2)\) throughout the gap. The electric field in material \(i\) is \(D_r/\epsilon_i\). Thus the normal field can jump at \(c\) even though normal displacement is continuous. To predict the measured voltage, integrate the radial field outward from \(a\) to \(b\); the positive quantity obtained is the inner voltage minus the outer voltage. This yields

\[\frac1C=\frac{V_a-V_b}{Q}=\frac1{4\pi}\left[\frac{1/a-1/c}{\epsilon_1}+\frac{1/c-1/b}{\epsilon_2}\right].\]

The sum is naturally a sum of inverse capacitances since the same flux passes through both layers and their voltage drops add. Replacing the two dielectric constants by a simple average would weight every radial thickness equally, despite the field being stronger near the small inner sphere. Setting the two permittivities equal cancels the arbitrary interface location, a useful check on the integration.

Decide where a small amount of dielectric matters most

Choose \(a=10.0\) mm, \(c=20.0\) mm, \(b=40.0\) mm, \(\epsilon_1=4\epsilon_0\) and \(\epsilon_2=\epsilon_0\). The radial contributions after factoring out \(1/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\) are \((100-50)/4=12.5\) m⁻¹ and \(50-25=25.0\) m⁻¹. Hence \(C=4\pi\epsilon_0/(37.5\,{\rm m}^{-1})=2.97\) pF. A charge of \(0.300\) nC produces about 101 V, one third across the inner material and two thirds across the outer one. An all-vacuum device of the same radii has \(C=1.48\) pF.

For a further check integrate local energy density \(u=\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf D/2\) over each layer. The integration element \(4\pi r^2dr\) reduces the energy integral to \(Q^2(1/r_{\rm in}-1/r_{\rm out})/(8\pi\epsilon_i)\). The two contributions are again in the ratio one to two, summing to \(Q^2/(2C)=15.2\) nJ. Moving dielectric towards the inner conductor usually has a larger effect per unit volume because the undisturbed energy density is higher there. Near metal contact surfaces roughness or air pockets can make precisely this region differ from its intended permittivity.

At the interface voltage is continuous but radial field just inside the outer material is four times its value just inside the inner material for these permittivities. A finite jump in voltage would imply an infinitely strong field over zero thickness, absent any imposed dipole sheet. Inserting these interfacial fields into the constitutive law gives identical normal displacement from either side. This is a more local test than comparing the terminal capacitance: a calculation can accidentally reproduce a similar integrated voltage while placing the field discontinuity on the wrong side of the boundary.

Infer a charge distribution from a radial field scan

Two spheres of equal radius have the same positive total charge. One has uniform volume density, the other stores charge in a thin spherical shell. Apply Gauss’s law to spheres of varying radius and scale the result by \(Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0R^2)\).

Chapter 2 · Conductors and boundary-value problems

Use the uniqueness of the potential to replace a grounded conductor by an image charge and then recover its induced surface charge.

From first principles

Why the conductor voltage determines the exterior field

In electrostatic equilibrium a mobile charge cannot have a tangential electric force along a conductor. The surface is therefore at one potential. A grounded wire fixes that potential at zero. In the surrounding vacuum \(\mathbf E=-\nabla V\). Apply Gauss’s law to a small volume and use the divergence theorem:

\[\oint_{\partial\Omega}\mathbf E\cdot d\mathbf a=\int_\Omega \rho/\epsilon_0\,dV,\]
\[\nabla^2V=-\rho/\epsilon_0 .\]

Why is it enough to find one potential with the correct sources and boundary values? Let \(w=V_1-V_2\) for any two candidates with the same charges. The source cancels, leaving \(\nabla^2 w=0\). Their common prescribed voltage gives \(w=0\) on the boundary. Integrate the product rule for \(\nabla\cdot(w\nabla w)\):

\[\int_\Omega |\nabla w|^2\,dV=\oint_{\partial\Omega}w\,\partial_n w\,dA-\int_\Omega w\nabla^2w\,dV=0.\]

The integrand is nonnegative, so the two gradients coincide throughout the domain. For an exterior problem we also prescribe decay at infinity. An image charge placed outside the physical solution domain can therefore generate the exact field inside it, provided the potential satisfies all of these conditions. The induced surface charge then follows from the outward normal field just outside the metal.

Derivation and applications

Find the image charge of a grounded sphere

Curvature changes both the location and the size of an electrostatic image. Place a point charge \(q\) a distance \(a\) from the centre of a grounded conducting sphere of radius \(R<a\). Take vacuum outside, zero potential at infinity and no other sources. Axial symmetry suggests placing a trial image \(q'\) on the same axis at distance \(b<R\). At a surface point making angle \(\theta\) with the source axis, let \(d\) and \(d'\) denote the distances to charge and image respectively.

\[d^2=R^2+a^2-2Ra\cos\theta,\]
\[d'^2=R^2+b^2-2Rb\cos\theta.\]

To cancel the potentials at every angle, the two distances must be proportional by one constant. Choose \(b=R^2/a\). Both the constant and cosine terms in \(d'^2\) then equal those in \(d^2\) times \(R^2/a^2\). This fixes the required image strength through the grounded boundary condition.

\[\frac{d'}d=\frac Ra,\]
\[\frac qd+\frac{q'}{d'}=0,\]
\[q'=-\frac Ra q.\]

This construction has the physical charge as its only exterior singularity. It decays at infinity and satisfies the prescribed surface voltage, so uniqueness fixes the exterior potential. A Gaussian surface enclosing the metal but excluding the source encloses exactly the image in the mathematical field. Its flux therefore identifies the net induced charge with \(q'\). The force on the source points towards the centre. Using \(k=1/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\) gives its radial component

\[F_a=\frac{kqq'}{(a-b)^2}=-\frac{kq^2Ra}{(a^2-R^2)^2}.\]

Worked example

Predict the balance reading near a metal sphere

Let \(R=50.0\) mm, \(a=100\) mm and \(q=2.00\) nC. The image is \(-1.00\) nC at 25.0 mm from the centre. The source and image are consequently 75.0 mm apart. The attractive force is \(8.99\times10^9(2.00\times10^{-18})/(0.0750)^2=3.20\) µN. A position clamp must supply the same magnitude outwards to hold the charge stationary.

Second application

Recover a source distance from the grounding lead

A sphere of radius 30.0 mm acquires \(-0.900\) nC when a positive 3.00 nC source is introduced from infinity. Inverting the induced-charge ratio gives \(a=R(3.00/0.900)=100\) mm, or 70.0 mm clear of the surface. The limit \(a\gg R\) explains the small charge transfer from ground at long range. An isolated, initially neutral sphere would instead require an additional central image to keep its net induced charge zero.

Start from this chapter. Move the centre conductor off axis while holding both conductor potentials fixed. The radial logarithmic solution loses its symmetry. Which integral of the numerical field still defines capacitance?

OneSimulation laboratory · Recover cable impedance from a cross-section

Measure the voltage that a grounded enclosure can hold inside

Imagine a long grounded square tube, one metre on each side. Uniformity along the tube reduces the electrostatic problem to its square cross section. A prescribed volume charge \(\rho_0\sin(\pi x/L)\sin(\pi y/L)\), with \(L=1\ \mathrm m\), vanishes at all four walls. Ground fixes the boundary voltage. Does that also fix the interior voltage to zero?

Gauss's law with \(\mathbf E=-\nabla V\) gives \(-\nabla^2 V=\rho/\epsilon_0\). Each second derivative of the sine product contributes \(-(\pi/L)^2\) times that product. Thus \(V_\mathrm{centre}=\rho_0 L^2/(2\pi^2\epsilon_0)\). The numerical experiment replaces \(-\nabla^2V\) at each interior node by \((4V_C-V_N-V_S-V_E-V_W)/h^2\). The charge is a single eigenmode of that stencil, so one conjugate-gradient step from zero solves the discrete system exactly in exact arithmetic.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

At 2 nC/m³, 12 cells per side give 11.50889 V at the centre, compared with 11.44331 V in the continuum. Double the grid resolution. Explain why the error falls close to fourfold even though both discrete solves have converged. Then double the charge and predict which of absolute and relative error changes.

The CSV contains every midline grid value. Compare its centre row with the plot after choosing the same charge and grid. The browser applies the five-point operator directly. The Python version assembles and solves the coefficient-form PDE with OneSimulation.

How a right-angle conducting corner shares an induced charge

Two grounded conducting planes meet at a right angle, occupying the faces \(x=0,\ y>0\) and \(y=0,\ x>0\). A long uniformly charged thin filament of line charge \(\lambda>0\) runs parallel to their intersection through position \((a,b)\), with \(a,b>0\). The vacuum region is the first quadrant. Quantities below are per unit length along the filament; its radius is negligible compared with both distances to the metal. Find the force on the filament and how the grounded faces divide the induced charge. Knowing that the two metal faces have identical voltage does not tell us how much charge each supplies.

Construct a potential that satisfies both metal faces

For an isolated line, Gauss's law gives \(E_R=\lambda/(2\pi\epsilon_0R)\); integration gives \(V=-[\lambda/(2\pi\epsilon_0)]\ln(R/R_0)\). A single line's potential has no zero reference at infinity. Our image system will have zero total line charge, so all arbitrary \(R_0\) factors cancel. Reflect the real source in the vertical face and reverse its sign. Reflect it independently in the horizontal face and reverse its sign again. Neither added source can be left without its own partner at the other face. The fourth line at \((-a,-b)\) must be positive. Denote distances to the real and image lines by \(R_{++},R_{-+},R_{+-},R_{--}\) following their coordinates. Then

\[V=\frac{\lambda}{2\pi\epsilon_0}\ln\frac{R_{-+}R_{+-}}{R_{++}R_{--}} .\]

At \(x=0\) distances cancel pairwise in the ratio, and the same happens at \(y=0\). The solution tends to zero at large distance and its only singularity in the physical domain is at the real filament. The grounded Dirichlet boundary and appropriate behaviour at the corner and infinity fix the field uniquely. If the faces were instead free to hold different voltages, this particular superposition alone would not solve their boundary conditions.

Read the signed force from three image fields

At the source position remove its own singular field. The vertical reflection attracts horizontally with field \(-\lambda/(4\pi\epsilon_0a)\); the horizontal reflection attracts vertically by the corresponding expression with \(b\). The doubly reflected positive image repels along \((a,b)\), partly compensating both attractions. Multiplying by \(\lambda\) gives

\[\frac{\mathbf F}{L}=\frac{\lambda^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\left[\left(-\frac1a+\frac{a}{a^2+b^2}\right)\hat{\mathbf x}+\left(-\frac1b+\frac{b}{a^2+b^2}\right)\hat{\mathbf y}\right].\]

In the symmetric case \(a=b\) the two components are equal. Each is exactly half as large as the attraction to its corresponding isolated face. The second grounded conductor changes the charge distribution of the first; simply adding two single-plane forces would overestimate the force. If \(a\gg b\), the horizontal force towards the remote vertical wall decays as \(b^2/a^3\), while the downward force approaches its single-plane value. The charge and its nearest opposite image look more like a dipole to the distant face.

Find the charge split by two independent routes

On the bottom face the surface normal from metal into vacuum is \(+\hat{\mathbf y}\). Computing \(-\epsilon_0\partial_y V(x,0^+)\) gives

\[\sigma_{\rm bottom}(x)=-\frac{\lambda b}{\pi}\left[\frac1{(x-a)^2+b^2}-\frac1{(x+a)^2+b^2}\right],\]
\[x>0 .\]

The second term reduces the charge near the corner and forces the density to vanish there. Integration from zero to infinity uses the antiderivative \(b^{-1}\arctan[(x\mp a)/b]\). The two terms give \(-2\lambda\arctan(a/b)/\pi\). Exchanging \(a,b\) gives the vertical face charge. Their sum is \(-\lambda\) because the two acute arctangents add to \(\pi/2\). For \(a=20\) mm and \(b=10\) mm the bottom and vertical faces receive respectively 70.5% and 29.5% of the induced negative charge. The closer face collects the greater magnitude, although both are perfectly grounded.

A reciprocity argument provides a check with no surface differentiation. Remove the filament and solve an auxiliary problem with the vertical face at 1 V and the bottom at 0 V. In polar coordinates the bounded angular solution is \(w=2\theta/\pi\) after division by 1 V, because its second angular derivative vanishes and its face values agree. The singular junction between ideal differently biased electrodes belongs only to this auxiliary problem. The induced charge per length on the real vertical face is \(-\lambda w(a,b)=-2\lambda\arctan(b/a)/\pi\). This reproduces the integral result and interprets the measured charge split as an angular coordinate.

Anticipate the field near a corner before refining a mesh

Empty space immediately around the wedge vertex contains no real source if \(a,b>0\). In planar polar coordinates a separated harmonic function vanishing on the two faces has form \(r^\nu\sin(\nu\theta)\), with \(\nu=m\pi/\alpha\) for opening angle \(\alpha\) and positive integer \(m\). For the first-quadrant region \(\alpha=\pi/2\), the leading generic term is \(r^2\sin(2\theta)\). Its field tends to zero linearly with distance from the vertex. Expanding the explicit bottom-face density above around \(x=0\) indeed gives a term proportional to \(x\), providing a local check on the image construction.

It is easy to transfer an intuition about sharp metal tips to the wrong side of the metal. A conducting projection surrounded by a vacuum angle greater than \(\pi\) has a leading exponent below unity. Differentiating its potential then gives a field that can diverge as the ideal corner is approached. The inside corner of the two grounded planes in this problem has the opposite local behaviour. Mesh refinement near either corner should be informed by which opening angle belongs to the field domain.

Infer what two electrode meters can measure

The induced vertical charge accounts for exactly one quarter of the total magnitude. A second experiment scales both source coordinates by a common factor of two. Determine the locus of the original source, the new charge split and the force ratio. Which observation could distinguish the two radii?

Derive the result and check it independently

The auxiliary potential gives \(\theta=\pi/8=22.5^\circ\). Hence \(b/a=\tan(\pi/8)=\sqrt2-1\) and any distance along that ray gives the same ideal charge split. Doubling \(a,b\) preserves that ratio. In the force formula every term scales inversely with length, so both force components halve at unchanged \(\lambda\). A force measurement or a resolved surface charge profile can distinguish the distances. Integrating each face first discarded that spatial information. In a numerical solution, compare both electrode integrals and the local force; matching a global charge split alone leaves the distance scale untested.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §§3.1–3.2 · uniqueness and image methods

Trade unknown surface charge for known potential

A point charge held above a large grounded metal plane attracts charge towards the region beneath it. The induced surface density is nonuniform and initially unknown. Direct integration of Coulomb’s law would therefore introduce the very quantity one wants to determine. In a boundary-value formulation the potential of the metal is known instead. Assume a perfectly conducting plane at \(z=0\), vacuum above it and a positive point charge \(q\) at \(z=a>0\). Grounding fixes the plane at zero potential and allows charge to enter or leave it.

Away from the point source the potential obeys Laplace’s equation, and at the charge its singular behaviour is the Coulomb potential. These properties with the boundary values determine a unique solution. To see why, subtract two candidate solutions with the same charges and Dirichlet boundary conditions. Their difference \(w\) has zero Laplacian and vanishes on the boundary. Integrating \(\nabla\cdot(w\nabla w)=|\nabla w|^2+w\nabla^2w\) over the domain gives zero net surface term, including the limit at infinity for appropriately decaying fields. Hence the integral of \(|\nabla w|^2\) vanishes and \(w=0\) throughout.

Construct the image

Put a fictitious charge \(-q\) at \(z=-a\). At any point on the plane its distance equals the distance to the actual charge, so the two potentials cancel pointwise. In the upper half-space the fictitious source lies outside the solution domain. The combined potential has the correct single singularity, is zero on the metal and decays at infinity. By uniqueness it is the upper-half-space solution. With \(s\) the horizontal distance from the projection of the charge,

\[V(s,z)=\frac{q}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{s^2+(z-a)^2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{s^2+(z+a)^2}}\right].\]

It is important to keep the domain in view. The expression continued below \(z=0\) describes an auxiliary mathematical problem. Inside the real conductor electrostatic equilibrium gives a constant potential and zero field. The fictitious charge is a way to reproduce the valid exterior data, rather than a literal localisation of the induced charge.

Recover the induced charge and force

The field just outside a conducting surface has no tangential component. Its outward normal component is \(\sigma_s/\epsilon_0\) because the field inside is zero. Differentiate the potential with respect to height and set \(z=0^+\) to obtain \(\sigma_s(s)=-qa/[2\pi(s^2+a^2)^{3/2}]\). Integrating over rings \(2\pi s\,ds\) from zero to infinity gives total induced charge \(-q\). A nearby grounded conductor connected to a reservoir can supply that total charge even though its potential remains fixed.

At the position of the real charge its own singular field is excluded when calculating force. The induced field equals that of the image at distance \(2a\), so the attraction has magnitude \(q^2/(16\pi\epsilon_0a^2)\). Integrating the force from infinity gives interaction energy \(-q^2/(16\pi\epsilon_0a)\). Simply multiplying the image potential at the charge by \(q\) would double the magnitude because the induced distribution adjusts as the charge moves. The work integral automatically includes the changing response.

Quantify the localisation

Take \(q=1.0\) nC and \(a=20\) mm. The force magnitude is \(5.62\) µN. The surface density directly beneath the charge is \(-q/(2\pi a^2)=-0.398\) µC/m². Within a disk of radius \(S\) the integral gives \(-q[1-a/\sqrt{S^2+a^2}]\). Setting the fraction in brackets equal to one half yields \(S=\sqrt3\,a=34.6\) mm. The density peaks directly below the charge, but a disk wider than its height is needed to collect half the total.

Bring the charge twice as close while keeping \(q\) fixed. Both the peak density and the attraction quadruple, while the radius containing half the induced charge halves. This spatial prediction is sensitive to finite plate size. A real plate with a radius comparable to \(a\) cannot reproduce the infinite-plane surface distribution far from the centre, even when the potential at its surface is tightly controlled.

Only the half-space accessible to the physical charge enters the field-energy comparison. Extending the mathematical solution through the grounded boundary introduces a second singular source whose mechanical motion has no counterpart in the laboratory. This is especially important when computing energy by moving the real charge toward the plate.

How a patterned electrode is smoothed by distance

A grounded plane is one boundary condition. Another laboratory problem specifies a different potential at each position along a plane, using many narrow electrodes separated by small insulating gaps. Model the ideal limit by a prescribed function \(V(x,0)\) in vacuum occupying \(z>0\), uniform along \(y\). Away from charges electrostatic potential obeys \(\partial_x^2V+\partial_z^2V=0\). First impose \(V(x,0)=V_k\cos(kx)\) with \(k>0\) and require decay at large \(z\). Separability suggests \(V(x,z)=V_k\cos(kx)Z(z)\). Then \(Z''-k^2 Z=0\). Discard the growing exponential and set \(Z(0)=1\).

\[V(x,z)=V_k e^{-kz}\cos(kx),\qquad E_z=kV_ke^{-kz}\cos(kx).\]

Each sinusoidal boundary component is transmitted with a factor \(e^{-kz}\). By linearity a general periodic pattern decomposes into such harmonics, each decaying according to its own wavenumber. The constant component fixes the voltage reference or background field separately. Smoothing with height follows directly from Laplace's equation in a source-free half-space. It places a physical limit on how well a distant probe can resolve narrow electrode features even before instrument noise is considered.

Predict a non-sinusoidal surface measurement

Let a fabricated pattern have period \(d=4.00\) mm with a fundamental voltage amplitude of 2.00 V and a third harmonic of 0.600 V, both cosine waves with maxima at \(x=0\). At height \(z=0.500\) mm the attenuation exponents are \((2\pi/d)z=0.785\) and \(3(2\pi/d)z=2.356\). Hence the voltage amplitudes there are \(2.00e^{-0.785}=0.912\) V and \(0.600e^{-2.356}=0.0569\) V. The third-to-first amplitude ratio has fallen from 0.300 at the metal to 0.0624.

A probe sensitive instead to the normal field differentiates in \(z\) and multiplies each voltage component by its wavenumber. At the same height the fundamental contributes \(1.43\times10^3\) V/m and the third harmonic contributes \(268\) V/m at the shared maximum. Reporting a field ratio as though it were a voltage ratio would therefore exaggerate the surviving high spatial frequencies by a factor of three. Inverting the voltage law to recover the fabrication pattern multiplies measurement noise by \(e^{kz}\). Repeating a scan at two known heights allows a direct test: the ratio for each Fourier component should follow its own exponential independently of the unknown electrode voltage. A ground plane placed nearby on the other side would change the far boundary condition and require a different transfer factor.

For the same apparatus, suppose detector noise permits relative voltage harmonics down to one percent of their surface amplitudes. A harmonic remains individually detectable only if \(e^{-kz}>0.01\). At a height of 0.500 mm this requires spatial wavelength greater than \(2\pi z/\ln100=0.682\) mm at that noise level. The limit is a measurement criterion, not a strict cutoff in Laplace's equation: every finite wavelength has a nonzero transfer at finite height. An improved signal-to-noise ratio extends the accessible range only logarithmically. Reducing the probe height can therefore improve recovery more effectively than merely accumulating many additional scans.

Which electrode details reach a remote probe?

Set the potential on an infinite plane to a spatial sinusoid of wavelength \(L/n\), with no free charge above it. Separation in Laplace’s equation gives an exponentially decaying height envelope \(e^{-2\pi n y/L}\) for the bounded solution.

Chapter 14 · Building a potential from point responses · Chapter 15 · How far does an electrode pattern reach? · Chapter 16 · Spherical harmonics as measurements of boundary voltage

Problem sheet 1 · Fields from charge and metal boundaries

Test the symmetry and boundary-value arguments of Lectures 1 and 2 by predicting fields, energies and induced charges.

Hollow out a charged sphere

An insulating sphere of radius \(2a\) contains uniform charge density \(\rho\) except in a concentric empty cavity of radius \(a\). Derive the radial field in all three regions. Match it at the two boundaries and calculate the potential difference between the cavity and the outer surface for \(\rho=1.0\) µC/m³ and \(a=0.10\) m.

Enclose the remaining volume charge

In the cavity the concentric Gaussian sphere encloses zero charge and spherical symmetry gives zero field. In the charged material the enclosed charge is \(4\pi\rho(r^3-a^3)/3\), so \(E_r=\rho(r-a^3/r^2)/(3\epsilon_0)\). Outside it becomes \(7\rho a^3/(3\epsilon_0 r^2)\). The two expressions agree at \(2a\) and the material field tends to zero at \(a\). Integrating outward gives \(V(a)-V(2a)=\rho a^2/(3\epsilon_0)\), about 376 V. An off-centre cavity would break the symmetry used in this proof.

Place a grounded plane below a charge

A positive 4.0 nC charge sits 50 mm above a large grounded plate. Find the force magnitude and the radius collecting 80% of the negative induced charge. How do both change if the charge doubles at fixed height?

Use the image and integrate the surface charge

The point charge interacts with an image of magnitude 4.0 nC at separation 0.100 m. Its attractive force is \(kq^2/(2a)^2=14.4\) µN. The fraction within a disk is \(1-a/\sqrt{S^2+a^2}\). Requiring 0.8 gives \(S=\sqrt{24}\,a=0.245\) m. Doubling the charge leaves the fractional distribution and this disk radius unchanged, doubles the density at every point and quadruples the force. The comparatively large disk helps explain why finite plates can distort measurements of total induced charge.

Recover a potential in a charge-free gap

Two coaxial conducting cylinders of radii 5 and 20 mm are held at 100 and 0 V in vacuum. Neglect end effects. Solve Laplace’s equation in the gap, predict the reading at 10 mm, and find how far from the inner wall a probe must move to see half the inner-wall field.

Integrate the radial Laplacian

With cylindrical symmetry Laplace’s equation is \((rV')'=0\). One integration gives \(rV'=\text{constant}\) and the second gives a logarithm. Matching the wall potentials yields \(V=100\ln(20\,\mathrm{mm}/r)/\ln4\) V and \(E_r=100/(r\ln4)\) V/m with \(r\) in metres. At 10 mm the potential is 50 V. Since the field is proportional to \(1/r\), moving from 5 to 10 mm, a distance of 5 mm, halves the field. The halfway-potential position is the geometric mean of the two radii.

Chapter 3 · Magnetic flux and material response

Predict how a narrow air gap changes a toroidal coil’s flux, stored energy and inductance from Ampère’s law and the magnetic boundary conditions.

From first principles

How turns, permeability and a gap set the flux

Wind \(N\) turns around a closed magnetic core and drive a steady current \(I\). Use a core of length \(\ell_c\) and constant cross-section \(A\), interrupted by an air gap of length \(g\). Assume a linear core of permeability \(\mu_c\), a nearly uniform field through each cross-section and negligible leakage and fringing. Ampère’s law counts the linked current along a loop. Flux continuity through a thin pillbox at the interface gives a common normal \(B\):

\[H_c\ell_c+H_g g=NI,\]
\[B=\mu_c H_c=\mu_0 H_g.\]

The material relation \(B=\mu H\) is experimental input. Insert each material’s field into the loop balance, then multiply by area to obtain the flux \(\Phi=BA\):

\[\Phi\left(\frac{\ell_c}{\mu_c A}+\frac{g}{\mu_0 A}\right)=NI .\]

The bracket is the reluctance. Each turn links the same flux, so the flux linkage is \(N\Phi\). For a linear core the coil inductance follows from flux linkage per current:

\[L=\frac{N\Phi}{I}=\frac{N^2 A}{\ell_c/\mu_c+g/\mu_0}.\]

A gap can control \(L\) even when it occupies little of the magnetic path. Compare \(g/\mu_0\) with \(\ell_c/\mu_c\) before neglecting it. If the core saturates, the field-to-current ratio changes with the operating current.

Derivation and applications

Derive the inductance measured by a small alternating signal

A winding on a biased core can give different answers to two seemingly ordinary inductance measurements. Consider a narrow toroid with \(N\) turns, uniform cross-section \(A\), core path length \(\ell\) and one short air gap \(g\). Assume negligible leakage, fringing and hysteresis. Allow the core law \(H_c(B)\) to be nonlinear but single-valued. Flux conservation makes the induction \(B\) common to gap and core, while Ampère's law adds their distinct field integrals.

\[NI=\ell H_c(B)+\frac g{\mu_0}B,\]
\[\Lambda=NAB.\]

Here \(\Lambda\) is flux linkage. A slowly varying current superposed on a constant bias probes only a small interval of this curve. Differentiate the circulation equation about the operating induction \(B_0\) and define the differential permeability \(\mu_d=(dH_c/dB)^{-1}\) evaluated there.

\[N\,dI=\left(\frac{\ell}{\mu_d}+\frac g{\mu_0}\right)dB,\]
\[L_d=\frac{d\Lambda}{dI}=\frac{N^2A}{\ell/\mu_d+g/\mu_0}.\]

The induced terminal voltage after subtracting winding resistance is \(d\Lambda/dt\). Thus the locally measured inductance is the slope. A DC current and a flux measurement instead give the chord joining the bias point to the origin. Writing \(\mu_s=B_0/H_c(B_0)\) makes that expression

\[L_s=\frac{\Lambda_0}{I_0}=\frac{N^2A}{\ell/\mu_s+g/\mu_0}.\]

These definitions coincide for a straight constitutive curve through the origin. A large air gap also makes the two estimates converge because the same linear air term then dominates each denominator.

Worked example

Predict the response to a sinusoidal probe

Take \(N=100\), \(A=100\) mm², \(\ell=100\) mm and \(g=0.500\) mm. At the selected bias suppose \(\mu_d=200\mu_0\) and \(\mu_s=1000\mu_0\). The incremental effective gap is \(0.500+100/200=1.00\) mm, giving \(L_d=1.26\) mH. The chord yields \(2.09\) mH. A 1.00 kHz current of 10.0 mA peak therefore produces a reactive voltage of \(2\pi f L_d I=79.0\) mV peak, provided the excursion stays within the nearly constant local slope.

Second application

Infer the material slope from a voltage ramp

A second winding has \(N=80\), \(A=150\) mm², \(\ell=120\) mm and \(g=0.200\) mm. An inductive voltage of 96.0 mV during a 40.0 A/s ramp gives \(L_d=2.40\) mH. Reversing the slope formula yields \(\ell/(\mu_d/\mu_0)=\mu_0 N^2A/L_d-g=0.303\) mm, so \(\mu_d/\mu_0\simeq396\). The positive residual is a check on subtracting the gap contribution before attributing the measured slope to the core.

When does improving an iron core stop helping?

A narrow magnetic circuit has 20 cm of linear core material and a single adjustable air gap. The two regions have equal area. Neglect leakage and fringing. Predict whether buying material with twice the permeability can double the useful gap field.

Ampère's circuital law gives \(NI=B\ell_c/(\mu_0\mu_r)+Bg/\mu_0\), because the normal flux density is continuous and the area is constant. At zero gap the first term dominates. At \(g=\ell_c/\mu_r\) the two drops are equal. Notice that a millimetre of air can cost more magnetomotive force than the entire core.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Read the two curves at 1 mm for \(\mu_r=1000\). Explain why a change in gap width can matter more than a large change in core material. The ideal zero-gap end may exceed the range of a physically linear core.

A thick toroidal core carries different flux densities at its inner and outer edges

A closed toroidal core occupies inner radius \(a\), outer radius \(b\) and axial height \(h\), with rectangular cross section. An ideal closely distributed \(N\)-turn winding carries steady current \(I\). Assume negligible leakage outside the core, an axisymmetric toroidal field and a linear isotropic reversible constitutive law at first. A technician estimates the inductance by putting the full cross-sectional area on a circle halfway between the two radii. The estimate is convenient. The underlying field problem tells us where it is accurate, how early the inner material starts to saturate, and which region contributes most strongly to a measured flux linkage.

Use the correct circulation at each radius

For every circular path entirely within the core, Ampère's law for \(\mathbf H\) encloses the same free current \(NI\), independent of its radius. Cylindrical symmetry makes the tangential field constant on that particular circle. Hence \(H_\phi(r)=NI/(2\pi r)\). The inner material experiences the larger magnetising field. It is inappropriate to impose a uniform \(H\) across the full radial thickness simply because a single value of current feeds the winding.

Each radial strip of width \(dr\) presents area \(h\,dr\) normal to the flux. For uniform permeability \(\mu\), adding these strips gives the flux of one turn and linkage of the whole winding:

\[\Phi=\frac{\mu NI h}{2\pi}\ln(b/a),\]
\[L=\frac{N\Phi}{I}=\frac{\mu N^2h}{2\pi}\ln(b/a).\]

Contrast this with the arithmetic mean radius \(r_m=(a+b)/2\), which predicts \(L_m=\mu N^2h(b-a)/(2\pi r_m)\). Their ratio is \(\ln(b/a)/[(b-a)/r_m]\). For a narrow core write \(a=r_m(1-\delta), b=r_m(1+\delta)\). Expanding the logarithm gives \(L/L_m=1+\delta^2/3+\delta^4/5+\cdots\). The leading error is quadratic in fractional half-thickness. The symmetry of the cross section cancels the first-order error around its midpoint while leaving the inner edge particularly important for local field limits.

Test inductance using the energy instead of flux linkage

The magnetic energy density in a reversible linear material is \(B H/2\). The volume of one radial shell is \(2\pi r h\,dr\). Insert the local \(H\), integrate and obtain \(W_m=\mu N^2I^2h\ln(b/a)/(4\pi)=LI^2/2\). The radial integral includes \(1/r^2\) from squared field and \(r\) from the volume. Missing either yields the wrong radial weighting. A magnetostatic finite-element result can therefore be checked by both an integral of flux over a radial cross section and an integral of stored energy over the full material.

For \(a=10\) mm, \(b=30\) mm, \(h=8.0\) mm, \(N=80\) and \(\mu_r=1000\), the ideal small-signal inductance is \(11.25\) mH. The mean-radius estimate is \(10.24\) mH, about 9% below the exact result. At \(I=50\) mA the inner field is \(B(a)=0.0800\) T while the outer field is \(0.0267\) T. A fluxmeter reports the cross-sectional average, here \(0.0439\) T. It cannot on its own certify that the inner material stays below a specified knee on its magnetisation curve.

Put different materials at different radii

Suppose the radial region \(a<r<s\) has permeability \(\mu_1\) and the outer region \(s<r<b\) has \(\mu_2\), with neither material carrying a free interface current. The azimuthal \(\mathbf H\) is tangential to the material interface and remains continuous there as required. The tangential \(B\) may jump. Each material carries a parallel contribution to total flux:

\[L=\frac{N^2 h}{2\pi}\big[\mu_1\ln(s/a)+\mu_2\ln(b/s)\big].\]

The layers sit side by side across the cross section. Adding their reluctances in series would describe a different geometry, in which the same flux crosses successive materials along its path. For a uniform material the radius that divides the flux equally solves \(\ln(s/a)=\ln(b/s)\), so \(s=\sqrt{ab}\). In the 10–30 mm example this is 17.3 mm; half the flux occupies only 7.3 mm of the total radial thickness. Swapping a better and a poorer material between two equal-width radial bands therefore changes the measured inductance.

How a radial average can reveal a local material curve

Introduce \(X=NI/(2\pi)\). For one homogeneous reversible material the exact flux per height is \(\Phi/h=\int_a^b B(X/r)\,dr=X\int_{X/b}^{X/a}B(H)H^{-2}\,dH\). A terminal flux measurement samples a whole interval of magnetising field. The endpoints both move as current changes. Differentiating the last expression yields \(X^2\,d[\Phi/(hX)]/dX=aB(X/a)-bB(X/b)\). Thus a family of flux readings relates the material values on the inner and outer edges. If the lower-field portion of the material curve has been calibrated independently, this relation can help infer higher-field response from the toroidal measurement.

This inverse calculation differentiates measured data, amplifying flux noise and any systematic drift. A preferable check on a proposed \(B(H)\) curve is often to integrate it forward across the annulus and compare directly with the flux measurements over multiple currents. Agreement at just one bias does not constrain the full material slope. A second toroid machined from the same material with a different ratio of outer to inner radius changes the radial weighting and provides an additional test.

Identify the first loss of linearity

A measured reversible \(B(H)\) curve follows the stated linear permeability until \(B=0.20\) T, after which its differential slope decreases. At what current does the first radial shell reach the knee? Derive the differential inductance above that current without assigning one uniform permeability to the whole core.

Derive the result and check it independently

The first shell is at \(a\). Solve \(\mu NI/(2\pi a)=0.20\) T, giving \(I=0.125\) A. Farther out the local field is still lower. In a nonlinear reversible material the exact flux linkage is \(Nh\int_a^b B[NI/(2\pi r)]\,dr\). Differentiate with respect to \(I\) at fixed geometry and obtain \(L_d=N^2h(2\pi)^{-1}\int_a^b [dB/dH]_{H(r)}dr/r\). The derivative falls progressively as a larger radial region samples the reduced material slope. A sharp knee in the local curve can therefore become a gradual bend in a terminal inductance measurement.

Integrate digitised \(B(H)\) over successively finer radial rings and compare the local slope result with a symmetric finite difference of the total flux linkage. Disagreement can indicate inadequate radial resolution right where the inner shell first enters the knee. If the material has hysteresis, specify the branch and excitation protocol before treating a measured small-signal slope as a reversible derivative.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §§6.3–6.4 and 7.2.3 · magnetic response and inductance

Specify the path taken by the field

A toroidal winding wraps a ring of magnetic material. Large permeability can guide flux around the ring. Cutting a small gap into it can greatly reduce inductance. To understand this change we need to distinguish the field produced by free current from the magnetic response of the material. Write \(\mathbf B=\mu_0(\mathbf H+\mathbf M)\), where \(\mathbf M\) is magnetic moment per unit volume. For a linear isotropic material, \(\mathbf B=\mu_0\mu_r\mathbf H\). The approximation of a single constant \(\mu_r\) requires operation below strong saturation and negligible hysteresis over the field excursion.

Treat the cross-section as narrow compared with the mean radius and assume tightly distributed turns. Let the material path length be \(\ell_c\), gap length \(g\) and common area \(A\). Neglect stray flux and fringing as a first approximation when the gap is short compared with the transverse dimensions. These choices reduce a spatially varying field to one representative flux through each cross-section. They do not imply equal values of \(H\) in the two materials.

Calculate a coil field without an assumed magnetic path

Ampère's circulation law simplifies when symmetry makes the field uniform along a useful closed path. For a circular wire loop in free space, symmetry does not supply such a path through its aperture. In vacuum a steady thin-wire current instead contributes according to Biot–Savart, \(d\mathbf B=(\mu_0 I/4\pi)d\boldsymbol\ell\times\mathbf R/R^3\), where \(\mathbf R\) points from the wire element to the observation point. The formula assumes a complete steady circuit. For a laboratory coil the distant return leads should contribute little to the region being observed.

Take a circular loop of radius \(a\) and observe on its axis at coordinate \(z\). All wire elements share distance \(\sqrt{a^2+z^2}\). Their transverse contributions cancel in opposite pairs. The axial component of \(d\boldsymbol\ell\times\mathbf R\) is \(a\,d\ell\), and \(\oint d\ell=2\pi a\). For \(N\) tightly spaced turns the sum yields

\[B_z(z)=\frac{\mu_0NIa^2}{2(a^2+z^2)^{3/2}}.\]

A 50-turn coil of radius 40.0 mm carrying 0.800 A produces 0.628 mT at the centre. One radius away the ratio is \(2^{-3/2}=0.354\), giving 0.222 mT. Far along the axis the field falls as the inverse cube and is proportional to the magnetic moment \(NI\pi a^2\). Such a falloff is absent from a lumped magnetic circuit with a single prescribed uniform field. Which model is appropriate depends on whether a core guides most of the flux or the winding produces a field through open space.

Practise on sheet 2 · Choose the separation of a uniform-field coil pair. Further reading · University of Cambridge · David Tong, Electromagnetism · §3.2.3 Biot-Savart Law.

Apply the two circulation and flux laws

Magnetic flux continuity follows from \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). With negligible leakage, the same \(\Phi=BA\) crosses the core and the gap. Ampère’s law around the ring gives \(H_c\ell_c+H_g g=NI\) for \(N\) turns carrying current \(I\). Substitute \(H_c=B/(\mu_0\mu_r)\) and \(H_g=B/\mu_0\) to find

\[B=\frac{\mu_0NI}{g+\ell_c/\mu_r},\qquad \Phi=\frac{NI}{\ell_c/(\mu_0\mu_r A)+g/(\mu_0 A)}.\]

Each denominator contribution in the flux expression is called a reluctance. The sum follows from the distinct magnetic potential drops along one flux path. A gap as short as \(\ell_c/\mu_r\) accounts for half the total circulation even though it occupies only a small fraction of the length. For a ring of appreciable radial width, the ungapped toroidal field varies as \(1/r\) and flux should be integrated over the cross-section.

Link field energy to an electrical measurement

A coil’s flux linkage is \(N\Phi\) when each turn links the same flux. For a fixed, linear geometry \(L=N\Phi/I\). The voltage needed to raise the current is \(v=L\,dI/dt\) in the passive sign convention, omitting winding resistance. The power delivered to the magnetic field is \(vi\). Integrating as the current grows gives stored energy \(W=LI^2/2\). Equivalently, integrating \(B^2/(2\mu)\) over each region apportions this energy between the gap and the core.

Since the same \(B\) passes through both regions but their permeabilities differ, a unit volume of gap stores \(\mu_r\) times the energy of the same volume of core in this model. The ratio of total gap energy to total core energy is \(g\mu_r/\ell_c\). If permeability depends on field, a small-signal inductance measured around a bias is the derivative \(d(N\Phi)/dI\). It can differ from the ratio \(N\Phi/I\) inferred from a single point.

Estimate a coil with a machined gap

Choose \(N=100\), \(I=0.40\) A, \(\ell_c=0.20\) m, \(\mu_r=2000\), \(A=1.0\) cm² and \(g=0.50\) mm. The effective length in the field denominator is \(0.50+0.10=0.60\) mm. Thus \(B=0.0838\) T, \(\Phi=8.38\) µWb and \(L=2.09\) mH. The gap contributes five sixths of the circulation and stores five sixths of the \(0.168\) mJ total energy.

Closing the gap at the same current would predict \(0.503\) T from the linear law. The calculation itself supplies a reason to consult the real material curve before accepting that number. A measured inductance that departs from the prediction may reflect core saturation, imperfect contact at the joint or fringing that changes the effective gap area. Measuring a small current sweep at several gap widths helps distinguish material response from the geometry of a single fixed gap.

The current-to-flux ratio and the incremental response need not agree near magnetic saturation. If \(\lambda\) is the linked flux, a slow voltage measurement detects \(d\lambda/dt=(d\lambda/dI)\dot I\), so small AC perturbations about a DC operating point report the local slope. In contrast, taking a single ratio \(\lambda/I\) effectively joins that point to the origin. The two are equal in the linear magnetic-circuit model derived above. Deliberately sweeping the bias provides an operational way to find where that model stops describing the core.

Two windings sharing a flux path

A second coil wound on the same magnetic core allows the flux model to predict mutual induction. Choose both positive winding directions so currents create flux around the core in the same sense. In a linear core of reluctance \(\mathcal R\) with negligible leakage, the magnetic flux is \(\Phi=(N_1I_1+N_2I_2)/\mathcal R\). Each turn links that same flux. Multiplying by the number of turns in winding 1 or 2 gives flux linkages \(\lambda_1,\lambda_2\), hence

\[\lambda_1=L_1I_1+MI_2,\qquad \lambda_2=MI_1+L_2I_2,\qquad M=\frac{N_1N_2}{\mathcal R},\quad L_i=\frac{N_i^2}{\mathcal R}.\]

Equality of the two mutual coefficients expresses reciprocity in this linear, stationary arrangement. With imperfect flux linkage one often writes \(M=k_c\sqrt{L_1L_2}\). The stored energy \(W=(L_1I_1^2+2MI_1I_2+L_2I_2^2)/2\) must be nonnegative for arbitrary currents, requiring \(|M|\le\sqrt{L_1L_2}\). In the perfectly coupled limit opposing ampere-turns can cancel the common core flux. Leakage and winding resistance then control the small residual voltages seen in real instruments.

Open the secondary so that \(I_2=0\). Faraday's law in the passive terminal-voltage convention gives \(v_i=d\lambda_i/dt=N_i\,d\Phi/dt\). Their voltage ratio is the turns ratio, regardless of sinusoidal amplitude, provided winding drops are negligible. The input current still has to build the necessary flux. Inferring it requires the primary magnetizing inductance, which the turns ratio alone cannot supply.

Set a sinusoidal operating point

Use \(N_1=400\), \(N_2=100\), core area \(A=4.00\) cm² and linear reluctance \(\mathcal R=1.00\times10^6\) A/Wb. Then \(L_1=0.160\) H, \(L_2=0.0100\) H and \(M=0.0400\) H. Drive the primary with 8.00 V RMS at 100 Hz with secondary open. Integrating the sinusoidal voltage gives peak flux \(\Phi_{\rm pk}=\sqrt2V_{\rm rms}/(2\pi fN_1)=45.0\) μWb. The predicted peak flux density is \(0.113\) T, secondary voltage \(2.00\) V RMS and magnetizing current \(V_{\rm rms}/(2\pi fL_1)=79.6\) mA RMS.

At unchanged voltage but half the frequency, voltage ratio is unchanged while peak flux density and current double in this model. A measured current waveform that develops sharp peaks at low frequency can thus signal core saturation before any dramatic change in the nominal turns ratio. A fixed DC terminal voltage on an ideal resistance-free winding would instead keep changing the flux in time, outside the assumed sinusoidal operating condition.

A short electrical pulse can determine the sense of the windings before a sinusoidal test. Mark one terminal on each coil and increase primary current through the first marked terminal. The sign of the measured open-secondary pulse with respect to its marked terminal identifies whether the two positive winding conventions reinforce or oppose one another. Reversing one winding changes the sign of the mutual coefficient and of that pulse but leaves both self-inductances and the magnitude of the voltage ratio unchanged. A sign convention overlooked here becomes important when combining two coils in series, because their mutual induced voltages can either add or subtract.

Winding resistance also introduces voltage even at steady current, so a four-wire resistance measurement helps separate ohmic voltage from linkage changes during a slow pulse.

When does an air gap dominate a magnetic circuit?

A 0.25 m core with uniform cross-section carries 100 ampere-turns. In the linear, negligible-leakage model, the core and air gap share the same \(B\). Their magnetomotive drops add, giving \(B=\mu_0NI/(\ell/\mu_r+g)\).
