Chapter 17 · From a measured current to its magnetic field · Chapter 19 · How a magnet produces an exterior field

Chapter 4 · Induction in moving circuits

Separate transformer and motional electromotive forces, then use the mechanical and electrical power balances to check a moving conductor.

From first principles

Follow a charge around a moving loop

A straight conducting bar of length \(\ell\) slides right at speed \(v\) on two fixed rails. Close the circuit at the left and apply a uniform magnetic field \(B\) out of the page. Orient the loop counterclockwise as seen by the reader. In a moving conductor the Lorentz force per unit charge contains both an electric field and a motional term. Its circulation defines the signed electromotive force:

\[\mathcal E=\oint_{C(t)}(\mathbf E+\mathbf v_{\rm wire}\times\mathbf B)\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell .\]

In a static applied field the electric-field circulation vanishes. Only the right bar moves, and its motional force points down although positive circulation on the right edge points up. Its contribution is \(-B\ell v\). Independently, a bar at distance \(x\) encloses flux \(B\ell x\), so

\[\mathcal E=-B\ell v=-\frac{d}{dt}(B\ell x).\]

If instead the field varies while the bar moves, the fixed-path Faraday term adds \(-\ell x\dot B\). The two mechanisms combine by the product rule:

\[\mathcal E=-\ell x\dot B-B\ell\dot x=-\frac{d\Phi_B}{dt}.\]

For a resistive circuit with negligible self-inductance, \(I=\mathcal E/R\). The negative sign predicts clockwise current during expansion and an opposing magnetic force on the bar. The mechanical work required to keep it moving supplies the resistive heating.

Derivation and applications

Close the mechanical power balance of a rotating coil

A rotating loop drives current through an inductive circuit even while the magnetic flux through the loop is reversing. Consider \(N\) tightly wound turns of area \(A\) rotating at imposed angular speed \(\omega\) in a uniform static field \(B\). Take the angle between the loop normal and the field to be \(\theta=\omega t\). Use a constant total circuit resistance \(R\) and self-inductance \(L\). Neglect brushes, friction and radiation. The external flux linkage and its time derivative give the motional source voltage

\[\Lambda_{\rm ext}=NBA\cos\theta,\]
\[e=-\frac{d\Lambda_{\rm ext}}{dt}=NBA\omega\sin\theta.\]

Self-induction supplies a second voltage that depends on the rate of change of current. After the initial transient dies away, the loop equation has a sinusoidal solution with peak current \(I_0\) and lag \(\phi\):

\[L\dot i+Ri=e,\]
\[I_0=\frac{NBA\omega}{\sqrt{R^2+(\omega L)^2}},\]
\[\tan\phi=\frac{\omega L}{R}.\]

The field torque along the rotation coordinate is \(i\,\partial\Lambda_{\rm ext}/\partial\theta\). An external motor balances that torque, so its instantaneous power equals \(e i\). Multiplying the loop equation by current now separates irreversible heating from reversible storage.

\[P_{\rm motor}=ei=Ri^2+\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac12Li^2\right),\]
\[\langle P_{\rm motor}\rangle=\frac12 R I_0^2.\]

The storage term integrates to zero over a complete cycle, though for part of the cycle a falling current can return energy. The mean opposing torque magnitude follows by dividing mean mechanical power by the imposed angular speed.

Worked example

Measure the mean shaft torque

A teaching generator uses \(N=100\), \(A=20.0\) cm², \(B=0.200\) T and \(\omega=100\) rad/s. It produces 4.00 V peak. For \(R=3.00\) Ω and \(L=40.0\) mH, the reactance is 4.00 Ω and the impedance magnitude is 5.00 Ω. The 0.800 A peak current dissipates 0.960 W on average. The mean shaft torque required by the electrical load is therefore \(0.960/100=9.60\) mN m.

Second application

Read an inductance from the timing of a current maximum

In a separate apparatus the current maximum follows the voltage maximum by 2.50 ms at 50.0 Hz. The lag is \(2\pi(50.0)(0.00250)=\pi/4\), so \(\omega L=R\). With measured total resistance 12.0 Ω this gives \(L=12.0/(2\pi\,50.0)=38.2\) mH. A 6.00 V rms source would then deliver \(6.00^2/(2\times12.0)=1.50\) W of mean heating. Using only the resistance to infer current would double this power estimate.

Where does a moving bar's energy go?

A 40 g freely sliding conductor of active length 20 cm starts at 2 m/s. A uniform transverse field and a closed circuit turn its kinetic energy into heat. Ignore friction and circuit inductance. Predict whether doubling the magnetic field doubles the initial braking force.

The motional emf \(B\ell v\) drives \(I=B\ell v/R\). Lenz's law fixes the force opposite the velocity, so \(m\dot v=-(B\ell)^2v/R\). Separating \(dv/v=-dt/\tau\) gives \(\tau=mR/(B\ell)^2\). Multiplying the motion equation by \(v\) gives the independent power check \(-d(mv^2/2)/dt=I^2R\).

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Read the time to fall from 2 m/s to 1 m/s and compare it with \(\tau\ln 2\). Increase the resistance. Explain why a more resistive circuit brakes the bar less strongly.

How a spinning disk produces a voltage in an unchanging magnetic field

A conducting disk of thickness \(t\) rotates at angular velocity \(\omega\hat{\mathbf z}\) in a uniform stationary axial field \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\). A stationary brush contacts a circular hub at radius \(a\), another contacts the rim at \(b>a\), and a stationary lead connects them through a resistor. Model the contacts as full concentric rings so the disk current is radial and axisymmetric. The metal moves but the external circuit and the magnetic field pattern do not. A contour drawn on a sheet of paper encloses the same magnetic flux before and after one revolution. Nevertheless a voltmeter across the brushes reads a steady voltage. Which physical contour belongs in the emf calculation?

Separate the charge velocity from motion of the conductor

The Lorentz force on a charge in moving metal involves the full laboratory velocity. For motional emf around a conducting path, integrate the force per unit charge on charges carried with each local piece, \(\mathcal E=\oint(\mathbf E+\mathbf v_{\rm material}\times\mathbf B)\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell\). In steady operation the electrostatic contribution has zero circulation. On the disk \(\mathbf v_{\rm material}=\omega r\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}\), giving \(\mathbf v_{\rm material}\times\mathbf B=\omega r B\hat{\mathbf r}\); the stationary return lead contributes zero motional term. Following the path outwards through the disk and back through the load gives

\[\mathcal E=\int_a^b\omega B r\,dr=\tfrac12\omega B(b^2-a^2).\]

A material loop with every point tracked consistently obeys the moving-loop flux rule. The brush circuit continuously exchanges material at its sliding contacts; a line frozen into the disk does not stay joined to the same stationary wire. Using the derivative of flux through a fixed drawing of the apparatus omits that relative motion. This explains why the measured voltage remains steady even though the rotating charge carriers individually move in circles.

Derive the internal resistance directly from current continuity

Let disk conductivity be \(\sigma\) and positive current \(I\) flow radially outward inside it. Radial symmetry and charge conservation require \(J_r=I/(2\pi rt)\). The laboratory form of Ohm's law at nonrelativistic speed is \(J_r=\sigma(E_r+\omega rB)\) for this component. Therefore \(E_r=I/(2\pi\sigma tr)-\omega rB\). Integrating \(-E_r\) between hub and rim gives the actual terminal voltage:

\[V_{\rm rim}-V_{\rm hub}=\mathcal E-I R_d,\]
\[R_d=\frac{\ln(b/a)}{2\pi\sigma t}.\]

At open circuit charges accumulate until \(E_r=-\omega rB\), leaving zero radial conduction current and a positive rim voltage. With a load \(R_L\), \(I=\mathcal E/(R_L+R_d)\), neglecting contact and return-lead resistance. For a real narrow brush the spreading resistance is different; the full-ring symmetry was necessary for this logarithmic one-dimensional expression. Letting \(a\) shrink to exactly zero at finite current also makes this ideal radial resistance diverge. A real contact must have finite area.

Calculate the braking torque and test the energy balance

Current-driven charges experiencing \(\mathbf J\times\mathbf B\) transmit mechanical force to the metal. Here \(\hat{\mathbf r}\times\hat{\mathbf z}=-\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}\), so the magnetic force opposes the rotation. A shell of volume \(2\pi r t\,dr\) carries torque \(dT_z=-r\,J_r B(2\pi r t)\,dr=-IBr\,dr\). Integrating gives \(T_z=-IB(b^2-a^2)/2\). The positive shaft power needed to maintain constant speed is \(-T_z\omega=I\mathcal E\), precisely the sum \(I^2R_L+I^2R_d\). Independently integrating Joule heating \(J_r^2/\sigma\) over the disk gives \(I^2\ln(b/a)/(2\pi\sigma t)\). This independent volume calculation is sensitive to the same radial concentration of current that matters at the hub.

For \(a=5.0\) mm, \(b=50.0\) mm, \(B=0.20\) T and \(\omega=100\) rad/s, the open voltage is 24.75 mV. Let the entire internal path, including contacts, measure 20.0 mΩ and choose \(R_L=80.0\) mΩ. The current becomes \(0.2475\) A, the load receives \(4.90\) mW, and internal loss is \(1.23\) mW. The braking torque magnitude is \(61.3\) μN m, requiring \(6.13\) mW shaft power. Under the same geometry an open voltmeter can read the same source emf while producing essentially no magnetic braking through this external circuit.

Predict the electrical signature of a freely slowing rotor

Disconnect the motor while retaining the resistive load. Neglect bearing friction, mechanical windage and circuit inductance on the mechanical time scale. Let \(g=B(b^2-a^2)/2\), so \(\mathcal E=g\omega\) and \(T_z=-gI\). With total series resistance \(R\) the rotor's moment of inertia \(J\) obeys \(J\dot\omega=-(g^2/R)\omega\). Its speed and open-emf scale therefore decay exponentially with time constant \(JR/g^2\). Its kinetic energy decays twice as fast in the exponential because it is proportional to \(\omega^2\).

For the preceding disk \(g=2.475\times10^{-4}\) V s. If \(J=2.00\times10^{-6}\) kg m² and \(R=0.100\ \Omega\), the predicted speed e-folding time is 3.26 s. The initial kinetic energy at 100 rad/s is 10.0 mJ. With the 80 mΩ load present, 80% of that energy ultimately arrives at the load under the stated assumptions. A measured shorter decay even with the load disconnected points to mechanical or other electromagnetic loss. Comparing loaded and unloaded decay rates tests the braking law independently of an absolute shaft-torque measurement.

Use reversal and loading to distinguish a motional emf

Two runs at opposite angular velocity give open-circuit voltages \(+27.0\) mV and \(-23.0\) mV, with equal speed magnitudes and field. Under an 80.0 mΩ load, the speed-odd terminal voltage is 20.0 mV. Infer the motional emf, a speed-independent offset, and the effective internal resistance. Could one load reading locate where heat is generated?

Derive the result and check it independently

A speed-independent contact or thermoelectric offset survives averaging, giving \((27.0-23.0)/2=2.0\) mV. The half-difference gives a motional emf of 25.0 mV. Under load \(I=20.0\ {\rm mV}/80.0\ {\rm m\Omega}=0.250\) A. The speed-odd loss is 5.0 mV, so the inferred internal resistance is 20.0 mΩ. The measured terminal characteristic only identifies the total series loss. Separate voltage taps, a contact-resistance measurement, or a spatial heating measurement is needed to divide that loss among the disk, contacts and leads.

Reverse just \(B\) as a further test: the motional signal reverses again. If both speed and field reverse, its sign should remain unchanged. These paired observations test the bilinear \(\omega B\) dependence without using an assumed calibration of the brush offset.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §§7.1.2–7.1.3 and 8.3 · emf and mechanical work

Follow a charge around a circuit

A conducting rod slides on two rails in a static magnetic field. Although a flux meter sees the enclosed flux changing, a laboratory observer finds no time-varying applied magnetic field. The measured voltage comes from the force on charges carried with the moving rod. In a stationary loop beside an AC coil the situation is different: a changing magnetic field produces an electric field with nonzero circulation. A consistent treatment includes both mechanisms.

Choose a positive direction around a thin material circuit. The associated surface normal follows the right-hand rule. If a segment moves at velocity \(\mathbf v_{\rm wire}\), the electromotive force is the integral of \((\mathbf E+\mathbf v_{\rm wire}\times\mathbf B)\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell\) around it. In the quasistatic circuit description, the drift motion along a thin wire adds no tangential magnetic force. The integral has units of work per charge. Its sign fixes the direction of the current for a passive resistance.

Recover the same fields from different potentials

Zero magnetic divergence permits \(\mathbf B=\nabla\times\mathbf A\) in a contractible region, such as the interior of a ball. Combining this with Faraday's law yields \(\nabla\times(\mathbf E+\partial_t\mathbf A)=0\) and hence \(\mathbf E=-\nabla\phi-\partial_t\mathbf A\). The scalar potential alone cannot represent a nonzero electric circulation around a stationary loop. Still, individual values of the potentials are not unique. For any smooth single-valued \(\chi\), take \(\mathbf A'=\mathbf A+\nabla\chi\) and \(\phi'=\phi-\partial_t\chi\). The curl of a gradient vanishes, and the two new derivative terms in \(\mathbf E'\) cancel, so both fields and the Lorentz force stay fixed.

Inside a long ideal solenoid assume \(\mathbf B=B(t)\mathbf e_z\) and axial symmetry. Choose \(\phi=0\) and \(\mathbf A=B(-y,x,0)/2\). The resulting azimuthal electric field has magnitude \(r|\dot B|/2\). Another allowed choice uses \(\chi=Bxy/2\), which gives \(\mathbf A'=(0,Bx,0)\) and \(\phi'=-\dot Bxy/2\). Computing only \(-\partial_t\mathbf A'\) would misleadingly give a field pointing along the \(y\) axis everywhere. Including the scalar gradient restores \((\dot By,-\dot Bx,0)/2\).

At \(x=0.0400\) m, \(y=0.0300\) m and \(\dot B=0.500\) T/s both calculations give \((0.00750,-0.0100,0)\) V/m. A circle of radius 50.0 mm encloses a flux growing at \(3.93\) mWb/s and has circulation \(-3.93\) mV in the positive sense around \(z\). The measured loop emf is gauge independent. A voltage measured along a specified open path in a changing magnetic field also depends on the routing of its leads.

Practise on sheet 2 · Test a proposed change of potentials. Further reading · University of Cambridge · David Tong, Electromagnetism · §3.2.2 Gauge Transformations.

Differentiate the linked flux

Let the loop boundary move during a short interval. Its magnetic flux changes both because \(\partial_t\mathbf B\) changes across the original surface and because the perimeter sweeps a thin ribbon. Faraday’s field equation relates the first contribution to \(-\oint\mathbf E\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell\). The oriented ribbon gives the second contribution \(-\oint(\mathbf v_{\rm wire}\times\mathbf B)\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell\). Adding them gives \(\mathcal E=-d\Phi/dt\). The flux rule holds here because the moving contour is the circuit being followed.

Take a rectangular loop in the horizontal plane with uniform field \(B\) upwards. Its right side is a rod of length \(\ell\), moving outwards at speed \(v\) so the upward flux increases. Positive circulation seen from above is counterclockwise. The induced electromotive force is therefore negative and the current is clockwise. Equivalently, on the moving rod \(\mathbf v\times\mathbf B\) points down the page. This local force gives the same sign as the global flux law.

Close the energy balance

For negligible self-inductance and total resistance \(R\), the magnitude is \(I=B\ell v/R\). A current-carrying rod experiences force \(\mathbf F=I\boldsymbol\ell\times\mathbf B\), opposite to its velocity. Constant-speed operation needs a pulling force \(B\ell I\). Multiplying by speed gives \(P_{\rm mech}=B\ell Iv=I^2R\). The magnetic interaction redirects the transfer. The external pull supplies the energy dissipated as heat.

If the wire has appreciable self-inductance \(L\), its own flux changes when current changes. With constant applied speed the magnitude equation becomes \(L\,dI/dt+RI=B\ell v\) after the initial step in velocity. The current builds with time constant \(L/R\). At each instant the mechanical power balances Joule heating plus \(d(LI^2/2)/dt\). Predicting an immediate full drag force at the moment of starting would neglect the energy required to establish current.

Compare two measurements

Let \(B=0.40\) T, \(\ell=0.25\) m, \(v=3.0\) m/s and \(R=0.60\) Ω. The open-circuit emf magnitude is 0.300 V. Closing the circuit gives steady current 0.500 A and drag force 0.0500 N. Mechanical power and electrical heating both equal 0.150 W. A voltmeter with large internal resistance measures nearly the open-circuit value but draws little current, so it produces little braking. The induced voltage alone does not establish how much energy is transferred.

With \(L=0.12\) H the time constant is 0.20 s. At that time after closing, \(I=0.500(1-e^{-1})=0.316\) A. The mechanical power is 0.0948 W, of which 0.0599 W heats the resistor. The remaining 0.0349 W increases magnetic field energy. If the pulling force is removed entirely, speed can no longer be prescribed; the mechanical inertia must enter an additional equation. Under those conditions a single exponential computed at constant speed describes only part of the response.

A simple reversal checks all sign conventions in the experiment. Reverse the external magnetic field while keeping the prescribed velocity fixed. The induced voltage changes sign, and so does the steady current in a passive resistor. Their product in the electrical power is unchanged. The mechanical force depends on the product of current and magnetic field and therefore still opposes motion. The same circuit can operate as a motor if an independent supply forces current against its generator direction. The supply then contributes to the energy balance as a separate external port.

A changing flux can slow a moving magnet

Consider a small conducting loop of resistance \(R\) and inductance \(L\) fixed beside the path of an external magnet. Let \(x\) be the magnet's displacement and \(\Phi(x)\) its flux through the loop in a specified positive direction. Its motion supplies an electromotive force \(-\Phi'(x)\dot x\). Over a small region approximate the gradient by the constant \(K=\Phi'(x)\) and take the loop's self-inductance to be independent of position. The current equation is \(L\dot I+RI=-K\dot x\). It follows that a static magnet produces no sustained current in a resistive loop, even if its absolute magnetic flux is large.

Multiply the equation by \(I\). Inductive energy changes at rate \(d(LI^2/2)/dt\), and heat appears at rate \(RI^2\). Together these equal \(-KI\dot x\). Mechanical power supplied by the loop to the magnet is therefore \(KI\dot x\), giving electromagnetic force \(F=KI\). At sufficiently low frequency the inductive term is negligible and \(F\simeq-(K^2/R)\dot x\). A magnetic interaction has become an effective viscous damper through the combined action of induction and resistance.

For harmonic velocity with complex amplitude \(u\), the force amplitude is \(-K^2u/(R+i\omega L)\). Its component opposing velocity has effective damping \(c_{\rm em}=K^2R/(R^2+\omega^2L^2)\). The imaginary component returns stored energy during part of each cycle. Equating cycle-mean mechanical loss to mean resistor heating gives another way to verify which component really dissipates.

Compare motion below and above the electrical corner

Near a chosen working point let \(K=0.200\) Wb/m, \(R=2.00\) Ω, \(L=20.0\) mH and peak speed \(0.100\) m/s. The corner frequency in angular units is \(R/L=100\) rad/s. At \(\omega=10.0\) rad/s the induced emf amplitude is \(0.0200\) V and current amplitude is \(0.0200/\sqrt{2.00^2+0.200^2}=9.95\) mA. The average heat is \(RI_{\rm pk}^2/2=99.0\) μW. The low-frequency approximation gives \(K^2/R=0.0200\) N s/m and predicts \(100\) μW, sufficiently close here.

At \(200\) rad/s the same velocity amplitude gives current \(4.47\) mA and heat \(20.0\) μW. The smaller loss despite unchanged peak speed comes from a greater fraction of the emf supporting inductive voltage. At fixed nonzero frequency the limit \(R\to0\) eliminates dissipation while retaining induced current. The apparent divergence of the low-frequency approximation as resistance tends to zero is resolved because its assumption \(\omega L\ll R\) then fails. Measuring both force phase and heating provides independent tests of this simple localized flux model.

The approximation of constant flux gradient can itself be checked experimentally. Translate the magnet slowly through several known positions while measuring open-circuit emf and speed. Dividing the signed emf by the signed speed estimates the local gradient, without needing an absolute calibration of the magnet’s total flux. If the gradient changes sign across the motion, the induced current can have a distorted waveform even under perfectly sinusoidal displacement. The small-amplitude force response derived here should then be measured about a fixed mean position, or the full position-dependent gradient should be retained in the coupled electrical and mechanical equations.

Develop this calculation further

Chapter 20 · Where momentum waits between source and receiver · Chapter 29 · When a circuit element must be treated as a field

Problem sheet 2 · Flux linkage and mechanical work

Combine the magnetic-circuit model of Lecture 3 with Faraday’s law from Lecture 4.

Determine a gap from an inductance measurement

A 200-turn toroidal coil has cross-section 80 mm², core length 0.16 m and relative permeability 1600. A low-current test gives \(L=8.0\) mH. Infer the gap and determine the fraction of magnetic energy held there. State one reason this inference can misestimate the physical gap.

Convert the inductance to an effective length

For uniform area and negligible leakage, \(L=\mu_0N^2A/(g+\ell_c/\mu_r)\). The inferred effective length is \(0.503\) mm, of which 0.100 mm is contributed by the core. The gap is about 0.403 mm and the energy fraction is \(0.403/0.503=0.801\). Fringing gives the gap an effective area larger than the core area. Variations in actual incremental permeability would likewise bias a gap determined solely from an inductance reading.

Drop a sliding rod under gravity

Two vertical rails and their conducting top link form a closed circuit through a sliding rod of mass 0.020 kg and length 0.25 m. A uniform 0.50 T field is normal to the loop. The total resistance is 0.10 Ω. Derive the speed from rest, find the terminal speed and compare electrical power with gravitational power there. Neglect inductance and friction.

Solve the coupled force and current laws

The current magnitude is \(B\ell v/R\), giving upward drag \(B^2\ell^2v/R\). Thus \(m\dot v=mg-B^2\ell^2v/R\), with solution \(v=v_t(1-e^{-t/\tau})\), \(v_t=mgR/(B^2\ell^2)=1.26\) m/s and \(\tau=mR/(B^2\ell^2)=0.128\) s. At terminal speed the current is 1.57 A and heating is 0.246 W, matching \(mgv_t\). Changing resistance changes the terminal speed; the mechanically available power per second therefore also changes.

Drive a stationary loop with a changing field

A one-turn circular loop of area 0.012 m² and resistance 3.0 Ω lies normal to \(B(t)=0.080\cos(2\pi 50t)\) T. Find the peak emf, mean resistive power and total charge passing a reference point during a half-cycle from maximum positive to maximum negative flux. What changes if a second identical turn is added in series?

Distinguish derivative and integrated flux

The peak emf is \(A B_0\omega=0.302\) V. With negligible inductance, mean power is \(\mathcal E_0^2/(2R)=15.2\) mW. Integrating \(I=-\dot\Phi/R\) gives signed transported charge \(2AB_0/R=0.640\) mC in the selected positive direction. Two identical series turns double both the emf and the resistance. Current and half-cycle charge remain the same, while total mean power doubles. This assumes the same field threads each turn.

Choose the separation of a uniform-field coil pair

Place two identical coaxial coils at \(z=\pm d/2\) with the same current orientation. Use the single-coil axial field to choose \(d\) so that both the first and second derivatives vanish at the midpoint. Find its field for 100 turns per coil, radius 0.100 m and current 0.500 A.

Differentiate the axial field twice

Reflection symmetry makes the first derivative zero for every spacing. A single factor \((a^2+z^2)^{-3/2}\) has second derivative \(3(4z^2-a^2)/(a^2+z^2)^{7/2}\). Both coils contribute zero curvature at the midpoint if \(d/2=a/2\), hence \(d=a\). Adding their fields gives \(B(0)=(4/5)^{3/2}\mu_0NI/a=0.450\) mT. The first surviving midpoint variation is then fourth order in displacement.

Revisit chapter 3 · Calculate a coil field without an assumed magnetic path.

Test a proposed change of potentials

In a region of zero physical fields take \(\chi=sxt\) with \(s=0.300\) V/m. Transform from zero potentials, recover the fields, and determine the false acceleration inferred for a charge-to-mass ratio of \(2.00\) C/kg if the transformed scalar potential is forgotten.

Differentiate both potentials

The transformation gives \(A'_x=st\) and \(\phi'=-sx\). The spatially uniform vector potential has zero curl. Its time derivative contributes \(-s\mathbf e_x\) to the electric field, and the scalar gradient contributes \(+s\mathbf e_x\), giving zero acceleration. Keeping only the vector term would give \(-0.600\) m/s². Changing representation cannot cause a particle to start accelerating.

Revisit chapter 4 · Recover the same fields from different potentials.

Chapter 5 · Electromagnetic waves and energy flux

Derive the electromagnetic energy balance and use it to infer field amplitudes and transmitted energy from a measured wave intensity.

From first principles

Derive the local account of field energy

A material charge moving with velocity \(\mathbf v\) gains power \(q\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf E\). Summing gives the rate of work per volume \(\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E\). To find the energy reservoir supplying this work, take the scalar product of the Ampère–Maxwell law with \(\mathbf E\) and of Faraday’s law with \(\mathbf H\). Assume linear, time-independent, nondispersive material with scalar \(\epsilon,\mu\).

\[\mathbf E\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf H)=\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf J+\epsilon\mathbf E\cdot\partial_t\mathbf E,\]
\[\mathbf H\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf E)=-\mu\mathbf H\cdot\partial_t\mathbf H.\]

Subtract the first identity from the second. The left side is the divergence of the cross product \(\mathbf E\times\mathbf H\). On the right each field times its own derivative is half the derivative of its square:

\[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\frac{\epsilon E^2+\mu H^2}{2}\right)+\nabla\cdot(\mathbf E\times\mathbf H)=-\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E .\]

Integrate over a volume. Positive outward flux and positive work on matter both decrease the field energy remaining inside. The cross product has units W/m² and gives the direction of energy travel. This provides a way to calculate power crossing the insulation of a cable even when no conduction current crosses that surface.

Derivation and applications

Follow the energy into a charging capacitor

A capacitor can store energy in an insulating gap while its supply leads carry conduction current. Consider circular vacuum plates of radius \(a\) separated by \(d\ll a\). Use cylindrical coordinates, with a positive, nearly uniform electric field \(E\) along \(+z\). Charge slowly enough to neglect radiation and propagation delays. The edge of the uniform-field model represents a surface just inside the rim. During charging, the increasing electric displacement contributes to Ampère's circulation law around any concentric circle of radius \(r\leq a\).

\[2\pi r H_\phi=\frac{d}{dt}\int\epsilon_0 E\,dA=\epsilon_0\pi r^2\dot E,\]
\[H_\phi=\frac{\epsilon_0 r}{2}\dot E .\]

The magnetic field grows linearly with radius because the enclosed displacement current scales with area while the circumference scales with radius. When \(E\) and \(\dot E\) are positive, the cross product of axial electric field and azimuthal magnetic field points radially inward. Integrating over the side of the chosen cylinder gives the incoming electromagnetic power.

\[P_{\rm in}=-2\pi rd\,S_r=2\pi rd\,E H_\phi=\epsilon_0\pi r^2d\,E\dot E .\]

Express the electric energy in this same cylindrical part of the gap through its volume. Its time derivative agrees with the surface result at every radius. At the rim, the voltage and total charging current give a third way of measuring this rate.

\[\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\epsilon_0 E^2}{2}\pi r^2d\right)=P_{\rm in},\]
\[P_{\rm in}(a)=VI,\]
\[V=Ed,\]
\[I=\epsilon_0\pi a^2\dot E .\]

Worked example

Trace a voltage ramp

For \(a=40.0\) mm and \(d=1.00\) mm, the capacitance is \(\epsilon_0\pi a^2/d=44.5\) pF. A voltage ramp of 2.00 V/µs draws 89.0 µA. At 10.0 V, electrical input is 0.890 mW. The inward flux density at the rim is then \(0.000890/(2\pi\,0.0400\,0.00100)=3.54\) W/m². A cylinder of half the radius receives one quarter of the power because it encloses one quarter of the changing field energy.

Second application

Check a discharge with a local magnetic measurement

For a separate capacitor, \(d=2.00\) mm and \(E=3.00\) kV/m. A probe at \(r=20.0\) mm reads \(H_\phi=-2.00\) mA/m. The negative sign places the Poynting vector outwards. Its magnitude is \(3000(0.00200)=6.00\) W/m², so the energy leaves that cylinder at \(2\pi(0.0200)(0.00200)(6.00)=1.51\) mW. The axial voltage is still positive. The outward flux therefore identifies a decreasing field magnitude rather than a reversal of plate polarity.

Start from this chapter. At fixed applied voltage, doubling the stored electric energy doubles capacitance. Use the energy and charge estimates from the same cable cross-section as two independent checks.

OneSimulation laboratory · Recover cable impedance from a cross-section

Can a standing field contain energy without transporting it?

Let a normally incident plane wave meet a reflector. The electric-amplitude reflection coefficient \(r\) is real and may be negative. At perfect reflection, first predict the electric energy and the net power at the boundary. Vary the observation phase through a cycle.

A right-going wave obeys \(Z H_i=E_i\). A left-going wave obeys \(Z H_r=-E_r\). In units of \(\epsilon E_0^2/2\), the electric and magnetic energy densities are therefore the squares of the sum and difference of the two scalar waveforms. In their product the cross terms cancel, giving cycle-averaged flux proportional to \(1-r^2\).

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Try \(r=-1\) and zero phase. Locate a magnetic maximum where the electric field is zero. Advance by a quarter cycle and trace where the field energy has gone.

Follow energy through the bright and dark fringes of two crossing waves

Two coherent monochromatic plane waves of equal frequency and equal electric amplitude \(E_0\) travel in vacuum. Their wavevectors make angles \(+\theta,-\theta\) with the \(z\) axis in the \(xz\) plane. Both electric fields point along \(y\), and the waves are in phase at the origin. An intensity camera on the plane normal to \(z\) sees alternating bright and dark bands. Is a dark band devoid of electromagnetic energy? Inspecting the electric field alone would suggest yes. The magnetic field, Poynting flux and the observer's integration area give three complementary tests of that answer.

Construct electric and magnetic fields from the same phases

Write \(K=k\sin\theta\), \(k_z=k\cos\theta\), \(k=\omega/c\), and use complex amplitudes multiplying \(e^{-i\omega t}\). Then the two phases are \(e^{ik_z z}e^{\pm iKx}\). Their electric fields add to \(E_y=2E_0e^{ik_z z}\cos Kx\). For each wave separately \(c\mathbf B=\hat{\mathbf k}\times\mathbf E\). Its \(x\) components add with the same sign, while the \(z\) components have opposite signs. The total fields are therefore

\[cB_x=-2E_0\cos\theta\, e^{ik_z z}\cos Kx,\]
\[cB_z=2iE_0\sin\theta\, e^{ik_z z}\sin Kx.\]

At an electric node \(\cos Kx=0\) the magnetic field still oscillates in the longitudinal direction. Near the nodes it cannot be reconstructed by replacing the whole superposition with one plane wave propagating in \(+z\). Even though both constituents have \(E=cB\), that equality of pointwise magnitudes is not inherited by their sum. The phase \(i\) in the longitudinal magnetic term matters when calculating time-averaged transport.

Compute what the camera and a local energy measurement see

For complex peak-amplitude phasors, time-averaged flux is \(\langle\mathbf S\rangle=\operatorname{Re}(\mathbf E\times\mathbf B^*)/(2\mu_0)\), and mean energy density is \(\langle u\rangle=\epsilon_0(|E|^2+c^2|B|^2)/4\). Substitute the fields. The \(x\) flux contains a purely imaginary product between \(E_y\) and \(B_z^*\), so its cycle average vanishes. The remaining observables are

\[\langle S_z\rangle=2\epsilon_0 c E_0^2\cos\theta\,\cos^2Kx,\]
\[\langle u\rangle=\epsilon_0 E_0^2[(1+\cos^2\theta)\cos^2Kx+\sin^2\theta\sin^2Kx].\]

At a dark band \(\langle S_z\rangle=0\) but \(\langle u\rangle=\epsilon_0 E_0^2\sin^2\theta\). The oscillatory magnetic field there stores positive energy. At a bright band the ratio of mean flux to mean energy density reaches \(2c\cos\theta/(1+\cos^2\theta)\leq c\). Between them the ratio varies with \(x\); neither its bright-band value nor its spatial average is the velocity of an individual light ray in free space. Local instantaneous transverse energy exchange can occur even though the mean \(x\)-flux is zero.

Integrate over a complete fringe and compare with incoming power

The bright-to-bright period along the camera is \(\pi/K=\lambda/(2\sin\theta)\), obtained from the squared electric field. Over a whole period both squared trigonometric terms have mean \(1/2\). The area-averaged axial flux and energy density become \(\epsilon_0 cE_0^2\cos\theta\) and \(\epsilon_0E_0^2\). Independently, each incident wave carries mean intensity \(I_0=\epsilon_0c E_0^2/2\) along its own direction, whose projection normal to the camera is \(I_0\cos\theta\). Their sum exactly reproduces the integrated fringe measurement. Interference redistributes energy crossing the screen without changing this integrated power.

Take \(\theta=30^\circ\), \(\lambda=600\) nm and incident \(E_0=100\) V/m peak per wave. The fringe spacing is 600 nm. A bright band receives mean normal power density \(46.0\) W/m², while integrating over one complete period gives \(23.0\) W/m². A dark band contains mean energy density \(2.21\times10^{-8}\) J/m³. A sufficiently small electric-dipole detector can be blind at the node even though a magnetic probe responds there. An instrument that averages over many fringe periods cannot reconstruct this local distinction from the total collected power.

Distinguish an imperfect dark band from lost energy

Change one incident electric amplitude to \(E_1\) and the other to \(E_2\), leaving directions and polarisation as before. Each forward field still gives a magnetic \(x\)-component proportional to its electric field. Consequently \(\langle S_z\rangle=(\epsilon_0 c\cos\theta/2)[E_1^2+E_2^2+2E_1E_2\cos(2Kx)]\). The intensity extrema now scale as \((E_1+E_2)^2\) and \((E_1-E_2)^2\). Even a perfectly resolving coherent detector will report shallow minima if the beam powers are unbalanced.

If one electric amplitude is twice the other, the peak-to-trough power ratio is nine, and the visibility is \(2E_1E_2/(E_1^2+E_2^2)=0.80\). Independently block each beam in turn and measure its mean power. Their ratio is four, not two, because the sensor measures power. From those two blocked-beam readings predict the coherent fringe visibility before attributing any discrepancy to an unstable relative phase. Unequal amplitudes also give a nonzero area-averaged lateral Poynting flux proportional to \((E_1^2-E_2^2)\sin\theta\), consistent with the unequal transverse components of incident power.

Distinguish visibility from coherence and from spatial resolution

Replace the ideal point detector with a rectangular pixel of width \(w\), centred at \(x_c\), uniformly sensitive to normally incident energy flux. Derive the contrast reduction, and find the largest pixel width giving at least 90% of ideal visibility in the first lobe.

Derive the result and check it independently

Write \(\cos^2Kx=[1+\cos(2Kx)]/2\). The pixel average of the oscillating term is \(\cos(2Kx_c)\sin(Kw)/(Kw)\). Thus measured visibility \((S_{\max}-S_{\min})/(S_{\max}+S_{\min})=|\sin(Kw)/(Kw)|\). In the first lobe the equation \(\sin y/y=0.90\) gives \(y\simeq0.787\), so \(w\leq0.250\) fringe periods, approximately 150 nm for these waves. A pixel covering exactly one fringe period has zero measured visibility, despite perfect mutual coherence.

As an independent test, move a narrow aperture across the pixel or change the crossing angle. The predicted visibility depends on \(w\sin\theta/\lambda\). Poor coherence and unequal beam intensities can also suppress visibility, but those effects need not follow this spatial averaging law. Conservation of power and the fringe-contrast measurement test different properties of the field.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §§8.1.2 and 9.2.3 · Poynting flux and wave energy

Charge conservation requires a changing field term

Ampère’s magnetostatic law relates magnetic circulation to current. A surface passing through a wire detects a conduction current, whereas a second spanning surface between the plates of a charging capacitor may intercept none. The evolving electric field supplies the missing current. In vacuum, \(\nabla\times\mathbf B/\mu_0=\mathbf J+\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E\). Taking the divergence of both sides and using Gauss’s electric law gives \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf J+\partial_t\rho=0\), the continuity equation for charge.

Together with Faraday’s law and the two divergence laws, this term permits waves even in a region containing no material charges. In source-free vacuum take the curl of \(\nabla\times\mathbf E=-\partial_t\mathbf B\). Use \(\nabla\times(\nabla\times\mathbf E)=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E)-\nabla^2\mathbf E\) and substitute the Maxwell–Ampère law. The result is \(\nabla^2\mathbf E=\mu_0\epsilon_0\partial_{tt}\mathbf E\). The coefficient sets \(c=1/\sqrt{\mu_0\epsilon_0}\).

Read a propagating field

Take a sinusoidal electric field \(\mathbf E=E_0\cos(kz-\omega t)\,\hat{\mathbf x}\). The wave equation gives \(\omega=ck\), and Faraday’s law fixes \(\mathbf B=(E_0/c)\cos(kz-\omega t)\,\hat{\mathbf y}\). The divergence equations require these plane-wave fields to be transverse. Their relative orientation selects the positive direction of travel. Flipping the sign of the magnetic field alone with the same electric waveform would reverse the local energy flow but would violate Faraday’s law for this chosen propagation phase.

Wave speed and field amplitude answer different questions. A stronger sinusoidal source increases the energy in a given volume without changing its speed through linear vacuum. Changing to a lossless homogeneous nondispersive material changes the propagation speed to \(1/\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}\) and the impedance relating electric and magnetic fields to \(\sqrt{\mu/\epsilon}\). Near a source, the simple wave relation between amplitudes need not hold.

Identify work and transport locally

Dot the Maxwell–Ampère equation with \(\mathbf E\) and Faraday’s law with \(\mathbf B/\mu_0\). Use the identity \(\nabla\cdot(\mathbf E\times\mathbf B)=\mathbf B\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf E)-\mathbf E\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf B)\) to combine them. Rearranging gives Poynting’s theorem

\[\partial_t u+\nabla\cdot\mathbf S=-\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E,\qquad u=\frac{\epsilon_0E^2}{2}+\frac{B^2}{2\mu_0},\quad \mathbf S=\frac{\mathbf E\times\mathbf B}{\mu_0}.\]

Integrated over a volume, the equation says that net incoming electromagnetic power increases stored field energy or performs work on matter. In a vacuum plane wave the electric and magnetic energy densities are equal and \(\mathbf S=cu\,\hat{\mathbf z}\). In a standing wave counterpropagating energy flows can cancel on average while local fields and stored energy remain large. A detector of heating and a point probe of electric amplitude need not then respond in the same way.

Convert intensity into field and pulse energy

A broad, normally incident vacuum wave has mean intensity 2.0 W/m². For a sinusoid the cycle mean of \(\cos^2\) is one half. Therefore \(\langle S\rangle=E_0^2/(2Z_{\rm vac})\) with \(Z_{\rm vac}=\mu_0c\simeq377\) Ω. The electric peak is 38.8 V/m and the magnetic peak is \(1.30\times10^{-7}\) T. Using the RMS electric field instead gives \(\langle S\rangle=E_{\rm rms}^2/Z_{\rm vac}\). Mixing a peak-valued field with that RMS formula would overestimate the power by a factor of two.

An absorbing disk of area 0.010 m² intercepts a 3.0 ms pulse with this mean intensity throughout its flat top. Neglect edge effects and switching intervals. The energy received is \(2.0(0.010)(0.0030)=60\) µJ. In the lossless TEM line, the same flux principle becomes the circuit power \(VI\). The coaxial-line experiment reports \(Z_0=47.45\) Ω for the baseline cross-section. A 1 V RMS forward sinusoid there carries about 21.1 mW. A 1 V peak sinusoid carries half that mean power, which gives a direct check on the convention used by a voltage probe.

For a travelling plane-wave pulse, integrate the instantaneous Poynting flux over a plane normal to propagation and over the time during which the pulse crosses. The result is the total transported energy. Equivalently integrate field energy density throughout the pulse volume at one instant. The length of the pulse is its speed multiplied by its duration, so the two constructions agree. This provides a check when a detector reports time-averaged irradiance but an independent instrument reports energy per pulse; the averaging interval and illuminated area enter explicitly.

Which observer can see a purely magnetic field?

A charged particle responds to \(q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\) in one inertial frame. A frame moving alongside the particle initially sees no magnetic part of the force on it, so the assignment of a field as electric or magnetic cannot be frame independent. For a frame moving at velocity \(\mathbf u\) relative to the laboratory, let parallel and perpendicular denote components relative to \(\mathbf u\). The parallel fields remain unchanged. Transforming the electromagnetic field tensor gives the perpendicular components in SI units,

\[\mathbf E'_\perp=\gamma(\mathbf E_\perp+\mathbf u\times\mathbf B),\qquad \mathbf B'_\perp=\gamma(\mathbf B_\perp-\mathbf u\times\mathbf E/c^2),\quad \gamma=(1-u^2/c^2)^{-1/2}.\]

For crossed fields \(\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B=0\), choose \(\mathbf u=\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/B^2\). The vector triple product then gives \(\mathbf u\times\mathbf B=-\mathbf E\). If \(E<cB\), this is an admissible subluminal boost and the moving observer sees \(\mathbf E'=0\). The remaining magnetic field has magnitude \(\sqrt{B^2-E^2/c^2}\). A charge initially moving at \(\mathbf u\) in the laboratory has zero Lorentz force, in agreement with a stationary charge in the new frame.

Let \(\mathbf E=6.00\) MV/m along \(x\) and \(\mathbf B=0.0500\) T along \(z\). The required velocity is \(1.20\times10^8\) m/s along negative \(y\), about \(0.400c\). The moving frame measures \(B'=0.0458\) T. At \(E=cB\) the required speed would be \(c\) and no inertial rest frame eliminates either field. This is the relation for a vacuum plane wave. Its electromagnetic field cannot be removed by trying to travel beside the wave.

Practise on sheet 3 · Choose a frame for electric-dominated crossed fields. Further reading · University of Cambridge · David Tong, Electromagnetism · §5.3.2 The Electromagnetic Tensor.

Locate the energy entering a resistor

A steady resistor has constant fields and still receives a continuous supply of energy. Model a long straight cylindrical wire of radius \(a\), conductivity \(\sigma\) and resistive length \(\ell\). A DC current \(I\) flows in the positive axial direction. Assume a uniform current density, negligible end effects and a coaxial return sufficiently far away that the magnetic field immediately outside the wire has cylindrical symmetry. The internal axial electric field is \(E_z=I/(\sigma\pi a^2)\). Continuity of tangential electric field gives the same axial value immediately outside its lateral surface.

Ampère's law fixes the azimuthal magnetic field there at \(H_\phi=I/(2\pi a)\). Since \(\mathbf e_z\times\mathbf e_\phi=-\mathbf e_r\), the Poynting flux points radially inward. Multiplying its magnitude by the lateral area gives

\[P_{\rm in}=E_zH_\phi(2\pi a\ell)=\frac{I^2\ell}{\sigma\pi a^2}=I^2R .\]

The result is the familiar circuit power but obtained from fields on the surrounding surface. It accords with the local energy theorem: field energy within the chosen volume is steady while the inward flux supplies the material work \(\int\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E\,dV\). A radial electrostatic field can also be present because of surface charge establishing a voltage relative to the return. It produces longitudinal Poynting transport in the gap. Its direction and magnitude depend on the wider circuit, whereas the inward component above accounts for the resistive loss in the selected segment.

Audit the flux at different radii

Use \(\ell=1.00\) m, \(a=0.500\) mm, \(\sigma=1.00\times10^6\) S/m and \(I=1.00\) A. The cross-sectional area is \(7.85\times10^{-7}\) m² and the resistance is \(1.273\) Ω. The axial electric field is \(1.273\) V/m and the surface magnetic field is \(318\) A/m. Their product gives an inward energy flux density of \(405\) W/m². Integrating over \(2\pi a\ell=0.00314\) m² yields \(1.27\) W, matching the measured electrical input.

Now evaluate the field on an imaginary cylinder of radius \(r=a/2\) inside the wire. Uniform current density encloses only \(I/4\). Its magnetic field is half the surface value, and its area is also halved. The integrated inward power is therefore one quarter, \(0.318\) W. It supplies precisely the heat produced in one quarter of the wire volume. As \(r\) tends to zero the local inward flux vanishes with \(r\) while volumetric heating density remains finite. Taking the divergence in cylindrical coordinates reconciles these limits. A skin-effect current profile at high frequency would invalidate the uniform-field calculation and redistribute both heating and energy input toward the surface.

Measured current and terminal voltage determine the inward energy rate without measuring either field directly, but they cannot locate the path of the incoming energy elsewhere in the circuit. To calculate that path one can choose a coaxial geometry with known conductor and return potentials. The radial field in the dielectric then combines with the azimuthal magnetic field to give longitudinal flux toward the load. Approaching the resistive segment, part of this flux turns inward as demonstrated above. Choosing a different return path may change the exterior pattern substantially while preserving the same resistance and the same total power budget.

Carry energy from source to load through a coaxial gap

The same energy theorem closes the circuit picture if the wire is the inner conductor of a coaxial delivery line. In the uniform, low-loss part take inner radius \(a\), return-conductor inner radius \(b\), potential difference \(V\) and equal opposing currents of magnitude \(I\). A cylindrical Gaussian surface gives radial field \(E_r=V/[r\ln(b/a)]\) in the dielectric. Ampère's law gives \(H_\phi=I/(2\pi r)\). The axial Poynting flux density is therefore proportional to \(1/r^2\). Integrate over the dielectric annulus,

\[P_z=\int_a^b E_rH_\phi\,2\pi r\,dr=\frac{VI}{\ln(b/a)}\int_a^b\frac{dr}{r}=VI.\]

The familiar terminal formula has emerged by integrating power moving through the dielectric. It remains compatible with power crossing the lateral surface into a resistor at the line's end. The outer return conductor also matters: it sets the radial electric field geometry and provides the return current defining where the external magnetic field cancels in the coaxial model.

Take \(V=10.0\) V, \(I=0.200\) A, \(a=1.00\) mm and \(b=4.00\) mm. Delivered power is 2.00 W. The fraction flowing between \(a\) and any intermediate radius \(r\) is \(\ln(r/a)/\ln(b/a)\). At \(r=2.00\) mm this is one half. The outer part of the gap has greater cross-sectional area yet carries the other half because flux density falls outward. If the inner conductor develops finite resistance per length \(R'\) and leakage is negligible, a small segment loses \(I^2R'\,dz\) of longitudinal power. The longitudinal difference equals its radial inward flux, providing a local field interpretation of the ordinary circuit voltage drop.

Develop this calculation further

Chapter 20 · Where momentum waits between source and receiver · Chapter 21 · What an interface does to phase, field and power · Chapter 22 · Why dispersion and absorption belong together

Chapter 6 · Radiation from a small electric dipole

Relate a time-dependent electric dipole moment to its radiation pattern, total power and the conditions under which a far-field detector measures them.

From first principles

From a time-dependent current to dipole radiation

Take a localized, slowly moving source much smaller than the wavelength, in vacuum. At an observation distance \(r\) large compared with both source size and wavelength, the part of the retarded vector potential that survives at order \(1/r\) samples the whole small source at approximately one retarded time \(t_r=t-r/c\). Define its dipole moment \(\mathbf p=\int\mathbf r'\rho\,dV'\). Charge conservation with vanishing current at the outer boundary gives, by integration by parts,

\[\dot{\mathbf p}=-\int\mathbf r'\nabla'\cdot\mathbf J\,dV'=\int\mathbf J\,dV',\]
\[\mathbf A\simeq\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi r}\dot{\mathbf p}(t_r).\]

When taking a curl far from the source, differentiating the retarded time supplies a factor \(1/c\) and retains \(1/r\). Differentiating the geometrical prefactor gives a smaller \(1/r^2\) term. With \(\hat{\mathbf n}\) pointing to the detector, the leading fields are

\[\mathbf B_{\rm rad}=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi cr}\,\ddot{\mathbf p}(t_r)\times\hat{\mathbf n},\]
\[\mathbf E_{\rm rad}=c\,\mathbf B_{\rm rad}\times\hat{\mathbf n}.\]

The first cross product removes the longitudinal dipole acceleration. Thus a sinusoidal dipole has a dark radiation axis and maximum intensity broadside. The flux decreases as \(1/r^2\), just cancelling the area growth of a distant sphere. A constant dipole or one increasing at a constant rate has no such dipole radiation term.

Derivation and applications

Calculate how much dipole radiation a collector intercepts

An angular scan gives intensity along one line of sight. Turning it into collected power requires integrating the radiation pattern over the angles accepted by the optics. Assume a pointlike linearly oscillating electric dipole in vacuum, an observer many wavelengths away and a lossless collector. Its axisymmetric far-field power per unit solid angle is \(K\sin^2\theta\), where \(K\) sets the strength and \(\theta\) is measured from the oscillation axis. The solid angle of a narrow polar ring includes a factor of \(\sin\theta\) arising from its circumference.

\[dP=2\pi K\sin^3\theta\,d\theta,\]
\[P_{\rm tot}=2\pi K\int_0^\pi\sin^3\theta\,d\theta=\frac{8\pi K}{3}.\]

Consider a collector accepting the full azimuth from the positive axis out to half-angle \(\alpha\). Set \(u=\cos\theta\) in the integral. The term \(1-u^2\) is the angular weight and the limits in \(u\) run from \(\cos\alpha\) to one.

\[f_{\rm cap}=\frac{P(0\leq\theta\leq\alpha)}{P_{\rm tot}}=\frac34\int_{\cos\alpha}^1(1-u^2)\,du=\frac{2-3\cos\alpha+\cos^3\alpha}{4}.\]

For a collector surrounding the equator, take the band between \(\pi/2-\delta\) and \(\pi/2+\delta\). Its omitted power resides in two identical polar caps. Subtracting their contributions gives

\[f_{\rm band}=\frac{3\sin\delta-\sin^3\delta}{2}.\]

A hemisphere admits exactly one half of the power by symmetry. Near the axis the small-cap result begins as \(3\alpha^4/16\) for \(\alpha\) in radians, because both intensity and available solid angle shrink there.

Worked example

Collect fluorescence through a polar cone

A fixed dipole emits 8.00 nW and an objective in vacuum accepts a polar cone with \(\alpha=60.0^\circ\). Substituting \(\cos\alpha=0.500\) gives \(f_{\rm cap}=(2-1.50+0.125)/4=0.15625\). The incident optical power is 1.25 nW. If a downstream filter transmits 80.0 percent throughout this cone, the detector receives 1.00 nW. Randomly rotating emitters would require averaging the orientation before applying a collection factor.

Second application

Infer total power from an equatorial band

An annular monitor accepts all azimuths from 60.0° to 120.0°. Here \(\delta=30.0^\circ\) and \(f_{\rm band}=(1.50-0.125)/2=0.6875\). A calibrated reading of 5.50 µW implies total emitted power \(5.50/0.6875=8.00\) µW. The band covers only half of the sphere's solid angle, so treating this dipole as isotropic would overestimate the total by 3.00 µW.

How much radiation fits inside a cone?

An electrically short dipole radiates a far-zone intensity proportional to \(\sin^2\theta\). A detector subtends a circular cap of half-angle \(\alpha\) about the dipole axis. Guess whether a 45-degree cap intercepts one quarter of the radiated power.

A ring on the viewing sphere has area \(2\pi r^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\). The extra sine comes from area, even for an isotropic source. Integrating \(\sin^3\theta\) from 0 to \(\alpha\) and dividing by its integral from 0 to \(\pi\) gives \((2-3\cos\alpha+\cos^3\alpha)/4\).

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Move the cap to 90 degrees and explain the agreement of the two curves at this point. At a small angle the dipole fraction starts at order \(\alpha^4\), while the isotropic fraction starts at order \(\alpha^2\). Recover both leading powers using the ring argument.

A dipole pulse has different clocks for displacement, radiation and colour

A small neutral source changes its electric dipole moment along a fixed axis. Unlike a harmonic antenna operating indefinitely, this source begins and ends with negligible dipole moment, current and acceleration. Let its history in the source frame be \(p(t)=p_0\exp[-t^2/(2T^2)]\) along \(z\). Assume that the source size is much smaller than \(cT\), that the detector is far outside the source and its near zone over the main spectral band, and that the environment is vacuum. The aim is to infer both the time profile and total energy of the radiated pulse from this smooth change in charge distribution. At no point do we create or destroy net charge.

Translate the mechanical history into a detector trace

For a localized conserved source at leading electric-dipole order, the far electric field at fixed polar angle has magnitude proportional to \(\ddot p(t-r/c)\sin\theta/r\). The sign of a polar component depends on basis convention, but relative lobes in a single fixed detector keep their signs. Introduce reduced retarded time \(u=(t-r/c)/T\). Two derivatives give

\[\dot p=-\frac{p_0}{T}u e^{-u^2/2},\]
\[\ddot p=\frac{p_0}{T^2}(u^2-1)e^{-u^2/2}.\]

The source moment is largest at \(u=0\), its first derivative vanishes there, and its second derivative is negative with maximal magnitude. A detector sensitive to signed electric field observes a central lobe bracketed by oppositely signed weaker lobes. Its zero crossings are \(u=\pm1\); the side extrema are at \(u=\pm\sqrt3\), where their magnitude relative to the central peak is \(2e^{-3/2}=0.446\). A power detector squares the waveform and loses its signs. Inferring the original \(p(t)\) from a power-only trace therefore needs additional phase or source information.

Integrate the radiated energy in the time domain

The instantaneous angular power per emission time in the nonrelativistic electric-dipole approximation is \(dP/d\Omega=\ddot p^{\,2}\sin^2\theta/(16\pi^2\epsilon_0c^3)\). Integrating over directions gives \(P=\ddot p^{\,2}/(6\pi\epsilon_0c^3)\). Use \(dt=T\,du\), and expand \((u^2-1)^2=u^4-2u^2+1\). Gaussian moments are \(\int e^{-u^2}du=\sqrt\pi\), \(\int u^2e^{-u^2}du=\sqrt\pi/2\), and \(\int u^4e^{-u^2}du=3\sqrt\pi/4\) over the whole line. The time integral becomes

\[W=\frac{p_0^2}{6\pi\epsilon_0 c^3T^3}\,\frac{3\sqrt\pi}{4}.\]

Stretch the event to twice its duration at fixed maximum moment. The radiative field peak decreases by four, its peak power decreases by sixteen, but the pulse lasts twice as long, giving one eighth the total radiated energy. This strong time-scale dependence is a useful diagnostic: a trace with unchanged integrated energy following such a stretch would challenge the assumed fixed dipole amplitude or the dipole-only description.

Check the total with a frequency-domain measurement

Define \(\widetilde p(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^\infty p(t)e^{i\omega t}dt=p_0T\sqrt{2\pi}\exp[-(\omega T)^2/2]\). The transform of \(\ddot p\) is \(-\omega^2\widetilde p\), with no endpoint terms. Parseval's identity with this convention gives a one-sided energy spectrum

\[\frac{dW}{d\omega}=\frac{\omega^4|\widetilde p(\omega)|^2}{6\pi^2\epsilon_0c^3},\]
\[\omega>0 .\]

The factor of two for negative frequencies has already been included. Although \(|\widetilde p|\) peaks at zero frequency, radiation has no zero-frequency component for this pulse. Maximising \(\omega^4e^{-(\omega T)^2}\) gives \(\omega_{\max}=\sqrt2/T\). Integrating the spectrum over \(\omega>0\) reproduces the time-domain result because \(\int_0^\infty v^4e^{-v^2}dv=3\sqrt\pi/8\). This is an independent check of both the dipole differentiation and the Fourier convention. A spectrum plotted per unit wavelength would need the Jacobian \(|d\omega/d\lambda|\) and generally has a different peak.

For \(T=0.50\) ns, the two signed field zero crossings are 1.00 ns apart while maximum spectral energy per angular-frequency interval occurs near \(450\) MHz when converted to ordinary frequency. If the detector is placed 0.60 m farther along the same far-field ray, every feature arrives 2.00 ns later. After multiplying the signed fields by their respective radii and compensating this flight time, the traces should match. Failure of the scaling can reveal a near-field contribution or a frequency-dependent transfer function in the receiving system.

Two integral tests for the sign of a measured waveform

For a single far-field detector at fixed angle outside a radiation node the signed voltage, after calibrating its response, is proportional to \(\ddot p(t-r/c)\). The area of that trace is zero when \(\dot p\) has the same value before and after the event. Integrate the first time moment by parts: \(\int t\ddot p\,dt=[t\dot p]_{-\infty}^\infty-[p]_{-\infty}^\infty\). It too vanishes for the specified Gaussian pulse. These identities constrain both the relative signs and the wing amplitudes. A clipped recording can violate them even when the main central peak is accurately sampled.

The second time moment survives. Two integrations by parts give \(\int t^2\ddot p\,dt=2\int p\,dt\), equal to \(2\sqrt{2\pi}p_0T\) for the model. A common delay shifts \(t^2\) into a sum of quadratic, linear and constant terms. The lower two moments vanish, so the second moment is unchanged by that delay. This supplies an independent way to compare two radius-corrected detector traces without first finding the centre of a noisy lobe. Its usefulness depends on retaining the wings long enough to control the quadratic time weighting.

Recover a pulse timescale without identifying a carrier

An equatorial detector records a signed pulse whose central lobe crosses zero at 6.2 and 7.8 ns. A spectrometer, calibrated per unit ordinary frequency, finds the strongest signal near 280 MHz. Decide whether the observations fit the Gaussian dipole model. What should a detector on the dipole axis measure?

Derive the result and check it independently

The two time zeros bracket \(2T\), giving \(T=0.80\) ns and the source-centred retarded event at detector time 7.0 ns. The predicted spectral peak is \(f_{\max}=\sqrt2/(2\pi T)=281\) MHz. Converting from angular to ordinary frequency changes the spectral density by a constant \(2\pi\), so the peak is at the frequency just calculated. Both measurements agree within the stated precision. They still do not fix the absolute pulse energy without amplitude calibration and distance.

Along the dipole axis the transverse radiation field vanishes in this approximation. A finite nearby axial detector can respond to electrostatic or induction fields and a finite optical aperture can capture off-axis radiation. Changing distance and angular aperture separately can distinguish these effects. A signed time integral of the far electric trace should also vanish because it is proportional to the difference between final and initial \(\dot p\).

Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 5 · retarded fields and electric dipole radiation

From potentials to a distant detector

An alternating current in a short conductor moves positive and negative charge relative to one another. A current probe at the source and a field probe well outside it measure different aspects of the same charge motion. Consider a localised neutral source of size \(d\), oscillating at angular frequency \(\omega\) in vacuum. Assume \(d\ll\lambda=2\pi c/\omega\) so that phase changes across the source are small. The leading electric multipole is its dipole moment \(\mathbf p=\int\mathbf r'\rho(\mathbf r',t)d^3r'\).

A signal emitted at source time \(t_r=t-r/c\) reaches an observation point a distance \(r\) away at time \(t\). Keeping the leading distant term of the retarded vector potential gives \(\mathbf A\simeq\mu_0\int\mathbf J(\mathbf r',t_r)d^3r'/(4\pi r)\). By charge continuity the current integral equals \(\dot{\mathbf p}\). One sees this by differentiating the integral defining a component of \(\mathbf p\), substituting \(-\nabla'\cdot\mathbf J\) for \(\partial_t\rho\) and integrating by parts. No surface term remains for a localised source whose current vanishes at the enclosing boundary.

Extract the transverse radiation field

Curling the potential differentiates both \(1/r\) and the retarded time. The former produces a term falling as \(1/r^2\). Differentiating \(t_r\) produces a term proportional to \(\ddot{\mathbf p}/(cr)\), which dominates sufficiently far away. The resulting magnetic field is transverse to the direction of observation. The radiative electric field can be written as the transverse vector \(\hat{\mathbf r}\times(\hat{\mathbf r}\times\ddot{\mathbf p}(t_r))/(4\pi\epsilon_0 c^2r)\). The magnetic field is \(\hat{\mathbf r}\times\mathbf E/c\).

Take \(\mathbf p=p_0\cos(\omega t)\hat{\mathbf z}\). The electric amplitude is proportional to \(\omega^2p_0\sin\theta/r\), where \(\theta\) is the angle to the dipole axis. Along the axis no transverse component survives. In the equatorial plane the radiation is strongest. These conclusions concern the energy-carrying far field. The electrostatic dipole field and the induction field decay with higher inverse powers of radius and may dominate a probe whose distance is small on the wavelength scale.

Integrate over a sphere

Use the plane-wave expression for the cycle-averaged Poynting flux locally in the far zone. Multiplying by \(r^2\) gives the power per unit solid angle. Integration uses \(d\Omega=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi\) and \(\int \sin^2\theta\,d\Omega=8\pi/3\). For a peak dipole moment the result is

\[\frac{d\langle P\rangle}{d\Omega}=\frac{\omega^4 p_0^2}{32\pi^2\epsilon_0c^3}\sin^2\theta,\qquad \langle P\rangle=\frac{\omega^4p_0^2}{12\pi\epsilon_0c^3}.\]

The sphere has more area at larger radius while the intensity falls as \(r^{-2}\), so total power is independent of the chosen far-field radius. At fixed dipole moment, doubling frequency increases the radiated power sixteenfold. If the experiment instead fixes the amplitude of current through a short element, the resulting dipole moment falls with increasing frequency because current is the time derivative of charge. The change in what is controlled matters.

Check an angular scan

A calibrated small dipole radiates 3.0 mW in total. At \(r=10\) m in its far zone, the equatorial mean intensity is \(3\langle P\rangle/(8\pi r^2)=3.58\) µW/m². At 30° from the axis it is one quarter of that value, 0.895 µW/m². Treating the source as isotropic would conceal both the axial null and the enhanced equatorial intensity. A dipole probe receiving the field must also be correctly polarised to measure the predicted amplitude.

Move a correctly oriented equatorial probe from 10 to 20 m. Its measured electric amplitude should halve and its measured power per area should fall by four. Failure of this scaling could indicate reflections, inadequate distance from the source or a directional response in the probe. Combining this distance test with an angular scan distinguishes a radiation measurement from a mapping of the near electrostatic field.

An oscillating electric dipole with fixed orientation emits linearly polarized radiation at a generic far-field point. The electric field points along the projection of the dipole axis onto the plane transverse to the line of sight. Turning a linear receiving antenna through a right angle can therefore remove its signal even at a location of maximum emitted intensity. To distinguish antenna misalignment from a source-pattern null, repeat the scan with two orthogonal receiving orientations and add the received powers.

Radiation from a driven bound charge

A dipole need not be fed by a wire. An incident optical field can drive a bound charge, which reradiates part of the incident power. Model a charge of magnitude \(q\) and effective mass \(m\) attached to a harmonic restoring force \(-m\omega_0^2x\). Expose it to a spatially uniform sinusoidal electric field of peak amplitude \(E_0\) at angular frequency \(\omega\). Uniformity here requires the oscillator size to be small compared with the wavelength. Include linear damping rate \(\gamma\) through the term \(m\gamma\dot x\). Steady harmonic response gives a dipole amplitude

\[|p|=\frac{q^2E_0/m}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)^2+\gamma^2\omega^2}} .\]

The radiated power of a sinusoidal electric dipole with peak moment \(p\) is \(\omega^4|p|^2/(12\pi\epsilon_0c^3)\). Divide by the incident plane-wave intensity \(\epsilon_0c E_0^2/2\) to obtain a scattering cross section. Well below resonance, where the restoring term dominates, define the static polarizability \(\alpha_0=q^2/(m\omega_0^2)\) in SI units. Then

\[\sigma_{\rm sc}\simeq \frac{k^4}{6\pi}\left|\frac{\alpha_0}{\epsilon_0}\right|^2,\qquad k=\omega/c.\]

The strong fourth-power dependence originates in the dipole's radiating acceleration. The amplitude of the induced moment varies little in the low-frequency regime, but twice differentiating it in time produces a field amplitude proportional to \(\omega^2\). Squaring that field gives the fourth power. Near an internal resonance the polarizability itself varies rapidly and this simple colour dependence no longer applies.

Predict the ratio and magnitude of scattered light

Suppose a small isotropic object has \(\alpha_0/(4\pi\epsilon_0)=1.00\times10^{-30}\) m³, and both 450 nm and 650 nm light are far from its absorption bands. At 450 nm, \(k=1.40\times10^7\) m⁻¹. Substitution gives \(\sigma_{\rm sc}= (8\pi/3)k^4(10^{-30}\,{\rm m^3})^2\simeq3.18\times10^{-31}\) m². At 650 nm the cross section is smaller by \((650/450)^4=4.35\), about \(7.32\times10^{-32}\) m². Equal incident irradiances therefore give that same ratio of total scattered powers.

The angular pattern remains dipolar, so a small detector also needs a known collection angle and polarization geometry. At a direction parallel to the incident electric polarization the ideal driven dipole has a null, irrespective of wavelength. A distribution of particle sizes with some dimensions comparable to wavelength can fill such a null and alter the colour ratio. Comparing angular scans and wavelength scans separates these two experimental predictions rather than attributing every departure to a changed number of scatterers.

An ideal absorption-free dielectric particle still removes some power from the original forward beam by redirecting radiation. For a dilute ensemble one might infer scattering cross section from the weak reduction in collimated transmitted power, provided light scattered into the detector aperture is excluded. If the material also absorbs, the reduction measures extinction and is larger than scattering alone. Measuring total scattered power and forward-beam attenuation separately then distinguishes reradiated energy from internal dissipation. Comparing measurements at several concentrations checks whether neighbouring objects remain independent scatterers or instead interfere appreciably across the ensemble.

The polarizability here is in SI units. Tabulated polarizability volumes must first be multiplied by \(4\pi\epsilon_0\) before substitution in the SI cross-section expression.

Translate a dipole pattern into collected power

An electrically short dipole radiates far-field intensity proportional to \(\sin^2\theta\). A detector spanning a narrow band of polar angles and the full azimuth subtends \(2\pi\sin\theta\,d\theta\). Compare the two peak-normalised angular profiles.

Problem sheet 3 · Energy flux and radiation

Use the energy balance of Lecture 5 and the angular pattern of Lecture 6 to interpret remotely measured field data.

Reconcile two field instruments

A normally incident plane wave in vacuum gives a measured RMS magnetic field of 12 nT. Find the RMS electric field and mean intensity. An ideal absorber covers 25 cm²; determine its mean heating rate and explain how a standing wave would complicate the inference from a single magnetic probe.

Reconstruct the Poynting flux

For a forward plane wave \(E_{\rm rms}=cB_{\rm rms}=3.60\) V/m and \(\langle S\rangle=E_{\rm rms}^2/Z_{\rm vac}=0.0344\) W/m². The area is 0.0025 m², so mean power is \(86\) µW. A standing wave can have a magnetic antinode with substantial stored energy even though oppositely directed powers cancel. Then a measurement of the magnetic field alone fails to determine the net power delivered.

Infer total dipole radiation

A far-zone probe oriented correctly at \(r=8.0\) m and \(\theta=45°\) from an electric dipole axis reads 1.20 µW/m². Infer the equatorial intensity at the same distance, total radiated power and the reading at 16 m and 90°. State a test for an unwanted reflected wave.

Integrate the angular pattern

At 45° the factor \(\sin^2\theta\) is one half. The equatorial intensity is therefore 2.40 µW/m². Total power follows from \(P=(8\pi/3)r^2S_{\rm eq}\), giving 1.29 mW. Doubling radius at the equator gives 0.600 µW/m². A radial scan at fixed angle should make \(r^2S\) constant. Reproducible oscillations over fractions of a wavelength can reveal interference with a reflection.

Which quantity is fixed as frequency changes?

A very short current element of effective length \(\ell\) has \(\dot p=I\ell\), with sinusoidal current of peak amplitude \(I_0\). Express dipole radiation power in terms of current amplitude. Double the frequency at fixed current and then at fixed dipole moment. Compare and interpret the two power ratios.

Trace the frequency dependence

For a sinusoid \(p_0=I_0\ell/\omega\). Inserting this into \(P=\omega^4p_0^2/(12\pi\epsilon_0c^3)\) gives \(P=\omega^2I_0^2\ell^2/(12\pi\epsilon_0c^3)\). At fixed current it grows fourfold, while at fixed dipole moment it grows sixteenfold. If the original power were 0.50 mW, the outcomes would be 2.0 and 8.0 mW. At the higher frequency the same current displaces less charge per half-cycle. This explains why the two experiments impose different source conditions.

Choose a frame for electric-dominated crossed fields

In a laboratory \(E_x=12.0\) MV/m and \(B_z=0.0200\) T. Decide which field can vanish in an inertial frame. Determine the velocity of that frame and the magnitude of the remaining field. Use \(c=3.00\times10^8\) m/s.

Transform the magnetic component first

Here \(cB=6.00\) MV/m, less than \(E\). Set \(\mathbf u=c^2\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/E^2\) so \(\mathbf u\times\mathbf E/c^2=\mathbf B\). The velocity is \(1.50\times10^8\) m/s along negative \(y\). The remaining electric field is \(\sqrt{E^2-c^2B^2}=10.4\) MV/m, consistent with invariance of \(E^2-c^2B^2\). Eliminating the electric field instead would require \(E/B=6.00\times10^8\) m/s.

Revisit chapter 5 · Which observer can see a purely magnetic field?.

Chapter 7 · Multipole fields

Predict the leading far field of a localized charge distribution and use symmetry to decide how rapidly it decays.

From first principles

Why far-field measurements resolve moments in order

Place a static charge distribution inside a ball of radius \(a\). A distant detector at \(\mathbf r=r\hat{\mathbf n}\), \(r\gg a\), sums contributions with very similar travel distances. Begin with Coulomb’s potential, and factor the common \(r\) out of the denominator:

\[V(\mathbf r)=\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_0 r}\int \rho(\mathbf r')\left(1-2\frac{\hat{\mathbf n}\cdot\mathbf r'}{r}+\frac{|\mathbf r'|^2}{r^2}\right)^{-1/2}\,dV'.\]

For a small dimensionless \(z\), \((1+z)^{-1/2}=1-z/2+3z^2/8+\cdots\). Keeping terms through first order in \(a/r\) takes the detector direction outside the second integral:

\[V(\mathbf r)=\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\left(\frac{Q}{r}+\frac{\hat{\mathbf n}\cdot\mathbf p}{r^2}+\cdots\right),\]
\[Q=\int\rho\,dV',\]
\[\mathbf p=\int\mathbf r'\rho\,dV'.\]

In a neutral sample the leading coefficient vanishes exactly. The dipole term distinguishes viewing directions and gains one extra inverse power of distance. The electric field is the negative spatial gradient, so its monopole and dipole pieces fall as \(r^{-2}\) and \(r^{-3}\). An inversion-symmetric neutral distribution also has zero dipole and requires the next, quadrupole term. The omission of higher terms is controlled by source size relative to observation distance.

Derivation and applications

Translate the origin of a multipole expansion

A tabulated dipole moment can change when the coordinate origin moves. To track what a distant measurement actually fixes, consider a bounded static distribution of point charges \(q_j\) in vacuum. Let \(\mathbf r_j\) denote their positions relative to origin \(O\), and observe at \(\mathbf r=r\mathbf n\) with \(r\) much larger than every source distance. Expand the reciprocal distance in the Coulomb potential to first order in each source coordinate.

\[\frac{1}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_j|}=\frac1r+\frac{\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r_j}{r^2}+O\!\left(\frac{|\mathbf r_j|^2}{r^3}\right).\]

The coefficients of the first two terms are total charge \(Q\) and dipole moment \(\mathbf p\). Move the origin by vector \(\mathbf a\) without moving the apparatus. Subtract \(\mathbf a\) from every source coordinate and sum the moments again.

\[Q=\sum_j q_j,\]
\[\mathbf p'=\sum_jq_j(\mathbf r_j-\mathbf a)=\mathbf p-Q\mathbf a .\]

A neutral system consequently has an origin-independent dipole. For a charged system the observation vector also changes. To the same order, \(r'=|\mathbf r-\mathbf a|\) and the monopole at the new origin contributes precisely the apparent dipole that was removed from the source moment.

\[\frac Q{r'}+\frac{\mathbf n'\cdot\mathbf p'}{r'^2}=\frac Qr+\frac{Q\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf a+\mathbf n\cdot(\mathbf p-Q\mathbf a)}{r^2}+O(r^{-3}).\]

Multiplying by \(k=1/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\) restores voltage units. The cancellation holds for a distant observer relative to both origins. A finite-order numerical truncation can still differ at the first omitted order.

Worked example

Choose the centre of charge for two positive beads

Charges 2.00 nC at \(x=0\) and 1.00 nC at \(x=30.0\) mm have \(Q=3.00\) nC and \(p_x=30.0\) pC m. The shift \(a=p_x/Q=10.0\) mm sets the new dipole to zero. At the physical position \(x=1.00\) m the shifted monopole predicts \(kQ/0.990=27.24\) V. The exact two-charge expression \(k(2.00+1.00/0.970)10^{-9}\) gives 27.24 V to that precision. Higher moments explain the small residual.

Second application

Locate a neutral dipole from an axial voltage

Two opposite charges of magnitude 1.50 nC are separated by 8.00 mm. Their dipole moment is 12.0 pC m, pointing towards the positive charge. At a distance of 0.400 m from the midpoint on the positive axis, the leading voltage is \(kp/r^2=0.674\) V. Moving the coordinate origin 20 mm towards the positive charge leaves the moment at 12.0 pC m because \(Q=0\). Higher terms about the shifted origin account for the change in the numerical observer distance.

How far away is far enough to treat two charges as a dipole?

Take a positive and a negative charge, each of magnitude 1 nC, a distance \(2a\) apart. Observe on the positive axis at \(z>a\). Choose a tolerance of one per cent before looking at the graph and predict the required distance.

Superposition gives \(V=kq[(z-a)^{-1}-(z+a)^{-1}]=2kqa/(z^2-a^2)\). Replacing the denominator by \(z^2\) gives the dipole term. Dividing its difference from the exact expression by the exact expression leaves exactly \((a/z)^2\) on this axis.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

The one-per-cent distance is \(10a\). Use two separations to check it on the curves. If both charges are doubled, explain separately what happens to the absolute error and to the required distance for a relative tolerance.

Reconstruct the orientation of a quadrupole from six voltage probes

Suppose a small charge distribution is confined near a known origin and has zero total charge and zero electric dipole moment. A device surrounds it with probes at a common radius \(R\) much larger than the source diameter. Each probe reports the electrostatic potential relative to infinity. Axial measurements are easy to interpret when the source is known to share the instrument's axes. Here the internal orientation is unknown. The task is to infer a tensor from a set of readings, predict another reading, and identify which unseen source features the data leave undetermined.

Extract the angular form from the Coulomb integral

For a source coordinate \(\mathbf s\) and observation direction \(\mathbf n\), expansion of the Coulomb kernel at \(\mathbf r=R\mathbf n\) gives \(R^{-1}+(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s)/R^2+[3(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s)^2-s^2]/(2R^3)+\cdots\). The first two terms integrate to zero by our source assumptions. Define

\[Q_{ij}=\int\rho(\mathbf s)(3s_i s_j-s^2\delta_{ij})\,d^3s,\]
\[V(R\mathbf n)=\frac{1}{8\pi\epsilon_0R^3}\mathbf n^T Q\mathbf n+O(R^{-4}).\]

This convention absorbs the factor of three into \(Q\); a tensor defined using the raw second moment would need a trace removal and a different numerical factor. The stated tensor is symmetric. Its trace vanishes identically because \(3s^2-s^2\delta_{ii}=0\) in three spatial dimensions. Thus the six independent entries of a general symmetric matrix reduce to five.
