The scalar \(\mathbf n^TQ\mathbf n\) is the quadrupole measured along the chosen direction, in C m². It has the same value for opposite probe directions.

Construct an invertible set of probe directions

Let each calibrated reading be \(w(\mathbf n)=8\pi\epsilon_0 R^3 V(R\mathbf n)\). Along the three Cartesian axes, \(w_x=Q_{xx}\), \(w_y=Q_{yy}\), \(w_z=Q_{zz}\). Their sum should vanish for a pure quadrupole. To obtain an off-diagonal component, point a probe at \(\mathbf n_{xy}=(\hat{\mathbf x}+\hat{\mathbf y})/\sqrt2\). Expanding the quadratic form gives \(w_{xy}=(Q_{xx}+Q_{yy})/2+Q_{xy}\). Two analogous diagonal-of-a-plane directions give \(Q_{xz},Q_{yz}\). Six measurements determine the five independent values with one trace check. More directions provide independent residuals for noise or the influence of unmodelled moments.

For a concrete observation, report readings in units of \(Q_*=1.00\times10^{-14}\) C m². Suppose the axial readings are \(0.50,0.50,-1.00\), while the positive bisectors of \(xy,xz,yz\) give \(2.00,-0.25,-0.25\). The trace check passes. The reconstructed off-diagonal entries are \(Q_{xy}=1.50Q_*\) and \(Q_{xz}=Q_{yz}=0\). The \(xy\) block has eigenvectors \((1,1,0)/\sqrt2\) and \((1,-1,0)/\sqrt2\) with eigenvalues \(2Q_*,-Q_*\). The \(z\) axis also has eigenvalue \(-Q_*\). The source's principal symmetry axis lies on the positive \(xy\) bisector in this quadrupolar description. Rotating the other two axes within their degenerate plane yields the same tensor.

Predict a reading, then test the radial dependence

For \(\mathbf n=(1,1,1)/\sqrt3\), the diagonal contribution is the trace divided by three and vanishes. The off-diagonal contribution is \(2Q_{xy}/3=Q_*\). At \(R=0.10\) m the corresponding predicted potential is \(Q_*/(8\pi\epsilon_0 R^3)=44.9\) mV. Put a second independently calibrated probe on the same ray at 0.20 m. Its quadrupole potential should be one eighth of that value, 5.62 mV. This is a new prediction using both an angle and a radius absent in the reconstruction.

Measure also at the opposite direction. Their difference cancels the quadrupole and all even angular orders while retaining odd moments, which can expose a residual dipole or octupole. Their average cancels the odd contributions but can still contain the monopole and higher even orders. Multiplying by \(R^3\) and seeking a common plateau over several radii is stronger than a single pairwise symmetry test. Because voltage signal weakens as \(R^{-3}\), a distant probe can reduce truncation bias yet amplify instrumental error when reconstructing \(Q\).

Estimate which principal axis is actually determined by the measurements

For a real symmetric \(xy\) block, an eigenaxis angle \(\varphi\) obeys \(\tan(2\varphi)=2Q_{xy}/(Q_{xx}-Q_{yy})\). Evaluate it using the signed numerator and denominator to select the appropriate quadrant. Perturbing our measured difference \(Q_{xx}-Q_{yy}\) from zero to \(+0.10Q_*\), while leaving \(Q_{xy}=1.50Q_*\), shifts the inferred principal axis from \(45^\circ\) to about \(44.0^\circ\). The large separation between the exceptional eigenvalue and the two smaller ones stabilises this axis.

Within the plane belonging to the two equal negative eigenvalues, even a very small measurement perturbation can pick out almost any pair of apparent principal axes. That numerical rotation does not establish two new physical directions in the source. Fit the unique axis and the nearly degenerate subspace separately, and compare changes in predicted probe voltages under plausible uncertainty. A common 1% radius calibration error also changes the inferred magnitude by approximately 3% because of the \(R^3\) factor, but leaves the inferred angular orientation unchanged if it affects every probe equally.

Identify what the reconstruction cannot see

All six readings receive the same unknown voltage offset. Can the trace check remove it? Would that procedure distinguish a meter offset from an actual nonzero monopole? What changes if the absolute reference is reliable but the source may also have a dipole?

Derive the result and check it independently

At a common radius a uniform voltage offset adds the same constant to each rescaled \(w\). A genuine quadrupole has zero axial sum, so under the stated zero-charge assumption the mean of the three axial readings estimates that common offset. Subtract it from every reading before the angular inversion. An actual monopole contributes the same angular offset on a single sphere and cannot be distinguished from an instrumental zero by this operation. Measurements at multiple radii separate a constant voltage offset, a \(1/R\) monopole and a \(1/R^3\) quadrupole, provided the potential reference stays stable when moving the probes.

A dipole is odd under reversal of the observation direction. Pairing each measurement with its antipodal partner and averaging first removes its contribution exactly in the multipole expansion. After that, fit the symmetric traceless tensor and predict more withheld directions. Distinct microscopic charge arrangements can share all five adjusted moments; the measured tensor describes the distant field to this order and generally does not identify a unique arrangement of internal charges.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §3.4 · multipole expansions and source moments

Reading a charge distribution from far away

The radiation of an oscillating dipole was described through its time-dependent moment. A static distribution also has a hierarchy of moments. Consider fixed charges \(q_a\) inside a sphere of radius \(a\) and an observation point \(\mathbf r=r\mathbf n\) in vacuum. Measuring the complete charge map from far away would require extraordinary precision. Two distributions sharing their first few moments produce nearly the same voltage there. We can quantify which differences survive.

Superposition gives the exact potential as a sum of \(q_a/(4\pi\epsilon_0|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_a|)\). Factor out \(r\) and expand \((1+x)^{-1/2}\) with \(x=-2\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r_a/r+r_a^2/r^2\). Keep both the first-order contribution from \(r_a^2\) and the squared contribution from \(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r_a\). They have the same order in source size.

\[\frac{1}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r_a|}=\frac1r+\frac{\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r_a}{r^2}+\frac{3(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r_a)^2-r_a^2}{2r^3}+O(a^3/r^4).\]

The total charge multiplies the first term. The second depends on the dipole moment \(\mathbf p=\sum q_a\mathbf r_a\). Translation of the coordinate origin by \(\mathbf d\) changes the dipole to \(\mathbf p-Q\mathbf d\). A neutral system therefore has an origin-independent dipole. When charge and dipole vanish, the leading quadrupole is likewise independent of the chosen origin.

From potential to a vector measurement

For an axial dipole the leading potential is \(p\cos\theta/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2)\). A probe measures a force per unit charge, requiring both radial and angular derivatives. The angular gradient contains \(1/r\) because the physical arc length is \(r\,d\theta\). This produces

\[E_r=\frac{2p\cos\theta}{4\pi\epsilon_0r^3},\qquad E_\theta=\frac{p\sin\theta}{4\pi\epsilon_0r^3}.\]

A voltage reading of zero on the equatorial plane thus coexists with a nonzero transverse field. The vector result also supplies a laboratory diagnostic. Doubling distance reduces an ideal dipole field by eight, while a residual net charge produces a fourfold reduction. A small net charge can dominate sufficiently far from a nominally neutral device.

A quadrupole on the bench

Put \(+1.0\) nC at \(z=\pm1.0\) cm and \(-2.0\) nC at the origin. Charge, dipole and all odd moments vanish. On the positive axis the exact result simplifies before expansion,

\[V(z)=kq\left(\frac1{z-a}+\frac1{z+a}-\frac2z\right)=\frac{2kqa^2}{z(z^2-a^2)},\qquad z>a .\]

At \(z=0.100\) m the leading term \(2kqa^2/z^3\) gives \(1.798\) V. The exact value is \(1.816\) V, so the relative error referred to the exact value is \(a^2/z^2=1.0\%\). In the equatorial plane the same arrangement gives \(2kq[1/\sqrt{r^2+a^2}-1/r]\), with a negative leading voltage of half the axial magnitude. Along directions where the angular factor of a leading moment vanishes, a relative-error estimate based on that term alone loses meaning. Compare absolute errors there or retain the next surviving moment.

A simple way to detect an experimental origin error is to compare voltages on opposite sides at equal nominal distances. For a pure axial dipole these voltages have equal magnitude and opposite signs. Translating either probe along the axis changes the magnitude because of the radial gradient. An apparent even contribution can then be caused by mismatched distances as easily as by a physical quadrupole. Reverse the source orientation while leaving the two probes fixed to separate a geometric offset from a source component that retains its sign.

A field gradient exerts a net force on a neutral dipole

Far-field multipole moments also determine how a localized source responds to an imposed field that varies little across it. Consider two charges \(\pm q\) separated by a small vector \(\mathbf d\) pointing from negative to positive charge. Their dipole moment is \(\mathbf p=q\mathbf d\). Expand the external potential around their midpoint \(\mathbf R\). The constant contributions cancel, leaving interaction energy \(q[V(\mathbf R+\mathbf d/2)-V(\mathbf R-\mathbf d/2)]\simeq-\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf E(\mathbf R)\). The next terms are negligible when the external field varies on a scale much longer than the separation.

For a fixed dipole orientation the force follows by taking the gradient with respect to the midpoint. In components \(F_i=p_j\partial_i E_j\). The electrostatic field is curl free, so this equals \(p_j\partial_jE_i\), the difference of the forces on the two ends obtained by direct expansion. A uniform field consequently exerts torque \(\mathbf p\times\mathbf E\) but no net force on a fixed neutral dipole.

An induced dipole changes its magnitude as the field is raised. For a linear polarizable object with \(\mathbf p=\alpha\mathbf E\), the work integrated over field buildup gives energy \(U=-\alpha E^2/2\). The one-half also follows by adding the internal energy cost \(p^2/(2\alpha)\) to the coupling \(-\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf E\) and minimizing over \(\mathbf p\). The force is then \(\mathbf F=(\alpha/2)\nabla E^2\) for real constant \(\alpha\) in a static field. Simply substituting \(\alpha E\) into the permanent-dipole energy before differentiating would double the answer.

Predict a force on a dielectric bead

A neutral bead in vacuum has static polarizability \(\alpha=4.00\times10^{-21}\) C m²/V. Locally on a symmetry axis the field is \(E_z=2.00\times10^4\) V/m and its axial gradient is \(3.00\times10^6\) V/m². Assume the bead is much smaller than the variation length \(E/|\partial_zE|=6.67\) mm and other field components vanish on that axis. The induced moment is \(8.00\times10^{-17}\) C m. Its axial force is \(\alpha E_z\partial_z E_z=2.40\times10^{-10}\) N, directed toward increasing squared field.

If the field amplitude of the electrodes is doubled without changing their geometry, both field and gradient double, so the force rises fourfold. A fixed, mechanically aligned permanent dipole would show only a twofold force increase. This scaling and a reversal of electrode polarity offer practical discrimination between the two models. Near a field zero an induced dipole and its leading force both vanish; finite-size and higher-moment effects must be considered if a measurable force persists there.

The validity condition on field gradients can be phrased in terms of an observable length scale for a real bead. Moving it by a fraction of a millimetre along the axis and measuring its induced moment probes the local derivative of field. A moment tracking field linearly while force follows its squared-field gradient supports the same polarizability in both tests. If the bead size becomes comparable to the variation length, different parts of it experience different applied fields. Higher induced moments and local field distortion then enter the force balance, so one scalar dipole polarizability is no longer sufficient.

Develop this calculation further

Chapter 16 · Spherical harmonics as measurements of boundary voltage · Chapter 28 · Why a small transparent sphere scatters light

Chapter 8 · Polarization of a dielectric sphere

Match electrostatic fields at a curved interface and infer a material’s induced dipole from its exterior potential.

From first principles

How a dielectric surface determines its internal field

Put a uniform, isotropic dielectric sphere of radius \(a\) and permittivity \(\epsilon\) in a uniform static field \(E_0\hat{\mathbf z}\) in vacuum. No free charge is supplied to the interface. Gauss’s law for the electric displacement therefore requires continuous normal \(D\), while electrostatic circulation requires continuous tangential \(E\). Write the potentials using the axial angular dependence \(\cos\theta\):

\[V_{\rm in}=-Ar\cos\theta,\]
\[V_{\rm out}=-E_0r\cos\theta+B\cos\theta/r^2.\]

These are harmonic functions. The interior expression is regular at the centre and the exterior correction decays at infinity. Continuity of potential at \(r=a\) gives \(A=E_0-B/a^3\). Obtain the radial fields by differentiating both potentials with respect to radius and reversing their signs. Normal displacement continuity then gives a second independent equation:

\[\epsilon A=\epsilon_0(E_0+2B/a^3),\]
\[(\epsilon+2\epsilon_0)A=3\epsilon_0E_0.\]

The interior field is uniform and has magnitude \(A\). The exterior correction is that of a dipole of moment \(4\pi\epsilon_0 B\) along the applied field. In a material indistinguishable from vacuum, \(A=E_0\) and the dipole vanishes. This limit checks both the radial derivative’s factor of two and the sign of the induced dipole.

Derivation and applications

Find the field inside a spherical cavity in a dielectric

A hollow pore samples the field inside a bulk insulator. A sphere makes the difference between the pore field and the surrounding macroscopic field analytically accessible. Let the host have static permittivity \(\epsilon_h\), the pore filling have permittivity \(\epsilon_c\), and their spherical interface have radius \(a\). Both media are linear, isotropic and homogeneous. There is no free interfacial charge. Far inside the host an applied uniform field \(E_\infty\) points along \(z\). Choose potentials with the same angular dependence as this forcing.

\[V_c=-Cr\cos\theta,\]
\[V_h=-E_\infty r\cos\theta+\frac{D}{r^2}\cos\theta .\]

The regular interior solution gives a uniform pore field of magnitude \(C\). The exterior term proportional to \(D\) is the decaying dipolar disturbance. Matching potential at \(r=a\) ensures the tangential field is continuous. Matching normal displacement uses \(E_r=-\partial_r V\) and introduces a factor of two in the exterior term.

\[C=E_\infty-\frac{D}{a^3},\]
\[\epsilon_c C=\epsilon_h\left(E_\infty+\frac{2D}{a^3}\right).\]

Eliminate the exterior coefficient with the first equation. The result depends on both permittivities even when the pore itself is empty. The radius controls the dipole term through its volume, while the uniform cavity field depends only on the contrast.

\[C=\frac{3\epsilon_h}{\epsilon_c+2\epsilon_h}E_\infty,\]
\[D=a^3\frac{\epsilon_c-\epsilon_h}{\epsilon_c+2\epsilon_h}E_\infty .\]

Equal permittivities recover a perfectly uniform field everywhere. A vacuum cavity in a host of very large positive permittivity has \(C/E_\infty\) approaching \(3/2\), with the far field inside the host held fixed.

Worked example

Read the voltage across an air pore

A transparent host has \(\epsilon_h=4.00\epsilon_0\) and a spherical air pore of diameter 0.600 mm. Under \(E_\infty=1.50\) kV/m, the field ratio is \(12/9=4/3\). The pore field is 2.00 kV/m and the magnitude of the potential difference between opposite axial poles is \(2000(0.000600)=1.20\) V. A diameter perpendicular to the applied field has both endpoints at the same potential.

Second application

Infer a pore filling from the measured field

In a separate host of relative permittivity 3.00, a calibrated probe finds a uniform cavity field equal to 0.750 of the host's distant field. Inverting the ratio gives \(\epsilon_c/\epsilon_0=3(3.00)/0.750-2(3.00)=6.00\). The filling is more polarisable than the host. As a check, the exterior coefficient is \(D/(a^3E_\infty)=(6-3)/(6+6)=0.250\), which yields the observed 0.750 field ratio by the potential condition alone.

Which field jumps at the pole of a dielectric sphere?

A linear dielectric sphere in vacuum sits in an otherwise uniform electric field. Follow the positive polar axis. Use the normal-displacement condition to predict the ratio of the fields before solving for either of them.

The regular interior potential is \(-Ar\cos\theta\), while the exterior adds a dipole to the background potential. Continuity of potential and of \(\epsilon E_n\) gives \(A/E_0=3/(\epsilon_r+2)\) and exterior dipole coefficient \((\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\) in units of \(E_0a^3\). The two curves show both one-sided limits at \(r=a\).

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Set the permittivity ratio to one. Then increase it until the interior field is below one fifth of the applied field. Does the exterior field at the pole grow without bound? Take the limit of the coefficient to explain your result.

A coated inclusion can have zero external dipole and a finite internal field

A spherical particle of radius \(a\) and relative permittivity \(\epsilon_c\) is surrounded by a concentric shell extending to \(b\), with relative permittivity \(\epsilon_s\). The host has relative permittivity \(\epsilon_h\) and supports an imposed uniform electrostatic field \(E_0\hat{\mathbf z}\) far from the particle. All three materials are homogeneous, linear and isotropic, with positive permittivities and no free interfacial charge. Can one choose the amount of coating so that a distant probe sees exactly the undisturbed field, even though the core still changes the internal field? The answer requires matching fields at two interfaces under the same applied excitation.

Choose radial functions before assigning interface conditions

With axial symmetry and a uniform applied field, only the \(\ell=1\) sector is driven. Spherical boundaries preserve this angular dependence. The two radial solutions of Laplace's equation are \(r\) and \(r^{-2}\). Reject the divergent term in the solid core, keep both in the shell, and allow a decaying dipole in the host. Write

\[V_c=-Ar\cos\theta,\]
\[V_s=(-Br+C/r^2)\cos\theta,\]
\[V_h=(-E_0r+D/r^2)\cos\theta .\]

Here \(D\) is a potential coefficient, distinct from electric displacement \(\mathbf D\). The latter enters through continuity of its normal component \(\epsilon E_r\), while continuity of potential fixes the common tangential electric field at each radius. At \(r=a\) these two equations reduce to \(A=B-C/a^3\) and \(\epsilon_c A=\epsilon_s(B+2C/a^3)\). Define \(m=(\epsilon_c-\epsilon_s)/(\epsilon_c+2\epsilon_s)\). Eliminating \(A\) gives \(C=a^3mB\) and \(A=(1-m)B\). This first interface problem determines how the core changes the mixture of regular and singular solutions in the shell, even before we choose a host.

Condense the inner sphere and shell into a surface response

Let \(f=(a/b)^3\) be the core volume fraction. At the outer surface the shell potential divided by \(-b\cos\theta\) is \(B(1-fm)\), and its normal displacement divided by \(\epsilon_0\cos\theta\) is \(\epsilon_sB(1+2fm)\). Their ratio defines the relative permittivity of a homogeneous sphere having the same boundary response to this excitation:

\[\epsilon_{\rm eq}=\epsilon_s\frac{1+2fm}{1-fm},\]
\[\frac{D}{b^3E_0}=\frac{\epsilon_{\rm eq}-\epsilon_h}{\epsilon_{\rm eq}+2\epsilon_h}.\]

The second relation follows by matching host potential and normal displacement at \(b\) just as for a single homogeneous sphere. It also checks limiting cases. At \(f=0\) the equivalent object has shell permittivity; at \(f=1\) substitution reduces it to \(\epsilon_c\). If the core and shell are identical, \(m=0\) and changing their nominal boundary does nothing.

To eliminate the exterior dipole choose \(\epsilon_{\rm eq}=\epsilon_h\), giving \(f=(\epsilon_h-\epsilon_s)/[m(\epsilon_h+2\epsilon_s)]\). A physical value requires \(0\leq f\leq1\); for positive distinct permittivities the host must lie between the shell and core responses. An arbitrary mismatched shell cannot always cancel the polarisation of its core. The condition concerns a uniform applied field in electrostatics. A nearby point source drives multiple angular orders with different radial responses.

Read all three fields in a neutral inclusion

Choose \(\epsilon_c=8,\epsilon_s=2,\epsilon_h=3\). Then \(m=1/2\), \(f=2/7\), and \(a/b=0.6586\). Under the matched condition \(D=0\), so continuity at \(b\) demands \(B(1-fm)=E_0\), giving \(B=7E_0/6\) and \(A=7E_0/12\). The core still sees a uniform field only 58.3% as large as the host's undisturbed field. At \(b\) the shell-side radial electric field at the north pole is \(B(1+2fm)=1.50E_0\). Multiplying by shell permittivity 2 recovers \(3E_0\), exactly the required host displacement. At the equator the tangential field agrees directly. These surface tests can reveal a wrong sign in \(C\) even if an exterior dipole fit accidentally vanishes.

For \(b=30\) μm in \(E_0=3000\) V/m, \(a=19.8\) μm and the voltage difference across the core diameter has magnitude \(2aA=69.2\) mV. The host would give \(2aE_0=119\) mV over the same distance. A far-field perturbation measurement alone cannot establish a particle's internal electric exposure. A small capacitive probe inserted into the core would perturb the solution, so a practical comparison must estimate that additional boundary disturbance.

Check the coating with a different spatial pattern

To test how specific the cancellation is, impose an axisymmetric \(\ell=2\) potential on a distant enclosing electrode. More generally the radial functions are \(r^\ell\) and \(r^{-\ell-1}\) for each angular order. Repeating the inner matching gives \(m_\ell=\ell(\epsilon_c-\epsilon_s)/[\ell\epsilon_c+(\ell+1)\epsilon_s]\) in place of \(m\). The outer surface now weighs the decaying term by \(f_\ell=(a/b)^{2\ell+1}\). The equivalent boundary permittivity for this order is \(\epsilon_s[1+(\ell+1)f_\ell m_\ell/\ell]/[1-f_\ell m_\ell]\). Setting \(\ell=1\) reproduces the expression derived directly above.

For the neutral-dipole geometry at \(\ell=2\), \(m_2=12/22\) and \(f_2=(2/7)^{5/3}\). These give an equivalent response near 2.36, clearly distinct from the host value of 3. A field-gradient experiment can thus reveal the inclusion even when the uniform-field exterior measurement gives an exact cancellation. This is a direct test of the assumption that the imposed field varies slowly on the scale of the coated sphere.

Quantify tolerance and distinguish neutrality from zero polarisation

Keep the outer radius fixed but let the manufactured core radius be 1% larger than the neutral value. Predict the sign and approximate size of the exterior dipole coefficient \(D/(b^3E_0)\). Why does summing all bound surface charges give a weaker check?

Derive the result and check it independently

A small fractional radius error gives \(\delta f\simeq3f(0.01)=0.00857\). Differentiate the equivalent permittivity: \(d\epsilon_{\rm eq}/df=3\epsilon_s m/(1-fm)^2\), which here is \(49/12=4.083\). Thus \(\delta\epsilon_{\rm eq}\simeq0.0350\). Near the matched point the dipole coefficient varies as \(\delta\epsilon_{\rm eq}/(3\epsilon_h)\), giving \(+0.00389\). The slightly increased high-permittivity core gives a weak dipole along the applied field. Inserting the actual perturbed radius in the full rational formula checks the linearisation.

Bound charge integrated over each complete spherical interface vanishes because its angular pattern is proportional to \(\cos\theta\). This is true for both matched and mismatched coatings. It guarantees absence of a monopole but says nothing about the dipole. A numerical verification should check continuity of radial displacement, tangential field and the exterior dipole coefficient separately; one integrated neutral charge reading cannot replace those tests.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §4.4.2 · boundary problems with linear dielectrics

An inclusion in a uniform field

Place a homogeneous dielectric sphere of radius \(a\) and permittivity \(\epsilon\) in vacuum. Distant electrodes produce a static uniform field \(E_0\mathbf e_z\) before insertion. Take the dielectric to be linear, isotropic and free of mobile charge. Bound charge can develop at its surface even though the object is electrically neutral. The monopole measurement from the previous lecture consequently misses the material response. The dipole moment provides a measurable alternative.

In each constant-permittivity region Gauss’s law for \(\mathbf D\) and \(\mathbf E=-\nabla V\) give Laplace’s equation. The background potential is \(-E_0r\cos\theta\), an angular pattern of degree one. A spherical interface does not couple different angular degrees. The allowed radial functions for this degree are \(r\) and \(r^{-2}\). Finiteness at the centre prohibits a singular interior term. Matching the imposed field far away fixes the growing exterior term.

\[V_{\rm in}=-Ar\cos\theta,\qquad V_{\rm out}=-E_0r\cos\theta+B\cos\theta/r^2.\]

Solve at the surface

Continuity of voltage gives \(A=E_0-B/a^3\). It also guarantees continuity of tangential field, as a tangential displacement compares points along the same surface potential. Normal displacement is continuous in the absence of free surface charge. Differentiating radially changes the sign and supplies a factor of two for the decaying term, so \(\epsilon A=\epsilon_0(E_0+2B/a^3)\). Elimination gives

\[A=\frac{3\epsilon_0}{\epsilon+2\epsilon_0}E_0,\qquad B=a^3\frac{\epsilon-\epsilon_0}{\epsilon+2\epsilon_0}E_0.\]

The uniform internal field polarizes the sphere along the field direction. Its bound surface charge is \(\sigma_b=(\epsilon-\epsilon_0)A\cos\theta\). Opposite hemispheres cancel in total charge. Their separation gives an induced dipole \(p=4\pi\epsilon_0B\). Equivalently integrate the uniform polarization over the volume. Both constructions give the same dipole, one from exterior voltage and one from microscopic charge displacement. When \(\epsilon=\epsilon_0\) the exterior perturbation vanishes. A very large positive permittivity suppresses the internal field and approaches the conductor result.

Predict a voltage difference

Take \(a=2.00\) mm, \(\epsilon=4\epsilon_0\) and \(E_0=30.0\) kV/m. The internal field is \(15.0\) kV/m, and \(B=1.20\times10^{-4}\) V m². Multiplication by \(4\pi\epsilon_0\) gives \(p=1.34\times10^{-14}\) C m. At an axial probe 10.0 mm from the centre the sphere increases voltage relative to the uniform-field baseline by \(B/r^2=1.20\) V. The opposite axial probe sees a shift of \(-1.20\) V. A differential measurement doubles the signal and removes a common voltage offset.

For the assumed uniform applied field the exterior dipole solution is exact at every point outside the sphere. A nearby field-producing electrode can spoil that simplification. A field that varies across the particle contains higher angular orders in its local expansion and their induced responses survive at the surface. In an experiment, repeating the measurement with a smaller sphere tests the predicted \(a^3\) law while reducing sensitivity to field curvature.

The assumption of real, constant permittivity is experimentally testable. A slowly varying applied field should give a dipole that follows the field with negligible lag. If the measured signal shifts in phase while frequency is raised, the response must be described by a frequency-dependent complex polarization. A static voltage estimate alone cannot supply that extra timescale.

Build an effective permittivity from many inclusions

A single dielectric sphere in a smooth applied field develops a dipole. A dilute suspension of such spheres can therefore alter the bulk capacitance of a sample. Suppose identical spheres of radius \(a\) and permittivity \(\epsilon_i\) occupy a small fraction \(f\) of a matrix with permittivity \(\epsilon_m\). The matrix is homogeneous, both materials are isotropic, and no conducting path or interfacial free charge is present. Each sphere sees nearly the matrix field when it is far from all neighbours. The single-sphere boundary calculation gives an excess dipole \(p=4\pi\epsilon_m a^3 b E_m\), with \(b=(\epsilon_i-\epsilon_m)/(\epsilon_i+2\epsilon_m)\).

The number of inclusions per unit sample volume is \(n=f/(4\pi a^3/3)\). At first order the excess polarization density is \(np=3f\epsilon_m b E_m\). The difference between \(E_m\) and the sample-average field is itself first order in \(f\), so replacing one by the other in this correction changes only higher-order terms. Comparing average displacement with \(\epsilon_{\rm eff}E\) gives

\[\epsilon_{\rm eff}=\epsilon_m[1+3fb+O(f^2)] .\]

This is a dilute effective-medium prediction. The absolute radius cancels at fixed volume fraction because each dipole scales as \(a^3\) while number density scales as \(a^{-3}\). The cancellation requires particles small compared with the scale of the imposed field. Aggregation, closely spaced pairs and a large occupied fraction can change local fields and produce a nonlinear dependence on concentration.

Measure a composite using two capacitor readings

A parallel-plate measurement cell has electrode area \(25.0\) cm² and gap 1.00 mm. Filling it with matrix alone gives \(\epsilon_m=2.00\epsilon_0\), hence \(C_m=44.3\) pF after subtraction of stray capacitance. Disperse spheres with \(\epsilon_i=8.00\epsilon_0\) at \(f=0.0200\). The contrast factor is \((8-2)/(8+4)=0.500\). Consequently \(\epsilon_{\rm eff}/\epsilon_m\simeq1+3(0.0200)(0.500)=1.030\). The expected reading is \(45.6\) pF, an increase of approximately \(1.33\) pF.

A second sample with the same volume fraction but twice the sphere radius should give the same leading result. A systematic change might indicate settling, aggregation, finite-gap effects or frequency-dependent interface polarization. In the equal-permittivity limit the contrast factor vanishes and no capacitive signal should remain. In the high-permittivity limit \(b\) approaches one, so the fractional increase saturates at approximately \(3f\) to first order. This bounded prediction gives a useful check when a simple arithmetic mixing rule appears to predict an arbitrarily large capacitance increase from an arbitrarily dilute concentration.

The hypothesis that the suspension is dilute can be tested without a microscopic field probe. Make a concentration series by adding material with known volume at fixed particle size. The predicted initial slope of relative capacitance versus volume fraction for the numerical pair is \(3b=1.50\). A linear fit restricted to the lowest concentrations estimates the contrast parameter and hence the inclusion permittivity if the matrix is known. Strong curvature at higher concentration then measures the breakdown of the independent-inclusion approximation. Extrapolating that curvature back as a new single-particle dielectric constant would blend collective and material effects.

Develop this calculation further

Chapter 18 · How microscopic dipoles produce macroscopic charge · Chapter 21 · What an interface does to phase, field and power

Problem sheet 4 · Far fields and induced moments

Infer moments and dielectric properties from potential measurements, including a measurement taken where a leading potential vanishes.

The axis and the midplane

Put charges \(+2.00\) nC and \(-2.00\) nC at \(z=+3.0\) mm and \(-3.0\) mm. Obtain the leading voltage at \(z=0.120\) m and the electric field at the same radial distance in the midplane. Explain which observable remains on the midplane.

Show the dipole calculation

The separation is \(6.0\) mm, giving \(p=1.20\times10^{-11}\) C m along positive \(z\). The axial potential is \(kp/r^2=7.49\) V. The midplane potential vanishes exactly by symmetry. There the leading field is \(-kp\,\mathbf e_z/r^3\), or \(62.4\) V/m towards negative \(z\). Although tangent derivatives in the midplane vanish, the voltage changes in the perpendicular direction. The small source ratio \(0.003/0.12=0.025\) makes the neglected relative finite-size corrections of order \(0.025^2\).

Measure the dielectric constant

A sphere of radius 3.0 mm in a field of 20.0 kV/m creates an additional axial voltage of 1.20 V at 15.0 mm. Determine its relative permittivity and predict the interior field. State one geometry change that tests this interpretation.

Show the inversion

The dipole coefficient is \(B=\Delta V r^2=2.70\times10^{-4}\) V m². Thus \(s=B/(a^3E_0)=0.500\). From \(s=(\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\) obtain \(\epsilon_r=(1+2s)/(1-s)=4.00\). The uniform interior field is \(3E_0/(\epsilon_r+2)=10.0\) kV/m. At twice the axial distance the extra voltage should fall to \(0.300\) V. Measuring the extra voltage requires subtracting the no-sphere reading, which increases in magnitude away from the origin.

A nearly neutral inclusion

A polarized sphere has induced moment \(3.0\times10^{-13}\) C m and a residual free charge of \(+1.0\) pC. Find the axial distance where charge and dipole produce equal potential magnitudes. For two probes placed at opposite axial positions, derive combinations that isolate the two contributions.

Show the sum and difference

Equating \(kQ/r\) and \(kp/r^2\) gives \(r=p/Q=0.30\) m, assuming the probes are outside the sphere. After subtracting imposed background voltage, the two readings are \(V_\pm=kQ/r\pm kp/r^2\). Their average isolates the monopole while their half difference isolates the dipole. At the crossover each isolated term has magnitude \(0.0300\) V. An even quadrupole would also enter the average, so repeat at a second radius to distinguish its faster \(r^{-3}\) potential decay.

Chapter 9 · Charge relaxation and magnetic diffusion

Distinguish two conductor timescales and estimate how deeply an alternating magnetic field penetrates a solid.

From first principles

Trace two relaxation processes back to charge and current

A homogeneous ohmic conductor obeys the measured material law \(\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf E\). Local conservation says that charge decreases where current flows out. Take the divergence of the material law, then use Gauss’s law for constant permittivity:

\[\partial_t\rho=-\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=-\sigma\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=-\frac{\sigma}{\epsilon}\rho,\]
\[\rho(t)=\rho(0)e^{-t\sigma/\epsilon}.\]

This equation describes local free-volume charge relaxation within the constant-conductivity model. The spatial redistribution of a magnetic field in the same body is a different measurement. At frequencies where displacement current is small compared with conduction current, write \(\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu\sigma\mathbf E\) and take a curl. Use Faraday’s law and the absence of magnetic monopoles to cancel the gradient of \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B\):

\[-\nabla^2\mathbf B=-\mu\sigma\,\partial_t\mathbf B,\]
\[\partial_t\mathbf B=\frac{1}{\mu\sigma}\nabla^2\mathbf B .\]

A magnetic disturbance varying on the length \(L\) has a diffusion time of order \(\mu\sigma L^2\), which grows as the square of the size. A charge-relaxation time \(\epsilon/\sigma\) contains no length. These estimates rely on the separate constitutive and frequency assumptions above. Extrapolating a static conductivity through arbitrarily high frequencies exceeds those assumptions.

Derivation and applications

Follow a magnetic field step into a metal

A switched field probes a conductor over a growing depth. Take a conducting half-space \(x>0\) with constant permeability \(\mu\) and conductivity \(\sigma\). Initially its magnetic field is zero. At \(t=0\) impose a uniform tangential field \(B_s\) at its planar surface and maintain it thereafter. Work at times longer than charge relaxation and switching transients, neglect displacement current, and assume no variation parallel to the surface. Ampère's and Faraday's laws, together with Ohm's law, reduce to a diffusion equation for the tangential induction.

\[\partial_t B=D\partial_x^2B,\]
\[D=\frac{1}{\mu\sigma}.\]

Since \(D\) has dimensions of area per time and the half-space supplies no length scale, try \(B=B_sf(\eta)\) with \(\eta=x/(2\sqrt{Dt})\). Differentiating at fixed \(x\) gives \(\partial_t\eta=-\eta/(2t)\) and differentiating at fixed \(t\) gives \(\partial_x\eta=1/(2\sqrt{Dt})\). Common factors cancel, leaving

\[f''+2\eta f'=0,\]
\[f'=C e^{-\eta^2}.\]

The surface imposes \(f(0)=1\) and the unaffected interior gives \(f(\infty)=0\). Integrate the Gaussian slope from \(\eta\) to infinity and normalise its integral at the surface. The result fixes both the shape and the rate at which any chosen field contour advances.

\[\frac{B(x,t)}{B_s}=\operatorname{erfc}\!\left(\frac{x}{2\sqrt{Dt}}\right),\]
\[\operatorname{erfc}(u)=\frac{2}{\sqrt\pi}\int_u^\infty e^{-s^2}ds.\]

This contour speed falls with time. A sinusoidal skin depth describes a steady, single-frequency measurement, while the step response has a penetration length that grows with the square root of elapsed time.

Worked example

Time the half-field crossing in aluminium

Take \(\sigma=3.50\times10^7\) S/m and \(\mu=\mu_0\). The diffusivity is \(0.0227\) m²/s. Half the applied field corresponds to \(\operatorname{erfc}^{-1}(0.5)=0.47694\). At \(x=2.00\) mm its arrival time is \(x^2/[4D(0.47694)^2]=0.193\) ms. A sensor at 4.00 mm would reach the same fraction at 0.773 ms as long as the far surface remains sufficiently distant.

Second application

Estimate conductivity from a single contour

In a thick nonmagnetic sample the 15.73 percent contour reaches 3.00 mm after 0.180 ms. Since \(\operatorname{erfc}(1)=0.1573\), this contour has \(x=2\sqrt{Dt}\). Its measured depth gives \(D=(0.00300)^2/(4\times0.000180)=0.0125\) m²/s and hence \(\sigma=1/(\mu_0D)=6.37\times10^7\) S/m. A conductivity inferred this way depends on knowing the permeability independently, since the field trace determines their product.

How deep does a time-dependent field penetrate?

A plane harmonic field enters a homogeneous good conductor. Assume \(\mu=\mu_0\) and conduction current much greater than displacement current. Mark the depth where amplitude is reduced to \(1/e\) before comparing the magnitude with the in-phase component.

Combining Faraday's law with \(\nabla\times H=\sigma E\) gives the diffusion equation \(\partial_t E=(\mu\sigma)^{-1}\partial_z^2 E\). With time factor \(e^{i\omega t}\) the decaying spatial root is \((1+i)/\delta\) where \(\delta=\sqrt{2/(\omega\mu\sigma)}\). Only its real part controls attenuation. Its imaginary part shifts the phase.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Raise frequency fourfold and predict the shift of the first zero of the in-phase field. Use the graph to distinguish this zero from disappearance of the full harmonic amplitude.

Why charge accumulates at the interface between two weak conductors

A pair of plane electrodes sandwiches two slightly conducting dielectric layers in series. A voltage step is applied and held for a long time. At the first instant the voltage divides as in a capacitor. Much later it divides as in two resistors. The terminal voltage is unchanged throughout, so what physical quantity moved between these limits? A probe located at the internal interface reveals the charge, its timescale and the field that can persist even after the two outer electrodes are shorted. This is a simple example of interfacial, or Maxwell–Wagner, relaxation.

Match total current rather than conduction current alone

Let layer \(i\) have thickness \(d_i\), absolute permittivity \(\epsilon_i\) and conductivity \(\sigma_i>0\). Set positive field from electrode 1 through layer 1 and layer 2 toward electrode 2. Large area and negligible edge effects make the fields \(E_1(t),E_2(t)\) uniform within each layer. Both media are linear, isotropic and nondispersive over the measurement band apart from their specified Ohmic currents. Assume no initial space charge, no interfacial reactions and a voltage rise slow compared with field transit time but fast compared with interfacial relaxation. When the applied voltage has become the constant \(V\),

\[d_1E_1+d_2E_2=V,\]
\[\sigma_1 E_1+\epsilon_1\dot E_1=\sigma_2 E_2+\epsilon_2\dot E_2 .\]

The second equation is continuity of the sum of conduction and displacement current densities. The conduction currents alone can differ while free charge accumulates on the interface. Define \(\sigma_f=\epsilon_2E_2-\epsilon_1E_1\). Differentiating it and using the second equation yields \(\dot\sigma_f=\sigma_1E_1-\sigma_2E_2\), an independent local balance that fixes the sign of any predicted sheet charge.

Derive the response without guessing a circuit

Eliminate \(E_2=(V-d_1E_1)/d_2\). Since \(V\) is constant after the rise, \(\dot E_2=-d_1\dot E_1/d_2\). Collecting the terms gives

\[(\epsilon_1d_2+\epsilon_2d_1)\dot E_1+(\sigma_1d_2+\sigma_2d_1)E_1=\sigma_2V .\]

Before conduction has had time to move appreciable charge, \(\sigma_f(0^+)=0\), so \(E_1(0^+)=\epsilon_2V/(\epsilon_1d_2+\epsilon_2d_1)\). In steady state the displacement derivatives vanish and \(E_1(\infty)=\sigma_2V/(\sigma_1d_2+\sigma_2d_1)\). The first-order equation therefore gives

\[E_1(t)=E_1(\infty)+[E_1(0^+)-E_1(\infty)]e^{-t/\tau},\]
\[\tau=\frac{\epsilon_1d_2+\epsilon_2d_1}{\sigma_1d_2+\sigma_2d_1}.\]

Substitution into the first voltage equation gives \(E_2(t)\), and subtraction of the two normal displacements gives \(\sigma_f(t)=\sigma_f(\infty)(1-e^{-t/\tau})\). Its final value can have either sign even when both applied fields always point in the same direction. If \(\sigma_1/\epsilon_1=\sigma_2/\epsilon_2\), the initial and final voltage divisions coincide. Both materials conduct and dissipate power, but this particular interfacial charge does not build up.

Reconstruct an interface charge from a voltage trace

Choose \(d_1=d_2=1.00\) mm, \(\epsilon_1=2\epsilon_0,\epsilon_2=6\epsilon_0\), \(\sigma_1=1.00\times10^{-9}\) S/m and \(\sigma_2=3.00\times10^{-10}\) S/m. With \(V=10.0\) V, the first layer initially drops 7.50 V but finally drops only 2.31 V. The time constant is 54.5 ms. After one time constant the first-layer drop is 4.22 V. The final sheet is positive, \(\epsilon_0[6(7692)-2(2308)]\simeq3.68\times10^{-7}\) C/m². Equal steady conduction currents \(2.31\,\mu\mathrm{A/m^2}\) through both layers independently confirm the final voltage division.

An alternative calculation uses admittances per area in the Laplace domain: \(Y_i/A=(\sigma_i+s\epsilon_i)/d_i\). The fraction of total voltage across layer 1 is \(Y_2/(Y_1+Y_2)\). Its pole is at \(s=-(\sigma_1d_2+\sigma_2d_1)/(\epsilon_1d_2+\epsilon_2d_1)\), precisely the time constant found without using a circuit. Its high- and low-frequency limits give the two electrode readings above. The familiar parallel resistor–capacitor representation of each layer now has an identifiable field origin and a way to test its validity.

Estimate conductivities from a step without measuring a tiny dc current

If the thicknesses and permittivities are known, the late-time voltage fraction and the measured relaxation time constrain two different combinations of the conductivities. In the equal-thickness example write \(f=V_1(\infty)/V=\sigma_2/(\sigma_1+\sigma_2)\). The transient gives the sum \(\sigma_1+\sigma_2=(\epsilon_1+\epsilon_2)/\tau\). Using \(f=0.231\) and \(\tau=54.5\) ms yields a sum of \(1.30\times10^{-9}\) S/m, with contributions \(\sigma_2\simeq3.0\times10^{-10}\) S/m and \(\sigma_1\simeq1.0\times10^{-9}\) S/m. The initial capacitive voltage division remains an independent check on the assumed permittivities and geometry.

This inversion becomes ill-conditioned if the initial and final voltage fractions nearly coincide. Although the formal time constant is still finite, the measured exponential then has very small amplitude and its decay time is hard to estimate against offsets or drift. A static leakage-current measurement can provide a more reliable conductivity constraint in that case. Fitting an arbitrary time constant to a trace that is experimentally flat would confuse a model parameter with a measured observable.

Short the electrodes after the charge has accumulated

Hold the previous setup until steady, then reduce the voltage quickly to zero and leave the outer plates shorted. Determine the two signed internal fields immediately after shorting, and decide what a late-time interface probe will record.

Derive the result and check it independently

The fast change cannot instantaneously remove free charge trapped on the internal interface, so the displacement jump stays at its pre-existing value. By linearity superpose a fast \(-10\) V step whose capacitive division changes \(E_1\) by \(-7500\) V/m and \(E_2\) by \(-2500\) V/m. Starting from the preceding steady fields leaves \(E_1=-5192\) V/m and \(E_2=+5192\) V/m. Equal thicknesses make their line integral zero, as required by the terminal short.

With \(V=0\) the same differential equation now has zero steady fields. Both internal fields decay to zero with 54.5 ms time constant, fed by the discharging interfacial sheet. Hence zero terminal voltage coexists temporarily with nonzero local electric fields, stored electrical energy and Ohmic heating. A numerical calculation enforcing continuity of normal displacement at all times would erase precisely the free interface charge that controls this memory. Check each field at the voltage step, charge conservation at the interface, and the eventual return to zero.

Fließbach, chapter 30 · applications of macroscopic field equations

What relaxes when a field is applied?

A metal plate inserted in a static electric field rapidly rearranges its free surface charge. A metal plate subjected to a changing magnetic field can carry eddy currents for an appreciable time. The two observations concern different field equations. Model a uniform material using \(\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf E\), \(\mathbf D=\epsilon\mathbf E\) and \(\mathbf B=\mu\mathbf H\), treating the coefficients as constants over the frequencies used.

Free charge conservation gives \(\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=0\). Within the uniform bulk, \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\rho/\epsilon\) gives \(\partial_t\rho=-(\sigma/\epsilon)\rho\). A prepared bulk charge perturbation therefore decays exponentially with characteristic time \(\tau_q=\epsilon/\sigma\). The derivation says nothing about the disappearance of total charge from an isolated body. Charge can move to its boundary, where the bulk equation with constant coefficients across the interface no longer applies. Extremely short formal times in good metals also probe frequencies beyond the constant-conductivity model.

Curl fields obey a different evolution

Use Ampère’s law without displacement current when \(\omega\epsilon\ll\sigma\) and the frequency lies within the material model. Combining \(\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu\sigma\mathbf E\) with Faraday’s law gives \(\nabla\times(\nabla\times\mathbf B)=-\mu\sigma\partial_t\mathbf B\). Because magnetic flux has zero divergence, the left-hand side is \(-\nabla^2\mathbf B\). Thus

\[\partial_t\mathbf B=D_m\nabla^2\mathbf B,\qquad D_m=\frac1{\mu\sigma}.\]

A spatial Fourier component with wavenumber \(k\) decays as \(\exp(-D_mk^2t)\). Long spatial scales persist longer because the electric field required to change a large magnetic flux drives stronger circulating currents. Dimensional analysis gives a diffusion time of order \(\mu\sigma L^2\), with a geometric factor determined by the actual boundaries.

An alternating field outside a copper block

Take a tangential field at the plane \(x=0\) bounding a conductor occupying \(x>0\). Substitute \(B(x,t)=\mathrm{Re}\{B_0e^{i\omega t-\kappa x}\}\) into diffusion. The admissible square root of \(\kappa^2=i\omega\mu\sigma\) has positive real part,

\[\kappa=(1+i)/\delta,\qquad \delta=\sqrt{2/(\omega\mu\sigma)} .\]

At depth \(\delta\) the amplitude has dropped to \(1/e\) and the phase lags by one radian. These are independent observables for testing the semi-infinite, constant-property solution. The surface excitation sets the boundary amplitude. It need not equal the field that an external coil would generate with the metal absent.

For copper take \(\sigma=5.8\times10^7\) S/m, \(\mu=\mu_0\) and \(f=1.00\) kHz. Then \(\delta=2.09\) mm. At \(x=4.00\) mm the field amplitude is \(0.148\) of its surface value and the phase lag is \(1.91\) rad. Raising frequency by a factor of four halves the penetration depth. If a plate thickness is comparable to \(\delta\), fields from the opposite face also affect the solution and a second boundary condition must replace the one-sided decay condition. This geometric check matters as much as the conductivity value when using an amplitude ratio to infer a material property.

The oscillating solution also predicts current concentrated near the surface, since Ampère’s law makes the current density proportional to the local spatial derivative of magnetic field. As frequency rises, less of the metal cross-section transports the alternating current at a given field amplitude. This mechanism helps explain why a conductor’s measured AC resistance can exceed its DC value even when the local conductivity is unchanged.

Relate surface fields to the power dissipated in a metal

The skin depth gives the distance over which current flows, yet a circuit measurement usually returns an impedance or a heating rate. Use the conducting half-space \(x>0\) and time convention \(e^{i\omega t}\). Choose magnetic field along \(z\), with phasor \(H_z=H_0e^{-(1+i)x/\delta}\). From Ampère's law without displacement current, the only current component is \(J_y=-\partial_xH_z=(1+i)H_z/\delta\). Ohm’s law therefore fixes the tangential electric field immediately inside the conductor.

\[Z_s=\frac{E_y(0)}{H_z(0)}=\frac{1+i}{\sigma\delta},\qquad R_s=\mathrm{Re}\,Z_s=\sqrt{\frac{\pi f\mu}{\sigma}} .\]

The surface resistance \(R_s\) is expressed in ohms even though neither field in this ratio is a terminal voltage or current. Current integrated over depth is a sheet current with units A/m equal to the surface magnetic amplitude. The real part of the surface impedance relates mean heating per unit area to this current, while the imaginary part describes reversible magnetic energy storage near the surface.

For peak phasors, mean inward Poynting flux is \(\mathrm{Re}[E_yH_z^*]/2=R_s|H_0|^2/2\). The same result must follow from local heating. Insert \(|J_y|^2=2|H_0|^2e^{-2x/\delta}/\delta^2\) into \(\int_0^\infty |J_y|^2/(2\sigma)\,dx\). The integral supplies \(\delta/2\), reproducing the surface formula. This equality is a sensitive check on the distinction between amplitude decay over \(\delta\) and squared-amplitude decay over \(\delta/2\).

Estimate loss in a microwave wall

Take copper with constant \(\sigma=5.8\times10^7\) S/m, \(\mu=\mu_0\) at \(f=10.0\) MHz. The skin depth is \(\delta=20.9\) μm and \(R_s=1/(\sigma\delta)=0.825\) mΩ. A nearly uniform tangential peak magnetic field of \(80.0\) A/m illuminates a wall patch of area \(0.0200\) m². Surface heating density is \(0.000825(80.0)^2/2=2.64\) W/m², corresponding to \(52.8\) mW for the patch. If the supplied field were an RMS amplitude the division by two would be inappropriate.

Increasing frequency fourfold at constant peak magnetic field doubles the loss and halves the skin depth. A coating 10 μm thick at the original frequency is less than one skin depth, so a calculation assuming all current decays within that coating is unreliable; substrate conductivity and the second interface matter. A very thick low-loss conductor can instead be represented by the surface condition without resolving the field throughout its interior. A numerical mode solver using that substitution should recover both the inferred loss and its square-root frequency dependence.

The complex sign in the impedance also makes a phase prediction. For the stated time convention the surface electric field leads the tangential magnetic field by 45 degrees. Squaring the electric amplitude alone and dividing by the bulk resistivity would give a volumetric rather than an areal heating rate. To obtain an areal quantity one must integrate over its penetration profile. A measured phase substantially different from 45 degrees can indicate a magnetic permeability with appreciable losses, a finite film thickness, or a displacement current no longer negligible compared with conduction.

A calibrated surface-field phase measurement tests this condition independently of absolute field amplitude.

Develop this calculation further

Chapter 22 · Why dispersion and absorption belong together · Chapter 29 · When a circuit element must be treated as a field

Chapter 10 · Waves in a hollow guide

Derive a rectangular guide’s cutoff and use its dispersion relation to distinguish phase delay from signal transit time.

From first principles

Why a conducting guide cuts off long waves

In vacuum, take a curl of Faraday’s law and substitute the source-free Ampère–Maxwell law. Use \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=0\). Each Cartesian electric-field component then obeys

\[\nabla^2 E_y=c^{-2}\partial_t^2 E_y.\]

Consider a rectangular guide with perfectly conducting walls at \(x=0,a\). Take a TE mode with \(E_y\) independent of \(y\) and a travelling factor \(e^{i(k_z z-\omega t)}\). The tangential electric field must vanish on the two side walls, so the lowest nonzero profile is \(\sin(\pi x/a)\). Differentiating that sine twice contributes \(-(\pi/a)^2\); differentiating the travelling factors gives

\[k_z^2=\omega^2/c^2-(\pi/a)^2,\]
\[f_c=\frac{c}{2a}.\]

The boundary condition consumes part of the squared wave number in the transverse direction. Below \(f_c\), \(k_z\) is imaginary and the solution bounded far down the guide decays. Above cutoff it is real. Differentiating the dispersion relation gives the speed of a narrowband envelope, \(d\omega/dk_z=c^2k_z/\omega\). Near cutoff it can be small even though the field between the metal walls still obeys the vacuum wave equation.

Derivation and applications

Measure penetration below waveguide cutoff

A guided TE mode can have nonzero field past a junction even below its propagation threshold. Consider the fundamental TE mode of an air-filled rectangular guide with broad dimension \(a\) and perfectly conducting walls. Let the section beyond the junction be uniform and extend far enough that there is no appreciable returning field. Its standing transverse profile fixes the transverse wavenumber at \(\pi/a\). Inserting this profile into the homogeneous wave equation leaves an ordinary equation for longitudinal amplitude \(F(z)\).

\[\frac{d^2F}{dz^2}+\left[\left(\frac{2\pi f}{c}\right)^2-\left(\frac{\pi}{a}\right)^2\right]F=0,\]
\[f_c=\frac{c}{2a}.\]

Below \(f_c\) the bracket is negative. Define its negative square root in magnitude as the positive decay coefficient \(\alpha\). Of the two exponential solutions only the one decaying towards the remote end satisfies the assumed boundary condition.

\[\alpha=\frac{\pi}{a}\sqrt{1-(f/f_c)^2},\]
\[\frac{F(z)}{F(0)}=e^{-\alpha z}.\]

Suppose two identical weak probes respond to local squared electric-field amplitude at identical transverse coordinates. Forming their ratio cancels the unknown excitation and the transverse mode profile. Their readings in decibels then supply a slope from which cutoff can be recovered.

\[\frac{S(z_2)}{S(z_1)}=e^{-2\alpha(z_2-z_1)},\]
\[10\log_{10}\frac{S(z_2)}{S(z_1)}=-\frac{20\alpha}{\ln10}(z_2-z_1).\]

A single decaying mode in a lossless semi-infinite guide stores reactive energy and has zero mean axial power. These probe readings track a local field. In a short section bounded by a second discontinuity, both exponentials can be required and axial power can pass through the combination.

Worked example

Predict the depth dependence of a probe signal

For \(a=30.0\) mm and \(c=3.00\times10^8\) m/s, \(f_c=5.00\) GHz. At 4.00 GHz the decay coefficient is \((\pi/0.0300)(0.600)=62.8\) m⁻¹. Over 20.0 mm the field amplitude falls to \(e^{-1.257}=0.285\) of its initial value. A square-law probe reads \(0.0810\) of its initial signal, corresponding to a drop of 10.9 dB.

Second application

Recover the guide width from two readings

At 6.00 GHz two identical probes 10.0 mm apart have signal ratio 0.250. Their field ratio is 0.500 and \(\alpha=\ln2/0.0100=69.3\) m⁻¹. The vacuum wavenumber is \(2\pi f/c=125.7\) m⁻¹, so \(\pi/a=\sqrt{125.7^2+69.3^2}=143.5\) m⁻¹. This gives \(a=21.9\) mm and cutoff 6.85 GHz, above the measuring frequency as required.

What changes at the cutoff of a rectangular guide?

For the lowest transverse electric mode of an empty perfectly conducting rectangular guide, the wider side sets \(k_c=\pi/a\). Move the width while holding the source frequency fixed. Predict where axial propagation becomes possible.

Separation of the transverse and longitudinal coordinates gives \(k_z^2=(2\pi f/c)^2-k_c^2\). Above \(f_c=c/(2a)\) the positive square root is the phase constant. Below it the magnitude of the imaginary root is the spatial decay rate. Both have units of inverse length and refer to different solutions.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Use 3 cm and 4 GHz. Read the attenuation constant and infer the distance for a factor \(e\) drop in amplitude. Widen the guide until 4 GHz propagates. Locate the boundary with the algebra and then with the operating-point marker.

How a narrow waveguide lengthens a pulse even with perfectly conducting walls

Launch a short microwave pulse into an empty, straight rectangular guide of width \(a\), with only its lowest transverse electric mode excited. The walls are ideal conductors and the receiver is matched, so there is neither Ohmic loss nor a reflected wave to explain any change in its shape. Yet a received pulse can be longer and lower in peak amplitude than the transmitted one. Group delay explains the arrival of the pulse's centre. To explain the altered width we must also find how that delay varies within its frequency band.

Follow the phase of each frequency separately

For the dominant mode the longitudinal propagation constant above cutoff is \(k(\omega)=c^{-1}\sqrt{\omega^2-\omega_c^2}\), with \(\omega_c=\pi c/a\). The transverse mode shape is set by the walls; the frequency dependence below comes from its longitudinal wavenumber. With convention \(e^{ikz-i\omega t}\), each spectral component accumulates phase \(k(\omega)L\) in a length \(L\). Let \(\Omega=\omega-\omega_0\) be detuning around the positive carrier. For a spectrum concentrated well above cutoff, expand

\[k(\omega_0+\Omega)=k_0+k'_0\Omega+\tfrac12 k''_0\Omega^2+\cdots,\]
\[k'_0=\frac{\omega_0}{c\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\omega_c^2}},\]
\[k''_0=-\frac{\omega_c^2}{c(\omega_0^2-\omega_c^2)^{3/2}} .\]

The term \(k'_0L\) shifts every frequency by the same first-order group delay. The negative \(k''_0\) means higher frequencies have a smaller delay than lower frequencies. Thus the fast and slow parts of the spectrum separate in time even if the magnitude of each transmitted Fourier coefficient is unchanged. The expansion also predicts its own failure near cutoff, where the higher derivatives increase and some input spectral components may fall below cutoff altogether.

Carry a Gaussian envelope through the Fourier integral

Choose analytic input \(E^+(0,t)=E_p e^{-t^2/(2T_0^2)}e^{-i\omega_0t}\), with the physical field given by its real part. The spectral envelope is proportional to \(e^{-T_0^2\Omega^2/2}\). Multiply by the propagation phase and set \(s=t-k'_0L\). Completing the square in the inverse transform requires integrating \(\exp[-(T_0^2-i k''_0L)\Omega^2/2-i\Omega s]\). The result, apart from the carrier, is

\[A(L,t)=\frac{E_p T_0}{\sqrt{T_0^2-i k''_0 L}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{(t-k'_0L)^2}{2(T_0^2-i k''_0 L)}\right].\]

Taking the magnitude gives \(T_L=T_0\sqrt{1+(k''_0L/T_0^2)^2}\), using the same width convention as the input. The magnitude peak ratio is \(\sqrt{T_0/T_L}\). The factor compensates the broadening so that the time integral of \(|A|^2\) stays fixed. The complex exponent also contains a quadratic phase in \(s\); thus the instantaneous frequency varies across the outgoing envelope. A power meter sees the broader envelope, while a phase-sensitive receiver can test the chirp. One should distinguish an amplitude width \(T\) in \(e^{-t^2/(2T^2)}\) from an intensity full width at half maximum \(2\sqrt{\ln2}\,T\).

Predict a flight time and time spread independently

Take \(a=20.0\) mm, \(L=4.00\) m and \(f_0=9.00\) GHz. Then \(f_c=7.495\) GHz, \(v_g=1/k'_0=0.554c\) and mean arrival delay is \(24.1\) ns. The dispersion coefficient is \(k''_0=-0.241\) ns²/m. For \(T_0=1.00\) ns the width grows to \(1.39\) ns and the magnitude peak falls to about 85% of its input value. The guided carrier advances in phase at speed greater than \(c\), but the group arrival and the causal front cannot be inferred from that phase speed.

Check the frequency sorting by evaluating \(L/v_g\) independently at two nearby frequencies. For weak bandwidth, their delay separation per angular-frequency separation must approach \(k''_0 L\). A direct numerical inverse transform provides a further test: sample the input spectrum densely enough to resolve the rapidly varying phase, multiply by \(e^{ik(\omega)L}\), and evaluate the time signal. Refine both bandwidth and frequency spacing. If the exact transform remains asymmetric after convergence, the quadratic expansion has reached its limit even when the solver itself has converged.

Estimate the frequency band in which a single dispersion coefficient suffices

The neglected cubic term in accumulated phase is \(Lk'''_0\Omega^3/6\). Differentiating the expression for \(k''\) yields \(k'''_0=3\omega_0\omega_c^2/[c(\omega_0^2-\omega_c^2)^{5/2}]\). Relative to the quadratic term its magnitude is \(\omega_0|\Omega|/(\omega_0^2-\omega_c^2)\). At a typical spectral detuning of \(1/T_0\) this ratio is about 0.058 for the 9 GHz example. The ratio doubles at twice that detuning, although the spectral amplitude there is already smaller. Testing the accumulated cubic phase itself is also necessary if the guide is sufficiently long.

Thus a nominally narrow fractional bandwidth \(\Delta f/f_0\) can still give a poor quadratic approximation near cutoff, where the smaller scale is \(f_0-f_c\). The analytic Gaussian also has arbitrarily early and late tails. Its fitted peak delay describes a prepared modulation and cannot time the first arrival of new information. A sharply switched source contains frequencies far outside the fitted Gaussian band, including components whose propagation approaches the vacuum light speed.

Test how close a measurement can approach cutoff

A second run uses \(f_0=8.00\) GHz, \(T_0=2.00\) ns and the same guide length. Estimate delay and width. How could a measured lower peak be distinguished from attenuation, filtering at cutoff or a reflection?

Derive the result and check it independently

At 8.00 GHz one finds \(v_g=0.350c\), \(k''_0=-1.36\) ns²/m, delay \(38.2\) ns and \(T_L=2\sqrt{1+(5.45/4)^2}=3.38\) ns. Measure the magnitude of the frequency-domain transmission after correcting for port response. Pure dispersion changes phase while preserving each above-cutoff spectral magnitude in this model. A systematic magnitude reduction over the band indicates losses or coupling, a sharp suppression near the cutoff indicates spectral filtering, and a periodic ripple or time-separated echo suggests a reflection.

A second guide length supplies another check: delay varies linearly with \(L\) and \(T_L^2-T_0^2\) varies quadratically at fixed band and input width. If only a short time window is recorded, a broader pulse can be partly clipped and look as if transmission lost energy. Extend the integration window before evaluating that claim.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §9.5.2 · transverse electric modes in a rectangular guide

Fields constrained by metal walls

Can a hollow metal tube carry an arbitrarily slowly varying electromagnetic wave? Take perfectly conducting walls, a uniform vacuum interior, width \(a\) along \(x\), height \(b\) along \(y\) and translation symmetry along \(z\). Tangential electric field must vanish on each wall. In free space a plane wave can have uniform transverse fields. Inside a hollow simply connected guide a nonzero transverse electrostatic potential difference between separate conductors cannot be maintained. The lowest guided pattern consequently has transverse variation.

For the simplest transverse-electric mode set \(E_y=E_m\sin(\pi x/a)\exp[i(\beta z-\omega t)]\), taking real parts for physical fields. At the side walls \(E_y\) is tangential and the sine vanishes. At the top and bottom the electric field is normal, as allowed by surface charge. Its zero divergence and accompanying magnetic field follow from Maxwell’s equations. In particular the curl includes an \(x\) derivative, giving a longitudinal magnetic component and the conventional label TE\(_{10}\).

Separate transverse and longitudinal wavenumbers

Substitute the field into the vacuum wave equation. The two spatial derivatives contribute \(-(\pi/a)^2\) and \(-\beta^2\), while the time derivative contributes \(-\omega^2/c^2\). Hence

\[\beta^2=\frac{\omega^2}{c^2}-\left(\frac{\pi}{a}\right)^2,\qquad f_c=\frac{c}{2a}.\]

Below cutoff the longitudinal dependence is exponential. In a long uniform section the bounded solution decays along the guide. A finite closed-off section can also have a growing exponential determined by reflections at its far end. Above cutoff the phase velocity is \(\omega/\beta\), while differentiating the dispersion relation gives the narrowband envelope velocity \(d\omega/d\beta=c^2\beta/\omega\). Their product is \(c^2\). Phase fronts move faster than \(c\) because the fixed transverse field variation contributes to total wavenumber. A modulation envelope in this lossless guide propagates more slowly than \(c\).

Choose a usable frequency band

For a guide with \(a=22.86\) mm and \(b=10.16\) mm, the first cutoff is \(6.56\) GHz. Higher-order patterns have transverse wavenumber squared \((m\pi/a)^2+(n\pi/b)^2\). The next candidate cutoffs are \(13.11\) GHz for TE\(_{20}\) and \(14.75\) GHz for TE\(_{01}\). Consequently a 10.0 GHz drive supports one propagating mode in this ideal guide.

At 10.0 GHz the group velocity is \(c\sqrt{1-(6.56/10)^2}=0.755c\). A 2.00 m section delays a narrow envelope by \(8.84\) ns. Its guided wavelength is \(c/[f\sqrt{1-(f_c/f)^2}]=39.7\) mm, exceeding the 30.0 mm free-space wavelength. Near cutoff the group velocity varies rapidly across a finite bandwidth, so quoting one envelope delay can conceal pulse distortion. Separately, real metal walls introduce attenuation and bends can excite evanescent modes even in the quoted single-mode band.

A practical way to measure the guided wavelength is to create a partial reflection at the termination. The forward and backward fields interfere and their stationary intensity maxima are separated by half the guided wavelength. At the example frequency the spacing should be about 19.9 mm. That measurement tests the propagation constant at one frequency. To test the group velocity, launch a short modulated burst or measure how transmitted phase changes between two nearby frequencies.

Close a guide to make a cavity

Placing conducting end plates at \(z=0\) and \(z=L\) turns a propagating guide mode into a standing field with discrete frequencies. For the TE pattern of the previous derivation, the electric field has only \(E_y\) and is tangential to both end plates. The conductor therefore requires it to vanish there. A consistent mode has \(E_y\propto\sin(\pi x/a)\sin(p\pi z/L)\) with positive integer \(p\). The transverse electric field still points normal to the walls at fixed \(y\), satisfying the original conditions. Combining longitudinal and transverse wavenumbers in the wave equation gives

\[f_{10p}=\frac c2\sqrt{\frac1{a^2}+\frac{p^2}{L^2}} .\]

The lowest such cavity resonance lies above the guide's cutoff even though no net power travels through the end plates. In a standing mode electric and magnetic energies exchange, and cycle-average net axial transport vanishes. Increasing cavity length at fixed \(p\) brings the frequency progressively nearer the guide cutoff. Conversely, if several longitudinal nodes fit into a short cavity, the longitudinal contribution can dominate the resonance frequency.

Weak finite conductivity or an output coupler causes stored energy to leave the mode. Define \(Q=\omega U/P_{\rm loss}\) with \(U\) the cycle-averaged stored energy and \(P_{\rm loss}\) the average dissipated or extracted power. When \(Q\) is approximately constant after switching off the drive, \(dU/dt=-\omega U/Q\) gives an energy decay time \(Q/\omega\). A field-amplitude measurement follows the square root of energy and consequently has twice that time constant.

Identify a resonance and estimate a loaded quality factor

Choose \(a=30.0\) mm, \(L=40.0\) mm and cavity height 12.0 mm. The lowest member of this family has \(f_{101}=(c/2)\sqrt{(1/0.0300)^2+(1/0.0400)^2}=6.25\) GHz using \(c=3.00\times10^8\) m/s. The parent guide’s cutoff is 5.00 GHz. Raising longitudinal index to two gives \(f_{102}=9.01\) GHz. Measured field nodes along the cavity distinguish these patterns from other possible modes near the same frequency.

After terminating the drive at the first resonance, suppose a weak probe's voltage amplitude falls by a factor \(e\) in 2.00 μs. Then \(Q=\omega\tau_{\rm amplitude}/2=\pi f\tau_{\rm amplitude}=3.93\times10^4\). The corresponding full resonance width is approximately \(f/Q=159\) kHz for a single weakly damped mode. A fit to detected power would return a 1.00 μs time constant instead. Increasing the probe coupling shortens the measured lifetime, so this ring-down gives the loaded quality factor. Measurements over several coupling strengths help separate internal wall losses from energy deliberately extracted by the probe.

The energy decay also predicts how much source power is needed to sustain a measured field. For example, if the resonant cavity in this measurement stores \(2.00\) nJ, total time-averaged loss is \(U/\tau_U=2.00\) mW. Source power actually coupled into the mode must replenish that loss in steady state. The source's forward power can be larger because some incident energy reflects at the coupling port. An independently measured coupling efficiency is therefore needed before converting a power-meter reading at the source into a stored field or a wall heating rate.

Read a waveguide delay near cutoff

In a lossless uniform guide with one propagating mode, \(\beta^2=(\omega^2-\omega_c^2)/c_m^2\). Compute \(v_p=\omega/\beta\) and \(v_g=d\omega/d\beta\). The bandwidth of a pulse must remain inside this propagating band for its group-delay estimate to be useful.
