Problem sheet 5 · Metal penetration and guide cutoff

Distinguish magnetic diffusion from waveguide cutoff by calculating their frequency and length dependence.

Invert a penetration measurement

At 400 Hz the tangential magnetic amplitude falls to \(1/e\) over 4.00 mm in a homogeneous nonmagnetic half-space. Infer conductivity, predict the phase lag at 6.00 mm, and find the frequency that halves the penetration depth.

Show the diffusion calculation

The measured length is \(\delta\). Inverting \(\delta^2=1/(\pi f\mu_0\sigma)\) gives \(\sigma=3.96\times10^7\) S/m. Depth divided by \(\delta\) is the phase lag in radians, giving 1.50 rad with amplitude ratio \(e^{-1.5}=0.223\). Since \(\delta\) scales with \(f^{-1/2}\), a 1.60 kHz drive gives 2.00 mm. A second nearby boundary or frequency-dependent permeability would spoil this inference.

A microwave pulse between walls

An empty rectangular guide has width 30.0 mm and height 12.0 mm. Find the first two cutoffs. At 8.00 GHz determine the group velocity and envelope transit time over 3.00 m. Use \(c=3.00\times10^8\) m/s.

Show the allowed band

The first cutoff \(c/(2a)\) is 5.00 GHz. Among the next candidates \(c/a=10.0\) GHz and \(c/(2b)=12.5\) GHz, the former is lower. Thus only TE\(_{10}\) propagates at 8 GHz. Its group velocity is \(c\sqrt{1-(5/8)^2}=2.34\times10^8\) m/s and transit takes 12.8 ns. The free-space value would be 10.0 ns. This applies to a narrowband excitation with negligible end-reflection delays.

Two decaying fields

Use the same guide below cutoff at 4.00 GHz. Derive its amplitude decay length, compute the amplitude ratio after 30.0 mm and explain how increasing frequency distinguishes this measured decay from conductor skin effect.

Show the field comparison

Subcutoff \(\beta=i\alpha\) with \(\alpha=(2\pi/c)\sqrt{f_c^2-f^2}=62.8\) m⁻¹. Its amplitude decay length is \(15.9\) mm, giving \(e^{-62.8(0.030)}=0.152\) after 30 mm in a long uniform guide. Raising frequency towards cutoff increases this length, whereas a good conductor's skin depth decreases. An ideal evanescent mode in a semi-infinite empty guide does not dissipate power. Metal penetration with finite conductivity does dissipate power through \(\mathbf J\cdot\mathbf E\).

Chapter 11 · Pulses on a transmission line

Use charge and flux conservation to predict the first voltages seen when a pulse meets a mismatched load.

From first principles

From a short cable segment to two travelling waves

Consider voltage \(V(z,t)\) from inner conductor to return, and current \(I(z,t)\) towards increasing \(z\). A short, uniform, lossless line segment of length \(\Delta z\) stores charge \(C'\Delta z\,V\) and links magnetic flux \(L'\Delta z\,I\). No current leaks through the dielectric. The current difference must charge the segment, and the voltage drop must drive its changing flux:

\[I(z)-I(z+\Delta z)=C'\Delta z\,\partial_t V,\]
\[V(z)-V(z+\Delta z)=L'\Delta z\,\partial_t I .\]

Divide by \(\Delta z\) and take its limit. Differentiate the voltage equation once more in space and eliminate the current derivative using the charge balance:

\[\partial_z V=-L'\partial_t I,\]
\[\partial_z I=-C'\partial_t V,\]
\[\partial_z^2 V=L'C'\partial_t^2 V .\]

For a forward wave \(V=F(t-z/v)\), comparing second derivatives gives \(v=1/\sqrt{L'C'}\). Substituting in the first-order relation shows the forward current is \(I=V/Z_0\), with \(Z_0=vL'=\sqrt{L'/C'}\) for the travelling part. A backward wave reverses that current sign. The line can therefore have a finite characteristic impedance even when its conductors dissipate no heat.

Derivation and applications

Sum repeated returns on a pulse transmission line

A step applied to a finite line can produce a staircase at the load. Model a uniform lossless transmission line with real characteristic impedance \(Z_0\), one-way travel time \(\tau\), resistive termination \(R_L\) and a source step \(V_s\) in series with resistance \(R_s\). Initially the line is unenergised. Until a reflection comes back, the source sees the characteristic impedance, giving the launched voltage \(V_+\). On reaching either termination an incident voltage wave produces a reflected fraction set by that termination.

\[V_+=V_s\frac{Z_0}{R_s+Z_0},\]
\[\Gamma_L=\frac{R_L-Z_0}{R_L+Z_0},\]
\[\Gamma_s=\frac{R_s-Z_0}{R_s+Z_0}.\]

For the source reflection, keep its step height fixed. The incremental wave therefore encounters just \(R_s\). The first arriving step at the load changes its voltage by incident plus reflected amplitudes. Each further round trip multiplies the corresponding incident step by \(\Gamma_L\Gamma_s\). Index the first arrival by \(n=0\).

\[\Delta V_{L,n}=(1+\Gamma_L)V_+(\Gamma_L\Gamma_s)^n,\]
\[t_n=(2n+1)\tau .\]

The voltage after the \(n\)th arrival is the partial sum of these steps. Provided the magnitude of the round-trip multiplier is below unity, the series converges. Substitution of the two voltage reflection coefficients recovers the expected DC divider exactly.

\[V_{L,\infty}=\frac{(1+\Gamma_L)V_+}{1-\Gamma_L\Gamma_s}=\frac{V_sR_L}{R_s+R_L}.\]

Successive steps alternate when the reflection coefficients have opposite signs. The spacing of changes measures travel time even when delays in the measuring electronics obscure the absolute launch time.

Worked example

Predict overshoot at a resistive receiver

Use \(V_s=3.00\) V, \(R_s=25.0\) Ω, \(Z_0=50.0\) Ω, \(R_L=100\) Ω and \(\tau=20.0\) ns. The launched step is 2.00 V. The load and source coefficients are \(+1/3\) and \(-1/3\). The receiver rises to 2.667 V at 20.0 ns, then falls by \(2.667/9=0.296\) V at 60.0 ns. Its eventual 2.400 V follows from the divider. Measuring only the first plateau would overestimate the steady voltage.

Second application

Read the source resistance from staircase increments

A separate 75.0 Ω line has a 225 Ω termination. Two consecutive load increments are \(+0.800\) V and \(+0.0800\) V, 50.0 ns apart. Their ratio is \(0.100=\Gamma_L\Gamma_s\) and \(\Gamma_L=0.500\), yielding \(\Gamma_s=0.200\). Inverting gives \(R_s=75.0(1.20/0.800)=112.5\) Ω. The one-way time is 25.0 ns, corresponding to 5.00 m at a measured propagation speed of \(2.00\times10^8\) m/s.

Start from this chapter. Predict how changing only the permittivity alters both wave speed and characteristic impedance. The OneSimulation solve supplies transverse line constants for the pulse calculation.

OneSimulation laboratory · Recover cable impedance from a cross-section

Watch a pulse reflect from a load

A lossless metre of transmission line has speed \(2.0\times10^8\) m/s and characteristic impedance 50 Ω. Launch a Gaussian voltage pulse from a matched source. It peaks at the source at 3 ns. Predict when it peaks at the far end and when the return reaches the source.

At the load, voltage adds and currents subtract. Substituting \(V_L=Z_L I_L\) gives \(\Gamma=(Z_L-Z_0)/(Z_L+Z_0)\). An observer at position \(x\) sees the incident shape delayed by \(x/v\), and the reflected shape by \((2L-x)/v\). This supplies an independent travel-time check for the moving curves.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Sweep time past 8 ns and 13 ns. At a short, explain why zero load voltage coexists with a finite reflected pulse. At a matched termination, trace the energy carried by the incident pulse.

A cable can attenuate a pulse without distorting its shape

A laboratory sends two pulses down a long uniform cable. A shorter pulse has more high-frequency content, so it is often expected to distort more. That expectation implicitly assumes a particular frequency dependence of the propagation constant. We will derive a case in which an entire waveform is simply delayed and reduced in amplitude, even though the line converts energy to heat. The calculation also identifies when adding a second loss mechanism changes the phase response and how to distinguish pure attenuation from imperfect matching at the ends.

Give both energy-storage mechanisms their own losses

Let \(L',C'\) be the series inductance and shunt capacitance per length. Include a series resistance \(R'\) per length and a shunt conductance \(G'\) per length. Assume that these four coefficients are positive constants over the pulse band, the line is uniform, and one transverse electromagnetic mode suffices. Charge and voltage balances on a short segment give

\[-\partial_zV=R'I+L'\partial_t I,\]
\[-\partial_zI=G'V+C'\partial_t V .\]

With time convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\) and a forward spatial dependence \(e^{-\Gamma z}\), their product and ratio yield \(\Gamma^2=(R'-i\omega L')(G'-i\omega C')\) and \(Z_{\rm line}^2=(R'-i\omega L')/(G'-i\omega C')\). Choose the forward branch \(\Gamma=\alpha-i\beta\), \(\alpha\geq0,\beta\geq0\), with the impedance branch satisfying the original first-order equations for a forward wave. In general both amplitude and phase change differently for different frequencies. A matched receiver must account for the actual line impedance rather than just a nominal lossless value.

Find the exact condition for undistorted propagation

Suppose \(R'/L'=G'/C'=\nu\), with units of inverse seconds. Both complex factors then share \(\nu-i\omega\), so \(\Gamma=\sqrt{L'C'}(\nu-i\omega)\) and \(Z_{\rm line}=\sqrt{L'/C'}\) is real and frequency independent. Define \(u=1/\sqrt{L'C'}\) and \(\alpha=\nu/u\). The forward transfer function over distance \(z\) multiplies each voltage component by \(e^{-\alpha z}e^{+i\omega z/u}\). Fourier inversion thus gives

\[V(z,t)=e^{-\alpha z}V(0,t-z/u),\]
\[I(z,t)=V(z,t)/Z_{\rm line}.\]

The delay sign can be checked by inserting the result in the time-domain equations. In fact \(V\) satisfies \(\partial_z^2V=L'C'(\partial_t+\nu)^2V\). One-way solutions satisfy the first-order propagation operator \(\partial_z+u^{-1}(\partial_t+\nu)\). Direct substitution gives zero for any differentiable input shape. Piecewise smooth pulses follow by continuity or by their Fourier representations. This is the Heaviside distortionless condition. It permits attenuation; it is a relationship between two loss-to-storage ratios.

Verify that the missing power becomes heat

Multiply the two original equations by \(I\) and \(V\) respectively, then add. The product rule yields

\[-\partial_z(VI)=R'I^2+G'V^2+\partial_t\!\left(\frac12L'I^2+\frac12 C'V^2\right).\]

The first two terms are positive local heating rates per length. For a forward pulse \(I=V/Z_{\rm line}\). At the matched loss ratios, \(R'/Z_{\rm line}^2=G'\). Conductor heating and dielectric leakage heating are therefore equal at fixed local voltage, although the resistances of the two physical paths play different roles. Integrating over all time removes the storage derivative if the line begins and ends quiet. The pulse energy crossing each section decays as \(e^{-2\alpha z}\), the square of its voltage attenuation. This provides a local and an integrated measurement of the same physical loss.

Take \(L'=0.25\) μH/m and \(C'=100\) pF/m. Then \(u=2.00\times10^8\) m/s and \(Z_{\rm line}=50.0\ \Omega\). For \(R'=0.050\ \Omega/\mathrm m\), choose \(G'=20.0\) μS/m, making both ratios \(2.00\times10^5\) s⁻¹. A 100 m line delays every part of a matched pulse by \(0.500\) μs and multiplies its voltage by \(e^{-0.10}=0.905\). Its transmitted energy is 81.9% of the launched energy, leaving 18.1% as loss along the line. A pulse measured to be ten per cent lower in voltage has therefore lost more than ten per cent of its transported energy.

Show why a constant attenuation and a linear phase are restrictive

The sufficient condition can also be obtained as a necessity if exact distortionless propagation is required over a continuous frequency range in this constant-coefficient model. Suppose \(\Gamma=\alpha-i b\omega\) with constant attenuation and delay per length \(b\). Squaring and matching constant, linear and quadratic terms against \((R'-i\omega L')(G'-i\omega C')\) yields \(\alpha^2=R'G'\), \(b^2=L'C'\) and \(2\alpha b=R'C'+L'G'\). Eliminating \(\alpha,b\) leaves \((R'C'-L'G')^2=0\). A line with only series resistance and zero shunt loss cannot satisfy that exact identity unless it is lossless altogether.

This test uses the functional dependence on frequency, rather than one measured attenuation value. In a real cable the skin effect makes \(R'\) increase with frequency, and a dielectric may have frequency-dependent polarisation loss. The two traces from two cable lengths therefore test a specified band of operation. An approximately straight phase line over a limited band can be entirely consistent with the measurements while extrapolating that fit to a much shorter pulse gives appreciable waveform error.

Estimate the effect of loss mismatch

A second cable has the same \(R',L',C'\) but negligible \(G'\). Does weak loss recover the same exact propagation law? Derive its leading high-frequency attenuation and give a procedure for detecting the missing exact cancellation.

Derive the result and check it independently

When \(\omega L'\gg R'\) and \(\omega C'\gg G'\), expand the square root around its lossless form. To first order the attenuation is \(\alpha\simeq[R'/Z_{\rm lossless}+G'Z_{\rm lossless}]/2\), with \(Z_{\rm lossless}=\sqrt{L'/C'}\); the phase is approximately \(\omega/u\). With negligible \(G'\) the asymptotic attenuation is \(0.00050\) m⁻¹. This is an approximation over a band. At sufficiently low frequency \(R'\) competes strongly with \(\omega L'\); then \(\Gamma\) behaves like the square root of frequency and the waveform distorts.

Send the same calibrated pulse through two lengths, compare complex spectra and form their ratio to suppress the source response. Under distortionless propagation the logarithm of its magnitude is constant across frequency and its unwrapped phase is linear. Periodic ripples can indicate connector reflections. Time-gating a clean first arrival, or matching the relevant band and checking a second cable length, helps distinguish distributed distortion from an echo. A single rise-time reading cannot by itself identify the underlying loss mechanism.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §9.5.3 · fields of a coaxial transmission line

A line resolves the travel time

A cable can store energy all along its length. When a change of source voltage travels an appreciable fraction of the cable during its rise time, a single lumped capacitor cannot encode both its arrival and its reflection. Model a uniform two-conductor line using inductance \(L'\) and capacitance \(C'\) per unit length. Omit conductor resistance and dielectric leakage initially. Let \(V(z,t)\) be voltage between conductors and \(I(z,t)\) current toward increasing \(z\).

A short section \(dz\) experiences an inductive voltage difference \(L'dz\,\partial_t I\). Its net incoming current charges \(C'dz\) at the rate \(\partial_t V\). Accounting for the positive current direction gives the pair

\[\partial_z V=-L'\partial_t I,\qquad \partial_z I=-C'\partial_t V.\]

Differentiating once more gives wave equations with \(v=1/\sqrt{L'C'}\). Substitution of a travelling waveform shows that a forward wave has \(I_+=V_+/Z_0\), while a backward wave has \(I_-=-V_-/Z_0\), where \(Z_0=\sqrt{L'/C'}\). The characteristic impedance thus describes the voltage-to-current ratio of one travelling component. A resistance meter at the end of a finite DC cable ordinarily measures something different.

How the load launches a return wave

At a resistive termination \(R_L\) the total voltage equals \(R_L\) times total current. Write \(V_-= \Gamma V_+\) and use the opposite current signs above. Rearrangement gives

\[\Gamma=\frac{R_L-Z_0}{R_L+Z_0},\qquad V_L=(1+\Gamma)V_+ .\]

An open end doubles voltage and cancels current. A shorted end cancels voltage and doubles current. With positive real \(Z_0\) the reflected fraction of incoming wave power is \(\Gamma^2\). A voltage reflection of either sign carries energy back toward the source. A matched resistor absorbs all the incident energy, a condition also useful for interpreting the first step in a time-domain voltage trace.

Locate and characterize a mismatch

A line with \(L'=250\) nH/m and \(C'=100\) pF/m has \(v=2.00\times10^8\) m/s and \(Z_0=50.0\) Ω. A 2.00 V source step with series resistance 50.0 Ω launches 1.00 V into a 30.0 m cable. Initially the source sees the characteristic impedance because no reflection has had time to return. At 150 ns the leading edge reaches a 150 Ω load, where \(\Gamma=0.500\). The load rises to 1.50 V and draws 10.0 mA.

The return step of 0.50 V reaches the source at 300 ns. The source resistance is matched, so it absorbs the returning wave with no further reflection and the source-end voltage becomes 1.50 V. The result agrees with the final DC divider. A measured return time locates the discontinuity through \(d=vt_{\rm return}/2\), while its signed amplitude identifies an impedance increase or decrease. Distributed losses must be estimated before using this amplitude ratio on a long real cable.

Changing the source resistance leaves the first arrival time unchanged while altering the incident amplitude. It also changes what happens when the first reflection returns. At a source whose internal resistance differs from the line impedance, part of that returning pulse is launched outward again. Repeated round trips then generate a staircase of voltages separated by twice the propagation time.

Match unequal impedances with a finite line segment

A resistive load can be matched at a selected frequency even when its resistance differs from the feeding line’s characteristic impedance. Insert a uniform lossless section of impedance \(Z_t\) and length \(\ell\) between the original line \(Z_0\) and the load \(R_L\). Inside the added section decompose voltage into forward and backward waves. With the load plane at \(z=0\), a convenient phasor convention gives \(V(z)=V_+e^{-i\beta z}+V_-e^{i\beta z}\) and \(I(z)=(V_+e^{-i\beta z}-V_-e^{i\beta z})/Z_t\). At \(z=0\) use \(V(0)/I(0)=R_L\) to remove the unknown reflection coefficient.

Evaluate the ratio at the input plane \(z=-\ell\). Collecting the exponentials into cosines and sines gives

\[Z_{\rm in}=Z_t\frac{R_L\cos(\beta\ell)+iZ_t\sin(\beta\ell)}{Z_t\cos(\beta\ell)+iR_L\sin(\beta\ell)}.\]

For an electrically short segment the input simply approaches the load. At a quarter wavelength \(\beta\ell=\pi/2\) and \(Z_{\rm in}=Z_t^2/R_L\). Setting this equal to \(Z_0\) gives \(Z_t=\sqrt{Z_0R_L}\). Reflections still occur inside the added section, but their summed contribution at its input cancels at the design frequency. This is a phase-dependent match, so its behaviour under a suddenly applied step differs from a resistor matched at every frequency.

Design a section and test its bandwidth

A 50.0 Ω source line drives a 100 Ω resistor at 100 MHz. Available cable has phase speed \(2.00\times10^8\) m/s. The required matching impedance is \(70.7\) Ω and the length is \(v/(4f)=0.500\) m. At design frequency the input impedance equals 50.0 Ω, whereas directly attaching the resistor would produce reflection amplitude \((100-50)/(100+50)=1/3\) and reflect 11.1 percent of the incident power.

To test the frequency limit without dividing by a nearly infinite tangent, evaluate the sine–cosine form at 50.0 MHz. The electrical length is \(\pi/4\), so the trigonometric factors cancel. Substituting the three real impedances gives \(Z_{\rm in}=66.7-23.6i\) Ω. The feeding-line reflection magnitude \(|(Z_{\rm in}-50)/(Z_{\rm in}+50)|\) is about \(0.243\), reflecting \(5.9\) percent of incident power. At 200 MHz the section is half a wavelength and the input again equals the bare load. A frequency sweep should therefore show a matched centre frequency and increasing reflected power away from it. Loss or an appreciably reactive load changes both the depth and position of that minimum.

A frequency-domain match can also be understood through a short series of time-domain reflections. The impedance first rises from 50.0 to 70.7 ohms and then rises again from 70.7 to 100 ohms. The two interface reflections have the same sign when considered locally. At a quarter wavelength, propagation through the matching section and back adds a half-cycle phase, making the returning contributions cancel after all internal reflections are summed. An abrupt step arrives at those interfaces at different times, so the earliest observed reflected step need not vanish. A narrowband sinusoid and a broadband step perform different tests of the same lossless device.

An actual connector of finite electrical length introduces another discontinuity that may be visible in either test.

Recover the load from a reflected pulse

Send a voltage step down a lossless line into a real load. Continuity gives \(\Gamma=(Z_L-Z_0)/(Z_L+Z_0)\). Use the original wave impedance for both incident and backward power, which makes reflected power scale as \(\Gamma^2\).

Chapter 21 · What an interface does to phase, field and power · Chapter 29 · When a circuit element must be treated as a field

Chapter 12 · Force carried by a field

Compute electrical pressure from a local stress balance and reconcile two energy calculations for a moving capacitor plate.

From first principles

Recover electrostatic pressure from virtual work

Place two large parallel, perfectly conducting plates of area \(A\) a distance \(d\) apart in vacuum. Ignore edge fields and disconnect the voltage source so that charge \(Q\) stays fixed. A pillbox at the metal surface gives \(E=Q/(\epsilon_0 A)\). To assemble the charge slowly, bring each increment \(dq\) across the voltage \(V(q)=qd/(\epsilon_0 A)\) already established:

\[U(Q,d)=\int_0^Q V(q)\,dq=\frac{Q^2d}{2\epsilon_0 A}.\]

Take a small displacement increasing \(d\). With the leads disconnected there is no electrical work input. The work done by the field is minus the change in stored energy. Thus the signed force tending to increase the gap and the magnitude of its pressure are

\[F_d=-\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial d}\right)_Q=-\frac{Q^2}{2\epsilon_0 A},\]
\[p=\frac{|F_d|}{A}=\frac{\epsilon_0 E^2}{2}.\]

The minus sign gives attraction. The factor one half also has a direct force interpretation. At one plate, only the field of the other sheet acts on its net charge. That sheet produces half the total gap field. If a voltage source remains connected, charge moves during the displacement and the source’s work must enter the same energy account.

Derivation and applications

Find the force drawing a dielectric into a capacitor

A partially inserted dielectric slab feels a lateral force even though the strong field between broad plates points across the gap. Use a plate width \(w\), overlap length \(\ell\), gap \(d\) and a slab filling the gap for an inserted distance \(x\). The slab has constant relative permittivity \(\kappa\). Assume quasistatic motion, uniform plate separation and \(d\) much smaller than both occupied lengths and the width. The two regions share a voltage and their charges add, so the leading capacitance and its position derivative are

\[C(x)=\frac{\epsilon_0 w}{d}[\ell+(\kappa-1)x],\]
\[C'=\frac{\epsilon_0w}{d}(\kappa-1).\]

First disconnect the supply, leaving total free charge \(Q\) fixed. A small inward displacement lowers the field energy. Energy conservation assigns that decrease to mechanical work, giving the inward electric force

\[F_Q=-\left.\frac{d}{dx}\frac{Q^2}{2C(x)}\right|_Q=\frac{Q^2}{2C^2}C' .\]

With a battery attached instead, moving the slab draws additional charge \(dQ=V\,dC\). The battery does work \(VdQ\), while the field energy increases by half that amount. The difference supplies the mechanical work. This accounts for the same instantaneous force in either experiment when the initial voltage is the same.

\[F_V\,dx=V\,dQ-d\!\left(\frac12CV^2\right)=\frac12V^2\,dC,\]
\[F_V=\frac12V^2C' .\]

The microscopic lateral force arises where the field bends near the dielectric boundary. The capacitance method recovers the integrated force without needing that local field profile. It loses accuracy near the positions where the slab first enters or completely fills the capacitor.

Worked example

Predict the pull at constant voltage

Let \(w=50.0\) mm, \(d=0.500\) mm, \(\kappa=3.00\) and \(V=100\) V. Then \(C'=1.77\) nF/m and the force is \(8.85\) µN. During 10.0 mm of insertion, capacitance increases by 17.7 pF. The battery supplies \(V^2\Delta C=177\) nJ, of which 88.5 nJ becomes mechanical work and the remainder increases the stored electric energy.

Second application

Follow the force after disconnecting the supply

A different isolated apparatus starts at 80.0 pF and 60.0 V. Inserting the slab by 20.0 mm raises its capacitance to 100 pF, giving \(C'=1.00\) nF/m. The conserved charge is 4.80 nC and final voltage is 48.0 V. Initial and final forces are 1.80 and 1.15 µN. The work delivered is the drop in field energy, \((Q^2/2)(1/C_i-1/C_f)=28.8\) nJ. Its average force of 1.44 µN lies between the endpoint values.

Does separating capacitor plates weaken the force?

Set an air-filled parallel-plate capacitor to the reference voltage at a 1 mm gap. In one run leave the source attached and in the other disconnect it. Neglect edge fields. Both start with the same charge and pressure.

Write \(E=V/d\) with a voltage supply and \(E=\sigma/\epsilon_0\) for fixed free charge per unit area. The electrical pressure \(p=\epsilon_0 E^2/2\) then gives different distance dependences. On the fixed-voltage path the source exchanges energy with the field as the capacitance changes.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Find the distance at which the fixed-voltage pressure falls to one quarter of its initial value. Double the reference voltage and explain why the crossing distance of the two curves stays fixed.

Why the two halves of a charged sphere pull apart while its net force is zero

A thin spherical conducting shell of radius \(a\) carries total free charge \(Q\) in vacuum. Its two hemispheres are joined by a mechanically thin seam near the equator. Everything is initially at rest; any slit in the surface is negligibly narrow compared with \(a\). The whole isolated shell has no preferred direction and experiences zero net electromagnetic force. A force gauge in the seam can nevertheless register a finite load. We will calculate the force on the upper hemisphere by two different choices of surface and then determine what changes if the charge fills the volume instead of sitting on the metal skin.

Start with the force on a charged surface element

Spherical symmetry gives \(\sigma=Q/(4\pi a^2)\), \(\mathbf E_{\rm out}=\sigma\hat{\mathbf r}/\epsilon_0\) immediately outside and \(\mathbf E_{\rm in}=0\). Multiplying a sheet's charge directly by its outside field would include the force the tiny element's own field would exert on itself. Near a smooth element approximate its local patch as planar. Its own fields on its two sides are opposite, \(\pm\sigma\hat{\mathbf r}/(2\epsilon_0)\); the field of the rest of the assembly is continuous over that infinitesimal separation. Averaging fields on the two sides therefore extracts the field acting on the element. The force density per area is \(+\sigma^2\hat{\mathbf r}/(2\epsilon_0)\), an outward pressure \(p\).

Each patch acts along its own radial normal. Integrating only the vertical component \(p\cos\theta\) over \(0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2\) gives \(2\pi a^2p\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin\theta\cos\theta\,d\theta=\pi a^2p\). The result is pressure times projected circular area, not pressure times the whole curved hemisphere area. Thus

\[F_{\rm upper}=\frac{Q^2}{32\pi\epsilon_0a^2},\]
\[F_{\rm lower}=-F_{\rm upper}\]

where the signs refer to the \(z\) axis. Reversing the charge signs reverses the electric fields, but the mechanical load stays outward. The whole object's zero net force states only that these internal electromagnetic forces have an equal and opposite partner.

Move the integration surface away from the charges

Instead surround the entire upper half-space by an infinite hemisphere closed by the equatorial plane. In electrostatics the total force on enclosed matter is the outward flux of the Maxwell stress tensor. On the closure at infinity the stress falls as \(1/r^4\), so area growth cannot leave a finite contribution. On the equatorial plane the outward surface normal for the upper region is \(-\hat{\mathbf z}\). The electric field lies parallel to this plane outside the metal and vanishes inside. Thus \(T_{zz}=-\epsilon_0E^2/2\) for radial distance \(s>a\), yielding upward traction on the upper half-space. Its integral is

\[F_z=\int_a^\infty\frac{\epsilon_0}{2}\left(\frac{Q}{4\pi\epsilon_0s^2}\right)^2 2\pi s\,ds=\frac{Q^2}{32\pi\epsilon_0a^2}.\]

This surface passes through an empty region with no electric charge and gives the same finite force. It resolves an apparent puzzle: the equatorial disk inside the conductor has zero field, but the entire cut plane includes an exterior annulus where the field transmits stress. A numerical integration on a small flat disk confined to the hollow interior would report zero because it omitted nearly all of the relevant control surface.

Estimate the load and the error of a finite stress integral

At \(a=30.0\) mm and \(Q=5.0\) nC, the pressure is \(0.0110\) Pa and the upward force is \(31.2\) μN. Consider checking this value in a field calculation restricted to equatorial radii \(a<s<R\). Since the stress integrand is proportional to \(s^{-3}ds\), that annulus captures fraction \(1-a^2/R^2\) of the analytic plane integral. Extending to \(5a\) captures only 96%. A finite closed control surface would supply the missing contribution on its outer cap. An open truncated plane can instead be extrapolated using the measured \(1/R^2\) tail. Both checks are independent of the surface-pressure argument.

A third consistency check uses uniform expansion of the whole conducting shell with fixed charge, for which \(U=Q^2/(8\pi\epsilon_0a)\). Then \(-dU/da=Q^2/(8\pi\epsilon_0a^2)=4\pi a^2 p\), the outward pressure integrated over all area during a common radial displacement. It is larger than the hemispherical seam load by a factor of four because these are different virtual displacements. Differentiating a known energy requires matching the change of geometry to the actual force measurement.

Translate the electromagnetic force into a seam measurement

For a narrow continuous join on the equator, let the tensile load transmitted across it be expressed as force per unit seam length. Rotational symmetry makes it uniform, so \(t_{\rm seam}=F_z/(2\pi a)=Q^2/(64\pi^2\epsilon_0a^3)\). In the numerical example this is \(1.66\times10^{-4}\) N/m. Four equally loaded supports in an ideal symmetric mount would each carry about \(7.8\) μN if they carry the entire vertical separating load. This mechanical conversion uses the projection integral for the actual displacement; the radial pressure at a single point is not itself the measured force.

At fixed \(Q\) doubling \(a\) cuts the total seam force by four. Connect the shell instead to a voltage supply holding \(V=Q/(4\pi\epsilon_0a)\) fixed as \(a\) changes and the charges supplied by the source change proportionally with \(a\). Substitution in the same static force formula now gives \(F_z=\pi\epsilon_0V^2/2\), independent of radius. The differing trends arise from comparing different prepared charges. A local field-stress calculation remains valid for either experimental protocol at its specified instantaneous state.

Repeat the force balance for a solid insulating sphere

Fill a sphere of the same radius with uniform fixed volume charge \(\rho=3Q/(4\pi a^3)\). Determine the force on all charge in the upper half and explain how the equatorial stress surface supplies the difference from the hollow metal case.

Derive the result and check it independently

Inside the uniformly charged sphere \(\mathbf E=\rho\mathbf r/(3\epsilon_0)\). Its vertical force density is \(\rho^2 z/(3\epsilon_0)\). The geometric moment of the upper half is \(\int z\,dV=\int_0^a z\pi(a^2-z^2)\,dz=\pi a^4/4\). Integration gives \(F_z=\pi\rho^2a^4/(12\epsilon_0)=3Q^2/(64\pi\epsilon_0a^2)\), one and a half times the shell result. Self-interaction of the charge in the upper half contributes zero net force by pair symmetry, allowing the volume integral over the total smooth field.

The exterior annulus still yields exactly the original shell force, since the exterior field depends only on total charge. Now the equatorial disk inside the insulating sphere also contains a radial field \(\rho s/(3\epsilon_0)\). Its stress adds \(\int_0^a[\rho^2 s^2/(18\epsilon_0)]2\pi s\,ds=\pi\rho^2a^4/(36\epsilon_0)\). Combining disk and exterior reproduces the direct volume integral. An exterior voltage scan alone cannot distinguish these two charge distributions, but a force measurement on the separating halves can.

Griffiths, 5th ed., §§2.5.3 and 8.2.2 · electrostatic pressure and Maxwell stress

Momentum at a boundary

A capacitor can pull its plates together across vacuum. The ordinary mechanical contact stress of a solid is absent in the gap, yet a surrounding surface still records a force. In vacuum, the electric field contributes the electrostatic stress tensor \(T_{ij}=\epsilon_0(E_iE_j-\delta_{ij}E^2/2)\). Integrating the traction \(\mathbf T\cdot\mathbf n\) around a body gives the electric force on enclosed material in a static configuration. An enclosing surface wholly in vacuum avoids having to specify internal material stresses.

To see the origin of this expression, use \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\rho/\epsilon_0\) and \(\nabla\times\mathbf E=0\). The derivative of \(E_i E_j\) contains a field-gradient term \(E_j\partial_jE_i\). Electrostatic symmetry of derivatives makes this \(\partial_i(E^2/2)\), which cancels the isotropic term in the divergence. The remaining term is \(\rho E_i\), the local electric force density. The divergence theorem converts the volume force integral into the stated surface integral.

Pressure on a conducting plate

For a planar metal surface the field immediately outside is normal. The magnitude of traction is then \(p=\epsilon_0E^2/2\). Another derivation splits the surface-sheet field symmetrically between its two sides. The charge feels the average of the limiting fields, excluding the sheet’s own force. With zero interior field the result is \(\sigma E/2\), identical since \(\sigma=\epsilon_0 E\). Using the full outside field as the field acting on every surface charge would double the force.

For plate area \(A\) and separation \(d\), neglect fringing and put \(C=\epsilon_0A/d\). If the plates are isolated with charge magnitude \(Q\), stored energy \(U=Q^2/(2C)\) increases linearly with separation. The signed force in the direction of increasing \(d\) is \(-\partial U/\partial d=-Q^2/(2\epsilon_0A)\). The force tends to close the gap, consistent with the traction calculation.

Include the supply in a fixed-voltage experiment

When a voltage source remains connected, \(Q\) varies as \(d\) changes. The source supplies work \(V\,dQ\). The relevant mechanical energy at fixed \(V\) is \(U-VQ=-CV^2/2\). Differentiating this expression gives \(F_d=(V^2/2)dC/dd=-\epsilon_0AV^2/(2d^2)\). The electrical stress formula gives the same instantaneous force for either boundary condition. What changes during motion is the relationship between field and separation.

Take \(A=0.0100\) m², \(d=1.00\) mm and \(V=500\) V. The field magnitude is \(5.00\times10^5\) V/m, the pressure is \(1.11\) Pa and attraction is \(0.0111\) N. Doubling the gap at fixed voltage quarters the force. Disconnecting first holds \(Q\) fixed and leaves force unchanged in the ideal wide-plate limit. A direct classroom test is to measure reaction force while controlling either voltage or charge. Departures as separation approaches plate width reveal fringing fields, which redistribute both stress and capacitance.

The planar calculation can be checked by dimensions before using any geometry. Electric-field energy density has units of joules per cubic metre, identical to newtons per square metre. It therefore has the units of pressure. This equality of dimensions alone cannot fix the direction of a force in a nonuniform field. Around the edge of a plate, for example, field lines bend and the tensor traction must be projected onto the direction being measured before integration.

Pressure from an incident and a reflected wave

For time-dependent fields the force on matter combines a flux of electromagnetic momentum through its boundary with the change of field momentum inside. A useful steady special case is a normally incident plane wave on a macroscopic stationary surface in vacuum. Its cycle-averaged energy flux is \(I\), and transport of energy \(dU\) carries momentum \(dU/c\) in the propagation direction. Let the surface reflect fraction \(\mathcal R\), transmit fraction \(\mathcal T\) into vacuum on the far side, and absorb fraction \(\mathcal A=1-\mathcal R-\mathcal T\). Assume all beams are collinear and neglect thermal reradiation in the mechanical measurement.

For an area \(A\) over time \(dt\), the incoming axial momentum is \(IA\,dt/c\). The reflected beam leaves with negative momentum and the transmitted beam with positive momentum. The difference delivered to the surface gives

\[p_{\rm rad}=\frac Ic(1+\mathcal R-\mathcal T)=\frac Ic(\mathcal A+2\mathcal R).\]

A fully absorbing sheet receives \(I/c\) even though there is no exiting optical wave. A perfect mirror receives \(2I/c\) because it reverses photon momentum. A perfectly transmitting transparent sheet with no reflection takes no average axial momentum in this stationary, vacuum-to-vacuum accounting. Interfaces bounding different media require a joint treatment of material and electromagnetic momentum; the simple outgoing-vacuum relation has intentionally fixed that complication.

The same mirror result follows from the stress at a conducting boundary. At normal incidence the tangential electric field cancels while magnetic field amplitudes add. Using the cycle-averaged magnetic pressure at the boundary then gives \(2I/c\). This provides an independent check on the momentum-flux interpretation without assigning a mechanical collision trajectory to a wave.

Predict a torsion-balance signal

Direct a continuous 2.00 W beam entirely onto a small mirror at normal incidence. Measured fractions are \(\mathcal R=0.960\), \(\mathcal T=0.0200\), leaving \(\mathcal A=0.0200\). Integrated axial force is \((2.00/c)(0.0200+1.920)=1.29\times10^{-8}\) N. Mount the mirror so its force acts 40.0 mm from a torsion fibre axis with a perpendicular lever arm. Torque is \(5.18\times10^{-10}\) N m. A torsion constant \(2.00\times10^{-7}\) N m/rad gives a static twist of \(0.00259\) rad or \(0.148^\circ\).

The absorbed power is \(0.0400\) W. Heating can drive air currents or slow distortions that also deflect the balance, particularly outside vacuum. Reversing the mechanical lever arm while leaving absorbed power constant reverses the optical torque. Modulating the beam and measuring both delay and amplitude provides an additional way to distinguish the rapid radiation force from a slower thermal response.

For a plane mirror at oblique incidence the reflected momentum reverses only its component normal to the surface. A beam of known total power fully intercepted by the mirror imparts normal force \(2P\cos\alpha/c\) when it is perfectly reflected at angle \(\alpha\) to the normal. If the controlled quantity is irradiance per area perpendicular to the beam and the physical mirror area is fixed, intercepted power adds another projection factor. The pressure per unit mirror area is then \(2I\cos^2\alpha/c\). Specifying whether power or surface footprint is held fixed is essential before comparing an angular torque scan with either formula.

Problem sheet 6 · Reflections, force and stored energy

Determine a cable fault and use energy accounting to diagnose electrical forces with different experimental controls.

A reflected step

A line of characteristic impedance 75 Ω and propagation speed \(2.00\times10^8\) m/s carries a 1.20 V step. A 0.360 V downward return step appears 480 ns after launch. Find the distance, reflection coefficient, load resistance and total first voltage at the load.

Show the fault reconstruction

The wave made a round trip, so the mismatch is \(vt/2=48.0\) m away. The signed ratio is \(\Gamma=-0.360/1.20=-0.300\). Invert the reflection law to get \(R_L=75(1+\Gamma)/(1-\Gamma)=40.4\) Ω. Voltage at the load rises to \(1.20(1-0.300)=0.840\) V on first arrival. Its total current, \((1.20+0.360)/75=20.8\) mA, gives the same resistance.

Weigh the electric pull

Horizontal vacuum plates of area 40.0 cm² are separated by 0.500 mm. Find the voltage producing 2.00 mN of attraction. If disconnected and separated quasistatically to 1.00 mm, determine mechanical work supplied and final voltage.

Show the two controlled stages

Pressure is \(F/A=0.500\) Pa and \(E=\sqrt{2p/\epsilon_0}=3.36\times10^5\) V/m. The supply must provide \(V=Ed=168\) V. After disconnection the field stays fixed for broad plates because charge and area are fixed. The opposing force remains 2.00 mN, so the applied mechanical work is \(F\Delta d=1.00\) μJ. The final voltage is \(336\) V. The increased electrical field volume accounts for the stored energy.

Check a field-energy inventory

A uniform 50 Ω lossless cable has propagation speed \(2.00\times10^8\) m/s. A rectangular 2.0 V forward pulse is 100 ns long. Find its length, the total travelling energy and its separate electric and magnetic parts. Where does the energy go at an ideal open termination?

Show the travelling inventory

The pulse occupies \(v\Delta t=20.0\) m. Its power is \(V^2/Z_0=0.080\) W and energy is \(8.0\) nJ. The distributed parameters follow from \(Z_0\) and \(v\), giving \(C'=1/(vZ_0)=100\) pF/m and \(L'=Z_0/v=250\) nH/m. Electric energy \(\tfrac12 C'\ell V^2\) is \(4.0\) nJ and magnetic energy is equal since \(I=V/Z_0\). At an open circuit the voltage reflection coefficient is \(+1\) and current cancels at the end. The reflected pulse ultimately carries back all 8.0 nJ; instantaneous electric and magnetic shares can change during overlap.

Account for the energy of a charging capacitor

Two circular plates of radius 5.00 cm, separated by 1.00 mm of vacuum, are charged by a constant current of 1.00 mA. At the instant their voltage is 10.0 V, find the displacement current, magnetic field at the plate rim, increase in electric energy per second and direction of electromagnetic energy flow. Neglect fringe fields in the gap.

Worked solution

The field between the plates is V/d = 10⁴ V/m. The capacitance is ε₀πR²/d = 69.5 pF. Since dV/dt = I/C, the electric flux changes at precisely the rate needed to give ε₀ dΦ/dt = I through the gap. Thus a circular Ampèrian path returns the same result whichever spanning surface one chooses.

Inside the disk only the fraction r²/R² of the changing flux is enclosed. At the rim B = μ₀I/(2πR) = 4.00 nT. The magnetic field is azimuthal. With the displacement current along the electric field, E × B points radially inward. The Poynting flux at the rim has magnitude EB/μ₀ = 31.8 W/m².

The side area is 2πRd = 3.14 × 10⁻⁴ m², giving 0.0100 W inward. The derivative of ½CV² is CV dV/dt = VI = 0.0100 W. Both calculations close the same energy balance. The uniform-field approximation describes the interior and integrated flux well for d much less than R. It cannot resolve the local fringe-field pattern right at the metal edges.

Can impedance identify a dielectric by itself?

A coaxial line with fixed radii and relative permittivity 2.25 measures 47.4537 Ω. A second sample measures 44.0381 Ω. Assuming concentric geometry and the same permeability, infer its relative permittivity. Use the laboratory’s offset-conductor run to construct a second possible explanation. Which additional measurement distinguishes these two explanations?

Work through the solution

For a homogeneous concentric TEM line \(Z_0=\sqrt{L'/C'}\) and \(C'\) scales with permittivity while \(L'\) is fixed. Thus \(\epsilon_{r,2}=2.25(47.4537/44.0381)^2=2.613\). An unchanged dielectric with a 0.40 mm offset gives the same impedance in the numerical study. Wave speed distinguishes them. Geometry cancels in \(L'C'=\mu\epsilon\) for a homogeneous TEM line. A dielectric change would reduce the speed by a factor \(\sqrt{2.25/2.613}=0.928\), while the offset alone leaves it unchanged.

Use the OneSimulation laboratory to check your answer

Chapter 13 · Local field laws at points, sheets and holes

Derive divergence and curl from what a probe encloses. Track a point charge through a regularisation and work out when a potential can be defined over an entire region.

What would a very small instrument measure?

Imagine six square flux meters on the faces of an empty cube of side \(h\). Each one measures the field component along its outward normal. A uniform field produces equal and opposite readings on opposite faces. A field whose \(x\)-component grows towards the right gives a positive difference between those two readings. To leading order their difference is \(h^2[E_x(x+h/2)-E_x(x-h/2)]\). Adding the three pairs and dividing by volume gives

\[\lim_{h\to0}\frac{1}{h^3}\oint_{\partial V_h}\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf n\,dS=\partial_x E_x+\partial_y E_y+\partial_z E_z.\]

The limit exists for a smooth field. Tiling a region with cubes then cancels all the interior-face readings. Only its outer surface remains. This explains why a local divergence controls a global flux even for an irregular boundary. In spherical symmetry a sphere of radius \(r\) makes the analogous measurement \(4\pi r^2 E_r\). Taking the difference between two nearby concentric spheres and dividing by the shell volume gives \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=r^{-2}d(r^2E_r)/dr\). The factor \(r^2\) records the changing area of the meter.

A circulation meter is a small oriented loop. For a rectangle in the \(xy\)-plane, positive traversal about \(+\hat{\mathbf z}\) compares \(E_y\) on its two vertical edges and \(E_x\) on its horizontal edges. Division by area gives \((\nabla\times\mathbf E)_z=\partial_xE_y-\partial_yE_x\). Neighbouring loops cancel shared edges, giving the surface-to-boundary rule known as Stokes’ theorem. Reversing the loop normal reverses the circulation. Curl measures local circulation density, even at a point where the vector itself happens to vanish.

Recover the charge lost by a pointwise derivative

The field of a point charge is \(q\hat{\mathbf r}/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2)\). Its radial divergence formula gives zero at every \(r>0\), whereas a sphere of any positive radius has flux \(q/\epsilon_0\). To make sense of both observations, replace the point by a smooth model potential of width \(a>0\),

\[V_a(r)=\frac{q}{4\pi\epsilon_0\sqrt{r^2+a^2}},\]
\[E_a(r)=\frac{qr}{4\pi\epsilon_0(r^2+a^2)^{3/2}}.\]

Differentiate \(r^2 E_a\) before dividing by \(r^2\). The two derivative terms combine to leave a sharply concentrated charge density:

\[\rho_a(r)=\frac{3qa^2}{4\pi(r^2+a^2)^{5/2}},\]
\[Q_a(R)=q\frac{R^3}{(R^2+a^2)^{3/2}}.\]

For each fixed \(R>0\), \(Q_a(R)\to q\) as \(a\to0\). For each fixed \(r>0\), \(\rho_a(r)\to0\). A probe with a smooth spatial response \(f\) measures \(\int f\rho_a\,d^3r\), which tends to \(qf(0)\). The object defined by this last operation is the three-dimensional delta distribution. Poisson’s equation for Coulomb’s potential therefore reads \(\nabla^2(1/r)=-4\pi\delta^3(\mathbf r)\). The singular part supplies exactly the missing flux.

Worked measurement with two collecting spheres

For \(a=2.0\) mm, a spherical meter at \(R=a\) encloses \(q/2^{3/2}=0.354q\). At \(R=5a\) it encloses \(125q/26^{3/2}=0.943q\). A mesh representing the smooth source needs cells considerably narrower than \(a\) to reproduce its peak. An integrated flux can already be close to the point-charge answer well outside that core. Reporting a peak and reporting an enclosed charge are distinct resolution tests.

Treat an interface as a source with a measured sign

An infinite uniform charge sheet at \(z=0\) generates \(E_z=\sigma\,\operatorname{sgn}(z)/(2\epsilon_0)\) when reflection symmetry makes both sides equivalent. Integrating its derivative over a thin interval gives the jump \(\sigma/\epsilon_0\) irrespective of the interval's thickness. Thus \(\partial_z E_z=(\sigma/\epsilon_0)\delta(z)\). The surface density has units C m\(^{-2}\) and the one-dimensional delta contributes m\(^{-1}\), as a volume density requires.

A finite-volume calculation can use that integral directly. For a cell of area \(A\) straddling the sheet the face-flux difference is \(A(E_z^+-E_z^-)=A\sigma/\epsilon_0\). As the cell becomes thinner its density estimate grows, while its charge stays fixed. If fields of \(+600\) and \(-200\) V/m are recorded just above and below a sheet, its density is \(\epsilon_0(800\ {\rm V/m})=7.08\ {\rm nC/m^2}\). Their mean, \(200\) V/m, is an independently applied background compatible with the same jump.

Ask what lies inside the spanning surface

Consider \(\mathbf F=C\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}/s\) outside a cylindrical excluded region, with \(s\) the distance from its axis. Its local curl vanishes. A circular traversal still has circulation \(2\pi C\). A disk spanning that circle cuts the excluded region, where the assumed smooth field is unavailable. Every sufficiently small contractible loop has zero circulation. A single-valued global scalar potential on the entire punctured region would require zero circulation on the large loop as well. This is why the topology of the domain is part of a potential formulation.

Measure the flux that a finite sphere captures

A point source would put all its charge inside every positive collector radius. For a 1 mm smooth core, a 2 mm collector encloses 71.6%. Increase the radius and infer which assumption an experimental flux measurement can resolve.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Test the instrument before using its reading

Recover a core width from one flux fraction

A calibrated spherical probe of radius \(R\) encloses \(q/2\) for the smooth source above. Determine \(a/R\) and predict the fraction enclosed at radius \(2R\).

Work through the solution

Raise the measured fraction to the power \(2/3\). It becomes \(R^2/(R^2+a^2)=2^{-2/3}\), so \(a/R=\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}=0.766\). The second meter collects \([1+(0.766/2)^2]^{-3/2}=0.814\) of the total, to three significant figures. Disagreement with both readings suggests an incorrect source shape or an off-centre probe.

Diagnose a circulation measurement

A loop encloses a very long changing flux tube. Outside it the magnetic field is negligible. Can the induced electric field have zero local curl at every point of the loop and a nonzero integral around it?

Work through the solution

Yes. Outside the tube \(-\partial_t\mathbf B=0\), and a local curl probe records zero. A spanning disk reaches inside the tube. Its integrated flux derivative gives \(\oint\mathbf E\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=-d\Phi_B/dt\). Doubling the loop radius leaves this integral fixed for the same enclosed flux; cylindrical symmetry then halves the azimuthal field on the larger loop.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, fifth edition, §§1.2–1.6 and 2.2 (publisher contents)
  • Fließbach, seventh edition, Distributionen

Chapter 14 · Building a potential from point responses

Solve one source problem exactly, reuse it for a distributed charge, and find what an electrode measures without solving for its surface charge point by point.

Start with the response to one thin sheet

Two large grounded conducting plates occupy \(x=0\) and \(x=L\). A charge sheet of surface density \(\sigma\) sits at \(x=s\), far from plate edges. Translational symmetry along each plate makes the potential a function of \(x\) alone. Integrating Poisson’s equation across the charge shows that \(V\) is continuous and \(V'(s^+)-V'(s^-)=-\sigma/\epsilon_0\). The potential is linear in each empty gap. It is helpful to remove the scale \(\sigma/\epsilon_0\) and call the remaining function \(G(x,s)\).

On the left write \(G=A x\) to satisfy the first plate. On the right write \(G=B(L-x)\) to satisfy the second. Continuity imposes \(As=B(L-s)\). The derivative jump gives \(-B-A=-1\), so \(A=(L-s)/L\) and \(B=s/L\). A single expression covers both segments:

\[G(x,s)=\frac{\min(x,s)[L-\max(x,s)]}{L},\]
\[-\partial_x^2G(x,s)=\delta(x-s).\]

This Green function has units of length in this one-dimensional reduction. A source near the left plate produces a steep left slope and a shallow right slope. The left conductor acquires the greater magnitude of induced charge. Exchanging detector and source coordinates leaves \(G\) unchanged. This symmetry will turn out to be a physical consequence of the linear field equation.

Assemble many sheets and retain the boundary voltages

A slice of thickness \(ds\) has sheet charge \(\rho(s)\,ds\). Linearity allows its contribution \(\rho(s)G(x,s)\,ds/\epsilon_0\) to be added to every other slice. If the plate voltages are \(V_0,V_L\), a straight line accounts for the source-free solution satisfying those voltages:

\[V(x)=V_0(1-x/L)+V_L x/L+\frac1{\epsilon_0}\int_0^L G(x,s)\rho(s)\,ds .\]

For uniform positive \(\rho\), split the integral at \(s=x\). The left piece is \((L-x)x^2/(2L)\), and the right piece is \(x(L-x)^2/(2L)\), before multiplication by \(\rho/\epsilon_0\). Together they yield \(V_{\rm charge}=\rho x(L-x)/(2\epsilon_0)\). Twice differentiating recovers the volume charge everywhere, and at either plate the charge contribution is zero.

Worked field measurement between unequal plates

Let \(L=10\) mm, \(V_0=0\), \(V_L=2.0\) V, and \(\rho=1.00\,\mu{\rm C/m^3}\). The straight-line background contributes 1.00 V at the midpoint. The uniform space charge contributes \(\rho L^2/(8\epsilon_0)=1.41\) V. The maximum potential can exceed both conductor voltages in the presence of a positive source. At the midpoint the charge contribution to the field vanishes by symmetry, so the field there still has its background value \(-200\) V/m. This gives two independent observables for the same solution.

Prove that source and detector can exchange roles

For two electrostatic states occupying the same region with a linear, symmetric permittivity tensor, define potentials \(V_1,V_2\) and charges \(\rho_1,\rho_2\). Multiply the first Poisson equation by \(V_2\), the second by \(V_1\), then subtract and integrate. The two terms containing products of gradients cancel after integration by parts because of tensor symmetry. With outward normal from the dielectric region one obtains

\[\int_\Omega (V_2\rho_1-V_1\rho_2)\,d^3x=\oint_{\partial\Omega}\big[V_1\mathbf n\cdot\epsilon\nabla V_2-V_2\mathbf n\cdot\epsilon\nabla V_1\big]\,dA .\]

With both outer boundary potentials zero, the surface term vanishes. Choosing a point source for each state shows that the potential per unit charge at the other point is the same in either order. Fixed geometry, material response and boundary type matter. Replacing a grounded electrode by an isolated electrode between the two measurements changes the problem.

Worked electrode reading from a weighting potential

Take state 1 to be the real charge \(q\) inside a cavity whose electrodes are all grounded. For state 2 remove the charge, set the right electrode to 1 V and the others to zero, and divide this potential by 1 V to define \(w\). The surface normal in the identity points into the metal, so \(\mathbf n\cdot\epsilon\nabla V_1\) is the free charge density on the metal face. The identity now gives \(Q_R=-q\,w(\mathbf x_q)\). For parallel plates \(w=x/L\). Moving \(12\) pC from \(0.20L\) to \(0.65L\) changes the right-electrode charge by \(-5.4\) pC. Its change on the other plate is \(+5.4\) pC, as an independent sum check. Device designers can reuse one computed \(w\) for many charge positions.

Move a charge sheet between grounded electrodes

Keep the plate separation at 10 mm. At 30% of the gap, a positive sheet of 3 nC/m² induces −2.1 and −0.9 nC/m² on the left and right plates. Move the sheet through the midpoint, then reverse its charge. Predict the sign of the slope jump each time.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Practise splitting the integral and reading an induced signal

Locate a hidden sheet using both electrodes

A grounded pair of plates has induced charges of \(-3.0\) pC on the left and \(-7.0\) pC on the right from a single positive sheet of finite illuminated area in the large-plate approximation. Infer its charge and dimensionless position. Does the pair determine the width if several sheets may be present?

Work through the solution

All induced charge sums to \(-q\), so \(q=10.0\) pC. The right fraction \(0.700\) places the sheet at \(s=0.700L\). For a cloud, linearity gives \(Q_R=-\int(x/L)\,dq\). The two readings yield total charge and first moment only. Two equal sheets at \(0.60L\) and \(0.80L\) give identical readings.

Bound a numerical influence coefficient

A discretisation gives \(G(0.2L,0.7L)=0.062L\) and \(G(0.7L,0.2L)=0.058L\). Which exact value should both approach? Does averaging establish convergence?

Work through the solution

Their target is \(0.2(1-0.7)L=0.060L\). The discrepancy may come from depositing source charge and sampling voltage with different weights. The two errors happen to cancel in the mean here. Refine the grid and perform each measurement separately. Reciprocity alone also cannot reveal a common bias in both entries.

Further reading

  • Fließbach, Randwertprobleme, pp. 59–67
  • Griffiths, §§3.1–3.2, boundary conditions, uniqueness and images

Chapter 15 · How far does an electrode pattern reach?

Solve Laplace’s equation in a strip, read decay lengths straight from boundary data and decide when a truncated series can predict an interior measurement.

Specify all the boundaries before searching for a formula

A long rectangular cross-section occupies \(0<x<a,\ y>0\). The side walls are grounded, the bottom edge carries a prescribed voltage \(f(x)\), and the potential decays as \(y\) grows. Physically the bottom edge may be an array of narrow driven electrodes separated by insulating gaps. The idealisation prescribes voltage in the limit of small gaps. There is no free charge in the interior and the dielectric is homogeneous. The problem is thus \(V_{xx}+V_{yy}=0\). A single field line or Gaussian surface does not tell us how a complicated \(f\) propagates upwards.

Try one product \(X(x)Y(y)\). Dividing the equation by the product gives \(X''/X=-Y''/Y\). Each side depends on a different variable, so both must be constant. Write the first constant as \(-k^2\). Testing zero and positive values for \(X''/X\) yields only the trivial solution satisfying both grounded sides. The negative choice yields \(X=A\sin kx+B\cos kx\). The left wall sets \(B=0\); the right wall requires \(k_n=n\pi/a\), \(n=1,2,\ldots\). For a fixed \(n\), \(Y\) is a sum of growing and decaying exponentials. The far boundary eliminates the growing branch.

Determine the coefficients by asking one question per mode

No individual mode can fit general data at the base. Add the admissible products and enforce the bottom voltage. Multiply by \(\sin(m\pi x/a)\) and integrate over the width. Products of distinct sine modes average to zero, while the squared sine integrates to \(a/2\). These measurements recover independent coefficients:

\[b_n=\frac2a\int_0^a f(x)\sin(n\pi x/a)\,dx,\]
\[V(x,y)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n\sin(n\pi x/a)e^{-n\pi y/a}.\]

Physically every extra oscillation across the base shortens the vertical penetration length. For \(a=40\) mm the first mode decays by a factor \(e\) every 12.7 mm; the third takes only 4.24 mm. Complicated boundary structure can leave a very simple interior field.

Worked cancellation that moves with height

Drive the base with \(f=3\sin(\pi x/a)+3\sin(3\pi x/a)\) volts. At the centre the two boundary voltages cancel since \(\sin(3\pi/2)=-1\). Above it the voltage becomes \(3(e^{-t}-e^{-3t})\) with \(t=\pi y/a\). Differentiate to locate its maximum: \(-e^{-t}+3e^{-3t}=0\) gives \(t=\frac12\ln3\). Thus \(y=0.175a\) and the reading reaches \(2/\sqrt3=1.155\) V, even above a point set to zero volts. A full solution at the centre depends on the surrounding boundary data.

Predict the influence of a finite electrode

Place a 5 V electrode on the central half of the base and ground the remainder. Direct integration yields

\[b_n=\frac{10\ {\rm V}}{n\pi}\big[\cos(n\pi/4)-\cos(3n\pi/4)\big].\]

Even modes vanish by reflection symmetry. The first two nonzero coefficients are \(b_1=4.50\) V and \(b_3=-1.50\) V. At \(x=a/2,y=a/4\), their combined central reading is \(4.50e^{-\pi/4}+1.50e^{-3\pi/4}=2.19\) V. A conservative bound on the discarded tail uses \(|b_n|\le20/(n\pi)\) V. Summing a geometric bound from \(n=5\) gives a maximum error of \(20e^{-5\pi/4}/[5\pi(1-e^{-\pi/4})]\approx0.046\) V. Away from the base this is an inexpensive error certificate.

At the electrode edge itself the series approaches the midpoint of the voltage jump and overshoots nearby when truncated. A real electrode has a gap and finite edge radius. Estimating the maximum surface field from this discontinuous idealisation makes the result depend on the chosen resolution. An interior voltage at a specified positive height is much better conditioned.

Change a physical boundary and rebuild the mode

An enclosure of finite height \(H\) with a grounded lid needs a mode which vanishes at \(H\) and equals one at the base. Solving the same vertical ODE now gives \(Y_n=\sinh[k_n(H-y)]/\sinh(k_nH)\). The transverse eigenvalues stay fixed because the side walls have not moved. For \(H=a/2\) and \(y=H/2\), the fundamental is transmitted with factor \(\sinh(\pi/4)/\sinh(\pi/2)=0.377\) instead of the open-strip value \(e^{-\pi/4}=0.456\). This difference is a way to diagnose an unmodelled nearby conductor.

Move a measurement plane away from patterned electrodes

The first boundary harmonic is fixed at 3 V. Set the third to 3 V as well. At the midpoint the boundary values cancel; 0.18 strip widths above it the two terms leave about 1.15 V. Follow the two curves to see which boundary details survive propagation into the gap.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Use a measurement to judge which boundary details survive

How accurately could the third harmonic be recovered?

At \(y=a/2\) an instrument can resolve a 10 mV Fourier amplitude. How large must the third harmonic at the base be? Compare the fundamental.

Work through the solution

Third-mode attenuation is \(e^{-3\pi/2}=0.00898\), so about \(1.11\) V is needed at the boundary. The fundamental needs only \(0.010e^{\pi/2}=0.0481\) V. Back-propagating a noisy field magnifies its finest scales first. Improving the number of model harmonics cannot compensate for a missing signal.

Check the energy of one harmonic

The base drives \(b\sin(kx)\) for one admissible \(k\). Integrate the electric energy per unit length perpendicular to the strip and check its dimensions.

Work through the solution

The two field components are \(-kb\cos(kx)e^{-ky}\) and \(kb\sin(kx)e^{-ky}\), so their squares sum to \(k^2b^2e^{-2ky}\). Integrating \(\epsilon E^2/2\) across \(a\) and upwards to infinity gives \(U'=\epsilon a k b^2/4\), in J/m. At fixed boundary amplitude a shorter wavelength stores more energy near the base despite penetrating less far.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §3.3.1, Cartesian separation of variables
  • Fließbach, Randwertprobleme

Chapter 16 · Spherical harmonics as measurements of boundary voltage

Reconstruct interior and exterior potentials from an angular voltage scan. Use orthogonality to isolate a dipole or quadrupole and quantify how rapidly fine angular structure disappears.

Why a sphere selects angular patterns

Consider a spherical surface of radius \(a\) covered by closely spaced insulated electrodes. They can prescribe a voltage varying with position, whereas a single connected conductor in electrostatic equilibrium can have only one voltage. Suppose there are no other charges, the surrounding permittivity is \(\epsilon_0\), and the reference is zero at infinity. At first let the applied voltage be independent of azimuth. In spherical coordinates the charge-free equation becomes

\[\frac1{r^2}\partial_r(r^2\partial_r V)+\frac1{r^2\sin\theta}\partial_\theta(\sin\theta\,\partial_\theta V)=0 .\]

Writing \(V=R(r)P(u)\) with \(u=\cos\theta\) and multiplying by \(r^2/(RP)\) separates the radial and angular parts. The angular operator becomes \(d[(1-u^2)dP/du]/du\); regularity at both poles selects eigenvalues \(\ell(\ell+1)\) for nonnegative integer \(\ell\). The regular eigenfunctions are the Legendre polynomials. The first three are \(1,u,(3u^2-1)/2\). Substitution of any of these into the angular equation verifies its eigenvalue.

For each angular order the radial equation reads \((r^2R')'=\ell(\ell+1)R\). Insert \(R=r^p\) to obtain \(p(p+1)=\ell(\ell+1)\). The two choices are \(p=\ell\) and \(p=-\ell-1\). The first stays regular at the centre; the second decays at infinity. These choices describe different physical domains for the same measured surface voltage.

Infer coefficients and the surface charge they require

On the spherical surface write \(f(u)=\sum_\ell b_\ell P_\ell(u)\). Legendre polynomials are orthogonal under the integral \(du\) from \(-1\) to 1; for equal indices the integral is \(2/(2\ell+1)\). The weight has a geometric origin: a narrow latitude strip subtends solid angle \(2\pi\sin\theta\,d\theta=-2\pi\,du\). Uniform steps in polar angle must be weighted by \(\sin\theta\) before estimating the angular coefficients.

\[b_\ell=\frac{2\ell+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}f(u)P_\ell(u)\,du,\]
\[V_{\rm in}=\sum_\ell b_\ell(r/a)^\ell P_\ell(u),\]
\[V_{\rm out}=\sum_\ell b_\ell(a/r)^{\ell+1}P_\ell(u).\]

At the boundary both sides have the prescribed potential. Their normal derivatives generally differ. For each order the outward fields at \(a\) are \(+(\ell+1)b_\ell P_\ell/a\) outside and \(-\ell b_\ell P_\ell/a\) inside. Gauss’s pillbox therefore assigns the thin electrode array a net sheet charge density

\[\sigma(u)=\frac{\epsilon_0}{a}\sum_\ell (2\ell+1)b_\ell P_\ell(u).\]

Worked inverse problem with three voltmeter readings

Suppose independent measurements establish that orders above two are negligible. At the north pole, equator and south pole a scan gives 4, 1 and 2 V. The first and last readings differ only in the odd term, hence \(b_1=(4-2)/2=1\) V. Their average gives \(b_0+b_2=3\) V and the equator gives \(b_0-b_2/2=1\) V. Solving yields \(b_0=5/3\) V and \(b_2=4/3\) V. On the north axis at \(r=a/2\) the predicted voltage is \(5/3+1/2+1/3=2.50\) V. The three readings alone would not exclude a higher-order angular pattern. Additional sample positions are needed to test that assumption.

Use radial decay to design a measurement

The exterior radial factor contains one extra power of \(a/r\) compared with the relative angular order. At \(r=2a\) a quadrupole potential has fallen to one eighth of its surface value. An order-six pattern has fallen to \(1/128\). A 20 mV order-six component at a distant detector would require 2.56 V at the surface. A 20 mV instrumental uncertainty has exactly the same amplification if one attempts a surface reconstruction from that single radius.

Worked cancellation and a charge measurement

For a pure quadrupole set \(a=50\) mm and \(b_2=3.0\) V. The north-pole surface density is \(5\epsilon_0 b_2/a=2.66\) nC/m²; at the equator it is half that magnitude with opposite sign. The net charge vanishes because \(\int_{-1}^{1}P_2\,du=0\). A charge meter that captures the entire array would read zero while a local probe measures finite voltage and field. Net charge measures precisely the \(\ell=0\) coefficient.

Recognise when a latitude scan is insufficient

A painted voltage pattern can vary with longitude as well. A periodic azimuthal factor has form \(e^{im\phi}\) with integer \(m\). The extra angular term in Laplace’s equation introduces \(-m^2/(1-u^2)\) into the Legendre equation. Regular solutions are associated Legendre functions with \(|m|\le\ell\). Combined with the azimuthal factor and normalisation they form \(Y_{\ell m}\). For an orthonormal convention the coefficient is the surface integral of \(Y_{\ell m}^*f\) over solid angle. The radial powers remain unchanged. In particular, \(f\propto\sin\theta\cos\phi\) is a dipolar pattern oriented along the \(x\) direction. Averaging every latitude ring would completely erase it.

Continue three boundary harmonics through a sphere

The boundary has a constant 1 V term, a 1 V dipole coefficient and an adjustable quadrupole. At half the sphere radius with a 2 V quadrupole, the pole reads 2.00 V and the equator 0.75 V. Compare inside and outside measurements with the same angular resolution.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Distinguish angular resolution from total charge

Locate a field null inside a sphere

Take \(b_1=1.2\) V, \(b_2=-1.0\) V, \(a=60\) mm, with all other coefficients zero. Find a point on the positive axis at which the full electric field vanishes.

Work through the solution

On the axis, symmetry sets transverse field to zero. The potential is \(1.2z/a-(z/a)^2\) volts. Its derivative vanishes at \(z/a=0.6\), or 36 mm. The local axial extremum is compatible with Laplace’s equation because transverse curvature has the opposite sign. The point is a saddle of the three-dimensional potential.

Is an interior maximum consistent with the measured boundary?

A code sampling a smooth, charge-free sphere reports 6.0 V at its centre; the largest voltage applied anywhere on the boundary is 4.0 V. Explain the discrepancy using the harmonics.

Work through the solution

At the centre all terms with \(\ell>0\) vanish. The remaining \(b_0\) equals the angular average of the boundary voltage and cannot exceed 4 V. The check isolates a cause to investigate: interior sources, incorrect boundary data or a defective discretisation. It does not require knowing the other coefficients.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §§3.3.2 and 3.4, spherical separation and multipoles
  • Fließbach, Legendrepolynome, pp. 80–88
  • Fließbach, Kugelfunktionen, pp. 99–107

Chapter 17 · From a measured current to its magnetic field

Construct the magnetic field of a steady source, calculate a coil pair’s uniform region and extract the first magnetic moment that survives at a large distance.

Identify a physically possible source

A wire carries a measured steady current. Its magnetic effect depends on the complete current path, including the return conductor. Charge conservation requires \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf J=0\) for a stationary distribution. A finite wire segment with current suddenly ending at an insulated tip would accumulate charge. The missing return path can be neglected in an approximation only after comparing its distance and field with those of the measured segment.

The magnetostatic field equations in vacuum are \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\) and \(\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J\). The first suggests writing \(\mathbf B=\nabla\times\mathbf A\). Adding any gradient to \(\mathbf A\) leaves \(\mathbf B\) unchanged. In unbounded space we may choose \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf A=0\) together with decay for a localised source. Applying curl to the potential gives \(\nabla\times(\nabla\times\mathbf A)=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\mathbf A)-\nabla^2\mathbf A\); the chosen divergence removes the first term.

\[\nabla^2\mathbf A=-\mu_0\mathbf J,\]
\[\mathbf A(\mathbf r)=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\int\frac{\mathbf J(\mathbf r')}{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r'|}\,d^3r'.\]

Each Cartesian component solves a scalar Poisson problem. Taking the observation-coordinate curl under the integral differentiates the distance kernel. Since \(\nabla(1/R)=-\mathbf R/R^3\), exchanging the order of the cross product yields the Biot–Savart integrand \(\mathbf J(\mathbf r')\times\mathbf R/R^3\). Ampère’s circulation law remains true for all steady currents. It gives a field value immediately only when symmetry constrains the field along a chosen path.

Turn the vector integral into an axial measurement

For a thin circular loop of radius \(a\) in the \(xy\)-plane, \(\mathbf J\,d^3r'\) becomes \(I\,d\boldsymbol\ell'\). At a point \(z\) on the axis every element has the same distance \(\sqrt{a^2+z^2}\). Opposite elements produce cancelling transverse fields. The surviving axial numerator integrates to \(2\pi I a^2\) because each tangent crosses the inward radial component of \(\mathbf R\) with the same orientation. Thus

\[B_z(z)=\frac{\mu_0 I a^2}{2(a^2+z^2)^{3/2}} .\]

For a 40 mm loop carrying 0.80 A, the central field is \(12.57\,\mu{\rm T}\). At \(z=a\) it is smaller by \(2^{3/2}\), or \(4.44\,\mu{\rm T}\). Reversing current reverses both readings. No field singularity exists on the axis; evaluating the ideal filament field directly on the wire instead requires a model of its finite cross-section.

Design a locally uniform field by cancelling curvature

Place two identical coaxial loops at \(z=\pm d/2\) carrying the same current. Their combined field is even in \(z\), so its first derivative at the midpoint is automatically zero. The second derivative sets the leading departure from uniformity. For a loop profile \(b(z)=C(a^2+z^2)^{-3/2}\), differentiation gives

\[b''(z)=3C\frac{4z^2-a^2}{(a^2+z^2)^{7/2}},\]
\[B_{\rm pair}''(0)=2b''(d/2).\]

Choosing \(d=a\) cancels the curvature, leaving a fourth-order axial variation near the centre. The midpoint field then equals \(\mu_0 I(4/5)^{3/2}/a\) for one turn on each loop. For \(a=60.0\) mm and \(I=0.50\) A this gives \(7.49\,\mu{\rm T}\). Identical compact \(N\)-turn windings scale that reading by \(N\) while their finite winding dimensions eventually change the profile. A ratio of two nearby field readings is often a better test of spacing than an absolute reading affected by current calibration.

Find which source information a distant detector retains

For \(r\gg a\), the exact axial loop field approaches \(\mu_0 I a^2/(2r^3)\). Define the dipole moment \(\mathbf m=I\pi a^2\hat{\mathbf n}\). The axial far field is then \(\mu_0m/(2\pi r^3)\). Expanding the vector-potential kernel for a general localised steady source explains the same dependence: the term proportional to \(\int\mathbf J\,dV\) vanishes for a closed current. Indeed, applying the divergence theorem to \(x_i\mathbf J\) gives \(\int J_i\,dV=0\) when currents vanish at the outer boundary. The antisymmetric part of the next moment produces

\[\mathbf m=\tfrac12\int\mathbf r'\times\mathbf J\,d^3r',\]
\[\mathbf A_{\rm dip}=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\frac{\mathbf m\times\hat{\mathbf r}}{r^2}.\]

For a planar loop the line-integral moment reduces to current times signed area. A detector far away therefore has difficulty distinguishing small loops with the same moment but different shapes. Closer readings, which retain the higher moments, are needed.

Find a pair of coils with a flat central field

Change the separation of two identical current loops. Each curve is divided by its own central field so that the graph tests uniformity. At Helmholtz spacing, a probe one quarter-radius off centre reads about 0.42% below the centre.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Check which coil measurement identifies the source

Test the dipole approximation at a specified distance

At \(z=3a\), how large is the error if the exact loop field is replaced by its axial dipole formula? Express it relative to the exact result.

Work through the solution

The approximation-to-exact ratio is \([1+(a/z)^2]^{3/2}=(10/9)^{3/2}=1.171\). The distant-source expression overpredicts by about 17.1%. At \(z=10a\) the excess falls to 1.50%. A distance of a few radii may be insufficient for precision work.

Choose a coil polarity for a gradient probe

The second coil of a symmetric pair is reversed. Predict the midpoint field and identify which derivative can now be nonzero there.

Work through the solution

The two equal axial fields cancel at the midpoint. With opposite currents the sum is an odd function, allowing a linear gradient. For a pair \(b(z-d/2)-b(z+d/2)\), its central derivative is \(-2b'(d/2)\). With \(d>0\), \(b'(d/2)<0\), so this wiring gives a positive axial gradient. Checking polarity distinguishes this arrangement from the uniform-field pair.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §§5.2–5.4, current sources and vector potential
  • Fließbach, Magnetischer Dipol

Chapter 18 · How microscopic dipoles produce macroscopic charge

Derive the volume and surface charges of a polarised body, determine the fields in layers and calculate which electrical work a capacitance measurement includes.

Replace a dense collection of dipoles by a smooth field

A small neutral molecule can have positive and negative charge centres separated by a displacement. Its dipole moment \(\mathbf p\) points from negative to positive. A volume containing many molecules but small compared with the macroscopic variation defines a dipole moment per volume \(\mathbf P\), in C/m². For observation points outside the body the sum of dipole potentials becomes

\[V(\mathbf r)=\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\int_\Omega\mathbf P(\mathbf r')\cdot\nabla'\frac1{|\mathbf r-\mathbf r'|}\,d^3r'.\]

The prime matters: differentiation with respect to source position has the opposite sign from differentiation with respect to observation position. Apply the product rule to \(\nabla'\cdot(\mathbf P/R)\) and integrate the divergence over the body. The result has exactly the Coulomb form for a volume distribution \(\rho_b=-\nabla\cdot\mathbf P\) and a surface distribution \(\sigma_b=\mathbf P\cdot\mathbf n\). Together the two carry zero total charge for a bounded polarised neutral object. The volume and surface terms can each be nonzero.

Worked slab with a polarisation gradient

In \(0<z<d\), prescribe \(P_z=P_0z/d\) over a broad slab. The bound volume charge is uniform at \(-P_0/d\). The lower face carries zero and the upper face \(+P_0\). Per unit area the negative volume contribution is \(-P_0\), cancelling the positive face. In an isolated large-plane approximation with no applied background, \(D_z=0\) outside and inside. Thus \(E_z=-P_0 z/(\epsilon_0 d)\) in the slab. Voltage rises from bottom to top by \(P_0d/(2\epsilon_0)\). With \(P_0=10\) nC/m² and \(d=2.0\) mm the rise is 1.13 V. Uniformly polarised material is the special case in which only the two faces carry charge.

Separate what an external circuit supplies

Gauss’s law says \(\epsilon_0\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\rho_{\rm free}+\rho_b\). Substituting the polarisation divergence and collecting terms defines \(\mathbf D=\epsilon_0\mathbf E+\mathbf P\) with \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf D=\rho_{\rm free}\). A small pillbox then gives the jump in the normal component of \(\mathbf D\) as the free surface density. A narrow electrostatic circulation loop gives continuity of tangential \(\mathbf E\). These two statements specify different components. A spatially varying material can make \(\nabla\times\mathbf D\ne0\) even when \(\nabla\times\mathbf E=0\).

For a reversible linear isotropic dielectric, measurements can supply \(\mathbf P=\epsilon_0\chi_e\mathbf E\). Then \(\epsilon=\epsilon_0(1+\chi_e)\) converts between \(\mathbf D\) and \(\mathbf E\). Permanent polarisation and history-dependent dielectrics require additional constitutive information. The prescribed-gradient example deliberately specified \(\mathbf P\) directly.

Turn boundary conditions into a measurable capacitance
