Place two clean dielectric layers of thicknesses \(d_1,d_2\) between broad electrodes. There is no free sheet at their interface. Both layers therefore have the same normal \(D=Q/A\) away from edges. Each voltage drop is \(D d_i/\epsilon_i\). Adding the drops gives

\[\frac CA=\frac{1}{d_1/\epsilon_1+d_2/\epsilon_2},\]
\[\frac{V_1}{V_2}=\frac{d_1/\epsilon_1}{d_2/\epsilon_2}.\]

Take one millimetre of relative permittivity 2 and one millimetre of relative permittivity 8. With 10 V applied, the first layer takes 8 V and the second 2 V. Their fields are 8 and 2 kV/m. The free plate density is \(1.42\times10^{-7}\) C/m². A simple arithmetic mean of the permittivities would give a wrong voltage split. The layers share normal displacement in this orientation. For strips lying side by side across the electrode face, the voltage is common and their capacitances add in parallel.

Track the source that holds the voltage

At fixed geometry, adding a small plate charge \(dQ\) requires reversible work \(V\,dQ\). With constant linear capacitance, integrating from zero to \(Q\) gives \(U=Q^2/(2C)\). In the layered capacitor this equals \((A/2)\sum_i d_i E_iD_i\). Changing a layer while \(Q\) stays fixed changes the mechanical work by the corresponding change in \(U\). Holding voltage fixed connects a source that supplies or removes charge. If insertion at fixed \(V\) increases capacitance by \(dC\), source work is \(V\,dQ=V^2dC\); the field stores only \(V^2dC/2\). The other half can appear as mechanical work by the electric force. Specifying the electrical control variable resolves an apparent sign ambiguity in capacitor force calculations.

Move a dielectric interface between voltage-controlled plates

The pair has a total thickness of 2 mm and an applied voltage of 10 V. The first dielectric has relative permittivity 2. Equal layer thicknesses and a second permittivity of 8 give slopes of 8 and 2 V/mm. Which physical charge would be missed by forcing those slopes to agree?

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Infer a hidden material property

Recover a permittivity from a voltage tap

Two layers of thickness 0.80 and 1.20 mm share 9.0 V. A tap shows a 3.0 V drop across the first layer of relative permittivity 4. Find the second relative permittivity.

Work through the solution

Equal normal displacement gives \(\epsilon_1 V_1/d_1=\epsilon_2 V_2/d_2\). The second drop is 6.0 V, hence \(\epsilon_{r2}=4(3.0/0.80)(1.20/6.0)=3.0\). A free interfacial sheet would supply a jump in \(D\) and invalidate this inversion.

Predict which bound charge a probe detects at the internal interface

In the equal-thickness, 10 V example choose the field direction positive from layer 1 to layer 2. Find the combined bound interface density and check against the field jump.

Work through the solution

With \(E_1=8000\), \(E_2=2000\) V/m, \(P_1=\epsilon_0 E_1\) and \(P_2=7\epsilon_0 E_2\). The top face of layer 1 and bottom face of layer 2 together carry \(P_1-P_2=-6000\epsilon_0=-53.1\) nC/m². This agrees with \(\epsilon_0(E_2-E_1)\) from Gauss’s law for total charge.

Practicum · locate free and bound charge from a buried voltage tap

Two broad metal electrodes at \(z=0\) and \(z=d_1+d_2\) are held at 10.0 V and 0 V. Between them lie linear isotropic dielectrics, first \(d_1=1.00\) mm with \(\epsilon_{r1}=2.00\), then \(d_2=2.00\) mm with \(\epsilon_{r2}=6.00\). A very thin immobile sheet of free charge may be trapped at their interface. An ideal nonperturbing probe there reads 4.00 V. Neglect fringing. On paper, sketch a candidate potential and label the electric field, displacement, and source of each surface charge. Can all of your labels fit the three voltage readings simultaneously?

Reconstruct the source from field jumps

Determine both field vectors, both displacement vectors, the free charge sheet and the bound interface charge. State the direction associated with a positive sheet density.

Follow the calculation and check the result

Set the interface normal from layer 1 to layer 2 along \(+\hat{\mathbf z}\). The potentials in the two charge-free interiors must be affine because \(\partial_z(\epsilon\partial_zV)=0\) there. The slopes are \(-6.00\) V/mm and \(-2.00\) V/mm. Therefore \(E_1=6000\) V/m, \(E_2=2000\) V/m and both point along the normal. Their displacements agree: \(D_1=2\epsilon_0(6000)=D_2=6\epsilon_0(2000)=106.25\) nC/m². Gauss's law for free charge gives \(\sigma_f=D_2-D_1=0\).

Compute the polarisation separately. \(P_1=\epsilon_0(6000)\) and \(P_2=5\epsilon_0(2000)\). Facing surfaces contribute \(+P_1\) and \(-P_2\), so \(\sigma_b=P_1-P_2=-35.42\) nC/m². A vacuum pillbox that counts all charge gives \(\epsilon_0(E_2-E_1)=-35.42\) nC/m², the same result by a different route. The signed density indicates charge per area, while the oriented normal fixes the sign in each jump equation.

Change one reading without changing either electrode

After a treatment, the tap reads 5.00 V with both plate voltages unchanged. A colleague assumes that equal voltage drops imply equal charge on facing electrodes. Test the inference, find the trapped charge, and close a global charge budget.

Follow the calculation and check the result

Now \(E_1=5000\) V/m and \(E_2=2500\) V/m because the equal voltage drops occur across different thicknesses. So \(D_1=88.54\) nC/m², \(D_2=132.81\) nC/m² and the trapped free sheet is \(+44.27\) nC/m². The conductor face charges are \(+88.54\) nC/m² on the low-\(z\) plate and \(-132.81\) nC/m² on the high-\(z\) plate. Added to the sheet they sum to zero, consistent with zero exterior displacement for the ideal screened setup.

At the internal interface the bound charge is \(P_1-P_2=\epsilon_0(5000-12500)=-66.41\) nC/m². Total charge there is \(-22.14\) nC/m² even though the trapped free sheet is positive. Check it using \(\epsilon_0(E_2-E_1)=-22.14\) nC/m². An instrument sensitive only to the electric-field jump would give the wrong sign if its reading were interpreted as free charge.

Test what the measurements can actually resolve

Suppose the second tap reading has uncertainty ±0.05 V and the other inputs are known exactly. Propagate the uncertainty to the inferred free surface charge, then propose a null experiment.

Follow the calculation and check the result

With \(V_m\) the tap reading, \(\sigma_f=\epsilon_0[\epsilon_{r2}V_m/d_2-\epsilon_{r1}(10-V_m)/d_1]\). Its slope is \(\epsilon_0(3000+2000)\) per volt in SI units, namely 44.27 nC/m² per volt. The inferred sheet is \(44.27\pm2.21\) nC/m². The uncertainty in the same tap is correlated between both drops, so treating the two inferred fields as independent would give a different uncertainty.

Remove the trapped charge and repeat with both electrodes at 0 V. The unique solution has zero field, and a residual tap voltage would indicate an offset or a violation of the material assumptions. A stronger differential test keeps the sheet and changes the applied voltage: the inferred jump in displacement should remain fixed while the two plate charges adjust. In a numerical boundary-value calculation, compare the reconstructed fluxes on either side of the interface and the integrated free charge, using the physical face permittivity.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §§4.2–4.4, bound charge, displacement and energy
  • Tong, Electromagnetism in Matter, polarisation and displacement

Chapter 19 · How a magnet produces an exterior field

Translate microscopic loop moments into volume and surface currents. Derive a shape-dependent internal field and learn which magnetic response a coil measurement can recover.

Add the fields of tiny current loops

A small current loop produces a dipole vector potential \(\mu_0\mathbf m\times\hat{\mathbf R}/(4\pi R^2)\) far from that loop. Coarse-grain a material into a dipole moment per volume \(\mathbf M\), in A/m. With \(\nabla'(1/R)=\hat{\mathbf R}/R^2\), the potential becomes an integral of \(\mathbf M\times\nabla'(1/R)\). Apply the curl product rule to \(\nabla'\times(\mathbf M/R)\), then integrate the curl over the body. For outward normal \(\mathbf n\) this gives

\[\mathbf A(\mathbf r)=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\left[\int_\Omega\frac{\nabla'\times\mathbf M}{R}\,d^3r'+\oint_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\mathbf M\times\mathbf n}{R}\,dS'\right].\]

Comparison with the vector potential of conduction currents identifies \(\mathbf J_b=\nabla\times\mathbf M\) and \(\mathbf K_b=\mathbf M\times\mathbf n\). These currents are a representation of the material dipoles. Neighbouring equal loop currents cancel where they share a boundary. At the outer surface a current remains unpaired. The curl measures how cancellation fails within a nonuniform specimen.

Worked magnetised cylinder

Let a very long cylinder of radius \(a\) have uniform axial \(M_0\hat{\mathbf z}\). Its bound volume current vanishes. Its side carries \(K_\phi=M_0\), and its flat end faces carry none in this model. The remaining current acts like a long solenoid. The internal field far from the ends is \(B_z=\mu_0 M_0\), while the exterior field tends to zero in the infinite-length limit. With \(M_0=4.0\times10^4\) A/m this gives 50.3 mT. A finite specimen has a return field near and beyond its ends; the far field of a finite magnet must eventually be dipolar.

What a loop linking a winding actually counts

Subtracting \(\mathbf J_b\) from magnetostatic Ampère’s law gives \(\nabla\times\mathbf H=\mathbf J_{\rm free}\) with \(\mathbf H=\mathbf B/\mu_0-\mathbf M\). A rectangular loop across an interface yields \(\mathbf n\times(\mathbf H_2-\mathbf H_1)=\mathbf K_{\rm free}\), while \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\) guarantees continuous normal \(B\). In a region free of conduction currents and with suitable topology one can introduce a magnetic scalar potential \(\mathbf H=-\nabla\phi_m\). Its divergence follows from \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf H=-\nabla\cdot\mathbf M\). This alternative calculation uses the same magnetisation as the current description. Both predict identical \(\mathbf B\).

Derive the demagnetising field of a sphere

Put a sphere of radius \(a\) with fixed uniform \(\mathbf M=M\hat{\mathbf z}\) in vacuum and assume no applied field. Inside and outside, \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf M=0\). The angular structure at the surface is \(M\cos\theta\), so use \(\phi_i=C r\cos\theta\) and \(\phi_o=C a^3\cos\theta/r^2\). They are harmonic, regular at the origin or at infinity, and continuous at \(a\); continuity ensures equal tangential \(H\). At the surface their outward fields are \(H_{i,r}=-C\cos\theta\) and \(H_{o,r}=2C\cos\theta\). With normal \(B\) continuous, \(2C=-C+M\), hence

\[\mathbf H_i=-\tfrac13\mathbf M,\]
\[\mathbf B_i=\tfrac23\mu_0\mathbf M .\]

The factor \(1/3\) encodes specimen shape. A very long axial cylinder with the same magnetisation has almost zero internal \(H\) far from its ends. The sphere’s exterior scalar potential corresponds to a dipole moment equal to magnetisation times volume.

Worked susceptibility seen from outside

For a reversible linear sphere with \(M=\chi H_i\) in a uniform applied field \(H_0\), superposition adds the applied field and the demagnetising contribution: \(H_i=H_0-M/3\). Solving yields \(M/H_0=\chi/(1+\chi/3)\). If a moment measurement gives \(M/H_0=1.50\), the intrinsic susceptibility is 3.0. As \(\chi\) grows, \(M/H_0\) approaches 3, and the inversion becomes very sensitive to moment uncertainty. Using \(B=\mu H\) without specifying where the field is measured can conceal this shape dependence.

Separate reversible energy from cycle loss

When ferromagnetic domains rearrange, \(B\) can depend on the prior excursion of \(H\). A single constant susceptibility then cannot describe an entire cycle. In a slow, approximately uniform closed magnetic circuit the source supplies incremental energy density \(H\,dB\). Returning to the initial magnetic state leaves the signed cycle integral \(\oint H\,dB\); its dissipated energy per volume is the positive area for the driven loop orientation. A measured loop area of 180 J/m³ at 50 cycles/s would dissipate 9.0 kW/m³, or 0.090 W in \(10\) cm³. Eddy-current loss can also enlarge a measured dynamic loop, so a rate sweep helps interpret that result.

Infer material response from an external field

The sphere reference curve gives an apparent susceptibility of 2.25 at intrinsic susceptibility 9. The adjustable ellipsoid starts at N = 0.33, giving 2.27. Try smaller demagnetising factors for elongated ellipsoids magnetised along their long axes. Determine when an apparent response would severely underestimate the intrinsic one.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Test a surface-current picture

What current remains if magnetisation falls towards the rim?

Take a long cylinder with \(M_z=M_0(1-s^2/a^2)\) inside. Find the azimuthal bound currents and the field on its axis.

Work through the solution

Cylindrical curl gives \(J_{b,\phi}=-dM_z/ds=2M_0s/a^2\). At \(s=a\) the surface current is zero. Regard a shell \(ds\) as a small solenoidal sheet \(K=J_{b,\phi}ds\). At the axis all shells contribute, giving \(B_z(0)=\mu_0\int_0^a J_{b,\phi}\,ds=\mu_0M_0\). Far from the ends the radial profile inside is \(B_z(s)=\mu_0M_z(s)\).

Invert a nearly saturated shape response

If \(M/H_0=2.70\) for a linear sphere, what is \(\chi\)? Estimate how an absolute uncertainty of 0.03 in the measured ratio propagates.

Work through the solution

Writing \(r=M/H_0\), one finds \(\chi=r/(1-r/3)=27\). Its derivative is \((1-r/3)^{-2}=100\), so the local uncertainty is about 3. Distinguishing two high susceptibilities requires precise specimen geometry and much better relative accuracy than the measured moment alone suggests.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §§6.1–6.4, magnetisation, bound currents and H
  • Tong, Electromagnetism in Matter, magnetic fields in matter

Chapter 20 · Where momentum waits between source and receiver

Derive the electromagnetic momentum balance, trace light pressure through a finite pulse, and account for the angular impulse when a magnetic flux decays.

Give a region a mechanical and a field account

Charges respond to Lorentz force density \(\mathbf f=\rho\mathbf E+\mathbf J\times\mathbf B\). To predict the total force on enclosed matter, one can measure the fields on a surrounding vacuum surface. First eliminate the sources. In vacuum Gauss’s law gives \(\rho=\epsilon_0\nabla\cdot\mathbf E\), and the Ampère–Maxwell law gives \(\mathbf J=(\nabla\times\mathbf B)/\mu_0-\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E\). Introduce \(\mathbf g=\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\mathbf B=\mathbf S/c^2\). Its time derivative supplies both \(\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E\times\mathbf B\) and \(\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\partial_t\mathbf B\). The first cancels the displacement-current term in the force; Faraday’s law rewrites the second.

For example, the electric terms now read \(\epsilon_0[\mathbf E(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E)-\mathbf E\times(\nabla\times\mathbf E)]\). Component \(i\) is \(\partial_j[\epsilon_0(E_iE_j-\delta_{ij}E^2/2)]\), with repeated Cartesian indices summed. The magnetic terms have the same form because \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). Integration gives

\[T_{ij}=\epsilon_0(E_iE_j-\tfrac12 E^2\delta_{ij})+\frac1{\mu_0}(B_iB_j-\tfrac12 B^2\delta_{ij}),\]
\[\mathbf F_{\rm matter}=\oint T\mathbf n\,dA-\frac d{dt}\int\mathbf g\,dV .\]

The normal points out of the chosen volume. Positive \(T_{xx}\) denotes tension along \(x\) in this convention. For a wave travelling in \(+x\) with transverse fields, \(T_{xx}=-u\), where \(u\) is energy density. On an inlet whose outward normal is \(-\hat{\mathbf x}\), the normal traction therefore points in \(+x\).

Measure the time before light reaches a detector

A finite plane pulse in vacuum has \(|\mathbf S|=cu\). Its total momentum is \(U/c\) along the propagation direction. Surround a freely absorbing target and a length of initially empty space upstream with a vacuum control surface. While a pulse enters but has yet to reach the target, the inlet stress integral is nonzero. The electromagnetic momentum inside grows at exactly the same rate. The minus time derivative in the total force cancels the inlet contribution and the target has zero force during this interval.

Worked optical impulse

A 0.60 mJ pulse is fully absorbed after crossing the inlet. Initially and finally the region contains no pulse. Integrating the balance over that full time removes the stored-field term. The target gains \(0.60\times10^{-3}/c=2.00\times10^{-12}\) N s. For ideal normal reflection the exiting pulse carries the opposite momentum, giving twice that impulse. For a transparent window with parallel faces at normal incidence the outgoing pulse may eventually have the same vacuum momentum as the incoming pulse, while the two faces still experience transient forces.

Convert distributed field momentum into a turning impulse

Now take a long coaxial arrangement: an inner narrow cylindrical shell of radius \(a\) carries free charge \(+\lambda\) per length; an outer shell of radius \(b\) carries \(-\lambda\). There is a uniform axial magnetic field \(B_0\) over the annular gap, with end effects negligible. For a definite preparation take an ideal long solenoid producing uniform field throughout \(r<b\), and confine the initial electric field to the gap. Between shells \(E_r=\lambda/(2\pi\epsilon_0 r)\). The field momentum density points towards negative azimuth because \(\hat{\mathbf r}\times\hat{\mathbf z}=-\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}\).

\[g_\phi=-\lambda B_0/(2\pi r),\]
\[L_{{\rm field},z}'=\int_a^b r g_\phi\,2\pi r\,dr=-\tfrac12\lambda B_0(b^2-a^2).\]

The prime denotes angular momentum per length. While the coil current decays slowly, Faraday’s law gives \(2\pi r E_\phi=-\pi r^2\dot B\) for radii up to \(b\). The torque on each shell per length is \(r\lambda_{\rm shell}E_\phi(r)\). Integrating from \(B_0\) to zero yields \(+\lambda B_0a^2/2\) on the inner shell and \(-\lambda B_0b^2/2\) on the outer. Their sum reproduces the initial field angular momentum. Clamps would receive those impulses if the shells were held fixed.

Worked sign and magnitude

With \(\lambda=20\) nC/m, \(B_0=0.25\) T, \(a=10\) mm and \(b=30\) mm, the initial \(L'_z=-2.0\times10^{-12}\) kg m/s. That is angular momentum per metre of apparatus. The outer shell receives nine times the inner impulse magnitude and rotates in the opposite sense. Torque from the induced azimuthal electric field supplies this transfer even though both shells started at rest.

Transfer angular momentum from a field into two shells

The inner radius is 10 mm and its line charge is +20 nC/m. For a 30 mm outer radius and an initial field of 0.25 T, the final shell impulses are +0.25 and −2.25 in units of 10⁻¹² kg m/s. Trace the four accounts during switch-off.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Choose a control surface for momentum

Find a pressure when transmission and reflection coexist

A normal beam of intensity 300 W/m² reaches a free-standing film in vacuum. Reflected power fraction is 0.20 and transmitted fraction is 0.50. Find the time-averaged pressure and identify the momentum carried away.

Work through the solution

With incident direction positive, outgoing momentum per area and time is \((T-R)I/c\). The deposited amount is \((1-T+R)I/c=0.70(300)/c=0.700\,\mu{\rm Pa}\). The absorbed part contributes \(0.30I/c\) and reflection adds \(0.40I/c\). The result assumes forward and backward beams remain normal to the film.

Reverse just the charges

In the coaxial example reverse both shell charges while keeping the axial field and current decay unchanged. Which quantities reverse?

Work through the solution

The radial field, stored azimuthal momentum and angular momentum reverse. Faraday’s induced \(E_\phi\) follows the same history because the flux decay is unchanged. Each electric force and angular impulse reverses since the charge signs reverse. This independent sign argument checks the cross product in the original momentum density.

Practicum · audit a pulse bouncing inside a partly absorbing cavity

A shutter has just prepared a short \(U_0=1.20\) mJ pulse travelling to the right in an otherwise empty one-dimensional cavity. Start the accounting after preparation. The left mirror reflects perfectly. At every encounter with the right mirror 60% of incident pulse energy is reflected, 30% passes into vacuum on the right, and 10% is absorbed. Pulse duration is short compared with the flight time across the cavity. Mirrors are mounted on massive force probes; neglect their displacement and delay any re-emission of heat. The challenge is to reconcile two large, oppositely signed readings with a small net impulse.

Audit one encounter and decide which surface terms matter

For energy \(U\) just before an encounter with the right mirror, calculate its mechanical impulse. Then find the impulse when the reflected packet hits the left mirror. Which momentum is still present between those events?

Follow the calculation and check the result

The incoming momentum is \(+U/c\). The two outgoing packets carry \(+0.30U/c\) and \(-0.60U/c\). Momentum balance therefore assigns the right mirror \(+(1-0.30+0.60)U/c=+1.30U/c\). This includes reversing the reflected packet and stopping the absorbed packet. The left mirror receives \(-2(0.60U)/c=-1.20U/c\) at the next collision. Between those events the cavity still contains \(-0.60U/c\) of field momentum. The complete system also contains the \(+0.30U/c\) transmitted packet outside the cavity.

Stop the movie at a specified time

Freeze the experiment just after the third right-mirror encounter and before the next left-mirror encounter. Find the remaining energy, radiated output, deposited heat, both probe impulses, and total field momentum.

Follow the calculation and check the result

Three incident energies in units of \(U_0\) form the sequence \(1,0.6,0.36\). Their sum is 1.96. The output energy is \(0.30(1.96)U_0=0.588U_0\), heat is \(0.196U_0\), and \(0.216U_0\) remains. The three fractions sum to unity. Right-probe impulse is \(+1.30(1.96)U_0/c=+2.548U_0/c\). Only two left reflections have occurred, so the left probe reads \(-2(0.60+0.36)U_0/c=-1.920U_0/c\). Their sum is \(+0.628U_0/c\).

The escaped packets carry \(+0.588U_0/c\), but the packet still inside carries \(-0.216U_0/c\). Including these two contributions gives measured plus field momentum \((0.628+0.588-0.216)U_0/c=U_0/c\), the initial value. Counting a third left-hand reflection at this time would violate the preselected event boundary. Since \(U_0/c=4.00\times10^{-12}\) N s, the probe readings are approximately \(+10.2\) and \(-7.68\) pN s.

Sum the full history in two independent ways

Continue until no macroscopic pulse remains. Find the impulse on each mirror, then derive their combined reading directly from exterior measurements without examining any reflection.

Follow the calculation and check the result

The geometric series of right hits sums to \(U_0/(1-0.6)=2.5U_0\). The right impulse is \(3.25U_0/c\). Summing every left hit gives \(-2(0.60)U_0/[(1-0.60)c]=-3.00U_0/c\). Thus the pair receives \(+0.25U_0/c\). All transmitted packets travel right and together carry \(0.30U_0/(1-0.60)=0.75U_0\). An external vacuum control surface therefore gives \((1-0.75)U_0/c\) immediately. The absorbed \(0.25U_0\) accounts for the energy stored as heat.

A detector without enough time resolution can recover the integrated exterior momentum even though it cannot resolve the alternating internal forces. Conversely, two large internal probe signals can coexist with a modest net exterior impulse. In a numerical time march record separate signed transfers and the energy still in flight after each boundary update, then vary the stopping time. A budget that closes only after the very last encounter cannot diagnose an intermediate sign mistake.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §§8.2.2–8.2.4, stress, momentum and angular momentum
  • Tong, Electromagnetism and Relativity, energy-momentum and angular momentum

Chapter 21 · What an interface does to phase, field and power

Match fields at an optical surface, derive reflection for each polarisation and follow energy when the refracted wave becomes evanescent.

Read refraction from the clock shared by two media

A plane wave strikes a flat interface \(z=0\) between transparent isotropic dielectrics. Their permeabilities are \(\mu_0\); the refractive indices \(n_1,n_2\) are real at the chosen frequency. Write every field as the real part of a complex amplitude times \(e^{i(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf r-\omega t)}\). Tangential electric and magnetic fields must match at every point \(x\) and every time \(t\) on the surface. This is impossible for independent exponential factors except where their temporal frequencies and tangential wave numbers agree. Therefore \(\omega_i=\omega_r=\omega_t\), \(\theta_r=\theta_i\), and

\[k_{\parallel}=\frac{\omega}{c}n_1\sin\theta_i=\frac{\omega}{c}n_2\sin\theta_t .\]

The interface fixes phase matching before it fixes amplitudes. Which amplitudes match depends on polarisation. Choose the \(xz\)-plane of incidence so that s polarisation has electric field along \(y\) and p polarisation has magnetic field along \(y\).

Use one amplitude convention that works for both polarisations

Use the tangential electric component for all amplitudes. Let \(Y\) be the ratio of the tangential magnetic magnitude to that electric component for a forward wave, with the corresponding sign reversing for a backward wave. From \(\mathbf H=(n/Z_0)\hat{\mathbf k}\times\mathbf E\), \(Z_0=\sqrt{\mu_0/\epsilon_0}\), the forward admittances are \(Y_s=n\cos\theta/Z_0\) and \(Y_p=n/(Z_0\cos\theta)\). The factors differ because an oblique p field has only a fraction \(\cos\theta\) of its magnitude tangential to the surface.

\[E_i+E_r=E_t,\]
\[Y_1(E_i-E_r)=Y_2E_t,\]
\[r=\frac{Y_1-Y_2}{Y_1+Y_2},\]
\[t=\frac{2Y_1}{Y_1+Y_2}.\]

For propagating waves the normal component of mean energy flux is \(Y|E_\parallel|^2/2\). Hence \(R=|r|^2\) and \(T=(Y_2/Y_1)|t|^2\). In a lossless interface \(R+T=1\). Some conventions reverse the reflected p-polarisation unit vector and therefore reverse the sign called \(r_p\). Comparing tangential components explicitly removes that ambiguity.

Worked oblique reading

For air to glass with \(n_1=1.0\), \(n_2=1.5\) at \(45^\circ\), Snell’s law gives \(\theta_t=28.13^\circ\). Dropping the common factor \(1/Z_0\), the s admittances are 0.707 and 1.323. Their reflection amplitude is \(-0.303\), giving 9.20% reflected power. The p admittances are 1.414 and 1.701. Their tangential reflection amplitude is \(-0.0920\), giving 0.847%. Giving only one reflectance for an unpolarised beam would hide this nearly elevenfold difference. At normal incidence the two admittances coincide for each medium, providing a sign and magnitude check.

Find the angle that cancels one reflected component

An interface is reflection-free for p polarisation when \(n_1/\cos\theta_i=n_2/\cos\theta_t\). Combine this condition with Snell’s law for distinct indices to get \(\theta_i+\theta_t=\pi/2\) and \(\tan\theta_B=n_2/n_1\). At the air–glass interface this angle is \(56.31^\circ\). A small index change shifts that angular minimum, making a polarised measurement a way to infer index. Absorption introduces a complex admittance and generally turns the exact zero into a finite minimum.

Keep phase matching beyond the critical angle

For incidence from large to small index, \(k_\parallel\) can exceed the magnitude \(n_2\omega/c\) allowed for medium 2. Solve the dispersion relation there for the normal wave number:

\[k_{z,2}^2=(n_2\omega/c)^2-k_\parallel^2,\]
\[k_{z,2}=i\kappa,\]
\[\kappa=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_0}\sqrt{n_1^2\sin^2\theta_i-n_2^2}.\]

The positive imaginary branch gives decay into \(z>0\). For a single such interface \(Y_2\) is imaginary, so \(|r|=1\); a reactive magnetic-to-electric ratio means zero average normal flux there. A field still extends into the second medium and can carry energy parallel to the surface. Another material brought within a decay length changes the boundary problem and can enable transmission.

Worked length-scale distinction

For \(n_1=1.5,n_2=1.0,\theta_i=50^\circ,\lambda_0=600\) nm, the critical angle is \(41.81^\circ\). The amplitude length \(1/\kappa\) is 169 nm. Intensity follows squared field and therefore falls by \(e\) over 84.3 nm. A detector using intensity must state which length it reports. Close to the critical angle the penetration length becomes large; finite beams then sample a range of angles around the ideal plane-wave value.

Find a polarisation zero and the onset of total reflection

Air into glass of index 1.5 gives a p-polarised reflection zero at 56.3°. Reverse the indices, predict the critical angle, then inspect what happens to each reflected power fraction as incidence becomes more oblique.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Read an amplitude measurement correctly

How can a transmitted field exceed the incident amplitude?

At normal incidence from index 1.8 to 1.2, calculate the reflected and transmitted tangential electric amplitudes and powers.

Work through the solution

Here \(r=(1.8-1.2)/(1.8+1.2)=0.20\), \(t=1.20\). Reflected power fraction is 0.04. The transmitted power fraction is \((1.2/1.8)(1.20)^2=0.96\). The reduced wave admittance converts the larger electric amplitude to a smaller power than the incident beam.

Find the angular acceptance of a total-reflection measurement

A liquid has unknown index \(n\). Light is incident from known glass of index 1.60, and total internal reflection first appears at \(57.0^\circ\pm0.2^\circ\). Infer \(n\) and a first-order uncertainty.

Work through the solution

The grazing transmitted wave obeys \(n=1.60\sin57.0^\circ=1.342\). Differentiation gives \(dn=1.60\cos57.0^\circ d\theta\), with \(d\theta\) in radians. The quoted angular interval corresponds to about 0.0030 in index. Index uncertainty of the glass would add another contribution.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §9.3, plane waves crossing dielectric interfaces
  • Tong, Electromagnetism in Matter, reflection and refraction

Chapter 22 · Why dispersion and absorption belong together

Obtain complex permittivity from a damped charge, relate its imaginary part to heating and distinguish wave crests, envelopes and a causal change in a signal.

Replace an instantaneous spring constant by a delayed response

A static dielectric constant is measured after the material has relaxed. At optical frequencies the bound charges have inertia and damping. Model a density \(N\) of independent bound charges of mass \(m\), charge \(q\), natural angular frequency \(\omega_0\) and damping rate \(\gamma>0\). A weak field gives a displacement satisfying \(m\ddot x+m\gamma\dot x+m\omega_0^2x=qE(t)\). The resulting polarisation is \(P=Nqx\). This model assumes small displacements and an isotropic response. A crystal with several resonances needs additional modes and oscillator strengths.

For a sinusoidal field with the convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\), differentiation multiplies a phasor by \(-i\omega\). Dividing the equation by its dynamic stiffness gives

\[\chi(\omega)=\frac{P}{\epsilon_0E}=\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega_0^2-\omega^2-i\gamma\omega},\]
\[\omega_p^2=\frac{Nq^2}{m\epsilon_0}.\]

Rationalise the denominator using \(A=\omega_0^2-\omega^2,\ B=\gamma\omega\). Then \(\chi'=\omega_p^2A/(A^2+B^2)\) and \(\chi''=\omega_p^2B/(A^2+B^2)\). The real part changes sign across the resonance. The imaginary part stays positive at positive frequencies with our convention. In the low-frequency limit the static spring law reappears, \(\chi(0)=\omega_p^2/\omega_0^2\).

Decide which part of the response dissipates work

The field delivers power density \(E\dot P\) to the bound charges. The polarisation current phasor is \(-i\omega\epsilon_0\chi E\). For peak-amplitude phasors the cycle average of two real oscillating quantities is half the real part of one phasor times the complex conjugate of the other. Thus

\[\langle E\dot P\rangle=\tfrac12\omega\epsilon_0\chi''|E|^2 .\]

This result also follows by multiplying the oscillator equation by \(N\dot x\). The kinetic and spring energy changes integrate to zero over one steady period, leaving \(Nm\gamma\langle\dot x^2\rangle\) as heat. Positive loss therefore has a mechanical interpretation independent of any phasor notation. Changing to an \(e^{+i\omega t}\) convention reverses the sign attached to the imaginary part, while the heat remains positive.

Worked response at resonance

Take \(\omega_p^2=0.20\,\omega_0^2\) and \(\gamma=0.10\,\omega_0\). At \(\omega=\omega_0\), \(A=0\) and \(\chi=2i\). If \(\omega_0=2.0\times10^{12}\) rad/s and \(E=100\) V/m peak, the absorbed power density is \(1.77\times10^5\) W/m³. This is local power where the field has that amplitude. Applying it throughout a thick absorbing slab would neglect attenuation of the field with depth.

Recover the actual lengths and speeds in a material

For relative permeability one and a plane wave \(e^{ikz-i\omega t}\), \(k=(\omega/c)\sqrt{1+\chi}\). Choose \(n=n'+i\kappa\) with \(\kappa\ge0\) for a passive medium. The field amplitude decays as \(e^{-\omega\kappa z/c}\) and intensity as \(e^{-2\omega\kappa z/c}\). At the resonance of the previous example, \(1+\chi=1+2i\). Equating real and imaginary parts of the squared index gives

\[n'^2-\kappa^2=1,\]
\[2n'\kappa=2,\]
\[n'=\sqrt{\tfrac12(\sqrt5+1)},\]
\[\kappa=\sqrt{\tfrac12(\sqrt5-1)} .\]

Here \(n'=1.272,\ \kappa=0.786\). At the stated angular frequency the intensity attenuation length \(c/(2\omega\kappa)=95.3\,\mu{\rm m}\). A finite slab also reflects at its surfaces. Inferring bulk loss from transmitted power alone requires accounting for those reflections.

For a narrow packet, expand the real propagation constant around its carrier: \(k'(\omega)\simeq k'_c+(\omega-\omega_c)\,dk'/d\omega\), where \(k'\) here denotes the real part of \(k\). The carrier phase moves at \(\omega_c/k'_c\). If attenuation and curvature vary little across the packet bandwidth, the envelope delay over length \(L\) is \(L\,dk'/d\omega\). Strong frequency-selective loss can reshape the peak so much that tracking it no longer measures the arrival of new information.

Link the two parts of susceptibility through causality

A causal linear material obeys \(P(t)=\epsilon_0\int_0^\infty h(\tau)E(t-\tau)\,d\tau\). Its susceptibility for our convention is \(\int_0^\infty h(\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}d\tau\). For a decaying response this expression is analytic when \({\rm Im}\,\omega>0\). Closing a contour in that half plane relates real and imaginary components by principal-value integrals. For a response decaying at large frequency one useful form is

\[\chi'(\omega)=\frac2\pi\,{\cal P}\int_0^\infty\frac{\omega'\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'^2-\omega^2}\,d\omega'.\]

Thus a narrow absorption band contributes to index variation at frequencies where the local absorption is small. A measurement over a short spectral window cannot uniquely evaluate the integral without information about loss outside it. The damped-oscillator poles lie below the real axis, as the causal convention requires.

Change the damping of a bound charge

Track the in-phase and out-of-phase parts of one oscillator response. With strength 0.50 and damping 0.20 in the plotted units, the absorptive part at resonance is 2.50. Compare that point with the frequency of maximum absorption as damping increases.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Use attenuation and loss as independent checks

Infer an extinction coefficient

After correcting for both surfaces, a liquid transmits 25% of its incident intensity in 0.50 mm at vacuum wavelength 1.00 µm. Find \(\kappa\) and the field-amplitude ratio.

Work through the solution

With \(T=\exp(-4\pi\kappa L/\lambda_0)\), \(\kappa=-\lambda_0\ln(0.25)/(4\pi L)=2.21\times10^{-4}\). The amplitude ratio is \(\sqrt T=0.50\). Using the amplitude decay law on intensity would overestimate \(\kappa\) by a factor of two.

Recover the conductivity limit

Set \(\omega_0=0\) in the charge model and express the response as \(J=\sigma E\). Find the low-frequency real conductivity.

Work through the solution

Using \(J=-i\omega P\) yields \(\sigma(\omega)=\epsilon_0\omega_p^2/(\gamma-i\omega)\). At \(\omega\ll\gamma\), \(\sigma\simeq\epsilon_0\omega_p^2/\gamma=Nq^2/(m\gamma)\). The absence of a restoring force lets charges drift steadily instead of settling to a finite static polarisation.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §9.4, absorption and dispersion
  • Tong, Electromagnetism in Matter, dispersion, Kramers–Kronig and the Drude model

Chapter 23 · Potentials, gauge freedom and the arrival of a signal

When a source changes at noon, which part of its history can affect a detector at twelve o’clock and one nanosecond? The answer emerges from Maxwell’s equations once we specify both the source and the boundary condition in time.

Why introduce two potentials?

A magnetic field with zero divergence can, on a contractible region, be written as \(\mathbf B=\nabla\times\mathbf A\). Faraday's law then says that \(\mathbf E+\partial_t\mathbf A\) has zero curl. Define its scalar potential as \(-\nabla\phi\). These two definitions solve the homogeneous Maxwell equations identically. Two inhomogeneous equations still determine how charge and current generate the fields. The potentials contain more components than necessary. For any smooth function \(\chi\), put

\[\mathbf A'=\mathbf A+\nabla\chi,\]
\[\phi'=\phi-\partial_t\chi.\]

The curl of a gradient vanishes. The two extra terms in the electric field cancel because mixed derivatives commute. This freedom lets us choose which equations to solve while keeping every force measurement identical.

Uncoupling the field equations

Insert the potentials into Gauss's law. This gives \(\nabla^2\phi+\partial_t\nabla\cdot\mathbf A=-\rho/\epsilon_0\). Now substitute them in Ampère–Maxwell and use the curl-of-curl identity. Keeping the gradient terms together gives

\[(\nabla^2-c^{-2}\partial_t^2)\mathbf A=-\mu_0\mathbf J+\nabla(\nabla\cdot\mathbf A+c^{-2}\partial_t\phi).\]

Choose the Lorenz condition \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf A+c^{-2}\partial_t\phi=0\). A gauge transformation can achieve it by solving the scalar wave equation for \(\chi\) with the negative of the original condition as source. Both potentials then obey wave equations with the same propagation speed. Under this choice, charge continuity is essential. Applying the wave operator to the Lorenz condition returns \(-\mu_0(\nabla\cdot\mathbf J+\partial_t\rho)\), which vanishes for a conserved source. Residual gauge functions obey the homogeneous wave equation.

Constructing the retarded kernel

In a source-free spherical shell a radial scalar field \(\psi\) obeys \(\partial_r^2(r\psi)=c^{-2}\partial_t^2(r\psi)\). The one-dimensional equation has an outgoing branch \(r\psi=f(t-r/c)\) and an incoming branch \(g(t+r/c)\). An isolated source with no incident wave selects the outgoing branch. The response to an instantaneous impulse is supported at \(t=r/c\). Its spatial normalisation follows by integrating the static kernel over a tiny sphere, where \(-\nabla^2(1/4\pi r)=\delta^3(\mathbf r)\). The causal kernel is therefore \(\delta(t-r/c)/(4\pi r)\). Integrating the source over time removes the delta function:

\[\phi(\mathbf x,t)=\frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\int \frac{\rho(\mathbf x',t-R/c)}{R}\,d^3x',\]
\[\mathbf A(\mathbf x,t)=\frac{\mu_0}{4\pi}\int \frac{\mathbf J(\mathbf x',t-R/c)}{R}\,d^3x',\]
\[R=|\mathbf x-\mathbf x'|.\]

Every volume element contributes at its own source time. If an incident wave is already present, add its homogeneous solution. In Coulomb gauge the scalar potential instead satisfies an instantaneous Poisson equation. Its instantaneous contribution to \(-\nabla\phi\) combines with \(-\partial_t\mathbf A\) to give the same causal electric field.

Two calculations with potentials

A pulse recorded at two distances

Measure an isolated outgoing spherical wave component in a source-free exterior. At radius \(0.30\) m its field trace peaks at \(10.0\) ns. At \(1.80\) m its peak arrives \(1.50/c=5.00\) ns later. The peak amplitude is \(0.30/1.80=1/6\) of the first. Multiplying each trace by its radius removes spreading. Shifting the second trace back by \(5.00\) ns then superposes them. For an electromagnetic dipole these statements describe its outgoing transverse radiation term along the same ray. Reactive near-field terms have different radial dependences.

Two vector potentials for a uniform field

For a constant magnetic field \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\) one choice is \(\mathbf A=Bx\hat{\mathbf y}\), \(\phi=0\). Choose \(\chi=-Bxy/2\). Its gradient adds \(-By\hat{\mathbf x}/2-Bx\hat{\mathbf y}/2\), giving \(\mathbf A'=(-By\hat{\mathbf x}+Bx\hat{\mathbf y})/2\). Its curl has \(z\) component \(B/2-(-B/2)=B\). The line integrals around any closed loop match because the integral of the added gradient is zero.

Compare the arrival of a compact pulse at two radii

This source-free outgoing component has a finite start and finish. A 1.80 m detector records the front 5.00 ns after a 0.30 m detector and measures one sixth of its amplitude. Can changing the pulse duration change either observation?

Problem sheet · delays and a time-dependent gauge

A finite source

Two ends of a source are 12 cm apart along a detector's line of sight. Bound the difference in retarded source times. At what frequency does the corresponding maximum phase span reach one radian?

Work through the solution

The triangle inequality gives \(|R_1-R_2|\leq0.12\) m. Thus \(\Delta t\leq0.400\) ns. For a harmonic source the maximum phase difference is \(2\pi f\Delta t\). One radian requires \(f=398\) MHz approximately. Well below that frequency one common retarded time can be accurate for this part of the phase. A dipole approximation also requires a source small compared with the observation distance.

A gauge with a time derivative

Start with zero potentials. Set \(\chi=\alpha xt\), where \(\alpha\) is constant. Determine both transformed potentials and check the fields.

Work through the solution

The new potentials are \(\mathbf A'=\alpha t\hat{\mathbf x}\) and \(\phi'=-\alpha x\). Hence \(-\nabla\phi'=+\alpha\hat{\mathbf x}\) and \(-\partial_t\mathbf A'=-\alpha\hat{\mathbf x}\). Their sum and the curl of the spatially uniform vector potential are both zero. The example also satisfies the Lorenz condition, consistent with \(\chi\) solving the homogeneous wave equation.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§10.1–10.2 · potentials and retarded sources
  • Fließbach, chapter 17 · general solution of Maxwell’s equations
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, §5.1 · retarded potentials

Chapter 24 · A moving charge, its retarded position and its radiation

A moving source moves while its field travels. Finding its position on the past light cone supplies an extra factor in its potential and explains why an accelerated charge can carry energy to arbitrarily large distances.

Locate the emission event

Let the charge follow the prescribed path \(\mathbf z(s)\). At time \(t\) and detector position \(\mathbf x\), define \(R(s)=|\mathbf x-\mathbf z(s)|\) and \(\mathbf n=(\mathbf x-\mathbf z)/R\). The emission time \(s\) solves \(h(s)=s+R(s)/c-t=0\). Differentiate to obtain \(h'=1-\mathbf n\cdot\boldsymbol\beta=\kappa\), with \(\boldsymbol\beta=\dot{\mathbf z}/c\). For a subluminal charge \(\kappa>0\), so the root is unique when it exists. Two emissions a time \(ds\) apart arrive only \(\kappa ds\) apart. Looking forwards along a fast charge's path shortens this interval.

Insert \(\rho=q\delta^3(\mathbf x'-\mathbf z(s))\) into the spacetime integral for the retarded potential. Integrate over \(\mathbf x'\) first. The remaining integral is proportional to \(\int ds\,\delta(h(s))/R(s)\). Changing variable from \(s\) to \(h\) introduces \(1/\kappa\). With the current density \(q\dot{\mathbf z}\delta^3(\mathbf x'-\mathbf z)\), the same argument yields the vector potential:

\[\phi=\frac{q}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\left[\frac{1}{\kappa R}\right]_{\rm ret},\]
\[\mathbf A=\left[\frac{\mathbf v}{c^2}\right]_{\rm ret}\phi .\]

Simply replacing the position of a static charge with its retarded position misses the Jacobian. Both the geometry and the current vary when taking derivatives of these potentials.

Distinguish persistent and radiative parts

Implicit differentiation of \(h=0\) gives \(\partial_t s=1/\kappa\) and \(\nabla s=-\mathbf n/(c\kappa)\). Apply these identities in \(-\nabla\phi-\partial_t\mathbf A\). Derivatives of the velocity supply an acceleration term, while the remainder can be expressed using the retarded velocity and geometry:

\[\mathbf E=\frac{q}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\left[\frac{(1-\beta^2)(\mathbf n-\boldsymbol\beta)}{\kappa^3R^2}+\frac{\mathbf n\times[(\mathbf n-\boldsymbol\beta)\times\dot{\boldsymbol\beta}]}{c\kappa^3R}\right]_{\rm ret},\]
\[\mathbf B=\frac{\mathbf n\times\mathbf E}{c}.\]

A detector subtends a fixed solid angle with area proportional to \(R^2\). The square of the first field falls as \(R^{-4}\) and its flux through the detector vanishes at large radius. The second field is transverse and its square falls as \(R^{-2}\), so finite power travels to infinity. Uniform motion leaves only the first term.

Recover the nonrelativistic radiation law

For \(\beta\ll1\), \(\kappa\simeq1\). If \(\theta\) is measured from the acceleration, the radiative electric-field magnitude is \(q a\sin\theta/(4\pi\epsilon_0c^2R)\) in absolute value. Its magnetic partner has magnitude \(E/c\). Therefore the outward Poynting flux is \(\epsilon_0cE^2\), and integration over a distant sphere gives

\[\frac{dP}{d\Omega}=\frac{q^2a^2}{16\pi^2\epsilon_0c^3}\sin^2\theta,\]
\[P=\frac{q^2a^2}{6\pi\epsilon_0c^3}.\]

The angular factor integrates to \(2\pi\int_0^\pi\sin^3\theta\,d\theta=8\pi/3\). At finite speed with acceleration parallel to velocity, the vector triple product in the numerator has magnitude \((a/c)\sin\theta\). The flux per unit arrival time contains \(\kappa^{-6}\). Converting to power per unit emission time multiplies by \(dt/ds=\kappa\), leaving the angular dependence \(\sin^2\theta/(1-\beta\cos\theta)^5\). The distinction matters whenever emissions bunch up at the detector.

Reading charge motion from a signal

A moving source changes its pulse spacing

A charge moving at \(0.60c\) directly toward a distant observer emits two brief disturbances \(2.0\) ns apart in the lab frame. It travels \(0.36\) m between emissions. The second propagation time is shorter by \(1.2\) ns, leaving an arrival interval of \(0.80\) ns. Viewed from behind, the interval is \(3.2\) ns. This calculation concerns arrival times. An observer exactly along a parallel acceleration sees zero radiation in that direction.

How much power misses the polar caps?

For a slow charge, collect radiation at angles \(60^\circ\leq\theta\leq120^\circ\). Put \(u=\cos\theta\). The collected fraction is \(\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}(1-u^2)\,du/(4/3)=11/16\). This equatorial belt spans half the solid angle but receives 68.75% of the power. Integrating with respect to \(\theta\) alone would overcount the polar regions.

Track the brightest direction of an accelerating charge

The two angular curves are each normalised to their own maximum. For parallel acceleration and β = 0.60, the maximum moves from 90° to about 31.8°. Compare peak location and the separately reported unnormalised peak height.

Problem sheet · inversion and beaming

Solve a retarded time

A charge moves along \(z=0.4cs\). At \(t=0\) a detector at \(z=3.0\) m receives its field. Find the emission time and the distance travelled by the signal.

Work through the solution

Before arrival the charge is behind the origin. Here \(R=3.0-0.4cs\) in metres, so \(0=0.6s+3.0/c\). The emission time is \(-16.7\) ns, at \(z=-2.0\) m. The propagation distance is 5.0 m. Using the current separation would predict only \(10.0\) ns of travel.

Locate the angular maximum

For parallel acceleration differentiate \(F(u)=(1-u^2)/(1-\beta u)^5\), \(u=\cos\theta\), and predict the maximum for \(\beta=0.6\).

Work through the solution

The numerator of \(F'\), after dropping positive factors, is \(5\beta-2u-3\beta u^2\). The physical root is \(u=5\beta/(1+\sqrt{1+15\beta^2})\). At 0.6 it equals 0.850, giving \(\theta\simeq31.8^\circ\). The other root lies outside the physical interval. At zero speed the limiting angle is \(90^\circ\), matching the dipole pattern.

Practicum · distinguish a large near field from outgoing power

A neutral dipole of size much less than the wavelength oscillates with complex moment \(p\,\hat{\mathbf z}\) at angular frequency \(\omega\). Two probes lie in its equatorial plane. An experimentalist proposes to estimate outgoing power as \(\epsilon_0c|E_\theta|^2/2\) times detector area at each radius. Is this plane-wave calibration valid close to the source? Compare field amplitudes, phases and a conserved flux before forming an opinion.

Recover the two equatorial fields from potentials

Use \(e^{-i\omega t}\), \(k=\omega/c\), \(x=kr\), and the Lorenz-gauge dipole potentials \(\phi=p\cos\theta\,(1-ikr)e^{ikr}/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^2)\), \(\mathbf A=-i\mu_0\omega p\,e^{ikr}\hat{\mathbf z}/(4\pi r)\). Recover \(E_\theta\) and \(B_\phi\) at \(\theta=\pi/2\).

Follow the calculation and check the result

At the equator \(\hat{\mathbf z}=-\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}\). Applying \(-r^{-1}\partial_\theta\) to the scalar potential gives \(p(1-ix)e^{ix}/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^3)\). The vector part \(i\omega A_\theta\) adds \(-p x^2 e^{ix}/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^3)\). Define the common complex scale \(K=p e^{ix}/(4\pi\epsilon_0r^3)\). Then

\[E_\theta=K(1-ix-x^2),\]
\[c B_\phi=K(-ix-x^2).\]

For the magnetic field use \(\nabla\times[f(r)\hat{\mathbf z}]=f'(r)\hat{\mathbf r}\times\hat{\mathbf z}\) with \(f=-i\mu_0\omega p\,e^{ikr}/(4\pi r)\). Both fields contain induction and radiation terms. The extra electrostatic term appears only in \(E_\theta\). It prevents the local field impedance from equalling the plane-wave value.

Find what two phase-sensitive probes would report

Multiply \(E_\theta(cB_\phi)^*\), retaining both quadratures. Determine the mean outward energy flux. How quickly does the out-of-phase component disappear relative to it?

Follow the calculation and check the result

The complex product is \(|K|^2(1-ix-x^2)(ix-x^2)=|K|^2(x^4+ix)\). Radiation measurements average real instantaneous products, so the mean radial flux is \(\epsilon_0c|K|^2x^4/2\), proportional to \(r^{-2}\) at every radius outside the ideal source. The imaginary component of the complex Poynting vector measures quadrature exchange; its ratio to the real part is \(x^{-3}\) in this convention. It does not add a second time-averaged outward power.

At \(kr=0.50\) the quadrature component is eight times the real part. At \(kr=2.00\) it is one eighth. A spherical integral restores the angular factor \(\sin^2\theta\) and area \(r^2\), yielding \(\omega^4|p|^2/(12\pi\epsilon_0c^3)\), independent of radius. This checks the result against the dipole radiation formula while retaining the full near-zone amplitudes.

Quantify the bias of an electric-only reading

Compute the ratio of the proposed plane-wave estimate to the true mean radial flux for the two probes. Design a measurement that can separate reactive and radiative fields.

Follow the calculation and check the result

The ratio is \(|1-ix-x^2|^2/x^4=1-x^{-2}+x^{-4}\). It is 13 at \(x=0.5\): a large electric field can coexist with comparatively little outward energy transport. At \(x=2\) the ratio is 0.8125; the same calibration now underestimates the flux by 18.75%. A monotonic intuition based on discarding all inverse powers except \(1/r\) can even miss the sign of the error.

For a phase-sensitive measurement, record \(E_\theta\) and \(B_\phi\) against the same clock, form the real part of their cross-product and compare \(r^2\langle S_r\rangle\) at several radii. Simultaneously examine the imaginary-to-real ratio and check its \(r^{-3}\) trend. For a finite-sized source keep the closest probe outside the source and test the dipole approximation by changing the observation angle. Other multipoles or a reflected wave break the simple three-term model.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§10.3, 11.2 · moving charges and power
  • Fließbach, chapter 23 · accelerated charge
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, §§5.2–5.3 · radiation from charges

Chapter 25 · Changing observers and classifying electromagnetic fields

Can an observer move so that a measured electric field disappears? Two numbers constructed from the fields settle the question before a velocity is chosen. The moving observer's force measurements explain why the classification works.

Measure fields at the same event

Take two inertial laboratories with parallel axes. The primed laboratory moves with constant velocity \(v\hat{\mathbf x}\) relative to the unprimed one. Compare fields at one spacetime event. The position labels and times assigned to that event differ by \(x'=\gamma(x-vt)\) and \(t'=\gamma(t-vx/c^2)\), where \(\gamma=(1-v^2/c^2)^{-1/2}\). Comparing both experiments at identical numerical position and time labels would usually compare different events.

How can a primed observer measure \(E'_y\)? Release a probe initially at rest in the primed frame. In the unprimed frame the probe has velocity \(v\hat{\mathbf x}\), so its transverse force is \(q(E_y-vB_z)\). The transverse momentum \(p_y\) is invariant under this boost, but along the probe's instantaneous trajectory \(dt'=dt/\gamma\). Thus \(dp'_y/dt'=\gamma dp_y/dt\), and \(E'_y=\gamma(E_y-vB_z)\). The other transverse component is \(E'_z=\gamma(E_z+vB_y)\). Longitudinal electric field is unchanged. A probe with adjustable primed velocity can also distinguish the magnetic force. The complete pair of rules is

\[\mathbf E'_\parallel=\mathbf E_\parallel,\]
\[\mathbf B'_\parallel=\mathbf B_\parallel,\]
\[\mathbf E'_\perp=\gamma(\mathbf E_\perp+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B),\]
\[\mathbf B'_\perp=\gamma(\mathbf B_\perp-\mathbf v\times\mathbf E/c^2).\]

Which differences survive a boost?

Examine perpendicular laboratory fields \(E_y=E\), \(cB_z=b\). In their common units the transformed pair is \(e'=\gamma(E-\beta b)\), \(b'=\gamma(b-\beta E)\). Squaring and subtracting cancels the cross terms. The factor \(\gamma^2(1-\beta^2)\) is unity. In a general orientation the parallel parts remain unchanged and the other pair of transverse components cancels in the same manner. The scalar product also remains unchanged. The two invariants are

\[I_1=|\mathbf E|^2-c^2|\mathbf B|^2,\]
\[I_2=\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B .\]

If \(I_2\neq0\), neither field can be transformed away. For nonzero orthogonal fields with \(I_1<0\), choose \(\mathbf v=\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/B^2\). Its speed is \(E/B<c\), and it cancels \(\mathbf E'\). If \(I_1>0\) choose \(\mathbf v=c^2\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/E^2\) to cancel \(\mathbf B'\). For nonzero orthogonal fields of equal strength in common units the required speed would be \(c\). A plane wave illustrates this null case. A zero field also has both invariants zero, so both vanishing invariants alone do not prove that a wave is present.

Two changes of observer

An observer travelling with drifting charges

Let \(E_y=1.80\times10^5\) V/m and \(B_z=1.00\) mT. The cancelling velocity is \(+1.80\times10^8\) m/s along \(x\), approximately \(0.600c\). Then \(B'_z=B_z\sqrt{1-E^2/(cB_z)^2}\simeq0.800\) mT. A charge moving with that primed laboratory feels zero force at the chosen event. In the laboratory, the electric and magnetic terms cancel directly. The statements concern one probe velocity; charges moving differently in the primed frame can experience magnetic force.

Devices following a light pulse

A plane pulse travelling in \(+x\) has \(E_y=cB_z\). A detector moving along \(+x\) measures \(E'_y/E_y=\gamma(1-\beta)=\sqrt{(1-\beta)/(1+\beta)}\). At \(\beta=0.60\) this is \(1/2\), and the intensity ratio is \(1/4\). Transform a phase \(kx-\omega t\) using \(k=\omega/c\). Its frequency receives the same factor \(1/2\). Both detectors obtain \(E'/B'=c\).

Why a neutral wire can have a charge density

For enclosed charge to agree between inertial descriptions, the charge and current densities must form the four-current \(J^\mu=(c\rho,\mathbf J)\). A boost gives \(\rho'=\gamma(\rho-vJ_x/c^2)\) and \(J'_x=\gamma(J_x-v\rho)\). For equal positive and negative charge densities producing a laboratory current, \(\rho=0\) but \(J_x\neq0\). In the moving laboratory the densities do not cancel. The change comes from how a simultaneous spatial slice intersects the worldlines of moving charge. Dividing a fixed list of particles by a contracted segment while keeping its endpoint events fixed would miscount particles crossing either endpoint.

Find a frame that cancels a field by varying its velocity

The axes are oriented as in the crossed-field derivation. For perpendicular fields E = 90 and cB = 150 kV/m, a boost at 0.60c cancels E and leaves cB′ = 120 kV/m. Exchange the two inputs and predict which field can now vanish.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · classification and measurements

Classify before calculating

Take a field with \(\mathbf E=E_0\hat{\mathbf y}\) and \(c\mathbf B=2E_0\hat{\mathbf y}+E_0\hat{\mathbf z}\). Could either field disappear? Explain why consulting the difference of squares alone gives incomplete information.

Work through the solution

The magnetic magnitude is \(\sqrt{5}E_0/c\), making \(I_1=-4E_0^2\). However \(I_2=2E_0^2/c\neq0\). A frame with either field zero would have \(I_2=0\), contradicting its observed invariant value.

A density reconstructed from a current

A laboratory measures \(\rho=0\) and \(J_x=9.0\) A/m². A second lab moves at \(0.60c\). Determine its current density and charge density, including signs.

Work through the solution

Here \(\gamma=1.25\). The transformed current is \(+11.25\) A/m². The transformed density is \(-1.25(0.60)(9.0)/c=-2.25\times10^{-8}\) C/m³. Inserting these into \(c^2\rho'^2-J_x'^2\) returns \(-81\) in squared current-density units, a check independent of sign conventions in the voltage probes.

Practicum · reconstruct a crossed-field trajectory from two clocks

A positron initially rests in a laboratory with uniform crossed fields \(\mathbf B=B\hat{\mathbf z}\), \(\mathbf E=E\hat{\mathbf y}\), with \(B=10.0\) mT and \(E/(cB)=0.600\). Ignore radiation losses and edge effects. Predict its maximum speed, arrival point after one cycle, and energy gain halfway through the cycle. Work by transforming whole events between observers. A velocity transformation alone cannot predict the travel time.

Choose the frame in which the trajectory is simplest

Find a frame with zero electric field, transform the initial particle velocity, and derive the orbit radius and frequency in that frame. Define what a “cycle” means for this measurement.

Follow the calculation and check the result

Boost along \(+x\) at \(v_d=E/B=0.600c\) with \(\gamma_d=1.25\). Then \(E'_y=0\) and \(B'_z=B/\gamma_d=8.00\) mT. Initially the particle has \(v'_x=-v_d\), so its primed energy is constant at \(\gamma_dmc^2\). With positive charge \(e\), its initial magnetic force points towards \(+y\). Defining \(\Omega'=eB'/(\gamma_dm)\) and \(R'=\gamma_dm v_d/(eB')\), one obtains

\[x'=-R'\sin(\Omega't'),\]
\[y'=R'[1-\cos(\Omega't')].\]

Differentiating verifies the initial velocity and yields the rotating transverse acceleration. The primed radius is approximately 0.160 m and the primed period \(T'=2\pi/\Omega'\) is 5.58 ns. One cycle means two successive events at which the primed particle returns to its initial location and velocity. In the laboratory these events occur at different positions.

Measure time and the maximum lab speed

Transform the coordinates of the first return event to the laboratory. At what part of its orbit does the particle reach its maximum lab speed? Why is it incorrect to use a single constant \(\gamma_d\) to convert every subinterval along the curved path?

Follow the calculation and check the result

For a full primed period \(\Delta x'=0\). The inverse Lorentz transformation gives \(\Delta t=\gamma_dT'=6.98\) ns and \(\Delta x=\gamma_dv_dT'=1.26\) m. Hence the average lab drift is \(0.600c\) along \(+x\). For an arbitrary subinterval the formula is instead \(\Delta t=\gamma_d(\Delta t'+v_d\Delta x'/c^2)\). Away from rest or half-period endpoints its second term generally contributes.

The primed momentum has largest positive \(x'\) component at half a period, when \(v'_x=v_d\). Then \(v_x=2v_d/(1+v_d^2/c^2)=0.88235c\), \(v_y=0\). The lab energy transformation \(\mathcal E=\gamma_d(\mathcal E'+v_dp'_x)\) confirms that this is the energy maximum and hence the speed maximum. At the end of a period the particle returns to laboratory rest even though the apparatus has seen a net displacement.

Check the energy with an independent voltage measurement

At half a cycle compute the work performed by the actual laboratory electric field from the displacement alone. Compare with the relativistic kinetic energy and explain how it can be zero over a whole cycle.

Follow the calculation and check the result

At the top of the primed orbit \(y'=2R'\). A boost along \(x\) leaves \(y=2R'\) unchanged. The electric work is \(eE(2R')=2\gamma_d^2\beta_d^2mc^2=1.125mc^2\), or about 575 keV. The lab maximum speed gives \(\gamma_{\max}=(1+\beta_d^2)/(1-\beta_d^2)=2.125\), so its kinetic energy is the same \(1.125mc^2\). The magnetic force performs zero work throughout. During the second half the particle moves back down in \(y\), and the electric field removes exactly this kinetic energy.

At a fixed laboratory event a numerical trajectory can be checked against both \(\mathcal E-mc^2=eEy\) and the parametric orbit. Comparing the two paths at equal numerical values of \(t\) and \(t'\) would compare different events and could create apparent errors in both period and speed. At each successive cusp the lab velocity momentarily vanishes; the motion on either side reverses its transverse component. The cusp spacing supplies an additional check on the drift distance.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§12.1–12.3 · relativity and field transformations
  • Fließbach, chapter 22 · field transformations
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 4 · relativity

Chapter 26 · From local symmetry to Maxwell’s equations

Some electromagnetic equations describe the geometry of a potential. Others describe its response to charge. Tensor notation identifies the distinction precisely, and a variational argument shows why the source must obey a local conservation law.

Build an antisymmetric tensor from four potentials

Write \(x^\mu=(ct,x,y,z)\), \(\eta_{\mu\nu}=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\), \(A^\mu=(\phi/c,\mathbf A)\), and \(J^\mu=(c\rho,\mathbf J)\). Greek indices run from 0 to 3 and repeated upper and lower indices are summed. Lowering a spatial index changes its sign. Thus \(\partial^\mu=(c^{-1}\partial_t,-\nabla)\). This convention fixes every sign below.

Form \(F^{\mu\nu}=\partial^\mu A^\nu-\partial^\nu A^\mu\). Then \(F^{0i}=\partial_t A_i^{\rm cart}/c+\partial_i\phi/c=-E_i^{\rm cart}/c\). For spatial components \(F^{12}=-\partial_x A_y+\partial_y A_x=-B_z\) with Cartesian subscripts on the right. Antisymmetry leaves six independent quantities. These are the three electric and three magnetic components. Adding a four-gradient to the potential leaves \(F\) unchanged because its mixed derivatives commute.

Unpack the two tensor equations

Consider \(\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}=\mu_0J^\nu\). For \(\nu=0\) the only terms are spatial: \(\partial_iF^{i0}=\nabla\cdot\mathbf E/c=\mu_0c\rho\), which is Gauss's law. For \(\nu=1\), \(F^{01}=-E_x/c\), \(F^{21}=B_z\), \(F^{31}=-B_y\). The equation gives \((\nabla\times\mathbf B)_x-c^{-2}\partial_tE_x=\mu_0J_x\). Cyclic permutations give the remaining components. The other half of Maxwell's equations is an identity:

\[\partial_\lambda F_{\mu\nu}+\partial_\mu F_{\nu\lambda}+\partial_\nu F_{\lambda\mu}=0 .\]

Insert the definition of \(F\). Each second derivative cancels a partner of opposite sign. With three spatial indices the identity says \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\); with a time index it gives Faraday's law. Applying \(\partial_\nu\) to the sourced equation also gives zero on the left by antisymmetry, enforcing \(\partial_\nu J^\nu=0\).

Vary the potential at fixed external current

We seek a Lorentz scalar built quadratically from the fields, linearly from the source and potential, and locally from their values at a spacetime event. In SI units choose

\[S=\frac1c\int d^4x\left[-\frac{1}{4\mu_0}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-J^\mu A_\mu\right].\]

The factor \(1/c\) converts \(dx^0\) to seconds. The two antisymmetric terms in \(\delta F_{\mu\nu}\) contribute equally. Thus the field part varies as \(-F^{\mu\nu}\partial_\mu\delta A_\nu/\mu_0\). Integrate by parts, fixing \(\delta A_\nu=0\) on the boundary. The remaining arbitrary variation multiplies

\[\frac1{\mu_0}\partial_\mu F^{\mu\nu}-J^\nu=0 .\]

Under \(A_\mu\mapsto A_\mu+\partial_\mu\chi\) the source term changes by \(-J^\mu\partial_\mu\chi\). Integration by parts leaves a boundary term and \(+\chi\partial_\mu J^\mu\). Gauge independence of the action for arbitrary interior \(\chi\) therefore requires charge continuity. The same conservation law has appeared by a second route.

Read the action in familiar variables

Why a plane wave has zero field Lagrangian density

A direct contraction gives \(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=2(B^2-E^2/c^2)\). The vacuum field Lagrangian density is \(\epsilon_0E^2/2-B^2/(2\mu_0)\), whereas the physical energy density is the sum of those two positive terms. For a plane wave \(E=cB\), the Lagrangian density vanishes; the energy density equals \(\epsilon_0E^2\). At \(E=100\) V/m instantaneous energy density is \(8.85\times10^{-8}\) J/m³. The cancellation in the action carries a distinct physical meaning from an empty field.

Test the stationary potential inside a capacitor

Between plane electrodes at \(0,V_0\), take \(\phi=V_0[x/L+\eta\sin(\pi x/L)]\). Each trial curve has the correct endpoints. For no space charge the static functional per area is \(U=\epsilon_0\int_0^L(\partial_x\phi)^2dx/2\). Its cross term integrates to zero because the sine perturbation vanishes on the boundaries, and the squared cosine averages to \(1/2\). Consequently \(U/U_{\rm exact}=1+\pi^2\eta^2/2\). A perturbation with \(\eta=0.10\) raises the functional by 4.93%. The flattening of the energy near \(\eta=0\) tests stationarity directly.

Perturb the potential while keeping both electrodes fixed

Every trial curve has the prescribed endpoints. At amplitude 0.10 the trial energy is 4.93% above the exact value. Compare positive and negative perturbations, then double the amplitude and predict how the energy penalty changes.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · gauge and variational checks

Introduce a known static charge

For fixed boundary potentials, vary \(I[\phi]=\int (\epsilon_0|\nabla\phi|^2/2-\rho\phi)\,dV\). Which PDE follows? Why is it necessary to specify the sign of the linear term?

Work through the solution

Integrating the gradient term by parts produces \(\delta I=\int(-\epsilon_0\nabla^2\phi-\rho)\delta\phi\,dV\), because \(\delta\phi\) vanishes on the electrodes. Stationarity gives \(-\epsilon_0\nabla^2\phi=\rho\). Reversing the source sign would reverse the curvature induced by positive charge. This functional includes a source coupling and generally differs from just the stored field energy.

Contract with a particle velocity

At an instant a particle is at rest. Insert \(u^\mu=(c,0,0,0)\) in \(f^\mu=qF^{\mu\nu}u_\nu\) to recover its spatial force. Why is the time component zero?

Work through the solution

Here \(F^{i0}=E_i/c\) and \(u_0=c\), so \(f^i=qE_i\). Antisymmetry gives \(F^{00}=0\). At that instant the velocity vanishes and hence so does the rate of work. More generally \(u_\mu f^\mu=q u_\mu F^{\mu\nu}u_\nu=0\), since the velocity product is symmetric. This preserves the particle's invariant rest mass.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §12.3 · relativistic electrodynamics
  • Fließbach, chapter 19 · Lagrangian formalism
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 4 · covariant equations and Maxwell action

Chapter 27 · Cavity eigenmodes, measured linewidth and ring-down

A cavity has both a pitch and a decay time. One is set predominantly by the field pattern and the boundaries. The other measures how much stored energy escapes per cycle. A spatial perturbation can reveal where a particular mode stores its electric energy.

Make a conducting box satisfy every boundary condition

Let the empty cavity occupy \(0<x<a,\ 0<y<b,\ 0<z<d\). Its ideal conducting walls require zero tangential electric field. Choose an electric field in the \(y\) direction independent of \(y\). This automatically gives \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E=0\). The field is normal to the two \(y\)-walls and must vanish at the four \(x,z\)-walls. Write \(E_y=X(x)Z(z)e^{-i\omega t}\). The wave equation reduces to \(X''/X+Z''/Z=-\omega^2/c^2\). Sines separately satisfy the vanishing boundary values. Thus a family of normal modes is

\[E_y=E_0\sin(m\pi x/a)\sin(p\pi z/d)e^{-i\omega t},\]
\[f_{m0p}=\frac c2\sqrt{(m/a)^2+(p/d)^2},\]
\[m,p=1,2,\ldots .\]

Faraday's law then gives \(\mathbf B=(\nabla\times\mathbf E)/(i\omega)\). Its components are proportional to \(-\partial_zE_y\) and \(+\partial_xE_y\); its normal component vanishes at the appropriate walls. This family is transverse electric relative to the \(z\) axis. It does not exhaust the transverse magnetic modes or the modes with \(y\)-dependence. Whether a particular solution is excited depends on the overlap of the source with its field pattern.

Track energy between electric and magnetic form

Choose a real mode proportional to \(\cos\omega t\). The associated magnetic field is proportional to \(\sin\omega t\). At an electric maximum the stored energy is \(U=\epsilon_0\int E_{\rm peak}^2dV/2\). Both squared sines average to \(1/2\), giving \(U=\epsilon_0 E_0^2 abd/8\). A quarter-cycle later the magnetic field stores the same energy. For a weakly leaking resonator, define \(Q=\omega U/P_{\rm lost}\) using cycle-averaged lost power and slowly varying total energy. At fixed \(Q\),

\[\frac{dU}{dt}=-\frac{\omega}{Q}U,\]
\[U(t)=U_0e^{-\omega t/Q},\]
\[E_{\rm env}(t)=E_{\rm env}(0)e^{-\omega t/(2Q)} .\]

A field probe measuring voltage follows the amplitude envelope. A square-law power detector follows the energy envelope. A ring-down fit delivers \(Q=\omega\tau_U=\omega\tau_E/2\), depending on which time constant is reported. Independent weak loss channels have additive powers and therefore additive inverse quality factors.

Connect a time trace to a frequency scan

The Fourier amplitude of a decaying positive-frequency signal is proportional to \(\int_0^\infty e^{-\omega_0t/(2Q)}e^{i(\omega-\omega_0)t}dt=[\omega_0/(2Q)-i(\omega-\omega_0)]^{-1}\). Its squared modulus is a Lorentzian with full width at half power \(\Delta\omega=\omega_0/Q\). This derivation resolves a common experimental ambiguity: the two half-power points have opposite detunings, and the width spans both. The approximation assumes a single mode and damping small on the scale of the carrier frequency.

Measuring geometry and locating an antinode

A rectangular resonator

Set \(a=60.0\) mm, \(d=80.0\) mm, \(b=25.0\) mm. The \(101\) mode gives \(f=3.123\) GHz. At \(Q=6000\) the energy decay time is \(306\) ns and the field decay time is \(612\) ns. The full power linewidth is \(520\) kHz. Increasing \(b\) changes the stored energy at fixed peak field but leaves this ideal mode frequency unchanged. Other mode families can move as \(b\) changes.

A small dielectric probe

Write the electric eigenproblem as \(\nabla\times\mu_0^{-1}\nabla\times\mathbf E=\omega^2\epsilon\mathbf E\). Multiply by the complex conjugate field and integrate. The wall term vanishes and the numerator becomes \(\int |\nabla\times\mathbf E|^2/\mu_0\,dV\). First-order perturbation of the stationary quotient gives \(\delta f/f=-\int\delta\epsilon|\mathbf E|^2dV/(2\int\epsilon|\mathbf E|^2dV)\). A weak permittivity increase of 2% in a region containing 3% of the original electric-field integral shifts the mode down by \(3\times10^{-4}\), approximately \(0.94\) MHz here. At an electric node the first-order shift vanishes. Large beads distort the field and require a new eigenproblem.

Read a cavity decay with an amplitude or power detector

The cavity width is fixed at 60 mm. With 80 mm length and Q = 6000, the energy falls to 1/e in 0.306 µs. Read the field amplitude there and decide which trace a square-law detector would follow.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · two ways to measure loss

Extract internal loss

A port-coupled resonator has measured \(Q_L=3000\). An independent calibration gives external \(Q_e=12000\). Find internal \(Q_i\) and the fraction of power leaving through the port.

Work through the solution

Since \(1/Q_L=1/Q_i+1/Q_e\), the internal quality factor is 4000. The port power fraction is \((1/Q_e)/(1/Q_L)=1/4\). Closing the port in the idealised model leaves \(Q=4000\).

Compare scans and ring-downs

A single mode near 500 MHz has a half-power width of 100 kHz. After drive removal how long until the field amplitude halves?

Work through the solution

The linewidth gives \(Q=5000\), so \(\tau_E=Q/(\pi f)=3.18\) μs. The field halves after \(\tau_E\ln2=2.21\) μs. At the same instant the energy has fallen to one quarter. This inference requires that frequency jitter has not broadened the measured line.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §9.5 · guided waves
  • Fließbach, chapter 21 · cavity modes

Chapter 28 · Why a small transparent sphere scatters light

A particle can redistribute light without consuming it. The calculation starts with an electrostatic boundary-value problem and ends with power crossing a faraway sphere. Connecting the two limits predicts a wavelength dependence that can be tested with colour.

Find the induced dipole by matching two potentials

Take a sphere of radius \(a\) and relative permittivity \(\epsilon_r\) in vacuum. Its size must be small compared with the wavelength everywhere, including inside the particle. Across its diameter, the incident wave then looks almost like a uniform electric field \(E_0\hat{\mathbf z}\). Spherical symmetry and the applied field single out the angular factor \(\cos\theta\). Choose \(\phi_{\rm in}=-Cr\cos\theta\) and \(\phi_{\rm out}=-E_0r\cos\theta+D\cos\theta/r^2\). Continuity of tangential electric field is equivalent here to continuity of potential. With zero free surface charge, the normal displacement is also continuous. At \(r=a\),

\[C=E_0-D/a^3,\]
\[\epsilon_r C=E_0+2D/a^3.\]

Eliminate \(C\) to obtain \(D=a^3E_0(\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\). The coefficient of the dipole potential is \(p/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\). Hence \(p=\alpha_0 E_0\) with \(\alpha_0=4\pi\epsilon_0a^3(\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\). The internal field \(3E_0/(\epsilon_r+2)\) explains the denominator. For \(\epsilon_r=1\) the disturbance vanishes. In the perfectly polarizable limit the internal field tends to zero.

Match a small dipole to an outgoing wave

Use complex amplitudes with time dependence \(e^{-i\omega t}\). The far electric field is \((k^2/4\pi\epsilon_0 r)[(\hat{\mathbf n}\times\mathbf p)\times\hat{\mathbf n}]e^{ikr}\), \(k=\omega/c\). Squaring its amplitude and multiplying by \(\epsilon_0 c/2\) gives the mean intensity. Divide by the incident intensity \(\epsilon_0c|E_0|^2/2\). The differential scattering cross section is then \(k^4|\alpha|^2\sin^2\theta/(16\pi^2\epsilon_0^2)\), with \(\theta\) measured from the dipole axis. Integrating the angular factor gives \(8\pi/3\):

\[\sigma_{\rm sca}=\frac{k^4|\alpha|^2}{6\pi\epsilon_0^2}\simeq\frac{8\pi}3 k^4a^6\left|\frac{\epsilon_r-1}{\epsilon_r+2}\right|^2 .\]

The sixth power originates in the coherent addition of dipoles throughout a volume proportional to \(a^3\). The fourth power in frequency comes from the second time derivative of the dipole in the radiative electric field. A cross section has units of area and measures removed power divided by incident intensity.

Where the scattered energy comes from

The incident field does work at mean rate \(\langle\mathbf E\cdot\dot{\mathbf p}\rangle=(\omega/2)\operatorname{Im}(\alpha)|E_0|^2\). Dividing by incident intensity yields \(\sigma_{\rm ext}=k\operatorname{Im}(\alpha)/\epsilon_0\). A strictly real electrostatic \(\alpha_0\) would predict no extracted power while predicting positive scattered power. The static calculation captured the leading dipole amplitude but omitted its small radiation-induced phase delay. For a lossless electric dipole its leading radiative correction satisfies

\[\frac1\alpha=\frac1{\alpha_0}-i\frac{k^3}{6\pi\epsilon_0}.\]

Indeed \(\operatorname{Im}\alpha=k^3|\alpha|^2/(6\pi\epsilon_0)\), making extinction equal to scattering. In an absorbing particle their difference is absorbed power per incident intensity. Finite-size dispersion and higher multipoles require additional terms once \(ka\) grows.

Size, colour and the limits of inference

A particle doubles in radius

For dilute spheres of the same weakly dispersive material, doubling \(a\) gives \(2^6=64\) times the scattered power per particle at fixed incident intensity. If the total volume of dielectric is held fixed, the number of spheres falls by eight. The whole sample then scatters eight times the power, provided phases between different particles are effectively uncorrelated and multiple scattering is negligible. The two questions describe different samples.

A frequency-independent contrast

For \(a=20\) nm, \(\epsilon_r=2.25\) and vacuum wavelength \(600\) nm, \(ka=0.209\) and \((\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)=0.2941\). The leading cross section is \(5.58\times10^{-19}\) m², around \(4.4\times10^{-4}\) of the geometric area. At \(450\) nm the model predicts 3.16 times that cross section. A large discrepancy can indicate dispersion, additional multipoles, or a distribution of particle sizes.

Compare the scattering of several particle sizes

For radius 20 nm, relative permittivity 2.25 and wavelength 600 nm, the predicted cross section is about 0.56 nm². Halve the radius and estimate the drop before reading the plot. The curve uses a frequency-independent dielectric contrast.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · inverse measurements

Estimate a radius ratio

Two dilute suspensions have the same number density, composition and optical path length. In the single-scattering regime one produces twice the scattered intensity at the same angle. Infer the ratio of particle radii.

Work through the solution

The proportionality to \(a^6\) gives \(a_2/a_1=2^{1/6}=1.122\). A 12.2% change in radius doubles the signal. If counts or material contrasts are unknown, the same intensity change has multiple explanations.

Recover Thomson scattering

A free electron is driven by a weak field. Use \(m\ddot x=-eE_0e^{-i\omega t}\) to find its dipole and cross section. Why does the wavelength law change?

Work through the solution

The displacement amplitude is \(eE_0/(m\omega^2)\). Multiplying by electronic charge gives \(\alpha=-e^2/(m\omega^2)\). The \(\omega^{-4}\) in \(|\alpha|^2\) cancels \(k^4\). The cross section becomes \((8\pi/3)[e^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0mc^2)]^2\). This classical approximation assumes nonrelativistic electron motion and negligible recoil.

Further reading
