  • Fließbach, chapter 25 · scattering of light
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 5 · Rayleigh and Thomson scattering

Chapter 29 · When a circuit element must be treated as a field

Can a short pair of conductors be called a capacitor? At low frequencies it stores energy almost electrostatically. As propagation time becomes important, the same open conductors support standing waves and their input current no longer grows proportionally to frequency.

Build a transmission line from small field volumes

Consider a uniform two-conductor line supporting a transverse electromagnetic wave. Let \(V(z,t)\) measure the transverse potential difference and \(I(z,t)\) the current toward \(+z\). Denote its inductance and capacitance per unit length by \(L'\) and \(C'\). For the moment assume ideal conductors, a linear lossless dielectric and negligible radiation. A segment \(dz\) stores charge \(C'V\,dz\). Charge balance gives \(I(z)-I(z+dz)=C' dz\,\partial_t V\). Its linked magnetic flux is \(L'I\,dz\); Faraday's law gives the voltage drop \(V(z)-V(z+dz)=L' dz\,\partial_t I\). Division by \(dz\) yields

\[\partial_z I=-C'\partial_t V,\]
\[\partial_z V=-L'\partial_t I .\]

Differentiate the second relation with respect to \(z\) and substitute the first. The result is \(\partial_z^2 V=L'C'\partial_t^2V\), with speed \(u=1/\sqrt{L'C'}\). An outgoing wave \(F(t-z/u)\) satisfies \(V/I=Z_0=\sqrt{L'/C'}\). The electric and magnetic field energies per length are \(C'V^2/2\) and \(L'I^2/2\), and they are equal for this travelling wave. Multiplying the two equations by \(V\) and \(I\) also gives a one-dimensional conservation law: \(\partial_z(VI)=-\partial_t(C'V^2/2+L'I^2/2)\).

Derive the input admittance of an open end

Use \(e^{-i\omega t}\) time dependence, and let the far end \(z=\ell\) be open so \(I(\ell)=0\). Then \(\partial_zV(\ell)=0\). The solution is \(V(z)=V_L\cos k(\ell-z)\), where \(k=\omega/u\). Since \(\partial_zV=i\omega L'I\), the current is \(-i V_L\sin k(\ell-z)/Z_0\). At the input,

\[Y_{\rm in}=-\frac{i}{Z_0}\tan(k\ell),\]
\[Y_{\rm lumped}=-i\omega C'\ell,\]
\[\frac{Y_{\rm in}}{Y_{\rm lumped}}=\frac{\tan x}{x},\]
\[x=\frac{\omega\ell}{u} .\]

At small \(x\), \(\tan x/x=1+x^2/3+O(x^4)\). Thus a circuit approximation can be assigned an error estimate. Near \(x=\pi/2\) the input voltage approaches a node and the ideal input admittance diverges. Losses round the pole in a real line. Beyond this frequency even the sign can change, although the far end remains open.

Two decisions about model size

A 15 cm open lead

Take \(u=2.00\times10^8\) m/s and \(Z_0=50.0\,\Omega\). Then \(C'=1/(Z_0u)=100\) pF/m and the lumped capacitance is 15.0 pF. At 20.0 MHz, \(x=0.09425\); the exact admittance exceeds the capacitive prediction by 0.297%. At 200 MHz, \(x=0.9425\), and the excess is about 46.0%. A component measured on an open fixture can therefore appear to acquire substantial extra capacitance at high frequency.

A time trace on the same lead

The one-way flight time is \(0.75\) ns. A step launched down the line first sees the characteristic impedance. Information about the open end returns only after \(1.50\) ns. At an open end an incident positive voltage wave is reflected with the same sign. The current waves cancel there, enforcing zero terminal current, while the terminal voltage doubles at their first overlap. For a source matched to \(Z_0\), the returning wave is absorbed at the source. This time-domain argument provides a check on the origin of the standing-wave solution.

A second circuit limit from magnetic energy

In the slow-variation limit, linked flux in two fixed coils is linear in both currents. For a reciprocal medium, \(\lambda_1=L_1I_1+MI_2\), \(\lambda_2=MI_1+L_2I_2\). Integrate the supplied power \(I_1\dot\lambda_1+I_2\dot\lambda_2\) to obtain \(U=(L_1I_1^2+2MI_1I_2+L_2I_2^2)/2\). Completing the square gives \(2U=L_1(I_1+MI_2/L_1)^2+(L_2-M^2/L_1)I_2^2\). A passive magnetic energy requires \(|M|\leq\sqrt{L_1L_2}\). The sign of \(M\) depends on terminal orientations. This bound is an independent way to check a measured or numerically inferred circuit model.

Measure the range of a lumped capacitor model

The line has speed 2.00 × 10⁸ m/s and impedance 50 Ω. For a 15 cm open section its first quarter-wave pole is 333 MHz. At 60% of that frequency the distributed admittance exceeds the lumped value by 46%. Move the open end and compare the two curves.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · estimating the cost of a circuit approximation

Set a one-percent frequency limit

For a 0.30 m open line with speed \(1.8\times10^8\) m/s, estimate the highest frequency for a 1% correction in input admittance. State the approximation used.

Work through the solution

Set \(x^2/3=0.01\), giving \(x\simeq0.173\) and \(f=xu/(2\pi\ell)=16.5\) MHz. The next term is \(2x^4/15\simeq0.00012\), so the estimate slightly overstates the allowable frequency. Bisection on \(\tan x/x=1.01\) gives \(x\simeq0.1722\).

Check a claimed mutual inductance

A two-coil model has \(L_1=4\) mH, \(L_2=9\) mH and \(M=7\) mH. Find the current combination that reveals a problem.

Work through the solution

Choose \(I_1=-MI_2/L_1=-1.75I_2\). The surviving coefficient is \(9-49/4=-3.25\) mH, giving a negative stored energy for every nonzero \(I_2\). A passive reciprocal model with these self-inductances can support at most 6 mH of mutual inductance in magnitude.

Further reading

  • Fließbach, chapter 26 · oscillating circuits
  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§7.2, 9.5 · inductance and guided waves
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 3 · induction and inductance

Chapter 30 · From wavefronts to rays, focusing and caustics

A ray is a prediction extracted from the phase of a wave. Following its amplitude at the same time reveals the point at which a ray calculation loses its validity. A gently graded transparent material gives a solvable example of both effects.

Derive the phase and transport equations together

In a lossless isotropic dielectric with a slowly varying refractive index \(n(\mathbf r)\), consider one scalar polarisation component satisfying \(\nabla^2\psi+k_0^2 n^2\psi=0\), where \(k_0=\omega/c\). This scalar description works when vector-polarisation coupling is negligible over the scales of interest. Set \(\psi=A(\mathbf r)e^{ik_0S(\mathbf r)}\) with a slowly varying real amplitude. Differentiation twice gives

\[0=\nabla^2 A+i k_0[2\nabla A\cdot\nabla S+A\nabla^2S]+k_0^2 A(n^2-|\nabla S|^2).\]

If the material and amplitude vary little over a wavelength, first equate the leading \(k_0^2\) term to zero. At the next order the \(k_0\) term vanishes. The two results are the eikonal equation and transport equation:

\[|\nabla S|^2=n^2,\]
\[\nabla\cdot(A^2\nabla S)=0 .\]

The first fixes the optical length accumulated between neighbouring wavefronts. The second transports energy down a thin ray tube. Across a tube of area \(\mathcal A\) perpendicular to the phase gradient, \(A^2 n\mathcal A\) is constant to this order. In a uniform medium a spherical tube grows as \(r^2\), giving \(A\propto1/r\), consistent with the outgoing wave.

Follow the normal to a wavefront

Choose arclength \(s\) along the ray and \(\hat{\mathbf t}=\nabla S/n\). In the eikonal equation take a spatial derivative. Since \(\nabla S\) is a gradient, \((\nabla S\cdot\nabla)\nabla S=\nabla(|\nabla S|^2)/2=n\nabla n\). Divide by \(n\). The ray equation is

\[\frac{d}{ds}(n\hat{\mathbf t})=\nabla n .\]

A transverse increase of index bends the ray toward larger optical index. If \(n\) depends only on the coordinate normal to layers, the component of \(n\hat{\mathbf t}\) parallel to them stays constant. Thus \(n\sin\theta\) is conserved for an angle measured from the normal, recovering Snell's law. The same equation follows by requiring stationary optical length \(\int n\,ds\) when varying the path with fixed endpoints.

Solve a graded-index profile

For paraxial rays travelling close to \(+z\), take \(n(y)\simeq n_0(1-g^2y^2/2)\). Assume \(|gy|\ll1\) and \(|dy/dz|\ll1\). Then \(ds\simeq dz\), \(\hat t_y\simeq y'\), and \(d(ny')/dz\simeq n_0y''\) to leading order. Since \(\partial_y n=-n_0g^2y\), the trajectory solves \(y''+g^2y=0\). Initial height \(y_0\) and slope \(s_0\) give

\[y(z)=y_0\cos(gz)+\frac{s_0}{g}\sin(gz),\]
\[y'(z)=-g y_0\sin(gz)+s_0\cos(gz).\]

For a collimated bundle, all \(s_0=0\). The transverse magnification \(\partial y/\partial y_0=\cos(gz)\) vanishes simultaneously for all rays at \(z_f=\pi/(2g)\). This is a caustic of the ray description. In this planar example the ray density grows as \(1/|\cos(gz)|\). Its divergence signals that the neglected \(\nabla^2 A\) becomes important. The underlying wave stays finite and develops diffraction near the focus.

A focus and a reconstructed gradient

Refocus an off-axis bundle

Take \(g=20\ {\rm m^{-1}}\), initial heights between \(-1\) and \(+1\) mm and zero slopes. The predicted first focus is \(78.5\) mm downstream. The extreme rays acquire slopes of magnitude \(0.020\) at the focus, well within the paraxial regime. Tilt every initial ray by 0.010 rad. At \(z_f\) the common height shifts to \(s_0/g=0.50\) mm. The focal length is insensitive to this common tilt in the parabolic approximation.

Estimate the index from a photograph

Two successive crossings of initially parallel symmetric rays are 120 mm apart. Crossings occur at \(gz=\pi/2+m\pi\), so \(g=\pi/0.120=26.2\ {\rm m^{-1}}\). At a height of 2 mm, the predicted fractional index decrease from the central index is \(g^2y^2/2=1.37\times10^{-3}\). Resolving this small contrast through repeated bending can be easier than resolving it through a single refraction measurement.

Focus and tilt a bundle of paraxial rays

At gradient 20 m⁻¹, five parallel rays meet after 78.5 mm. A common tilt of 10 mrad displaces their meeting point by 0.50 mm. Follow the bundle through a crossing and decide why the geometric density ceases to predict a finite intensity there.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · optical path and diffraction

A travel-time check

A ray passes through two homogeneous layers of thicknesses \(a,b\), indices \(n_1,n_2\), with lateral endpoint separation \(D\). Write travel time as a function of crossing coordinate \(x\). Recover the interface condition.

Work through the solution

The time is \(T(x)=[n_1\sqrt{a^2+x^2}+n_2\sqrt{b^2+(D-x)^2}]/c\). The derivative vanishes when \(n_1 x/\sqrt{a^2+x^2}=n_2(D-x)/\sqrt{b^2+(D-x)^2}\). The two fractions are the sines of the angles to the interface normal. Each segment's length has positive second derivative, making this stationary path a local minimum in this geometry.

Estimate the diffraction scale

A circular pupil of numerical aperture 0.030 focuses vacuum light of wavelength 600 nm. Estimate the first dark-ring radius and compare it with a ray focus.

Work through the solution

For a uniformly illuminated circular aperture in the paraxial limit the first Airy minimum is \(0.61\lambda_0/\mathrm{NA}\), giving \(12.2\) μm. Different pupil shapes change the numerical factor. The wavelength dependence is absent from the ray crossing, so a ray trace alone cannot distinguish two experiments with identical geometry but different wavelengths.

Further reading

  • Fließbach, chapter 38 · geometrical optics
  • Fließbach, chapter 36 · interference and diffraction

Chapter 31 · Radiation reaction and the energy stored near a charge

A charge can radiate at an instant when a proposed frictional force would do no work. To account for the energy one needs three measurements: mechanical work on the charge, power leaving in radiation and a reversible change in its nearby field.

A moving oscillator tests a friction hypothesis

For a point charge moving nonrelativistically in SI units define \(\tau=q^2/(6\pi\epsilon_0mc^3)\). The Larmor formula gives \(P_{\rm rad}=m\tau a^2\). Suppose a charge reaches a turning point of a slow sinusoidal oscillation. Its velocity there is zero and its acceleration is maximal. Radiation carries positive power away, yet any finite force on the particle does zero mechanical work at that instant. Equating instantaneous mechanical loss to instantaneous radiated power cannot account for the turning point.

Integrate over a smooth acceleration history. The product rule gives \(d(\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf v)/dt=\dot{\mathbf a}\cdot\mathbf v+a^2\). Rearranging shows that a local force \(\mathbf F_{\rm self}=m\tau\dot{\mathbf a}\) can recover the Larmor loss between endpoints where \(\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf v\) agrees:

\[\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\mathbf F_{\rm self}\cdot\mathbf v\,dt=m\tau[\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf v]_{t_1}^{t_2}-\int_{t_1}^{t_2}P_{\rm rad}\,dt .\]

This identity motivates the nonrelativistic Abraham–Lorentz term. An integrated energy argument only fixes its work under the chosen endpoint conditions. Deriving the full local self-force requires analysing the near field, regularising the point-source singularity and identifying the measured particle mass. These assumptions delimit the classical effective equation.

Follow the energy that can return to the particle

Let \(K=mv^2/2\), \(E_S=-m\tau\,\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf v\). Write \(m\mathbf a=\mathbf F_{\rm ext}+\mathbf F_{\rm self}\), dot with \(\mathbf v\), and use the product rule once more. The result is

\[\mathbf F_{\rm ext}\cdot\mathbf v=\frac{d}{dt}(K+E_S)+P_{\rm rad}.\]

The acceleration-dependent quantity \(E_S\), called the Schott energy, represents a reversible bound-field term after regularisation. Its sign depends on motion; it is a changeable contribution relative to an energy reference. For constant acceleration over a short nonrelativistic interval, the self-force vanishes, but \(dE_S/dt=-m\tau a^2\). The decrease supplies the radiated energy. Switching the acceleration on and off modifies the budget near the endpoints. Extending a constant-acceleration segment to infinite time would break the assumed slow-speed model.

Why taking the third-order equation literally fails

Take a constant mass and one Cartesian direction. With no external force the equation becomes \(a-\tau\dot a=0\), whose general solution includes \(a=Ae^{t/\tau}\). Standard position and velocity data leave \(A\) undetermined. This runaway is incompatible with a passive free particle. The prescription \(a(t)\to0\) after an applied force has ended removes that branch, but integrating backward gives

\[a(t)=\frac1{m\tau}\int_t^\infty e^{-(s-t)/\tau}F_{\rm ext}(s)\,ds .\]

The integral samples future forces. A discontinuous idealised force can give apparent preacceleration over order \(\tau\). For an electron \(\tau\simeq6.27\times10^{-24}\) s. On such scales an elementary classical point-charge description is inadequate. For smooth forcing varying over \(T\gg\tau\), instead use the leading equation \(a=F_{\rm ext}/m+O(\tau/T)\) on the right. To first order in \(\tau/T\),

\[m a=F_{\rm ext}+\tau\,\frac{dF_{\rm ext}}{dt}.\]

For a force depending on position and velocity, evaluate its total derivative along the leading-order motion. This reduced-order equation selects the smooth, perturbative dynamics without adding independent initial acceleration data.

A spectral width and a phase-resolved budget

Radiative damping of a weakly bound oscillator

Let \(F_{\rm ext}=-m\omega_0^2x\). Then \(\ddot x+\tau\omega_0^2\dot x+\omega_0^2x=0\) to first order. The amplitude falls with e-folding time \(2/(\tau\omega_0^2)\). At \(\omega_0=3.0\times10^{15}\) s⁻¹ the formal electron model gives \(35.4\) ns, with \(\tau\omega_0=1.88\times10^{-8}\). The weak-damping approximation is internally consistent. Actual atomic linewidths additionally depend on quantum matrix elements and other decay channels. The calculation describes a classical bound oscillator with a chosen restoring force.

The same energy viewed at two phases

Impose \(v=v_a\sin\omega t\), so \(a=\omega v_a\cos\omega t\) and \(\dot a=-\omega^2v_a\sin\omega t\). In units \(P_0=m\tau\omega^2v_a^2\), radiation power is \(\cos^2\omega t\) while mechanical self-force power is \(-\sin^2\omega t\). At phase zero all the instantaneous radiation is supplied by decreasing bound-field energy. A quarter period later the radiation vanishes at the corresponding retarded emission phase, but the self-force still removes mechanical energy. Over one period the integrated bound-field term vanishes, so the mean mechanical loss is \(P_0/2\).

Close the energy accounts at a chosen emission phase

The radiated power, the self-force work on the particle and the rate of change of bound-field energy sum to zero at every emission phase. With zero mean speed and phase zero, their dimensionless values are +1, 0 and −1. Shift the mean speed and decide which of the three readings must stay the same.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · how local is radiative friction?

Calculate a complete pulse budget

Take \(a(t)=a_0\sin(\pi t/T)\) for \(0<t<T\), zero elsewhere, with initial velocity zero. Calculate final speed, total radiated energy and the ratio of radiation to final kinetic energy.

Work through the solution

The velocity gain is \(2a_0T/\pi\). Integrating \(a^2\) yields \(E_{\rm rad}=m\tau a_0^2T/2\). The final kinetic energy is \(2ma_0^2T^2/\pi^2\), so the ratio is \(\pi^2\tau/(4T)\). Both endpoints have zero acceleration. The time-integrated external work must therefore equal the sum of the final kinetic energy and radiated energy. Choose \(a_0T\ll c\) and \(T\gg\tau\) for this calculation.

Diagnose a spurious trajectory

A computation with no force doubles its acceleration every few multiples of the electron radiation-reaction time. Would closer timesteps necessarily remove the instability?

Work through the solution

The exact homogeneous third-order equation already has \(a=Ae^{t/\tau}\). A smaller step can track that unphysical branch more accurately. A model restricted to smooth weak radiation reaction instead evolves the reduced second-order equation. In free motion this gives \(a=0\) identically. Compare physical timescales and equations before interpreting a converged numerical trajectory as an observable.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§11.2–11.3 · radiated power and radiation reaction

Chapter 32 · Diffraction from an aperture of finite width

Geometrical optics predicts a sharp edge and an infinitely narrow ideal focus. A scalar diffraction integral describes how contributions with different phase interfere. A slit gives an exact far-field reference against which to test an oscillatory numerical integral.

Accumulate phase across an aperture

Let coherent monochromatic light pass through a slit of full width \(a\) in the plane \(z=0\). The slit is long in its other direction and illuminated by a normally incident uniform plane wave. Work with a scalar component in free space. Suppose observation distance \(z\) is large compared with \(a,|x|\) and the wavelength \(\lambda\). Contributions from aperture point \(\xi\) to detector position \(x\) carry a phase \(kR\), \(k=2\pi/\lambda\). The path expansion is

\[R=\sqrt{z^2+(x-\xi)^2}=z+\frac{(x-\xi)^2}{2z}-\frac{(x-\xi)^4}{8z^3}+\cdots .\]

Approximating slow amplitude and obliquity factors by their axial values while retaining the quadratic phase gives the Fresnel integral. Check the dropped phase \(k|x-\xi|^4/(8z^3)\ll1\) over both the aperture and detector range. Its smallness is more restrictive than simply calling the angle small in a high-frequency problem. The scalar aperture model also assumes that edge polarisation and finite screen thickness are negligible.

Reduce the integral to two dimensionless variables

Set \(s=\xi/a\), \(u=ax/(\lambda z)\) and \(N=a^2/(\lambda z)\). Expanding the square splits off a common phase depending only on \(x,z\). Normalise away that phase and the common geometrical amplitude, leaving the shape integral

\[F(u,N)=\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\exp[i\pi N s^2-2\pi ius]\,ds,\]
\[I_{\rm rel}=|F|^2 .\]

Here \(a\) denotes the full slit width. Definitions using the half-width produce Fresnel numbers four times smaller. The maximum phase of the \(s^2\) term is \(\pi N/4\). This sets a quantitative criterion for dropping it. In the far-field limit \(N\to0\), direct integration of the remaining exponential gives \(F(u,0)=\sin(\pi u)/(\pi u)\) and \(F(0,0)=1\) by continuity. The first zeros occur at \(u=\pm1\). Each slit half has an opposite-phase partner at this observation angle.

Discretise complex amplitude before forming intensity

Divide the slit into \(M\) equal cells. For their midpoint coordinates \(s_j=(j+1/2)/M-1/2\), evaluate \(\phi_j=\pi N s_j^2-2\pi us_j\) and accumulate the real and imaginary parts:

\[F_M=\frac1M\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}(\cos\phi_j+i\sin\phi_j),\]
\[I_M=(\operatorname{Re}F_M)^2+(\operatorname{Im}F_M)^2 .\]

Summing \(|e^{i\phi_j}|^2\) first gives a flat result and loses all interference. An estimate for the largest phase step is \(\pi(N+2|u|)/M\). Reduce it while checking convergence at specified points. For a smooth integrand, composite midpoint quadrature has error of order \(M^{-2}\) in complex amplitude at fixed \(u,N\). Relative error in intensity near a zero can be arbitrarily large. There compare absolute intensities or complex amplitudes. Agreement between a coarse grid and the far-field formula at large \(N\) is not a valid convergence test.

Design a detector scan and identify a false minimum

Choose a distance that resolves the first minimum

A slit has full width \(a=100\) μm in light of \(\lambda=500\) nm. At \(z=1.0\) m the Fresnel number is 0.020 and the dropped aperture phase is at most 0.0157 rad. The first far-field minimum lies at \(x=\lambda z/a=5.0\) mm. At \(z=20\) mm the same slit gives \(N=1\). Dropping 0.785 rad at the aperture edge can materially change the measured distribution. Moving the detector closer also changes the physical width associated with one unit of \(u\).

How much light fills the nominal dark point?

At \(u=1\) the Fraunhofer amplitude vanishes. For small \(N\), expand the chirp to first order. The remaining integral is \(i\pi N\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}s^2e^{-2\pi is}ds\). The imaginary odd integrand vanishes; two integrations by parts give the real integral \(-1/(2\pi^2)\). Hence \(F(1,N)\simeq-iN/(2\pi)\) and \(I_{\rm rel}(1,N)\simeq N^2/(4\pi^2)\). At \(N=0.10\) the predicted residual intensity is \(2.53\times10^{-4}\). A correctly converged computation can have a nonzero value at a nominal far-field dark point.

Integrate the slit and refine the aperture mesh

Here the slit width is the entire opening. At Fresnel number 0.10 the first far-field dark point has residual intensity about 0.000253 on the converged scale. Change the mesh and the Fresnel number independently to distinguish numerical error from a physical filling of the minimum.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · reconstruction and convergence

Use two measured minima

For wavelength 630 nm and distance 1.20 m, the separation of the first minima is 9.0 mm. Recover the slit width and check the implied far-field assumption.

Work through the solution

The distance from the centre to either first zero is 4.5 mm. Thus \(a=\lambda z/x=168\) μm. Then \(N=a^2/(\lambda z)=0.0373\), and the maximum neglected quadratic phase is 0.0293 rad. With the stated precision the use of the far-field formula is plausible. The relevant length in the inverse calculation was half the measured separation.

Choose and validate a quadrature

For \(|u|\leq3\) and \(N=4\), require the estimated phase change between adjacent source nodes to remain below 0.25 rad. What grid size is suggested? How would you estimate the remaining error?

Work through the solution

The bound gives \(M>\pi(4+6)/0.25\simeq126\), so 128 cells meet the criterion. Compare 128 and 256 cells at the centre and near the first fringes. In the second-order convergence regime the fine-grid amplitude error is approximately one third of the coarse-to-fine difference. Verify the trend with 512 cells if high accuracy is needed. Refining the detector sampling alone cannot fix inadequate sampling of the slit.

Further reading

  • Fließbach, chapter 35 · Huygens principle
  • Fließbach, chapter 36 · interference and diffraction

Chapter 33 · Energy, momentum and a charged particle’s worldline

A field may turn the momentum, change its magnitude, or do both. Beginning with a measurable clock carried by the particle gives a force law that remains well behaved even when increasing momentum produces little visible change in speed.

Use ticks of a comoving clock

A massive particle has a timelike worldline. In a laboratory with spacetime coordinates \(x^\mu=(ct,\mathbf x)\), the invariant interval along its path gives \(c^2d\tau^2=c^2dt^2-d\mathbf x^2\), so \(d\tau=dt/\gamma\), \(\gamma=(1-v^2/c^2)^{-1/2}\). Its four-velocity \(u^\mu=dx^\mu/d\tau=\gamma(c,\mathbf v)\) has fixed squared norm \(c^2\). Multiplication by the measured rest mass gives four-momentum \(p^\mu=(\mathcal E/c,\mathbf p)\), with \(\mathcal E=\gamma mc^2\), \(\mathbf p=\gamma m\mathbf v\). Squaring and differentiating at fixed rest mass gives

\[\mathcal E^2=m^2c^4+p^2c^2,\]
\[d\mathcal E=\frac{c^2\mathbf p}{\mathcal E}\cdot d\mathbf p=\mathbf v\cdot d\mathbf p .\]

Use \(\dot{\mathbf p}=q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\). Taking its scalar product with \(\mathbf v\) shows \(d\mathcal E/dt=q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v\). A uniform magnetic field changes momentum direction while conserving its magnitude and energy. Resolving a general force along and across the velocity gives \(F_\parallel=\gamma^3 m a_\parallel\) and \(F_\perp=\gamma m a_\perp\). These factors follow from differentiating \(\gamma\mathbf v\), without assigning the particle a changing rest mass.

Integrate motion in a constant electric field

Choose a positive charge initially at rest with \(E\) along \(+x\), and neglect other forces. A negative charge follows the reflected trajectory. Define \(s=qEt/(mc)\). Lab momentum grows linearly, \(p=qEt\). Substitute it into the energy relation and use \(v=c^2p/\mathcal E\). Integration with \(x(0)=0\) gives

\[\gamma=\sqrt{1+s^2},\]
\[\frac vc=\frac{s}{\sqrt{1+s^2}},\]
\[x=\frac{mc^2}{qE}\left(\sqrt{1+s^2}-1\right).\]

Multiplying the distance by \(qE\) recovers kinetic energy \(\mathcal E-mc^2\). A second check comes from the clock. Integrating \(d\tau=dt/\sqrt{1+s^2}\) gives \(\tau=(mc/qE)\operatorname{arsinh}s\). With rapidity \(\eta=qE\tau/(mc)\), \(s=\sinh\eta,\ \gamma=\cosh\eta\). The comoving proper acceleration is \(qE/m\) even while the laboratory acceleration decreases as \(\gamma^{-3}\). Expanding at early times yields \(v=qEt/m+O(t^3)\) and \(x=qEt^2/(2m)+O(t^4)\).

Read an energy from the curvature of a track

In a uniform magnetic field along \(z\), longitudinal momentum and energy remain fixed. For transverse motion the change in momentum direction has magnitude \(p_\perp v_\perp/r\). Equating it with \(|q|v_\perp B\) gives \(r=p_\perp/(|q|B)\). The angular frequency is \(\omega_c=|q|B/(\gamma m)\), and the longitudinal distance travelled per turn is \(2\pi p_\parallel/(|q|B)\). The sign of the charge determines the sense of rotation for a specified field direction. Radius alone measures transverse momentum. To reconstruct total momentum from a helical path one must also measure the pitch.

Reconstruct two ideal laboratory observations

When does the classical speed fail?

For an electron starting at rest in a uniform field of magnitude 100 kV/m, the momentum magnitude reaches \(mc\) after \(mc/(eE)=17.0\) ns. A Newtonian extrapolation then predicts speed \(c\). The relativistic result is \(c/\sqrt2=0.707c\), kinetic energy \((\sqrt2-1)mc^2=212\) keV and travel distance \(2.12\) m. The potential difference over this distance is 212 kV, giving the same kinetic energy independently. These are ideal constant-field values for a prescribed test particle.

A helical track with a missing component

In \(B=0.20\) T a singly charged particle has circular projection of radius \(5.0\) mm and advances \(20\) mm along the field per revolution. Using elementary charge \(e\), one obtains \(p_\perp=1.60\times10^{-22}\) kg m/s and \(p_\parallel=1.02\times10^{-22}\) kg m/s. For an electron \(p/(mc)=0.696\), \(\gamma\simeq1.22\), and the kinetic energy is \(112\) keV. Assuming the track lay entirely in a transverse plane would underestimate its kinetic energy. A heavier singly charged particle with the same track has the same momentum but a different kinetic energy.

Compare a particle clock with a lab timer

In a 100 kV/m uniform field, an initially stationary electron reaches about 0.706c after 17 ns lab time, while its own clock advances roughly 15 ns. Identify where the Newtonian extrapolation crosses the light-speed line and inspect the kinetic energy at that time.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · frame and trajectory checks

Measure the elapsed proper time

For the electric trajectory starting from rest, let the final momentum be \(2mc\). Find the ratio of elapsed proper time to lab time, and compare it with the instantaneous final \(1/\gamma\).

Work through the solution

Since \(s=2\), the elapsed-time ratio is \(\operatorname{arsinh}(2)/2=0.722\). At the endpoint \(1/\gamma=1/\sqrt5=0.447\). The particle was slower earlier. Applying its final clock rate to the entire trajectory would discard its acceleration history.

Predict an orbit in crossed fields

Take perpendicular fields with \(E/(cB)=0.50\) and a particle initially at rest in the laboratory. Describe its motion in the frame moving at \(\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/B^2\), and recover its average transverse drift direction.

Work through the solution

The boost speed is \(0.50c\). The primed electric field vanishes, the magnetic field is \(B/\gamma_d\) and the initial particle velocity is opposite the boost. Its primed motion is circular with constant speed. After every complete primed period its primed position returns to its starting value. Transforming the displacement between successive returns gives \(\Delta x/\Delta t=v_d\) along the drift direction, independently of the sign of charge. Opposite signs reverse the sense of the primed circle.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §12.2 · relativistic mechanics
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 4 · relativistic particle motion

Chapter 34 · Capacitance as a matrix of electrostatic energies

Three conductors occupy a grounded metal enclosure. Charging one can induce charge on another even when its voltage stays fixed. To predict a measurement we must specify which conductors have fixed voltage, which are isolated and what defines the reference potential.

Construct unit-voltage solutions

Consider a connected dielectric region with stationary geometry, linear reciprocal permittivity tensor \(\boldsymbol\epsilon(\boldsymbol r)\), no free volume charge, and perfectly conducting boundaries. For conductor \(j\), define \(u_j\) as the solution of \(\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol\epsilon\nabla u_j)=0\) which equals one on conductor \(j\) and zero on all other boundaries. When a grounded enclosing wall is used, each of these basis solutions also vanishes on that wall. Linearity and uniqueness give \(\phi(\boldsymbol r)=\sum_j V_j u_j(\boldsymbol r)\). Setting just one conductor to unit voltage determines an entire column of responses, measurable by holding the other potentials fixed.

Take \(\boldsymbol n\) to be the unit normal pointing out of the dielectric, into the conductor. Since \(\boldsymbol D=-\boldsymbol\epsilon\nabla\phi\), the conductor's charge facing the dielectric is \(Q_i=\int_{S_i}\boldsymbol n\cdot\boldsymbol\epsilon\nabla\phi\,dS\). The two sign reversals, one from the electric field and one from the conductor's outward normal, cancel. The coefficients \(C_{ij}=\partial Q_i/\partial V_j\) consequently integrate the flux from each basis solution.

\[Q_i=\sum_jC_{ij}V_j,\]
\[C_{ij}=\int_\Omega \nabla u_i\cdot\boldsymbol\epsilon\nabla u_j\,d^3r .\]

To obtain the volume formula, apply the divergence theorem to \(u_i\boldsymbol\epsilon\nabla u_j\). Only the gradient product survives in the volume. Only boundary \(i\) survives on the surface because it is the sole boundary where \(u_i=1\). Reciprocal permittivity makes the volume formula symmetric under \(i\leftrightarrow j\); capacitance readings must therefore agree when drive and pickup electrodes are exchanged under the same conditions.

Identify the reference and the energy

The electrostatic energy is \(U=\frac12\int\boldsymbol E\cdot\boldsymbol D\,d^3r=\frac12\boldsymbol V^TC\boldsymbol V\). Its derivative with respect to \(V_i\) equals \(Q_i\) at fixed geometry. When the enclosing wall is included as an additional voltage degree of freedom, adding the same constant to all voltages leaves every gradient unchanged. The basis solutions including the wall sum to one. The full matrix then annihilates the vector of ones, its row sums vanish, and energy depends only on potential differences. Fixing the wall voltage removes that null mode. The reduced matrix acting on inner voltages can have nonzero row sums and is positive definite for a connected enclosure with positive permittivity.

For example, two electrodes have pair capacitance \(c=4\) pF, with additional \(g_1=2\) pF and \(g_2=3\) pF from each to the wall. The energy is \([g_1V_1^2+g_2V_2^2+c(V_1-V_2)^2]/2\) in pJ for volt units. Differentiate to obtain reduced \(C_{11}=6,\ C_{22}=7,\ C_{12}=C_{21}=-4\) pF. At \(V_1=2,V_2=-1\) V the charges are \(16,-15\) pC and the energy is 23.5 pJ. Including the wall restores total charge zero for the charges facing the dielectric; the wall carries \(-1\) pC.

Float one terminal and read a different capacitance

Disconnect the second electrode with initial charge zero. Its potential adjusts to satisfy \(0=-cV_1+(g_2+c)V_2\), giving \(V_2=4V_1/7\). Substituting into \(Q_1\) yields \(C_{\rm in}=g_1+c-c^2/(g_2+c)=26/7\) pF. With electrode two grounded instead, \(C_{\rm in}=6\) pF. Both results came from the same electrostatic matrix; a voltmeter or guard changes the boundary conditions. The plot compares the charge delivered as the driven voltage changes. Vary the mutual coupling, and check that eliminating an isolated electrode agrees with differentiating the energy after minimising over its voltage for zero charge.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on calibrated terminal measurements

A measured reduced matrix has diagonal entries 8 and 9 pF and off-diagonal entries \(-3\) pF. Recover the three pair capacitances to one another and to a reference enclosure. Predict the wall charge at \(V_1=1,V_2=2\) V.

Work through the solution

The mutual pair is 3 pF. Subtracting it from the diagonals gives 5 and 6 pF to the wall. The driven charges are \(8-6=2\) pC and \(-3+18=15\) pC. The enclosure-facing surface supplies the remaining \(-17\) pC.

In the worked example electrode two is isolated with a trapped \(Q_2=7\) pC. Determine the voltage induced when \(V_1=0\), and the small-signal input capacitance measured by varying \(V_1\).

Work through the solution

Solve \(7=-4V_1+7V_2\) to find \(V_2=1+4V_1/7\) V. The input charge is then \(-4+(26/7)V_1\) pC. The trapped charge shifts the intercept while leaving the incremental capacitance \(26/7\) pF in this linear dielectric model.

Why can an inferred matrix with a negative eigenvalue fail even if its two diagonal entries are positive?

Work through the solution

Choosing potentials proportional to that eigenvector would make the quadratic field energy negative. In a passive static dielectric with positive permittivity the field integral is nonnegative for every potential pattern. Positivity constrains the off-diagonal terms as well, for two terminals requiring \(C_{11}C_{22}\ge C_{12}^2\).

Further reading · MIT 8.07, conductors and the capacitance matrix.

Chapter 35 · How a conductor admits a field step and a sinusoid

A time-varying magnetic field enters a conducting slab from its surface. A suddenly imposed constant field continues to penetrate with elapsed time. A sinusoid establishes a persistent depth profile. Both behaviours follow from the same initial–boundary value equation.

Reduce Maxwell's equations to a diffusion equation

Use a uniform stationary Ohmic medium with scalar conductivity \(\sigma\) and permeability \(\mu\). Work on length and time scales for which displacement current is small compared with conduction current, \(\omega\epsilon/\sigma\ll1\) for each important Fourier component. Then \(\nabla\times\boldsymbol B=\mu\sigma\boldsymbol E\). Taking a curl and using Faraday's law gives \(\nabla\times\nabla\times\boldsymbol B=-\mu\sigma\partial_t\boldsymbol B\). The identity \(\nabla\times\nabla\times\boldsymbol B=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol B)-\nabla^2\boldsymbol B\), together with absence of magnetic monopoles, leaves

\[\partial_t\boldsymbol B=D_m\nabla^2\boldsymbol B,\]
\[D_m=\frac1{\mu\sigma}.\]

\(D_m\) has units of area per time. Larger conductivity slows penetration: a small electric field can drive larger currents that oppose the changing applied field. An ideal mathematical step contains arbitrarily high frequencies outside this reduction. The result describes observations made after the brief electromagnetic start-up, on distances large enough for a continuum Ohmic description.

Solve the initial–boundary value problem for a step

Fill \(x>0\) with conductor and impose a tangential field \(B(0,t)=B_0\) for \(t>0\), with the interior initially field-free. At each finite time require \(B(x,t)\to0\) as \(x\to\infty\). There is no imposed length scale, so try \(B/B_0=F(\eta)\) with \(\eta=x/(2\sqrt{D_mt})\). The time derivative produces \(-\eta F'/(2t)\); the second space derivative times \(D_m\) gives \(F''/(4t)\). Therefore \(F''+2\eta F'=0\). First integrate \(F'=Ae^{-\eta^2}\), then impose \(F(0)=1,F(\infty)=0\). The normalisation \(\int_0^\infty e^{-s^2}ds=\sqrt\pi/2\) gives

\[\frac{B(x,t)}{B_0}=\operatorname{erfc}\!\left(\frac{x}{2\sqrt{D_mt}}\right).\]

Any fixed fractional field level moves with \(\sqrt t\). The depth at half amplitude is \(x_{1/2}=2\operatorname{erfc}^{-1}(0.5)\sqrt{D_mt}=0.9539\sqrt{D_mt}\). For copper represented by \(\sigma=5.8\times10^7\,\mathrm{S/m}\), \(\mu=\mu_0\), \(D_m=0.01372\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). At \(0.10\,\mathrm{ms}\) the half-depth is \(1.12\,\mathrm{mm}\). Reaching twice that depth requires \(0.40\,\mathrm{ms}\).

Solve for a periodic steady state

At the same surface impose \(B(0,t)=\operatorname{Re}B_0e^{i\omega t}\) for long enough that the transient has passed. Seek \(B=\operatorname{Re}B_0 e^{i\omega t-kx}\). Substitution gives \(D_m k^2=i\omega\). Select the root with positive real part, \(k=(1+i)/\delta\) with \(\delta=\sqrt{2D_m/\omega}\). Thus

\[B(x,t)=B_0e^{-x/\delta}\cos(\omega t-x/\delta).\]

At one skin depth the amplitude is \(e^{-1}\) of the surface amplitude and the oscillation lags by one radian. For the same model copper at 1 kHz, \(\delta=2.09\,\mathrm{mm}\), while the sinusoidal half-amplitude depth is \(\delta\ln2=1.45\,\mathrm{mm}\). A half-step depth and an exponential e-fold depth are different measurement definitions. Frequency and elapsed time are independent controls in the two experiments. In a slab comparable in thickness to either penetration scale the second interface supplies another boundary condition.

Read the two profiles at a shared probe

The graph plots the step response and the sinusoidal envelope against physical depth. Place the probe at 2 mm, vary the step duration and check the square-root law using the half-field depth. Hold that time fixed while quadrupling frequency. Only the periodic envelope shrinks by a factor of two in length. Time-dependent field and envelope both use normalised field-amplitude units.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on inversion and currents

A step profile has half-field depth \(2.0\,\mathrm{mm}\) after \(0.30\,\mathrm{ms}\). Infer \(D_m\) and \(\sigma\) if \(\mu=\mu_0\).

Work through the solution

\(D_m=(0.002/0.9539)^2/0.00030=0.01465\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). Conductivity is \(1/(\mu_0D_m)=5.43\times10^7\,\mathrm{S/m}\). A single profile measures the product \(\mu\sigma\) unless permeability is independently known.

If \(B=B_z(x,t)\), find the sign and surface magnitude of \(J_y\) for a positive ideal step. Compare the current integrated over depth with the imposed field.

Work through the solution

Ampère gives \(J_y=-\partial_xB_z/\mu\). The surface derivative is \(-B_0/\sqrt{\pi D_mt}\), hence current is positive and its magnitude falls as \(t^{-1/2}\). Its integral over all \(x>0\) is \(B_0/\mu\). The current spreads over a growing region while maintaining the total sheet current dictated by the field difference.

At a probe the AC envelope is 0.10 of the surface amplitude. What is the phase lag, and what is its time-delay equivalent at 1 kHz?

Work through the solution

Solve \(e^{-x/\delta}=0.1\), so \(x/\delta=\ln10=2.303\) rad. At 1 kHz this equals \(2.303/(2\pi\,1000)=0.367\,\mathrm{ms}\). This is the phase delay of a single sinusoid. A pulse contains many frequencies with different attenuation and phase shifts.

Further reading · MIT Electromechanical Dynamics, magnetic diffusion and sinusoidal skin effect.

Chapter 36 · Static screening and collective charge oscillations

In a neutral ion–electron gas a small positive probe draws an excess of electrons. A displaced electron fluid also experiences a restoring electric field and can oscillate. The equilibrium screening length and the collective frequency involve the same density but describe different experiments.

Count the electrons in a weak electrostatic potential

Assume fixed singly charged positive ions of uniform density \(n_0\), and classical electrons in local thermal equilibrium at temperature \(T_e\). Electron potential energy in an electric potential \(\phi\) is \(-e\phi\), where \(e>0\). The Boltzmann distribution relative to distant neutral plasma is \(n_e=n_0\exp[e\phi/(k_BT_e)]\). The net plasma charge density \(e(n_0-n_e)\) is negative near a small positive potential. Expand the exponential when \(|e\phi|\ll k_BT_e\). Including a prescribed weak external charge density gives

\[(\nabla^2-\lambda_D^{-2})\phi=-\rho_{\rm ext}/\epsilon_0,\]
\[\lambda_D^2=\frac{\epsilon_0 k_BT_e}{n_0e^2}.\]

For a point source in an unbounded uniform plasma the source-free spherical equation at \(r>0\) is \((r\phi)''=(r\phi)/\lambda_D^2\). Discard the growing exponential at infinity. The flux through a sufficiently small sphere fixes the coefficient of the \(1/r\) singularity, yielding \(\phi=Qe^{-r/\lambda_D}/(4\pi\epsilon_0 r)\) as the ideal linear Green function. A literal point charge makes \(\phi\) large arbitrarily close to it. Applying this linear expression as a microscopic prediction there would violate the assumption that produced it.

At \(r=\lambda_D\) the potential is \(e^{-1}\) times the bare Coulomb value at that same radius. The electric field is its derivative, so its ratio to the unscreened field is \((1+r/\lambda_D)e^{-r/\lambda_D}\). At one Debye length this is \(2/e=0.736\). Equating the field ratio to the potential ratio would incorrectly remove a derivative term. If several mobile thermal species can equilibrate, the linear charge responses add, giving inverse squared screening lengths that add.

Displace the electron fluid before the ions move

Now consider a rapid, small longitudinal displacement \(\xi(x,t)\) of a cold electron fluid against the uniform positive background. Particle conservation gives density perturbation \(\delta n=-n_0\partial_x\xi\). The resulting charge perturbation is \(e n_0\partial_x\xi\). For a nonzero spatial Fourier mode Gauss's law gives \(E=en_0\xi/\epsilon_0\), with any uniform externally imposed field excluded. Newton's law \(m_e\ddot\xi=-eE\) then gives

\[\ddot\xi+\omega_p^2\xi=0,\]
\[\omega_p^2=\frac{n_0e^2}{m_e\epsilon_0},\]
\[\omega_p\lambda_D=\sqrt{\frac{k_BT_e}{m_e}}.\]

This frequency is in radians per second. The oscillation cycles at \(f_p=\omega_p/(2\pi)\). The length–frequency product equals a one-dimensional thermal-speed scale. The derivations used different closures: the static density had time to approach thermal equilibrium, while the oscillation neglected pressure gradients, collisions and ion motion. At appreciable \(k\lambda_D\), pressure and kinetic effects change the longitudinal frequency and damping. A fully kinetic model is required to predict Landau damping.

Infer density and temperature from two measurements

At \(n_0=10^{16}\,\mathrm{m^{-3}}\) and \(k_BT_e=2\,\mathrm{eV}\), the expressions give \(\lambda_D=105\,\mu\mathrm m\) and \(f_p=898\,\mathrm{MHz}\). Doubling temperature alone increases screening length by \(\sqrt2\) and leaves this cold oscillation frequency fixed. Quadrupling density at fixed temperature halves the length and doubles the frequency. With a measured frequency \(1.80\,\mathrm{GHz}\), density would be about four times the above value. An independently measured \(\lambda_D\) then determines \(k_BT_e=m_e(2\pi f_p\lambda_D)^2\).

Set density and temperature in the graph and compare screened potential and field ratios at a selected physical distance. Use the readout to turn an oscillation period into a density estimate. Check the continuum hypothesis by estimating \(N_D=4\pi n_0\lambda_D^3/3\), the number of particles in a Debye sphere. It is approximately \(4.9\times10^4\) at the worked point, making a collective description plausible. A small \(N_D\), degeneracy or strong coupling would undermine the thermal mean-field treatment.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on independent spatial and temporal tests

A probe measures \(f_p=0.90\,\mathrm{GHz}\) and \(\lambda_D=210\,\mu\mathrm m\). Infer approximate density and thermal energy relative to the worked example.

Work through the solution

The frequency is essentially unchanged, so density is approximately \(10^{16}\,\mathrm{m^{-3}}\). The doubled screening length at fixed density requires four times the thermal energy, about 8 eV. The independent product check gives a thermal speed of roughly \(1.19\times10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\).

How far from a weak source does the screened potential fall to 1 percent of the bare value? Calculate the field ratio at that point.

Work through the solution

The distance is \(r=\lambda_D\ln100=4.605\lambda_D\). The field ratio is \((1+4.605)/100=0.0561\). These are ratios to the appropriate unscreened quantities at the same physical position.

If both electrons and singly ionised positive ions with equal temperature and density can rearrange in the static experiment, compare the new screening length with the electron-only value. Does this change the electron-frequency formula on a time scale where ions are stationary?

Work through the solution

Both species respond to weak potential with charge-density changes proportional to \(-q_s^2 n_s\phi/(k_BT_s)\). The two identical inverse squared lengths add, yielding \(\lambda_D/\sqrt2\). On the much shorter oscillation time scale the ions remain the fixed background in the cold electron derivation.

Further reading · MIT Introduction to Plasma Physics, shielding and plasma oscillations.

Chapter 37 · Reconstructing a partially polarised electromagnetic wave

A detector averages enormous numbers of optical cycles. Its horizontal and vertical readings may be equal for a circularly polarised wave, a diagonal linearly polarised wave, or unpolarised light. Which additional intensities distinguish these preparations?

Start from what a polarising analyser measures

Take a quasi-monochromatic beam propagating in a fixed direction, resolved in transverse axes \(x,y\). Use complex field amplitudes with time factor \(e^{-i\omega t}\). Absorb common power and impedance factors into their units. The time- or ensemble-averaged coherency matrix \(J=\langle\boldsymbol E\boldsymbol E^\dagger\rangle\) has four real degrees of freedom because it is Hermitian. An analyser selecting the normalised complex state \(\boldsymbol a\) passes amplitude \(\boldsymbol a^\dagger\boldsymbol E\) and measures \(I_a=\boldsymbol a^\dagger J\boldsymbol a\). Thus every analyser projects the same matrix onto a distinct polarisation.

Choose \(S_0=J_{xx}+J_{yy}\), \(S_1=J_{xx}-J_{yy}\), \(S_2=2\operatorname{Re}J_{xy}\), and \(S_3=-2\operatorname{Im}J_{xy}\). For this convention the state \((1,i)/\sqrt2\) has positive \(S_3\). Naming it by its explicit Jones vector avoids a handedness ambiguity between observer viewing directions. Inverting those definitions gives

\[J=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}S_0+S_1&S_2-iS_3\\S_2+iS_3&S_0-S_1\end{pmatrix}.\]

Horizontal and vertical linear analysers therefore give \((S_0\pm S_1)/2\). The \(+45^\circ,-45^\circ\) pair gives \((S_0\pm S_2)/2\). The circular pair \((1,\pm i)/\sqrt2\) gives \((S_0\pm S_3)/2\). Every pair sum recovers total intensity, providing an internal calibration check. Subtracting the complementary measurements cancels a common additive detector offset in each Stokes difference, though \(S_0\) still needs a dark correction.

Derive the physically allowed domain

Because \(I_a\ge0\) for every analyser, both eigenvalues of \(J\) are nonnegative. Solve its quadratic characteristic equation using the trace \(S_0\) and determinant \((S_0^2-S_1^2-S_2^2-S_3^2)/4\). The eigenvalues are \((S_0\pm|\boldsymbol S|)/2\), where \(\boldsymbol S=(S_1,S_2,S_3)\). Consequently \(P=|\boldsymbol S|/S_0\) lies between zero and one for a beam with nonzero intensity. A single fully polarised Jones state gives rank one and \(P=1\). An incoherent mixture of two orthogonal states of equal intensity has \(J=(S_0/2)I\) and \(P=0\).

Suppose a corrected data set gives \(I_x,I_y=70,30\); \(I_{+45},I_{-45}=65,35\); \(I_+,I_-=60,40\) in the same units. The pair sums agree at 100. Reconstructed parameters are \((100,40,30,20)\), giving \(P=\sqrt{2900}/100=0.539\). The eigenvalues are 76.93 and 23.07. Subtracting the smaller times the identity leaves a rank-one contribution of intensity 53.85 and an unpolarised remainder of intensity 46.15. This decomposition describes the observed second moments; several microscopic preparations can produce the same \(J\).

Predict a rotating analyser and identify its blind spot

For \(\boldsymbol a=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha)\), expanding the projection and using double-angle identities gives \(I_\alpha=[S_0+S_1\cos2\alpha+S_2\sin2\alpha]/2\). A linear analyser alone is blind to \(S_3\), even when sampled at hundreds of angles. In the example the extrema are \(50\pm25\), with the maximum at \(\alpha=\frac12\operatorname{atan2}(30,40)=18.43^\circ\). The linear fringe visibility 0.50 is slightly below the total degree 0.539. Inserting a quarter-wave plate with Jones matrix \(\operatorname{diag}(1,-i)\) before the same analyser transforms \(S_2\) into \(S_3\) in the intensity formula. One can now obtain the previously hidden signed correlation from readings at \(45^\circ\) and \(135^\circ\).

The experiment prepares an adjustable polarised fraction with azimuth \(\psi\) and ellipticity angle \(\chi\), mixed with an unpolarised background. Its normalised Stokes vector is \(P(\cos2\chi\cos2\psi,\cos2\chi\sin2\psi,\sin2\chi)\). Make the unassisted intensity scan flat by choosing circular polarisation, then insert the fixed retarder virtually and predict the recovered difference. Setting \(P=0\) makes both scans flat. This comparison shows which measurement discriminates the two cases.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on instrument design

A linear scan alone has \(I_{\max}=75,I_{\min}=25\). Give the range of total degrees of polarisation allowed and the maximum possible magnitude of \(S_3\).

Work through the solution

The linear magnitude is 50 and \(S_0=100\). Thus \(0.5\le P\le1\), and the positivity bound gives \(|S_3|\le\sqrt{100^2-50^2}=86.60\). A circular analyser measurement fixes the remaining magnitude and sign.

An ideal beam contains 60 units of pure horizontal light and 40 units of unpolarised light. Compare its Stokes vector with a coherent superposition of horizontal and vertical components with intensities 80 and 20 and zero relative phase.

Work through the solution

The incoherent mixture has \((100,60,0,0)\) and \(P=0.6\). The coherent preparation has \((100,60,80,0)\) since \(2\sqrt{80\times20}=80\), and \(P=1\). Their horizontal and vertical readings coincide. A diagonal analyser gives 50 for the mixture and 90 for the coherent preparation.

All three measured component differences equal 70 at measured total 100. Can a physical beam have these parameters?

Work through the solution

They imply \(|\boldsymbol S|=70\sqrt3=121.2>S_0\) and a negative matrix eigenvalue. Check dark subtraction, gain calibration, alignment and whether the source changed between readings. A physical fit with stated uncertainties is required before interpreting the data as an optical state.

Further reading · ETH Zürich, Stenflo on coherency and Stokes parameters.

Chapter 38 · Recovering a vector field from divergence, curl and boundary data

A numerical field solver returns three components at every point. Divergence measures sources and curl measures circulation, yet neither measurement detects a spatially uniform offset. Helmholtz decomposition makes precise what can be recovered, how to compute it, and what the boundary must supply.

Invert the vector Laplacian in open space

Let \(\boldsymbol F\) be smooth on all of space and rapidly decreasing, together with its needed derivatives, so integration by parts has no surface term at infinity. The identity \(\nabla^2\boldsymbol F=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol F)-\nabla\times(\nabla\times\boldsymbol F)\) reduces reconstruction to three scalar Poisson inversions. Define \(G(\boldsymbol r)=1/(4\pi|\boldsymbol r|)\); distributionally \(-\nabla^2G=\delta^3\). For \(d=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol F\) and \(\boldsymbol c=\nabla\times\boldsymbol F\), convolution with \(G\) gives

\[\Phi=G*d,\]
\[\boldsymbol A=G*\boldsymbol c,\]
\[\boldsymbol F=-\nabla\Phi+\nabla\times\boldsymbol A .\]

To verify, first note that \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol c=0\); a supplied curl field must satisfy this compatibility condition. Moving the derivative through convolution gives \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol A=G*(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol c)=0\). The divergence of the proposed \(\boldsymbol F\) is \(-\nabla^2\Phi=d\), and its curl is \(\nabla(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol A)-\nabla^2\boldsymbol A=\boldsymbol c\). Any difference from the original field has zero divergence and curl, hence componentwise zero Laplacian. Smoothness and decay on all space force this harmonic difference to vanish. The reconstructed vector pieces are unique in this setting, while adding a gradient to an unrestricted vector potential still leaves its curl unchanged.

Derive the Fourier projectors

On a periodic grid separate the mean mode first. For a nonzero Fourier wavevector \(\boldsymbol k\) with convention \(e^{i\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol r}\), differentiation multiplies by \(i\boldsymbol k\). A longitudinal component is parallel to \(\boldsymbol k\), so its amplitude must reproduce the scalar product \(\boldsymbol k\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol F}\). Subtraction leaves a perpendicular component:

\[\widehat{\boldsymbol F}_L=\frac{\boldsymbol k(\boldsymbol k\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol F})}{|\boldsymbol k|^2},\]
\[\widehat{\boldsymbol F}_T=\widehat{\boldsymbol F}-\widehat{\boldsymbol F}_L.\]

The first projector squared equals itself, and the two pieces are orthogonal. Thus their squared magnitudes add mode by mode, even for complex amplitudes with the Hermitian inner product. Equivalently the longitudinal piece has zero curl and the transverse piece has zero divergence. At \(\boldsymbol k=0\), the denominator vanishes and both derivative measurements are zero. A known mean, applied uniform field or boundary condition supplies that information. Setting the mean to zero silently would discard an actual applied bias.

Recover a patterned measurement

Take a two-dimensional periodic field \(\boldsymbol F=\boldsymbol b+\boldsymbol a\cos(2x+y)\) in dimensionless periodic coordinates with \(\boldsymbol a=(3,1)\), \(\boldsymbol b=(1,0)\). Its measured divergence is \(-7\sin(2x+y)\). Its out-of-plane curl is \(+\sin(2x+y)\), from \(\partial_xF_y-\partial_yF_x\). Projection with \(\boldsymbol k=(2,1)\) gives \(\boldsymbol a_L=(2.8,1.4)\), \(\boldsymbol a_T=(0.2,-0.4)\). The squared amplitudes are 9.8 and 0.2, summing to the original 10; only 2 percent of the nonconstant modal power is transverse. Neither differentiated pattern contains the bias. At phase zero the observed x-component is four, of which 2.8 is longitudinal, 0.2 transverse and one is uniform.

The graph scans phase, showing the x-component of the measured field, the two recovered varying pieces and the mean. Change the input amplitudes until \(\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol a=0\), then until \(\boldsymbol k\times\boldsymbol a=0\). Changing the mean should move only the total and constant terms. In a discretised solver the differentiation symbol used in a projection must match the numerical divergence being tested; the exact spectral \(i\boldsymbol k\) and a finite difference need not represent the same grid operator.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Specify a boundary on a finite domain

One convenient decomposition writes \(\boldsymbol F=\nabla u+\boldsymbol T\) with \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol T=0\) and \(\boldsymbol T\cdot\boldsymbol n=0\) on the boundary. Solve the Neumann problem \(\nabla^2u=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol F,\ \partial_nu=\boldsymbol F\cdot\boldsymbol n\). The divergence theorem makes these data compatible. The potential is determined up to an additive constant in a connected domain, which does not affect its gradient. Orthogonality follows from integrating \(\nabla u\cdot\boldsymbol T\) by parts: both the volume divergence and boundary normal term vanish. Holes can admit nontrivial fields with zero curl and divergence, so reconstructing a full vector field from its derivatives alone can require additional circulation data.

Tutorial on recovering missing information

With \(\boldsymbol k=(2,1)\), a sensor returns divergence \(-5\sin(2x+y)\) and curl \(-5\sin(2x+y)\). Infer the cosine amplitudes \(a_x,a_y\).

Work through the solution

From the divergence, \(2a_x+a_y=5\). From the curl, \(2a_y-a_x=5\). Solving gives \(a_x=1,a_y=3\). The projected amplitudes are \((2,1)\) and \((-1,2)\), with equal squared magnitude. The mean remains independent of both readings.

On an annulus \(a<r<b\), consider \(\boldsymbol H=\boldsymbol e_\theta/r\). Which derivative and boundary data vanish? Propose one measurement that distinguishes it from zero.

Work through the solution

Its planar divergence vanishes and its out-of-plane curl is \(r^{-1}\partial_r(rH_\theta)=0\) on the annulus. Its normal component vanishes at both circular boundaries. Nevertheless circulation around any circle surrounding the hole is \(2\pi\). The axis where the field is singular is excluded, so the all-space uniqueness argument does not apply.

A computed projection claims longitudinal power 6, transverse power 4, and original power 13 in one nonzero Fourier mode. Is this consistent with exact orthogonal projection in the same norm?

Work through the solution

No. The two projected vectors are perpendicular, so their powers must sum to the original 13. Check whether powers include the same normalisation, whether the mean was included, and whether the solver and diagnostic use the same differentiation symbol and inner product.

Further reading · University of Virginia, Helmholtz's theorem and uniqueness · Rochester electromagnetic theory, transverse and longitudinal projections.

Chapter 39 · A ring, a disk and an electric-field maximum in empty space

Gauss’s law gives the flux through a closed surface. For a finite circular source the field varies across that surface. Adding the fields of actual charge elements predicts both the near field and a surprising axial maximum when the centre is removed.

Decide what symmetry eliminates

A uniformly charged ring of radius \(s\) lies in the \(xy\) plane. A detector on the \(z\) axis is a distance \(R=\sqrt{s^2+z^2}\) from every charge element. Contributions transverse to the axis cancel in diametrically opposite pairs. The potential adds as a scalar, giving \(dV=dq/(4\pi\epsilon_0 R)\) for the whole ring charge \(dq\). Choose zero potential at infinity. Differentiating with respect to detector position gives \(dE_z=z\,dq/(4\pi\epsilon_0R^3)\). The same factor \(z/R\) appears if each Coulomb force is projected directly onto the axis. At the ring centre the potential is finite and the field is zero.

Construct an annulus with uniform surface density \(\sigma\), inner radius \(a\) and outer radius \(b\). A radial interval \(ds\) contributes \(dq=2\pi\sigma s\,ds\); forgetting \(s\) would assign equal charge to equal widths at the centre and rim. Integrating and differentiating yields

\[V(z)=\frac{\sigma}{2\epsilon_0}\left[\sqrt{z^2+b^2}-\sqrt{z^2+a^2}\right],\]
\[E_z(z)=\frac{\sigma z}{2\epsilon_0}\left[\frac1{\sqrt{z^2+a^2}}-\frac1{\sqrt{z^2+b^2}}\right].\]

The potential is even in \(z\) and the field odd. When \(a>0\) every point on the central axis is in empty space and both functions are smooth there. This smoothness changes in the disk limit.

Investigate two noncommuting limits

Set \(a=0\) before moving the probe to the plane. The potential then contains \(-|z|\) and the field contains \(\operatorname{sgn}(z)\). Thus \(E_z(0^+)-E_z(0^-)=\sigma/\epsilon_0\), just the charge-sheet jump. By contrast, taking \(z\to0\) first at any positive hole radius gives zero field. The order of shrinking the hole and approaching its centre determines whether the probe sits on material. A grid that places its probe exactly at \(z=0\) for both geometries can conceal the difference.

At large positive \(z\), Taylor-expand the two square roots before subtracting. The \(z\) terms cancel. Since \(Q=\pi\sigma(b^2-a^2)\),

\[V(z)=\frac{Q}{4\pi\epsilon_0z}\left[1-\frac{a^2+b^2}{4z^2}+O(b^4/z^4)\right],\]
\[E_z=\frac{Q}{4\pi\epsilon_0z^2}\left[1-\frac{3(a^2+b^2)}{4z^2}+\cdots\right].\]

The axial dipole term is absent by reflection symmetry. For very large \(z\) a numerical difference between almost identical square roots loses precision. Multiply by the conjugate to evaluate \(V=\sigma(b^2-a^2)/[2\epsilon_0(\sqrt{z^2+b^2}+\sqrt{z^2+a^2})]\).

Find an extremum without violating the vacuum field equations

For a nonzero hole the field grows linearly near the origin and decreases as an inverse square in the far field. Differentiate \(z/\sqrt{z^2+s^2}\) to get \(s^2/(z^2+s^2)^{3/2}\). Equating the two edge contributions gives

\[z_{\max}^2=\frac{b^2a^{4/3}-a^2b^{4/3}}{b^{4/3}-a^{4/3}} .\]

This is a maximum of one component along one line. No interior maximum of the electrostatic potential is implied. A lateral displacement would break the ring-pair cancellation and requires integration over the azimuthal coordinate. On axis the field also equals the difference between the fields of two disks of common density.

Plan two field measurements

Locate a peak in a centimetre-scale annulus

Choose \(a=10\) mm, \(b=20\) mm and \(\sigma=1.0\) nC/m². The maximum occurs at \(z=0.4934b=9.87\) mm. At this point \(2\epsilon_0 E_z/\sigma=0.25992\), so \(E_z=14.7\) V/m. A point charge approximation would incorrectly predict that the field increases indefinitely as the detector approaches the hole. The missing charge near the centre matters even though the whole source has nonzero net charge.

Turn a desired error into a measurement distance

For the same radius ratio the leading relative correction to the monopole field is \(0.9375(b/z)^2\). To keep this correction near 1%, choose \(z/b\simeq9.68\), about 0.194 m here. Substituting this distance in the exact expression checks the term neglected in the estimate. For a radial charge distribution with changing sign the monopole could cancel altogether, invalidating the inference.

Integrate an annulus ring by ring

The graph compares midpoint integration over physical ring areas against the analytic axial field. A hole radius of half the outer radius puts the maximum at z/b = 0.4934. Move the inner edge and refine the source cells separately. A changing peak location and a shrinking numerical error have different causes.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · what can an axial probe tell you?

Locate the peak of a narrow ring

Shrink the annulus to a narrow ring of fixed total charge and radius \(a\). Find the field maximum directly and compare the answer with the picture above.

Work through the solution

The ring field is proportional to \(z/(z^2+a^2)^{3/2}\). Its derivative is proportional to \((a^2-2z^2)/(z^2+a^2)^{5/2}\). Thus the maximum on the positive axis occurs at \(a/\sqrt2\). Keeping total charge fixed requires the surface density to grow as the width shrinks.

Compute with a nonuniform surface density

Suppose the density increases linearly from zero at the centre of a disk to \(\sigma_0\) at its outer radius \(b\). Describe an integration rule and give two independent checks that do not require an analytic field formula.

Work through the solution

Divide the radius into cells with midpoint \(s_j\). Assign each cell its exact area \(\pi(s_{j,+}^2-s_{j,-}^2)\), multiply by \(\sigma_0s_j/b\), and add the axial ring field. The summed source charge must converge to \(2\pi\sigma_0 b^2/3\). At large \(z\) the computed field times \(4\pi\epsilon_0z^2\) approaches this same charge. At each fixed positive \(z\), double the cell count and check convergence separately from the far-field comparison.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§2.1.4, 2.3.4 and 3.4 · integration and distant fields
  • Fließbach, chapter 5 · Coulomb law

Chapter 40 · The same metal sphere connected to ground or electrically isolated

A grounded sphere can draw charge through a wire. An isolated one can only rearrange what is already present. Both are equipotentials, but the second problem needs a different image system and gives a different force at long range.

Find the image by matching distances on a sphere

A positive test charge \(q\) sits at \(z=d>a\) outside a conducting sphere of radius \(a\) centred at the origin. In the exterior vacuum region the potential has the known Coulomb singularity at \(q\) and is otherwise harmonic. Look for an auxiliary point \(s\hat{\mathbf z}\) inside the metal whose distance from each surface point is proportional to the distance from the real charge. The squared distances at polar angle \(\theta\) are \(a^2+s^2-2as\cos\theta\) and \(a^2+d^2-2ad\cos\theta\). Matching their constant and cosine terms yields \(s=a^2/d\) and \(R_s=(a/d)R_q\).

A charge \(q'=-qa/d\) at the auxiliary point then cancels the real potential on the sphere. The superposition vanishes at infinity. Uniqueness for prescribed potentials makes this the actual exterior solution. The mathematical source is outside the physical domain of the solution. A surface enclosing just the conductor can be evaluated using the equivalent exterior field, so the total induced charge is \(q'\). It arrived through the grounding connection.

Change the constraint by adding one image

For an isolated sphere of prescribed total charge \(Q_c\), its surface potential is a constant that must be determined. Add a central charge \(q_0=Q_c-q'\). Its surface contribution \(q_0/(4\pi\epsilon_0 a)\) is uniform, preserving equipotentiality, while the total equivalent enclosed charge becomes \(Q_c\). The resulting sphere potential is \(V_s=Q_c/(4\pi\epsilon_0a)+q/(4\pi\epsilon_0d)\). The grounded and neutral isolated solutions agree only when the geometry or external charge makes their net-charge constraints coincide.

Use Gauss's boundary condition \(\sigma=-\epsilon_0\partial_r V|_{a^+}\). The grounded result and its modification are

\[\sigma_g(\theta)=-\frac{q}{4\pi a}\frac{d^2-a^2}{(d^2+a^2-2ad\cos\theta)^{3/2}},\]
\[\sigma_c(\theta)=\sigma_g(\theta)+\frac{q_0}{4\pi a^2}.\]

For a neutral sphere the charge layer facing the source is negative, while a sufficiently distant part of the surface becomes positive. Integrating over the sphere involves the area element \(2\pi a^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\). A uniform angular average without that weight miscounts the sharply concentrated polar charge.

Take a force at the real charge, then take a large-distance limit

Evaluate the electric field of the image charges only at the real charge. The grounded force \(F_z\) and its neutral-isolated counterpart are

\[F_g=-\frac{q^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\frac{ad}{(d^2-a^2)^2},\]
\[F_{\rm neutral}=F_g+\frac{q^2}{4\pi\epsilon_0}\frac{a}{d^3}.\]

At \(d\gg a\), the grounded force begins at order \(d^{-3}\) because the grounded sphere acquires charge proportional to \(1/d\). In the neutral case that leading term cancels, leaving \(-2q^2 a^3/(4\pi\epsilon_0 d^5)\). The conductor responds mainly as an induced dipole. This cancellation offers a demanding numerical check at large separation. For the grounded case the energy relative to infinite separation follows from integrating \(-F_g\) with respect to \(d\), yielding \(U=-q^2a/[8\pi\epsilon_0(d^2-a^2)]\). It also equals \(qV_{\rm image}(d)/2\), with the half arising as the conductor polarises continuously during assembly.

Compare two preparations with identical geometry

Unplug a sphere before approaching it

Set \(a=20\) mm, \(d=40\) mm and \(q=1.0\) nC. The auxiliary image lies at 10 mm and has charge \(-0.50\) nC. Grounding gives \(F_g=-4.99\) μN. For a neutral isolated sphere the added central \(+0.50\) nC exerts \(+2.81\) μN and the total is \(-2.18\) μN. Its surface potential is \(q/(4\pi\epsilon_0d)\simeq225\) V. In both experiments the metal still has no interior electric field in static equilibrium.

Disconnect after the charge is already present

If the wire is removed at \(d=2a\), the sphere keeps its acquired \(-q/2\). With the real charge still at \(2a\) the exterior solution is unchanged. Move it to \(4a\). The new off-centre image is \(-q/4\) and the central image is also \(-q/4\). The net conductor charge remains \(-q/2\), and its potential becomes negative. A calculation that continually sets the sphere voltage to zero after disconnection would model an ongoing exchange of charge with an external reservoir.

Measure the charge density of two spheres with different connections

With the source at two sphere radii, the grounded surface draws a total charge −q/2; the neutral isolated sphere retains zero total charge. Change the distance and the trapped initial conductor charge. Inspect the source-facing pole and the far side before deciding whether a net force can be repulsive.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · charge balance and a near-surface limit

Recover a plane image from a curved surface

Let \(d=a+h\) with \(h\ll a\). Show that the near-surface force on the charge approaches the grounded plane result and determine when the central neutralising image matters.

Work through the solution

Use \(d^2-a^2=2ah+h^2\). The leading grounded force is \(-q^2/(16\pi\epsilon_0h^2)\), identical to attraction to an image \(-q\) a distance \(2h\) away. The central neutralising field is of order \(q/(4\pi\epsilon_0a^2)\), smaller by order \((h/a)^2\) in the force budget.

Where does a neutral sphere change surface charge sign?

For \(d=2a\) and \(Q_c=0\), solve \(\sigma_c=0\) for the polar angle. How can a sphere still attract the external charge with zero net charge?

Work through the solution

In units \(q/(4\pi a^2)\) the equation is \(-3/(5-4\cos\theta)^{3/2}+1/2=0\). Therefore \(\cos\theta=(5-6^{2/3})/4\) and the sign change occurs near \(65^\circ\). Negative surface charge is closer to the positive real charge; the inverse-square field weights its attraction more strongly than repulsion by distant positive charge.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§2.5, 3.1.6, 3.2.4 · conductors and image methods
  • Fließbach, chapter 7 · boundary-value problems

Chapter 41 · How a circular wall selects Bessel modes

A voltage pattern is imposed on the end of a long grounded tube. Its penetration length depends on the tube radius through a number that looks unfamiliar in a Cartesian Fourier series. Deriving that number also explains the proper radial weight for projecting data.

Separate Laplace’s equation without flattening the circular wall

Take an empty cylinder of radius \(a\) extending from \(z=0\) to large positive \(z\). Its side is grounded. A segmented end electrode imposes the continuous axisymmetric potential \(V(r,0)=V_0[1-(r/a)^2]\), matching zero at the rim. In cylindrical coordinates \(\nabla^2V=r^{-1}\partial_r(r\partial_rV)+\partial_z^2V=0\). Set \(V=R(r)Z(z)\). A decaying solution requires \(Z=e^{-kz}\) with \(k>0\). Then

\[(rR')'+k^2rR=0 .\]

Using \(\xi=kr\) gives Bessel's equation of order zero. The regular solution \(J_0(\xi)\) starts at one with zero derivative. Its series follows by substituting \(R=\sum c_m\xi^{2m}\): the recurrence is \(c_{m+1}=-c_m/[4(m+1)^2]\). The independent solution diverges logarithmically at the origin and would introduce an unmodelled line source. The side boundary requires \(J_0(ka)=0\), so \(k_n=j_n/a\) with \(j_n\) the positive zeros of \(J_0\). The first three are approximately \(2.40483,5.52008,8.65373\).

Derive the radial weight and coefficients

Multiply the equation for \(R_m\) by \(R_n\) and subtract the reversed equation. Integration gives \((k_m^2-k_n^2)\int_0^a rR_mR_n\,dr\) equal to a boundary term. At \(a\) both functions vanish; at zero their derivatives are regular and the factor \(r\) vanishes. Hence distinct modes are orthogonal with the radial weight \(r\). That factor represents the area of a circular ring. The squared norm follows from the derivative identities \(J_0'=-J_1\), \((tJ_1)'=tJ_0\), using the known zero at \(a\):

\[\int_0^1 xJ_0(j_nx)^2\,dx=\frac{J_1(j_n)^2}{2},\]
\[b_n=\frac{2\int_0^1 x(1-x^2)J_0(j_nx)\,dx}{J_1(j_n)^2}.\]

Use \((xJ_1(jx))'=jxJ_0(jx)\) to integrate the numerator by parts. The endpoint factor \(1-x^2\) vanishes at \(x=1\); the remaining integral is \(2j^{-1}\int_0^1 x^2 J_1(jx)\,dx=2J_2(j)/j^2\). At a zero of \(J_0\), \(J_2(j)=2J_1(j)/j\). Thus \(b_n=8/[j_n^3J_1(j_n)]\), with alternating signs. The full solution is

\[V(r,z)=V_0\sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n J_0(j_n r/a)e^{-j_n z/a}.\]

Each partial sum exactly grounds the side wall and decays toward infinity. The representation of the end electrode improves as modes are added. For a finite cylinder closed at \(z=L\), replace each exponential by \(\sinh[k_n(L-z)]/\sinh(k_nL)\). This satisfies both end conditions and tends to the long-cylinder result at fixed \(z\) as \(L\) grows.

Estimate a leakage length and a modal error

How far does the slowest mode survive?

For \(a=12\) mm the first modal decay length is \(a/j_1=4.99\) mm. Far from the end, two axis probes separated by 5.0 mm should read voltages in ratio about \(e^{-2.40483(5/12)}=0.367\). The first coefficient \(b_1\simeq1.108\) is larger than unity because the higher modes bring the prescribed central boundary value back to exactly \(V_0\). A fit using \(V_0e^{-j_1z/a}\) would get the correct distant slope but the wrong amplitude.

Predict the accuracy of a one-mode model

The second coefficient is about \(-0.140\). On axis the magnitude of its contribution relative to the first is approximately \(0.126\,e^{-(5.520-2.405)z/a}\). At \(z=a\) this is 0.56%. At \(z=0\) higher modes cannot be neglected at the same tolerance. Near a radial node of a chosen reference mode a relative error becomes unstable; use absolute potential error measured in units of \(V_0\) there.

Reconstruct a circular electrode pattern with Bessel modes

Each retained mode exactly satisfies the side-wall condition. At the end, one mode alone overestimates the central voltage by about 10.8%. Move the detector deeper into the tube and compare three and eight modes until their interior traces coincide to the precision you need.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · source weighting and a radial inverse problem

Explain a nonzero axis slope in a file of radial data

Someone fits the central potential by \(V=A+Br\) near the cylinder axis. What does a nonzero \(B\) imply in the continuum problem?

Work through the solution

A smooth axisymmetric Cartesian scalar must have zero radial derivative at the axis. The term \(Br\) would give \(r^{-1}\partial_r(r\partial_rV)=B/r\), incompatible with a smooth source-free interior and finite homogeneous axial curvature. An interpolation can manufacture this feature if it treats the axis as an arbitrary material boundary.

Infer tube radius using only distant measurements

Two distant on-axis probes separated by 3.00 mm have a voltage ratio of 0.500. Infer \(a\) under first-mode dominance, then design a check against residual higher modes.

Work through the solution

From \(e^{-j_1\Delta z/a}=1/2\), \(a=j_1\Delta z/\ln2=10.4\) mm. Move both probes farther from the end by the same amount. A persistent ratio supports the one-mode approximation. If higher modes remain, their changing relative weight changes the ratio. Very small voltages may instead expose the noise floor and lead to an apparently shallower decay.

Further reading

  • Fließbach, chapter 10 · cylindrical boundary-value problems
  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §3.3 · separation of variables

Chapter 42 · Forces and torques from a matrix of flux linkages

Two measurements made while holding current fixed and flux fixed can produce different torque curves. They agree on the torque of the same physical state. A derivation from electrical work identifies precisely what must be held fixed when differentiating.

Keep track of each electrical and mechanical port

Let \(\theta\) be the mechanical angle of a pair of coils, \(i_j\) the currents entering their positive terminals and \(\lambda_j\) their linked fluxes, including all turns. Use ideal windings, a reversible magnetic medium and negligible radiation. Faraday's terminal relation is \(v_j=d\lambda_j/dt\). With positive torque \(T\) doing work on the moving rotor, energy balance reads

\[dW_m=\sum_j i_j\,d\lambda_j-T\,d\theta .\]

The natural variables of magnetic energy \(W_m\) are fluxes and position. Consequently \(i_j=(\partial W_m/\partial\lambda_j)_\theta\) and \(T=-(\partial W_m/\partial\theta)_\lambda\). A current-controlled experiment supplies or removes electrical work during motion. Define the coenergy \(W'_m=\sum i_j\lambda_j-W_m\). Its differential is \(\sum_j\lambda_j\,di_j+T\,d\theta\), giving \(T=(\partial W'_m/\partial\theta)_i\). Coenergy is a Legendre transform. Its numerical value equals stored energy for linear zero-bias materials but can differ for nonlinear response.

Derive reciprocity, positivity and torque

Take \(\boldsymbol\lambda=L(\theta)\mathbf i\) with a two-port inductance matrix. From the existence of \(W'_m\), mixed derivatives at fixed angle commute, so \(\partial\lambda_1/\partial i_2=\partial\lambda_2/\partial i_1\). This gives \(L_{12}=L_{21}=M\) for the given terminal orientation. With positive self-inductances, positive stored energy requires \(L_1L_2-M^2>0\) for independent currents in a nonsingular model. Integrating coenergy at fixed angle gives \(W'_m=\mathbf i^T L\mathbf i/2\). The energy in its own natural variables is \(W_m=\boldsymbol\lambda^T L^{-1}\boldsymbol\lambda/2\).
