Differentiate \(L^{-1}L=I\) to obtain \((L^{-1})'=-L^{-1}L'L^{-1}\). Thus both torque formulas at the same state give

\[T=\tfrac12\mathbf i^T\frac{\partial L}{\partial\theta}\mathbf i .\]

For constant self-inductances and \(M=M_0\cos\theta\), \(T=-M_0i_1i_2\sin\theta\). Reversing one winding reverses the coordinate definitions of its current and mutual inductance together, preserving the physical result. Under prescribed fluxes the same formula holds with currents \(\mathbf i=L(\theta)^{-1}\boldsymbol\lambda\) recalculated at every angle.

Compare a current supply with two flux-conserving loops

Start at the same state, then turn the coils

Use \(L_1=4.0\) mH, \(L_2=9.0\) mH and \(M_0=3.0\) mH. At \(\theta_0=60^\circ\), let currents be \(2.00\) A and \(1.00\) A. The flux linkages are \(9.50\) and \(12.0\) mWb. At this configuration \(T=-5.20\) mN m and the energy is \(15.5\) mJ. The units follow from an inductance times current squared, which is energy, while angle in radians is dimensionless.

Move to \(90^\circ\). Keeping both currents fixed gives \(T=-6.00\) mN m. Keeping both fluxes fixed instead gives \(i_1=9.50/4.0=2.375\) A and \(i_2=12.0/9.0=1.333\) A because the mutual term has vanished. The new torque is \(-9.50\) mN m. The two paths through state space now represent genuinely different experiments. For the fixed-current path the field energy falls by 3.00 mJ. The sources absorb 6.00 mJ and the external mechanical agent supplies 3.00 mJ, consistent with the first law.

A nonlinear constitutive curve

For one winding model a reversible flux linkage \(\lambda=L_\ell i+\Lambda\tanh(i/I_s)\), where positive \(L_\ell,\Lambda,I_s\) may depend on geometry. Integration gives \(W'_m=L_\ell i^2/2+\Lambda I_s\ln\cosh(i/I_s)\); \(W_m=i\lambda-W'_m\). Only in the linear weak-current limit do they coincide. A measured hysteresis loop would make this single-valued construction invalid without additional state variables describing material history.

Compare turning a rotor at fixed current and at fixed flux

Both protocols start at 60°. With initial currents 2 A and 1 A, the torques there coincide at −5.20 mN m but reach −6.00 and −9.50 mN m at 90°. Reverse one initial current and identify the terms changed by the corresponding constraints.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · independent torque measurements

Test a computed torque numerically

How would a finite-difference experiment recover torque in the preceding fixed-flux state without differentiating any matrix entries symbolically?

Work through the solution

Take the known \(\boldsymbol\lambda\), invert \(L\) at \(\theta\pm h\) separately and compute \(W_\pm=\boldsymbol\lambda^TL_\pm^{-1}\boldsymbol\lambda/2\). Estimate \(T=-(W_+-W_-)/(2h)\) using \(h\) in radians. At \(90^\circ\) the result converges to \(-9.50\) mN m. Reusing the same currents at both perturbations silently changes what the finite difference measures.

Infer the size of the conservative part of coupling

At fixed angle a small current cycle on the \(i_1,i_2\) plane produces \(\oint(\lambda_1di_1+\lambda_2di_2)\neq0\) after correcting lead resistance. What does this say about a proposed coenergy function?

Work through the solution

An exact differential has zero integral around every closed path. A repeatable nonzero result means that a single-valued coenergy of the two currents alone cannot describe these measurements. Check frequency-dependent losses, hysteresis and whether an unmeasured mechanical coordinate moved. Making the inductance matrix symmetric by averaging two readings alone cannot explain the residual work.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§7.2.3–7.2.4 · mutual inductance and magnetic energy
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 3 · induction and magnetic energy

Chapter 43 · Where current and energy go in a charging capacitor

A wire carries current to the centre of a plate, yet charge appears across its face and the energy increases in the gap. Following that current over differently chosen surfaces gives a spatial test of charge conservation and an independent power budget.

A circulation must give one answer for every spanning surface

Surround the charging lead by a loop. A flat surface inside the lead intercepts conduction current; a surface stretched into the insulating gap can avoid the lead altogether. Applying the magnetostatic law to both would assign different circulation to the same loop. Charge continuity and Gauss's law instead give

\[\nabla\cdot(\mathbf J+\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E)=-\partial_t\rho+\partial_t\rho=0 .\]

Consequently the flux of this sum through any two surfaces with the same boundary agrees. Ampère–Maxwell equates the circulation of \(\mathbf B/\mu_0\) to that common enclosed current in vacuum. In a material with identified free current the macroscopic balance uses \(\mathbf H,\mathbf J_f,\partial_t\mathbf D\), incorporating polarisation. The divergence identity verifies consistency; the magnetic field generated by the additional term is a physical prediction of Maxwell's equation.

Resolve the charging current across the plate

Take circular metal plates of radius \(a\), separation \(d\ll a\), connected at their centres. Work at times long compared with \(a/c\) and neglect small edge and lead corrections. In the gap \(E_z=V/d\) is approximately uniform with sign chosen from positive to negative plate. \(Q=\epsilon_0\pi a^2 E_z\), so \(\partial_tD_z=I/(\pi a^2)\) where \(I=\dot Q\). A circle of radius \(r<a\) spanning the gap intercepts displacement current \(I r^2/a^2\). Azimuthal symmetry gives

\[B_\phi(r)=\frac{\mu_0 I r}{2\pi a^2}\quad (r<a),\]
\[B_\phi(r)\simeq\frac{\mu_0 I}{2\pi r}\quad (r>a).\]

The exterior expression assumes the return conductor lies farther away and retains the leading large-plate approximation. Magnetic field is continuous at the idealised rim. Now cut a ring of radius \(r\) into the positive plate. The portion of supplied current needed to charge its central disk is \(Ir^2/a^2\); the rest crosses that ring outwards as conduction current. Thus \(2\pi r K_r=I(1-r^2/a^2)\). Its apparent \(1/r\) singularity at the centre is rounded off by the finite contact patch. Distributed charging is compatible with current entering through one point.

Locate the power flow in the gap

The Poynting vector in the gap is \(\mathbf S=\mathbf E\times\mathbf H=-E_zH_\phi\hat{\mathbf r}\). For increasing field it points radially inward. Integrate the inward flux through the cylindrical side of a smaller pillbox of radius \(r<a\) and thickness \(d\):

\[P_{\rm in}(r)=2\pi r d E_z H_\phi(r)=VI\,\frac{r^2}{a^2}\]
\[\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\epsilon_0}{2}E_z^2\pi r^2d\right).\]

Thus energy crosses the side before being stored throughout that subvolume. At \(r=a\) the result equals the terminal power \(VI\). This derivation uses the leading quasistatic fields. A rapidly changing current also produces induction fields and time-varying magnetic energy; in that regime the full Poynting theorem includes them. During discharging, \(\dot Q\) reverses and the direction of radial power flow reverses.

Predict two independent instrument readings

Sample midway to the rim

Let \(a=20\) mm, \(d=0.50\) mm and \(I=4.0\) mA. At \(r=10\) mm a gap circle encloses 1.0 mA displacement current. Its magnetic field is 20.0 nT. The outward conduction current crossing the same radius in the positive metal is 3.0 mA, corresponding to \(K_r=0.0477\) A/m. The gap reading and the plate reading describe different surfaces; their sum recovers the lead current in this construction.

Record a finite charge ramp

The ideal capacitance here is 22.3 pF. A constant \(I=4.0\) mA for 20.0 ns raises the voltage by 3.60 V from zero. The source supplies \(\int VI\,dt=C V_{\rm final}^2/2=144\) pJ. At the half-radius pillbox only one quarter of this energy appears, 36.0 pJ. A real pulse has finite rise and fall times. The simple uniform-field solution is accurate away from abrupt switching transients when the spectral content is well below \(c/a\).

Measure a displaced current and a radial conduction current separately

At 20 mm plate radius, 4 mA lead current and a 10 mm probe radius, 1 mA crosses the gap disk and 3 mA crosses the metal ring. The graph compares both currents over the entire plate. Reverse the current or enlarge the plate and compare the magnetic reading at the selected fractional radius.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · challenge a misleading measurement

Determine the sign during reversal

The capacitor field still points from the positive to the negative plate, but its magnitude is decreasing. Which of \(B_\phi,S_r,E_z\) changes sign relative to an increasing field with the same instantaneous voltage?

Work through the solution

The voltage and \(E_z\) keep their signs. \(I=C\dot V\) reverses, so the azimuthal magnetic field reverses. Hence radial energy flow reverses and leaves the pillbox. A measurement of voltage alone does not determine the direction of energy transport.

What changes if the centre contact is replaced by a rim feed?

Assume a uniform annular feed deposits charge symmetrically at the outer edge. Predict the signed metal current crossing a circle of radius \(r<a\) if positive is radially outward.

Work through the solution

All charge accumulating inside the test circle must now enter from larger radii. The inward flux is \(Ir^2/a^2\), so \(2\pi rK_r=-Ir^2/a^2\). The gap displacement current for the same uniform charging history is unchanged. The external lead and return geometry determines the remainder of the global magnetic field configuration.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§7.3.1–7.3.3, 8.1 · displacement current and energy
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 3 · displacement current

Chapter 44 · A lossy half-space seen through its surface impedance

An absorbing medium transmits a complex field and converts electrical work into heat. Matching one complex number at its surface predicts reflection. Integrating the heating through its depth provides an independent check that is especially revealing near the crossover between dielectric and conductor.

Choose a branch that decays into the material

Let a stationary plane wave enter the homogeneous half-space \(z>0\) at normal incidence. Use \(e^{-i\omega t}\) and fields proportional to \(e^{ikz}\), real \(\epsilon,\mu>0\) and Ohmic \(\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf E\), \(\sigma\geq0\). Ampère's law combines the two currents into \(-i\omega(\epsilon+i\sigma/\omega)\mathbf E\). Faraday's law yields \(H_y=kE_x/(\omega\mu)\). Eliminating \(H\) gives

\[k^2=\omega^2\mu\epsilon(1+i t),\]
\[t=\frac{\sigma}{\omega\epsilon},\]
\[k=\beta+i\alpha .\]

Choose \(\beta>0,\alpha\geq0\) so the interior field weakens with depth. Let \(k_d=\omega\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}\). Equating real and imaginary parts gives \(\beta^2-\alpha^2=k_d^2\), \(2\beta\alpha=k_d^2 t\), and hence

\[\frac{\beta}{k_d}=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{1+t^2}+1}{2}},\]
\[\frac{\alpha}{k_d}=\frac{t}{2(\beta/k_d)},\]
\[Z=\frac{E_x}{H_y}=\frac{\omega\mu}{k}.\]

The rational expression for \(\alpha\) avoids subtracting nearly equal numbers for small \(t\). As \(t\to0\), \(\alpha/k_d\simeq t/2\). For \(t\gg1\), \(\beta\simeq\alpha\simeq\sqrt{\omega\mu\sigma/2}\): skin depth \(1/\alpha\) measures field-amplitude decay and the power has the shorter e-folding depth \(1/(2\alpha)\).

Read the complex reflection and the power entering the surface

Let the incident medium be lossless with impedance \(Z_1\). Continuity of tangential \(E,H\) gives \(1+r=h,\ (1-r)/Z_1=h/Z\), where \(h=E(0^+)/E_{\rm inc}\). Solving,

\[r=\frac{Z-Z_1}{Z+Z_1},\]
\[h=\frac{2Z}{Z+Z_1},\]
\[1-|r|^2=Z_1\operatorname{Re}(1/Z^*)|h|^2.\]

The flux relation follows by dividing the real part of \(E H^*/2\) by the incident intensity. It includes a real admittance and is generally different from \(|h|^2\). The imaginary part of the complex Poynting vector measures exchange in quadrature with the drive. In the good-conductor limit \(Z\simeq(1-i)\sqrt{\omega\mu/(2\sigma)}\). Its positive real part is essential for passive heating, even where the reflected voltage is almost the negative of the incident one.

Recover the entire inward power from a volume measurement

Ohmic heating per volume is \(\langle p_J\rangle=\sigma|E(0)|^2 e^{-2\alpha z}/2\). Integrating from zero to infinity gives \(P_J/A=\sigma|E(0)|^2/(4\alpha)\). Use \(2\beta\alpha=\mu\omega\sigma\) to rewrite it as \(|E(0)|^2\beta/(2\omega\mu)\). This equals the inward mean surface flux from \(E H^*/2\). No separate efficiency factor is needed. The fraction of surface power dissipated before depth \(D\) is \(1-e^{-2\alpha D}\), so 90% of the heat is deposited by \(D=\ln10/(2\alpha)\), about 1.15 field skin depths.

Compare two frequency regimes

A crossover with comparable currents

Consider material whose lossless impedance equals the incident medium’s and whose loss ratio is \(t=2\). Then \(\beta/k_d=1.272\), \(\alpha/k_d=0.786\), and \(Z/Z_1=0.569-0.352i\). Direct complex division gives a reflected power fraction of about 0.120. The balance enters the half-space and ultimately heats it. One lossless phase length \(k_d^{-1}\) into the material, the remaining forward power is \(e^{-1.572}=0.208\) of its surface value. This illustrates substantial heating even though most incident power enters the material.

An amplitude probe and a calorimeter

Suppose a field-amplitude probe reads 1/e of its surface value at depth 0.80 mm. Then \(\alpha=1250\) m⁻¹, the power half-depth is \(\ln2/(2\alpha)=0.277\) mm and 90% of the absorbed energy is deposited by 0.921 mm. These predictions do not require knowing the incident intensity. Disagreement between the two types of measurement can reveal spatially varying \(\sigma\) or a reflected wave from a finite back surface.

Close a reflection and heating budget at every depth

At loss ratio 2 and matched lossless impedances, about 12% of the incident power is reflected. The remaining power gradually becomes heat. Change loss and impedance independently and check that the surface reflection, transmitted flux at depth and integrated heat always add to one.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · determine a material from two probes

Recover material parameters

At frequency \(f\) in a nonmagnetic half-space, phase measurements give \(\beta\) and amplitude measurements give \(\alpha\). Recover \(\sigma,\epsilon\), and specify the consistency condition for the dielectric model used here.

Work through the solution

Solve for \(\sigma=2\beta\alpha/(\mu_0\omega)\) and \(\epsilon=(\beta^2-\alpha^2)/(\mu_0\omega^2)\). A medium with positive real permittivity in this simple Ohmic model requires \(\beta>\alpha\). In the good-conductor limit their difference is small, making the inferred permittivity sensitive to measurement errors.

When does an infinite depth assumption fail?

For a slab of thickness \(D\), the back-face field reflection magnitude is 0.50. Bound the return amplitude at the entrance relative to the forward wave inside, ignoring subsequent round trips. How thick keeps it below 1%?

Work through the solution

The round trip attenuates the field by \(e^{-2\alpha D}\). Require \(0.50e^{-2\alpha D}<0.01\), giving \(D>\ln50/(2\alpha)=1.96/\alpha\). Phase may suppress the measured amplitude at selected frequencies, but the magnitude bound does not depend on that cancellation.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§9.4.1–9.4.2 · conducting media and reflection
  • Fließbach, chapter 34 · dispersion and absorption

Chapter 45 · Two polarisations moving through a uniaxial crystal

A crystal can send two pulses in different directions even when their wavefronts have the same normal. The distinction emerges by applying the divergence condition to electric displacement and measuring energy flow with the Poynting vector.

A field transverse to the wavevector need not be the electric field

Let a transparent, nonmagnetic uniaxial medium have \(\boldsymbol\epsilon/\epsilon_0=\operatorname{diag}(n_o^2,n_o^2,n_e^2)\). The optic axis is \(z\); dispersion over the bandwidth under study is negligible. For a harmonic plane wave with wavevector in the \(xz\) plane, write \(\mathbf k=(k_x,0,k_z)\). Source-free Maxwell equations require \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf D=0\), \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf B=0\), \(\mathbf k\times\mathbf E=\omega\mathbf B\) and \(\mathbf k\times\mathbf H=-\omega\mathbf D\). Eliminating the magnetic field gives

\[\mathbf k\times(\mathbf k\times\mathbf E)+k_0^2(\boldsymbol\epsilon/\epsilon_0)\mathbf E=0,\]
\[k_0=\omega/c.\]

An electric field along \(y\) satisfies this system with \(k^2=k_0^2n_o^2\). This ordinary mode has transverse \(\mathbf E,\mathbf D\) and energy travels parallel to \(\mathbf k\). A second mode has \(\mathbf E\) in the \(xz\) plane; \(\mathbf D\) is transverse but generally not parallel to \(\mathbf E\).

Find the phase index and the direction of energy separately

The remaining two Cartesian equations form a matrix with diagonal \(k_0^2 n_o^2-k_z^2,\ k_0^2 n_e^2-k_x^2\) and off-diagonal elements \(k_xk_z\). Its determinant vanishes for nontrivial fields. Expanding cancels the fourth-order cross term and gives

\[\frac{k_x^2}{n_e^2}+\frac{k_z^2}{n_o^2}=k_0^2,\]
\[\frac1{n_{\rm ph}(\theta)^2}=\frac{\sin^2\theta}{n_e^2}+\frac{\cos^2\theta}{n_o^2},\]

where \(\theta\) is the angle from the optic axis to the wavevector. Along the axis both modes have the same index; perpendicular to it the extraordinary phase index is \(n_e\). For the latter mode choose transverse \(\mathbf D\propto(\cos\theta,0,-\sin\theta)\). Applying the inverse permittivity tensor gives \(\mathbf E\propto(\cos\theta/n_o^2,0,-\sin\theta/n_e^2)\). The magnetic field is along \(y\), so the energy flow angle \(\psi\) from \(z\) satisfies

\[\tan\psi=-\frac{E_z}{E_x}=\frac{n_o^2}{n_e^2}\tan\theta.\]

The group velocity obtained by differentiating \(\omega(\mathbf k)=c\sqrt{k_x^2/n_e^2+k_z^2/n_o^2}\) has the same direction in this nondispersive model. This independent construction is normal to the constant-frequency ellipse. At an interface it is the tangential component of \(\mathbf k\), fixed by phase matching, that selects the transmitted wavevector. The energy path can then diverge from the corresponding wave normal.

A crystal plate as a beam displacer and as a retarder

A normally incident beam with an oblique optic axis

Let \(n_o=1.50,\ n_e=1.60\) and let the plate normal make \(45^\circ\) to the optic axis. At normal incidence the phase normal is the plate normal for both modes. For the extraordinary wave \(\psi=\arctan(2.25/2.56)=41.3^\circ\). Relative to its wave normal the energy travels about \(-3.7^\circ\) toward the optic axis. Across a 10.0 mm thick plate the lateral displacement relative to the ordinary beam is approximately 0.65 mm. This describes propagation inside a parallel plate and neglects edge clipping.

A plate that turns linear input into circular output

Propagate perpendicular to the optic axis so both eigenmodes travel in the same direction. Their accumulated phase difference is \(\Delta=k_0(n_e-n_o)d\). Linear incident polarisation at \(45^\circ\) to the eigenaxes has equal amplitudes. When \(|\Delta|=\pi/2\) modulo \(2\pi\), those orthogonal components have a quarter-period phase difference and form circular polarisation, assuming equal transmitted amplitudes. At 600 nm with \(n_e-n_o=0.10\), the smallest such thickness is \(1.50\) μm. A larger multi-order plate can give the same retardance at this wavelength but responds more strongly to bandwidth and thickness errors.

Recover retardance from a pair of polarisers

Place the crystal axes at \(45^\circ\) to the input polariser and use a second polariser crossed with the first. Express the output field in the two crystal axes, then project onto the crossed analyser; its amplitude is proportional to \((e^{i\Delta}-1)/2\). Thus the transmitted fraction is \(\sin^2(\Delta/2)\). An arbitrary phase offset common to both modes disappears. A single intensity reading determines neither the sign of \(\Delta\) nor its full order. Varying thickness or scanning wavelength can resolve the branch; the material dispersion then enters the interpretation.

Predict the transverse displacement of a crystal plate

Choose principal indices 1.50 and 1.60 and a 45° wave normal. The extraordinary beam is displaced by about −0.65 mm through a 10 mm plate; the ordinary beam follows the normal. Swap the principal indices and compare the sign and magnitude of walk-off.

Problem sheet · use energy and phase observations

Infer birefringence from a walk-off angle

At \(\theta=45^\circ\), an extraordinary energy ray makes \(\psi=40^\circ\) to the optic axis. Assuming the same model, recover \(n_e/n_o\). Is the medium positive or negative uniaxial?

Work through the solution

The ratio is \(\sqrt{\tan45^\circ/\tan40^\circ}=1.092\). The extraordinary principal index exceeds the ordinary one, a positive uniaxial material. A measurement referenced to the plate normal must first be converted to the angle from the optic axis used in the equation.

Calculate the contrast loss caused by angular misalignment

An ideal half-wave plate has its eigenaxis at angle \(\alpha\) to the input polariser. Keep the analyser crossed and find the intensity transmitted when the angle is \(40^\circ\) instead of \(45^\circ\).

Work through the solution

Projection into the crystal axes and back out gives \(I/I_0=\sin^2(2\alpha)\sin^2(\Delta/2)\). At half-wave retardance the factor from phase is unity, and the misaligned transmission is \(\sin^2 80^\circ=0.970\). The reduction alone cannot distinguish angular error from a small error in retardance.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§4.3–4.4 and 9.1.4 · constitutive laws and polarisation
  • Fließbach, chapter 33 · wave solutions in media

Chapter 46 · Radiation when the electric dipole cancels

Two optical dipoles can cancel in the long-wavelength electric-dipole approximation and still radiate. Expanding their source phase shows which moments survive, where the remaining light goes, and how a small asymmetry restores a different angular pattern.

Keep the source phase inside the radiation integral

For a localised harmonic current \(\mathbf J(\mathbf x')e^{-i\omega t}\), take a detector far compared with source size and wavelength. In the outgoing potential set \(1/R\simeq1/r\), while retaining \(kR\simeq kr-k\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf x'\), where \(\mathbf n=\mathbf r/r\). This approximation requires the quadratic path phase across the source to be negligible. The radiative electric field is proportional to the transverse part of the current integral

\[\mathbf C(\mathbf n)=\int\mathbf J(\mathbf x')e^{-ik\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf x'}\,d^3x',\]
\[\mathbf E_{\rm rad}=\frac{i\mu_0\omega e^{ikr}}{4\pi r}[\mathbf C-\mathbf n(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf C)] .\]

For small source size \(a\) with \(ka\ll1\), expand the exponential. Continuity and integration by parts give \(\int J_i\,d^3x=\dot p_i\) for electric dipole \(\mathbf p=\int\rho\mathbf x\,d^3x\). The first correction is \(-ik n_j\int J_i x_j\,d^3x\). Split this integral into its symmetric and antisymmetric parts. Define \(Q_{ij}=\int\rho x_i x_j\,d^3x\) and \(\mathbf m=\frac12\int\mathbf x\times\mathbf J\,d^3x\). Differentiating \(Q_{ij}\) and using charge conservation yields

\[\int J_i x_j\,d^3x=\tfrac12\dot Q_{ij}-\epsilon_{ijk}m_k,\]
\[\mathbf C=\dot{\mathbf p}-\frac{ik}2\dot Q\,\mathbf n+ik\,\mathbf n\times\mathbf m+\cdots .\]

The trace of \(Q\) contributes a vector along \(\mathbf n\); the far-field transverse projection removes it. Thus only its traceless electric quadrupole part can radiate. The antisymmetric contribution is the magnetic dipole. Both occur at the next order in this source-phase expansion. Their importance depends on source symmetry and on the actual sizes of their transition moments.

Construct an electric quadrupole with two displaced dipoles

Put narrow identical \(z\)-directed electric dipoles at \(z=\pm d/2\) and drive them with moments \(+p\) and \(-p\), perfectly out of phase. Their total electric dipole is zero. The currents are collinear with their displacement, so this geometry also gives no net magnetic dipole. In direction \(\theta\) to the axis the single-dipole field has factor \(\sin\theta\). The additional two-source factor is \(e^{-ikd\cos\theta/2}-e^{ikd\cos\theta/2}\). In units of the equatorial intensity of one dipole the complete pattern is

\[F(\theta)=4\sin^2\theta\,\sin^2\!\left(\tfrac12kd\cos\theta\right)\simeq(kd)^2\sin^2\theta\cos^2\theta .\]

This has nodes on the axis from transversality and in the equatorial plane from source cancellation. The leading lobes peak at \(45^\circ\) and \(135^\circ\). Integrating over solid angle, \(\int_{-1}^{1}(1-u^2)u^2\,du=4/15\); the single-dipole integral is \(4/3\). The pair-to-single total-power ratio is \((kd)^2/5\) at leading order. A fixed single-dipole amplitude is the reference; normalising each curve to its own maximum would hide this suppression.

Relate the symmetry argument to optical transitions

In the long-wavelength quantum interaction the electric-dipole term contains a matrix element of position, odd under spatial inversion. Between two states of the same definite parity, the integrand for an electric dipole moment changes sign on inversion and integrates to zero. The electric quadrupole contains two position factors and has even parity. The magnetic dipole is an axial vector and also has even parity. Either next-order channel can therefore satisfy parity between such states, subject to angular-momentum and other selection rules. Classical source symmetries illuminate the long-wavelength expansion; actual atomic emission rates require the relevant quantum matrix elements and state populations.

Estimate the strength and detect imperfect cancellation

An optical-scale separation

For \(d=20\) nm and \(\lambda=600\) nm, \(kd=0.2094\). The leading integrated pair-to-single power ratio is \(0.00877\), under 1%. In the \(45^\circ\) direction the dimensionless intensity is \(4(1/2)\sin^2[kd/(2\sqrt2)]\simeq0.01095\). Tripling \(d\) at fixed wavelength should initially give roughly nine times the power; check the exact sine before applying that extrapolation.

An equatorial leak

Reduce the opposing dipole magnitude to \((1-\delta)p\). Expanding the squared two-source factor gives \(\delta^2+4(1-\delta)\sin^2(kd\cos\theta/2)\). At the equator the second term is zero, leaving intensity fraction \(\delta^2\) relative to one dipole. A measured fraction 0.0025 corresponds to a 5% amplitude imbalance if this is the only imperfection. A phase mismatch can also fill the node, so the observation cannot distinguish both mechanisms without additional phase information.

Break the symmetry of an optical dipole pair

Compare a coherent two-source calculation against its first long-wavelength approximation. At d/λ = 0.035, equal and opposite dipoles emit about 1% of the power of a single dipole. Introduce a 5% amplitude mismatch and check the equatorial node. Then increase spacing to test the limit of the expansion.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · which moment radiates?

Separate the amplitude and power scaling

For the symmetric pair suppose the separation is halved with the two moments and frequency unchanged. Predict the leading power change at \(45^\circ\) and at \(90^\circ\). Where is a ratio meaningful?

Work through the solution

At \(45^\circ\) the leading field is proportional to \(kd\), so the intensity drops by a factor of four. At \(90^\circ\) the exact two-source phase difference is zero for every spacing. Both intensities vanish there and their ratio is undefined. A finite measurement at that angle probes symmetry breaking or background light.

Compare with a closed current loop

A small circular loop carries uniform oscillating current \(I\) and has area \(A\). Identify its leading moment and the directions in which its radiation vanishes.

Work through the solution

The net electric dipole of this ideal divergence-free closed current is zero. Its magnetic dipole is \(IA\hat{\mathbf n}_{\rm loop}\). The radiation term contains \(\mathbf n\times\mathbf m\), hence vanishes along the loop axis and peaks transverse to it. At leading order the loop can radiate strongly in its equatorial plane, unlike the collinear electric-quadrupole pair.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§11.1.3–11.1.4 · magnetic dipoles and general sources
  • Fließbach, chapter 24 · dipole radiation

Problem sheet 7 · Project · Infer a charge distribution from a grounded tube

A voltage probe sits on the centreline of a long square metal tube. Derive a reference field, decide what the probe can identify and test a numerical estimate of the charge density with OneSimulation.

Construct a forward problem that checks its own units

The walls of a square of side \(L=1\) m are held at zero. Along the tube nothing changes. Assume a vacuum and \(\rho=\rho_0\sin(\pi x/L)\sin(\pi y/L)\). Find the potential throughout the cross-section, explain the sign of the voltage and identify the dimensionless shape parameter if \(L\) changes.

Work through the solution

Gauss's law gives \(-\nabla^2V=\rho/\epsilon_0\). Each second derivative on the sine product returns \(-(\pi/L)^2\) times that product. Thus \(V=\rho_0L^2\sin(\pi x/L)\sin(\pi y/L)/(2\pi^2\epsilon_0)\). The factor \(\rho_0L^2/\epsilon_0\) has units V, and the coordinates are \(x/L,y/L\). Positive charge makes the interior positive relative to the grounded walls. The grounded boundary and Poisson uniqueness fix the solution.

Infer the charge before opening the solver

At the centre an instrument measures \(5.72\pm0.05\) V. Determine the inferred peak charge density with uncertainty, assuming the sine shape is independently known. If a second probe is placed at \((L/4,L/2)\), what should it measure? Which error affects both estimates in the same way?

Work through the solution

Divide the reading by \(L^2/(2\pi^2\epsilon_0)\), or about 5.72165 V per nC/m³, to obtain \(1.000\pm0.009\) nC/m³ after rounding. The second voltage is the first times \(\sin(\pi/4)\), about 4.04 V. A gain error common to both instruments biases the inferred charge while leaving their voltage ratio unchanged.

Verify the mesh and distinguish forward from inverse error

Use the grounded-square experiment at 1 nC/m³. Compare 6, 12 and 24 cells across the tube. Run the same PDE in OneSimulation and check its centre row. Derive the sign of the grid error and the corresponding direction of bias if that grid were used to infer the charge.

Work through the solution

The discrete Laplacian eigenvalue is \(8\sin^2(\pi/(2n))/h^2\), \(h=L/n\). Because \(\sin t<t\) for positive \(t\), it is smaller than the continuum eigenvalue \(2(\pi/L)^2\). The discrete voltage is therefore high for the same density. Centre values for the three grids are about 5.8542, 5.7544 and 5.7298 V. Fitting the observed voltage with this grid would lower the charge estimate. The leading error decreases like \(n^{-2}\).

Show precisely what one probe misses

Add an unknown volume charge proportional to \(\sin(2\pi x/L)\sin(\pi y/L)\). Predict its centre signature and design a measurement to isolate it even in the presence of the original mode.

Work through the solution

The extra potential is proportional to the same shape divided by \(5\pi^2/L^2\). It vanishes at \(x=L/2\). The original centre probe cannot detect it at any amplitude. Place probes at \(x=L/4\) and \(3L/4\), both at \(y=L/2\). Their difference cancels the symmetric first mode and doubles the antisymmetric second. This is an identifiability gain from a change of measurement, independently of mesh refinement.

Problem sheet 8 · Project · Trace energy from a wave to the material supporting it

A plane wave illuminates a transparent dielectric and then a metal surface. Use field boundary conditions, flux, mechanical reaction and dissipation to account for the same energy in different descriptions.

Start with field amplitudes

A normally incident 300 V/m peak plane wave arrives from vacuum at a lossless nonmagnetic glass of refractive index 1.5. Assume a semi-infinite medium. Derive the reflected and transmitted field amplitudes by matching tangential electric and magnetic fields. Check the net cycle-averaged power.

Work through the solution

Tangential continuity gives \(1+r=t\) and \(1-r=1.5t\), where the minus sign follows from the reversed magnetic field of the reflected wave. Hence \(r=-0.2\), \(t=0.8\). The reflected electric amplitude is \(-60\) V/m and the transmitted amplitude 240 V/m. Reflectance is \(r^2=0.04\) and power transmittance is \(1.5t^2=0.96\). Squaring the transmitted electric amplitude ratio alone would omit the wave impedance.

Locate stored energy on the incident side

Use the incident and reflected energy experiment. Predict the two boundary contributions at phase zero with \(r=-0.2\). Explain how standing structure is compatible with a constant time-averaged net flux.

Work through the solution

At the boundary the normalised electric part is \((1+r)^2=0.64\) and the magnetic part \((1-r)^2=1.44\). The product \(EH\) has cancelling mixed terms between opposite propagation directions. Averaging over a period leaves the incident flux times \(1-|r|^2\), independent of position in the homogeneous incident region.

Replace the glass by a good conductor

For \(\sigma=5.8\times10^7\) S/m, \(\mu=\mu_0\) and 100 kHz, derive the skin depth and compare with the penetration experiment. At what depth is only one per cent of the surface Joule power density left?

Work through the solution

\(\delta=1/\sqrt{\pi f\mu\sigma}\approx0.209\) mm. Field amplitude decays as \(e^{-z/\delta}\), so time-averaged local Joule heating proportional to \(|E|^2\) decays as \(e^{-2z/\delta}\). Setting this to 0.01 gives \(z=\delta\ln(100)/2\approx0.481\) mm. Confusing intensity with amplitude would double the inferred depth.

Check the global heat budget and a mechanical limit

If the known internal surface field has peak amplitude \(E_s\), integrate the Joule heating over a thick conductor. Separately consider an ideal lossless mirror with incident time-averaged intensity \(I\). How can its heat absorption vanish while it experiences a force?

Work through the solution

The heat per unit area and time is \((\sigma |E_s|^2/2)\int_0^\infty e^{-2z/\delta}dz=\sigma |E_s|^2\delta/4\). This equals the inward mean Poynting flux in the good-conductor limit, using \(1/Z=(1-i)\sigma\delta/2\) for the stated phasor convention. At a perfect mirror the momentum flux reverses, giving pressure \(2I/c\). A stationary mirror can exchange momentum without doing mechanical work.

Problem sheet 9 · Project · Measure a cavity and identify two different loss mechanisms

A narrow optical resonance can identify a cavity's round-trip phase and total decay. Use a time trace, a spectrum and a change of coupling to distinguish information that a single measurement cannot supply.

Establish what resonates

In a one-dimensional ideal cavity of length \(L\) in vacuum the tangential electric field vanishes at two perfectly conducting end planes. Derive the allowed wavelengths and the frequency spacing. For \(L=0.15\) m calculate the spacing. Which assumption lets the spacing remain constant across the spectrum?

Work through the solution

A standing mode is proportional to \(\sin(kz)\) with \(kL=m\pi\). Thus \(\lambda_m=2L/m\) and \(f_m=mc/(2L)\). Adjacent modes stay about 0.9993 GHz apart. A frequency-independent phase speed and frequency-independent boundary phases have been assumed. Mirror phase dispersion or a dispersive fill changes the spacing.

Reconcile a ring-down trace with the spectral line

For a mode at \(f_0=10\) GHz the stored energy falls by \(1/e\) in 0.2 μs after the drive is removed. Compute the energy decay rate, quality factor and expected full power linewidth. Compare energy and field-amplitude time constants in the cavity experiment.

Work through the solution

Write \(U=U_0e^{-\kappa t}\). The rate is \(5.0\times10^6\) s⁻¹. The field amplitude falls as \(e^{-\kappa t/2}\), with time constant 0.4 μs. The driven response has denominator \((\kappa/2)^2+(\omega-\omega_0)^2\). The full width at half maximum in ordinary frequency is \(\kappa/(2\pi)=0.796\) MHz, giving \(Q\simeq12566\). These expressions agree with \(Q=\omega_0U/P_\mathrm{loss}\).

Infer loss rates from a second measurement

The initial linewidth includes an intrinsic energy decay rate \(\kappa_i\) and output-coupling rate \(\kappa_e\). A calibrated modification halves \(\kappa_e\) while keeping \(\kappa_i\) fixed, lengthening the energy decay time to 0.25 μs. Find each rate and the fraction of stored energy that could leave through the port before the change.

Work through the solution

Originally \(\kappa_i+\kappa_e=5\times10^6\) s⁻¹. After the change \(\kappa_i+\kappa_e/2=4\times10^6\) s⁻¹. Subtraction gives \(\kappa_e=2\times10^6\) and \(\kappa_i=3\times10^6\) s⁻¹. The initial out-coupled fraction is \(\kappa_e/(\kappa_i+\kappa_e)=0.4\). A lone linewidth constrains only the sum. The second experiment supplies independent information.

Challenge the assumption of a single stored mode

An observed photodetector signal oscillates on top of an exponential envelope. Describe a cause consistent with a linear cavity and an experiment that distinguishes it from a defective exponential integrator.

Work through the solution

If two modes are populated, the detector records \(|a_1e^{-i\omega_1t}+a_2e^{-i\omega_2t}|^2\). The cross term oscillates at their difference frequency. Narrow the excitation band or spatially select one mode and repeat. A numerical time-step test can identify solver error, but changing the prepared physical field tests the single-mode assumption directly.

Applied study

Derive the fields of a coaxial cable and follow how electromagnetic energy travels along it.

Where does energy travel in a cable?

A coaxial cable contains two conductors separated by an insulator. Current travels along the metal surfaces. To locate the energy delivered to a load, find the electric and magnetic fields in the space between them. Consider concentric, perfectly conducting cylinders of radii \(a\) and \(b\), separated by a uniform lossless dielectric of permittivity \(\epsilon\) and permeability \(\mu\). Treat a straight, uniform section far from connectors.

Place charge per unit length \(+q'\) on the inner conductor and \(-q'\) on the facing shield. A cylindrical Gaussian surface of radius \(r\), with \(a<r<b\), gives \(2\pi rD_r=q'\) per unit length. Integrating \(E_r=D_r/\epsilon\) from inner to outer conductor gives \(V=q'\ln(b/a)/(2\pi\epsilon)\). Thus \(C'=q'/V=2\pi\epsilon/\ln(b/a)\) is capacitance per metre. More charge fits at the same voltage when the dielectric permittivity rises. Fields and voltage at a dielectric interface.

Find the second energy store

Let a current \(I\) flow along the inner conductor and return on the shield. Ampère’s law gives a circumferential magnetic field \(H_\phi=I/(2\pi r)\). Integrate its energy density \(\mu H_\phi^2/2\) over the annular cross-section. The resulting magnetic energy per metre is \(\mu I^2\ln(b/a)/(4\pi)\). Equating this to \(L'I^2/2\) gives \(L'=\mu\ln(b/a)/(2\pi)\). The product \(L'C'=\mu\epsilon\) is independent of the two radii for this homogeneous line. Magnetic fields from steady currents.

Couple the fields into a travelling wave

A slice of cable of length \(dz\) gains charge when more current enters than leaves. Its charge is \(C'V\,dz\), giving \(\partial_z I=-C'\partial_t V\). The changing magnetic flux gives the complementary voltage drop \(\partial_z V=-L'\partial_t I\) by Faraday’s law. Differentiate once more to obtain \(\partial_{zz}V=L'C'\partial_{tt}V\). For a waveform travelling in the positive \(z\) direction, these equations require

\[v=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L'C'}},\qquad \frac{V}{I}=Z_0=\sqrt{\frac{L'}{C'}}.\]

Here \(Z_0\) is the ratio for a single travelling wave. The radial electric field crossed with the circumferential magnetic field gives axial Poynting flux. Indeed \(\int_a^b E_r H_\phi\,2\pi r\,dr=VI\). The surrounding dielectric carries this electromagnetic power in the ideal model.

What crosses the gap in a charging capacitor?

A changing electric field can also generate a magnetic field where there are no moving free charges. Charge a parallel-plate capacitor through a wire at steady current \(I\). A loop encircling the wire bounds a surface cut by the wire, but also a deformed surface passing between the plates. A consistent magnetic circulation for both surfaces requires the Maxwell term in Ampère’s law, \(\oint\mathbf H\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=I_{\rm free}+d\int\mathbf D\cdot d\mathbf A/dt\). Between wide circular plates the total electric displacement flux is the plate charge, so its derivative is exactly \(I\).

Take plate radius \(R=5.0\) cm and \(I=1.0\) A. Inside the gap, neglect fringing and assume slow charging, so that \(D\) is uniform over the plate area. A centred loop of radius \(r=2.5\) cm encloses the fraction \(r^2/R^2=1/4\) of the changing flux. The magnetic flux density there is \(B=\mu_0 I r/(2\pi R^2)=2.0\) µT. A loop just outside the plate radius encloses the full displacement current. Charge conservation, the electric field and the magnetic circulation thus give the same current whether the chosen surface cuts a wire or a dielectric.

A short pulse in polyethylene

Use \(a=0.450\) mm, \(b=1.475\) mm, \(\epsilon=2.25\epsilon_0\) and \(\mu=\mu_0\). The logarithm of the radius ratio is 1.187. The line constants are 105.44 pF/m and 237.43 nH/m. They give \(Z_0=47.45\) Ω and a propagation speed of \(2.00\times10^8\) m/s. A 1 V forward pulse has current \(1/47.45=21.1\) mA and instantaneous power 21.1 mW while its flat top passes a cross-section. It reaches the end of a 10 m line about 50 ns after launch.

Exercises on transmission and reflection

1. Change voltage without changing geometry

Double the amplitude of the forward pulse. Compare electric energy per metre, magnetic energy per metre and transmitted power with their original values.

Work out the energy factors

\(I=V/Z_0\) doubles. Thus the two energies \(C'V^2/2\) and \(L'I^2/2\) both quadruple, and \(VI\) also quadruples. Their ratio is unity for a travelling wave because \(L'/Z_0^2=C'\). The pulse speed stays fixed since neither line constant changed.

2. Match the load

Terminate the cable in \(R_L=2Z_0\). At the load the forward and backward voltages add, while their currents have opposite propagation signs. Find the reflected fraction of incident power.

Apply the boundary condition

Write \(V_++V_-=R_L(V_+-V_-)/Z_0\). This gives \(V_-/V_+=(R_L-Z_0)/(R_L+Z_0)=1/3\). Each wave’s power magnitude is proportional to its voltage squared, so \(1/9\) returns and \(8/9\) is absorbed. For \(R_L=Z_0\), the boundary condition accepts the incident wave without a reflected wave.

OneSimulation laboratory

Recover cable impedance from a cross-section

Compare recorded OneSimulation runs on this page. To test your own inputs, download the notebook and model files and run the model in an activated OneSimulation environment.

The cable notebook solves the electrostatic cross-section with the shield grounded, infers \(C'\) independently from charge and energy, and uses the homogeneous TEM relation for \(Z_0\). These steady field solutions give the constants needed for the pulse calculation. Coaxial cable field model.

  1. Baseline. Run both supplied STEP cross-sections and read the concentric row first. At 1 V, compare the two estimates of \(C'\) with 105.44 pF/m and \(Z_0\) with 47.45 Ω. Use a 0.1% relative tolerance.
  2. Change the dielectric. In the model cell set the multiplier of VacuumPermittivity() to 4.0 from 2.25 and rerun that cell and the line-constants cell. In the concentric result \(C'\) rises to 187.45 pF/m, \(L'\) stays near 237.43 nH/m and \(Z_0\) falls to 35.59 Ω. Explain all three changes from the field integrals.
  3. Move the inner conductor. Restore the original dielectric and compare the eccentric row with the baseline. Its centre is offset by 0.40 mm. On the close side the field concentrates. Expect about 113.61 pF/m and 44.04 Ω, within 0.1%. Check that changing the geometry alters impedance while leaving \(1/\sqrt{L'C'}\) unchanged to rounding.

Problem sheet 6 · Can impedance identify a dielectric by itself?

Mathematical field guide to electrodynamics

Begin with a voltmeter and a small surface. A voltage difference measures work per charge; electric flux measures a component of the field summed over area. Following those two measurements leads to the field equations. Later entries show how to choose boundary data, recognise a harmonic convention and check a result in a different inertial frame.

Choose a quantity you could measure

Begin with the field on the line between two large parallel plates. Take the left plate at \(+20\) V and the right at zero, separated by \(2.0\) cm. A positive test charge released between them accelerates to the right. Work per charge is \(20\,\mathrm{J/C}\); divided by \(0.020\,\mathrm m\) this gives \(E=1000\,\mathrm{V/m}=1000\,\mathrm{N/C}\). On a \(+3.0\,\mathrm{nC}\) bead the force is \(3.0\,\mu\mathrm N\). A voltmeter attached to the two plates still reads 20 V if their separation doubles at fixed potentials, even though the field halves.

This book uses SI. Magnetic flux density \(B\) is in tesla, magnetic field strength \(H\) in A/m, electric displacement \(D\) in C/m², and electric field \(E\) in V/m. In vacuum \(D=\epsilon_0E,\ B=\mu_0H\). For calculations here \(\epsilon_0\simeq8.8542\times10^{-12}\,\mathrm{F/m}\), \(\mu_0\simeq1.2566\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{H/m}\) and \(c=(\epsilon_0\mu_0)^{-1/2}\). If this is your first encounter with field theory, work the plate example, then the gradient and flux examples immediately below, before starting the charge chapter. Start with charge and potential.

Take a directional derivative before assigning a force

For a smooth scalar potential \(\phi\), a small displacement produces \(d\phi=\partial_x\phi\,dx+\partial_y\phi\,dy+\partial_z\phi\,dz=\nabla\phi\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell\). Work done by a static field on charge \(q\) is \(q\boldsymbol E\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=-q\,d\phi\). Equality for every displacement gives \(\boldsymbol E=-\nabla\phi\). The minus sign follows from the decrease in potential energy \(q\phi\) when the field does positive work.

In a patch with \(\phi=(3\,\mathrm{V/m^2})xy\), at \((x,y)=(2,1)\,\mathrm m\) the field is \((-3,-6,0)\,\mathrm{V/m}\). A \(1\,\mathrm{cm}\) displacement in \(+x\) raises the potential by \(0.030\) V; the field does negative work on a positive bead moving that way. Following any curve between two points in this static patch gives the same integral \(-\Delta\phi\). A time-varying magnetic flux changes that last measurement.

Attach the right area and volume to a coordinate

A radial increment is not itself a spherical volume. At radius \(r\), angular increments sweep arc lengths \(r\,d\theta\) and \(r\sin\theta\,d\varphi\), where \(\theta\) is the polar angle. Their product gives \(dA=r^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi=r^2d\Omega\). Integrating over the sphere gives \(4\pi r^2\), so \(dV=r^2dr\,d\Omega\). In cylindrical coordinates the analogous azimuthal arc is \(s\,d\varphi\), giving \(dV=s\,ds\,d\varphi\,dz\).

A uniformly charged sphere of radius \(3\,\mathrm{cm}\) contains only \(1/27\) of its charge inside \(1\,\mathrm{cm}\), since the volume integral scales as \(r^3\). A detector viewing a source through a polar cap of half-angle \(60^\circ\) subtends \(2\pi(1-\cos60^\circ)=\pi\) steradians. It collects one quarter of an isotropic source's total power. A dipole weights the same solid-angle elements unequally. Integrate the dipole radiation pattern.

Infer a local divergence from flux

Across the two opposite \(x\)-faces of a small rectangular box, the net outward flux of \(\boldsymbol F\) is approximately \([F_x(x+dx)-F_x(x)]\,dy\,dz\). Dividing by the volume and adding the other face pairs gives \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol F\). Internal faces cancel when boxes are joined. The resulting identity is \(\int_V\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol F\,dV=\oint_{\partial V}\boldsymbol F\cdot\boldsymbol n\,dA\) with outward normal. Applied to \(\boldsymbol D\), the flux counts free charge.

In a cube of side \(0.10\,\mathrm m\) let \(D_x=(2.0\,\mu\mathrm{C/m^3})x\) and other components vanish. The divergence is \(2.0\,\mu\mathrm{C/m^3}\), so the cube contains \(2.0\) nC of free charge. Directly, the \(x\)-faces differ by \(0.20\,\mu\mathrm{C/m^2}\) over \(0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}\), giving the same result. Moving the cube does not change the difference here. A single point's field magnitude alone would not give the enclosed charge.

Follow a loop with an oriented normal

A small loop in the \(xy\)-plane, traversed counterclockwise as seen from \(+z\), has circulation per area \(\partial_xF_y-\partial_yF_x=(\nabla\times\boldsymbol F)_z\). Summing loops that tile a surface cancels interior edges, leaving \(\oint_{\partial S}\boldsymbol F\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=\int_S(\nabla\times\boldsymbol F)\cdot\boldsymbol n\,dA\). Curl measures circulation density. A field can have a large magnitude parallel to a straight wire while having zero circulation around a distant closed loop.

Take a stationary loop of area \(0.020\,\mathrm{m^2}\), with uniform normal field \(B_z\) growing at \(0.30\,\mathrm{T/s}\). Faraday's law gives \(\oint\boldsymbol E\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=-6.0\,\mathrm{mV}\) in the stated counterclockwise orientation. Reverse the normal and path together; the reported circulation changes sign. A moving conducting loop also requires the magnetic force per charge \(\boldsymbol v\times\boldsymbol B\) in its measured emf. Work through a moving bar.

Keep point and sheet sources when differentiating

For \(r>0\), \(\nabla\cdot(\hat{\boldsymbol r}/r^2)=r^{-2}\partial_r(r^2/r^2)=0\). But every sphere enclosing the origin has outward flux \(4\pi\). A volume source must preserve that finite integral even as the sphere shrinks. Distributionally, \(\nabla\cdot(\hat{\boldsymbol r}/r^2)=4\pi\delta^3(\boldsymbol r)\). The delta has units \(\mathrm{m^{-3}}\) and obeys \(\int f(\boldsymbol r)\delta^3(\boldsymbol r-\boldsymbol a)dV=f(\boldsymbol a)\) for smooth test functions.

A sheet at \(z=0\) with density \(\sigma=2.0\,\mathrm{nC/m^2}\) instead has volume density \(\rho=\sigma\delta(z)\); integrating through the sheet over \(0.40\,\mathrm{m^2}\) gives \(0.80\) nC. A finite-volume model that smears the sheet over thickness \(h\) needs \(\rho\simeq\sigma/h\). Refining \(h\) doubles the peak if the thickness halves, while its integral stays fixed. Derive the source term from a test function.

Choose the boundary condition that matches the apparatus

Gauss's law and electrostatics give \(-\nabla\cdot(\epsilon\nabla\phi)=\rho_{\rm free}\) in a linear dielectric. If \(\epsilon\) varies, leaving it inside the divergence keeps the correct flux. A voltage source supplies Dirichlet data \(\phi=g\). Specified normal electric displacement supplies Neumann data \(-\epsilon\partial_n\phi=D_n\) for scalar \(\epsilon\). Prescribing normal flux everywhere requires \(\oint D_n dA=Q_{\rm free}\). A compatible pure Neumann problem fixes the potential up to a constant in a connected region. An isolated neutral conductor is equipotential at an unknown voltage, with its total charge constrained; a grounded conductor instead has a specified voltage and can exchange charge.

To test uniqueness with fixed electrode voltages, subtract two candidate solutions for the same source. Their difference \(u\) satisfies \(\nabla\cdot(\epsilon\nabla u)=0\) and vanishes on the electrodes. Integrating \(u\nabla\cdot(\epsilon\nabla u)\) by parts gives \(\int\epsilon|\nabla u|^2dV=0\) if the boundary term vanishes everywhere. For positive \(\epsilon\) the difference is constant and at a fixed electrode it is zero. Test a grounded-domain solution on a grid. Compare a floating electrode with ground.

Turn a point response into an extended-source solution

Let \(G\) solve \(-\nabla^2G(\boldsymbol r,\boldsymbol r')=\delta^3(\boldsymbol r-\boldsymbol r')\) with homogeneous boundary conditions. In vacuum, integrating weighted responses gives \(\phi(\boldsymbol r)=\epsilon_0^{-1}\int G(\boldsymbol r,\boldsymbol r')\rho(\boldsymbol r')\,dV'\). Applying \(-\nabla^2\) under the integral recovers \(\rho/\epsilon_0\). Nonzero prescribed boundary voltages supply an additional source-free solution. In free space \(G=1/(4\pi|\boldsymbol r-\boldsymbol r'|)\), with units of inverse length.

Put a point charge at \(z=a>0\) above a grounded infinite plane. Subtract the free-space response of a reflected charge at \(-a\). On the plane both distances agree, so the potential vanishes there; in the accessible half-space only the actual charge supplies a delta source. Uniqueness justifies the image construction. For \(q=1.0\,\mathrm{nC}\) and \(a=1.0\,\mathrm{cm}\), the force from the image has magnitude \(q^2/[4\pi\epsilon_0(2a)^2]=22.5\,\mu\mathrm N\), directed towards the plane. Construct the bounded Green function and its reciprocity.

Apply a pillbox and a ribbon at a material interface

Point a unit normal \(\boldsymbol n\) from medium 1 into medium 2. A shrinking Gaussian pillbox leaves its two faces and gives \(\boldsymbol n\cdot(\boldsymbol D_2-\boldsymbol D_1)=\sigma_f\) and \(\boldsymbol n\cdot(\boldsymbol B_2-\boldsymbol B_1)=0\). A narrow rectangular integration ribbon leaves the two tangential segments, giving \(\boldsymbol n\times(\boldsymbol E_2-\boldsymbol E_1)=0\) for finite magnetic fields and \(\boldsymbol n\times(\boldsymbol H_2-\boldsymbol H_1)=\boldsymbol K_f\) for a free sheet current and finite displacement field.

At a charge-free interface from vacuum to a linear dielectric with \(\epsilon_r=4\), suppose the normal and tangential components of \(\boldsymbol E_1\) are 800 and 300 V/m. Then \(E_{2n}=200\,\mathrm{V/m}\), while \(E_{2t}=300\,\mathrm{V/m}\). The normal displacement is \(7.08\,\mathrm{nC/m^2}\) on both sides; the total field angle changes. A sheet of bound charge is consistent with these conditions because the pillbox for \(\boldsymbol D\) counts only free charge. Work through polarisation and free charge.

Use a conservation law to identify a missing current

If charge leaves a fixed region through its surface at a positive rate, the charge inside must decrease. This gives \(\partial_t\rho+\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol J=0\) after applying the divergence theorem. Taking the divergence of \(\nabla\times\boldsymbol H=\boldsymbol J_f+\partial_t\boldsymbol D\) recovers the same statement: the divergence of a curl vanishes and \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol D=\rho_f\). The displacement-current term makes the field equation compatible with charging an insulator-bounded region.

A current of \(2.0\,\mathrm{mA}\) charges large capacitor plates of area \(5.0\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^2}\). With negligible fringing, \(A\,\partial_t D_n=I\), hence \(\partial_tD_n=0.40\,\mathrm{A/m^2}\). In a vacuum gap this means \(\partial_tE_n=4.52\times10^{10}\,\mathrm{V/(m\,s)}\). The conduction current crosses a wire section and the displacement current crosses a surface through the gap. Either spanning surface predicts the same magnetic circulation around its common rim. Follow the charging capacitor through space and time.

Reconstruct fields from potentials and test a gauge change

Locally, \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol B=0\) allows \(\boldsymbol B=\nabla\times\boldsymbol A\). Faraday's law then makes \(\boldsymbol E+\partial_t\boldsymbol A\) curl free and locally expressible as \(-\nabla\phi\). In simply connected regions the scalar potential for this combination is single-valued. Under \(\boldsymbol A'=\boldsymbol A+\nabla\chi,\ \phi'=\phi-\partial_t\chi\), the added terms in \(\boldsymbol E\) cancel by equality of mixed derivatives; the curl of the added gradient in \(\boldsymbol B\) is zero.

For a static uniform \(B_0\hat{\boldsymbol z}\), use either \(\boldsymbol A_1=B_0x\hat{\boldsymbol y}\) or \(\boldsymbol A_2=B_0(-y\hat{\boldsymbol x}+x\hat{\boldsymbol y})/2\). Their difference is the gradient of \(\chi=-B_0xy/2\). A rectangular loop of area \(0.050\,\mathrm{m^2}\) normal to \(B_0=0.20\,\mathrm T\) has \(\oint\boldsymbol A\cdot d\boldsymbol\ell=0.010\,\mathrm{Wb}\) in either gauge. Follow the gauge choice into time-dependent fields.

Use the double curl to choose between waves and diffusion

The identity \(\nabla\times(\nabla\times\boldsymbol E)=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol E)-\nabla^2\boldsymbol E\) connects curl measurements to a spatial operator acting on each Cartesian component. In a uniform source-free dielectric with \(\boldsymbol J=\sigma\boldsymbol E\) and \(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol E=0\), combine Faraday and Ampère–Maxwell to obtain \(\nabla^2\boldsymbol E=\mu\sigma\partial_t\boldsymbol E+\mu\epsilon\partial_t^2\boldsymbol E\). Setting \(\sigma=0\) gives wave speed \(1/\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}\). For harmonic fields the ratio of displacement to conduction currents is \(\omega\epsilon/\sigma\); when small, dropping the second time derivative gives magnetic diffusivity \(1/(\mu\sigma)\).

At \(1.0\,\mathrm{kHz}\), a vacuum wavelength is roughly 300 km. In a good conductor with \(\mu\simeq\mu_0\) and \(\sigma=5.8\times10^7\,\mathrm{S/m}\), the harmonic skin depth is only \(\sqrt{2/(\omega\mu\sigma)}=2.09\,\mathrm{mm}\). Inserting the vacuum wavelength as a penetration length in the conductor would miss the dominant term in its field equation. Compare a field step with a harmonic field.

Close an energy balance over a chosen volume

Dot the Ampère–Maxwell equation with \(\boldsymbol E\), dot Faraday's law with \(\boldsymbol H\), and use \(\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol E\times\boldsymbol H)=\boldsymbol H\cdot\nabla\times\boldsymbol E-\boldsymbol E\cdot\nabla\times\boldsymbol H\). For linear, time-independent, nondispersive media with symmetric constitutive tensors this gives \(\partial_tu+\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol S=-\boldsymbol J_f\cdot\boldsymbol E\), with \(u=(\boldsymbol E\cdot\boldsymbol D+\boldsymbol B\cdot\boldsymbol H)/2\) and \(\boldsymbol S=\boldsymbol E\times\boldsymbol H\). In vacuum the same formula holds directly. In dispersive matter the energy stored in the material's dynamical response must also be accounted for.

Suppose 3.0 W flows into a stationary resistor enclosure and 0.20 W leaves by other electromagnetic paths. During a transient the field energy inside rises at \(0.30\,\mathrm{J/s}\). The remaining \(2.50\,\mathrm W\) is delivered to matter. With a uniform Ohmic field \(E=10\,\mathrm{V/m}\), conductivity \(5\,\mathrm{S/m}\) and resistor volume \(0.005\,\mathrm{m^3}\), the independent volume integral \(\sigma E^2V\) gives that same 2.50 W. Derive energy transport from the fields.

Average a real product before interpreting a complex power

Choose peak phasors and write \(\boldsymbol E(t)=\operatorname{Re}[\widetilde{\boldsymbol E}e^{-i\omega t}]\), so \(\partial_t\) acts as \(-i\omega\). A forward attenuating wave can then be written \(e^{ikz-i\omega t}\) with positive \(\operatorname{Im}k\). Some chapters instead state \(e^{+i\omega t}\); conjugate the phasors and the spatial exponential consistently when switching conventions. Time-averaged physical powers have the same sign. Multiplying the two real fields produces constant and double-frequency terms; averaging removes the latter, giving \(\langle\boldsymbol S\rangle=\tfrac12\operatorname{Re}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol E}\times\widetilde{\boldsymbol H}^*)\). Using rms phasors absorbs the factor one half.

With orthogonal transverse peak amplitudes \(E=10\,\mathrm{V/m}\), \(H=0.040\,\mathrm{A/m}\) and a relative temporal phase of \(60^\circ\), the mean flux magnitude along their cross product is \(0.5(10)(0.040)\cos60^\circ=0.100\,\mathrm{W/m^2}\). A phase difference of \(90^\circ\) gives zero mean power even when both local amplitudes are large. Compare average and reactive power in a conductor.

Check two observers' fields without transforming every component

Fix SI components and the metric \((+,-,-,-)\), with \(x^0=ct\). The field combinations \(I_1=B^2-E^2/c^2\) and \(I_2=\boldsymbol E\cdot\boldsymbol B\) have the same values under proper Lorentz transformations. The first follows from the tensor contraction \(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=2I_1\); the second is proportional to the contraction with the dual tensor and changes sign under a spatial reflection. The factor \(1/c^2\) converts squared electric-field units into squared tesla. A plane wave has \(E=cB\) and perpendicular fields, giving two zero invariants even though it carries energy.

Take \(\boldsymbol E=(3.0\times10^5,0,0)\,\mathrm{V/m}\) and \(\boldsymbol B=(0,0,2.0\times10^{-3})\,\mathrm T\). Then \(I_1\simeq3.0\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{T^2}>0,\ I_2=0\). For an observer moving at \(\boldsymbol v=\boldsymbol E\times\boldsymbol B/B^2\), the perpendicular transformation gives \(\boldsymbol E'=\gamma(\boldsymbol E+\boldsymbol v\times\boldsymbol B)=0\). The required speed is \(1.5\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}<c\), along \(-y\). From the invariant, \(B'=\sqrt{I_1}\simeq1.73\,\mathrm{mT}\). Both observers can therefore check the same physical example with different field readings. Compare measurements at one event. Derive the tensor contractions.

Turn a derivation into a solver check

Choose a case with an exact result and use the same units, geometry, source and boundary data in the numerical problem. For a grounded square of side \(L\), choose \(\rho=\rho_0\sin(\pi x/L)\sin(\pi y/L)\). Applying \(-\nabla^2\) to the same sine product multiplies it by \(2\pi^2/L^2\). Therefore \(\phi_{\rm centre}=\rho_0L^2/(2\pi^2\epsilon_0)\). With \(L=1\,\mathrm m,\ \rho_0=2.0\,\mathrm{nC/m^3}\), the centre voltage is \(11.4433\,\mathrm V\).

A square mesh with \(N\) intervals per side has \(h=L/N\). The five-point negative Laplacian acting on that sampled sine has eigenvalue \(8\sin^2[\pi/(2N)]/h^2\). For \(N=12\) it predicts \(11.5089\,\mathrm V\); the discrete equations can be solved to roundoff and still differ from the continuum by \(0.0656\,\mathrm V\). Doubling \(N\) should reduce this nodal error by close to four because \(\sin x=x-x^3/6+\cdots\). Compare the calculated value, the source scaling and the refinement rate. These three observations distinguish a wrong sign or unit from a converged solution on a coarse mesh. Run the square-domain experiment and its OneSimulation example.
